[SECTION: LHC_CASE_001]
Case: Service 17945/25 (Baber Maseeh Vs Cantonment Executive Officer Sargodha etc).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Service 17945/25. Title: Service 17945/25 (Baber Maseeh Vs Cantonment Executive Officer Sargodha etc). Official Ref: 2026LHC5569. Author Judge: Justice Ms. Shireen Imran. Judgment Date (as displayed): 04-09-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5569.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 10 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Stereo .  H  C  J  D  A 38
JUDGMENT SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT ,  LAHORE
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
Writ Petition  No.  17945  of 20 2 5 .
Baber Masih
V ersus
Cantonment Executive Officer, Sargodha  & another
JUDGMENT
Date of hearing:  0 3 . 09 .2026 .
Petitioner  by:  Syed Hamid Raza Bukhari, Advocate.
Respondent s  by:  M ian Asghar Ali, Advocate .
SHIREEN IMRAN,  J :  Through this petition, petitioner
has called in question order dated 13.12.2024 passed by respondent
No.2, whereby  the  departmental appeal  filed by him  was  partly
accepted  and  consequently  vide  order dated  10.01.2025 ,  he  was
reinstate d  in  service  w.e.f.  26.11.2012 ,  the  date  when  he  was
dismissed  from service , however, his claim qua back benefits was
declined while treating his  period of absence  from duty  as Extra
Ordinary Leave (EOL) .
2.  Learned counsel for the petitioner contends that the petitioner
was appointed as a Sanitary Worker on 06.10.2011 and his services
were regularized on 30.12.2011 ; that ,  being implicated in  a baseless
criminal  case bearing  FIR No.655/2012 , he remained  in  judicial
W.P.  No. 17 945  of 202 5 .  2
custody ,  due  to  which  he  remained  absen t  from  duty  and
consequently ,  the  department  stuck  off  him from service vide order
dated  12.02.2014  and  that  after his  acquittal  from the  aforesaid
criminal case by this Court  vide judgment dated 11.11.2021 passed in
Criminal Appeal No.166 - J/2017  th ough  the departmental appeal filed
by the petitioner for his reinstatement in to  service was accepted  and
he was reinstated in service  w.e.f.  26.11.2012  but his  claim  q ua  back
benefits w as  declined  without considering the fact that all acquittals
are h onourable .  Reliance has been placed upon the judgments reported
as  Dr.  Muhammad  Islam  v.  Government  of  N. - W.F.P.  through
Secretary, Food, Agriculture, Livestock and Cooperative Department,
Peshawar and 2 others  ( 1998 SCMR 1993 ) ,  Attaullah Sheikh v.
WAPDA and others  [ 2001 PLC (C.S .) 316 ] ,  Tahir Kazmi and  another
v. Inspector General of P o lice,  Punjab ,  Lahore  and others  [ 2026 PLC
(C.S.)  5 10 ] ,  The  Province  of  Sindh  through  Chief  S e cretary
Government of Sindh and others v. Raj Kumar Lohana and others
[ 2026 PLC  (C.S.) 40 4 ] and  Ghulam Murtaza  v. District Police
Officer, Gujrat & others  ( 2026 SCMR 843 ) .
3.  Conversely,  learned  counsel  appearing  on  behalf  of  the
respondents submits that the instant petition is misconceived and has
been  filed  to  secure  an  undue  benefit.  It  is  contended  that  no
fundamental right of the petitioner has been infringed and as the
petitioner remained absent from duty and did not perform any work,
no  mala  fide  or  ill - will  can  be  attributed  to  the  departmental
authorities while declining his request for grant of back benefits.
W.P.  No. 17 945  of 202 5 .  3
Reliance has been placed upon the judgment of the Hon’ble Supreme
Court of Pakistan in Civil Petition Nos.1935 to 1938 of 2018 and
connected matters, titled  Shafique Ahmed & three others v. Provincial
Police Officer, KP & others , dated 03.12.2025.
4.  Arguments heard. Record perused.
5 .  Firstly, taking up the objection raised by the  learned  counsel
for the respondents  against maintainability of this  petition  in absence
of  infringement  of any fundamental right ,  this  Court is of the view that
t he petitioner seeks enforcement of his  fundamental  rights  guaranteed
under Article s  10 - A  of  the  Constitution  of the Islamic Republic of
Pakistan  (the Cons titution)  and service rights ,  arising from orders of
the competent authorities, and the impugned orders are amenable to
judicial  review  where  they  are  alleged  to  be  without  lawful
justification. Likewise, absence of  any  specific finding  against an
authority does not preclude this Court from examining whether the
impugned action is lawful, particularly where the challenge concerns
the denial of consequential service benefits following  reinstatement .
T hus ,  instant  petition  cannot  be  dismissed  on  the  point  of
maintainability ,  resultantly the objection under discussion is hereby
spurned .
6 .  Now reverting to merits of the case, it  is  observed that the
controversy essentially relates to the petitioner’s entitlement to back
benefits for the  period intervening his removal and reinstatement. The
petitioner was appointed  as Sanitary Worker  on 06.10.2011 and his
services were regularized on 30.12.2011. During service, he was
W.P.  No. 17 945  of 202 5 .  4
implicated in a criminal case and remained in judicial custody, owing
to which he could not attend his duties . He was ultimately acquitted
by this Court vide judgment dated 11.11.2021. Though  the  petitioner
has been reinstated in service by treating the period of his absence as
EOL  but  his request for grant of back benefits has been declined
despite the fact  that the authority, while reinstating  him  in service,
itself admitted that his absence was  due to the  circumstances  beyond
his control. Once the authority itself admitted that the absence of the
petitioner was not intentional  rather  it was  due to circumstances
beyond his control, his request for grant of back benefits was to be
considered  leniently.  It  is  trite  law  that  discretion  vested  in  an
authority should be exercised justly and  fairly . Dealing with  somewhat
similar situation, the Hon’ble Supreme Court of Pak istan in the case of
Shafique Ahmed & three others  (supra) has, inter alia, concluded as
under:  -
“ 5. *****  The core requirement for any legitimate
and legal administration of power now hinges on its
justification.  Sound  justification  for  government
actions  involves  establishing  the  cogency  and
persuasiveness of the rationale behind government
actions and requires such actions to be scrutinised
for their rationality and reasonableness.  Thus, if
Article 10 - A of the Constitution serves as a decisive
departure from the restrictive culture of authority, it
becomes evident what it aims to transition  towards:
a culture of justification. In this new model, every
exertion of power must stand on its own merits,
compelling  authorities  to  substantiate  their
decisions  through  sound  reasoning  rather  than
relying on the fear associated with the force they
command …
… …
7.  It  is  crucial  to  emphasise  that  similar
provisions  are  prevalent  across  nearly  all  Civil
Servants Acts applicable at both the Federal and
W.P.  No. 17 945  of 202 5 .  5
provincial levels. The pivotal focus of our discussion
lies  in  the  second  proviso  to  Section  17.  The
straightforward  language  of  the  second  proviso
clearly indicates that discretion to determine back
pay is conferred upon the authority responsible for
reinstating a civil  servant into service. Notably, the
command of the law is unequivocal; the granting of
such arrears is not an automatic consequence but
rather a decision for which the authority possesses
discretion, emphasising the equitable nature of such
power.  However,  it  must  be  recognised  that  this
legislation that bestows these discretionary powers
lacks  a  clearly  defined  set  of  conditions,
circumstances, and standards to guide the authority
in exercising its powers. This absence of specificity is
deliberate  and  serv es  a  distinct  purpose  as  the
discretion is inherently tied to the latitude granted by
the legislative enactment and allows the designated
official or authority to make determinations within a
defined spectrum.  The decisionmaker is not confined
to  the  belief  that  there  exists  only  one  correct
resolution. Instead, while the authority’s choices
should align with established legal principles of
reason and justice, its judgment must be rooted in
careful consideration and not arbitrary preferences.
Exercising dis cretion is a grave responsibility, a
sacred  trust  that  demands  utmost  diligence,
vigilance,  and  fairness.  This  power  should  be
wielded  with  precision,  rationality,  and  an
unwavering  commitment  to  the  principles
underpinning natural justice. Any deviation from
this meticulous duty threatens to compromise the
integrity  of  the  decision - making  process  and
jeopardises  the  foundational  elements  of
governance. The authority, serving as the custodian
of  this  discretion,  is  mandated  by  both  the
Constitution  and  serv ice  laws  to  act  with
impartiality,  fairness,  and  transparency.  It  must
consciously avoid actions that appear arbitrary or
whimsical  and  instead  embrace  a  thoughtful
approach  that  acknowledges  and  balances  the
diverse interests and rights of all parties involved.
The  decision - making  process  must  be  guided  by
relevant  factors,  with  precise  articulation  and
disclosure of the reasons for decisions. At its core,
the principle of fairness —  central to administrative
law -  compels  the  authority  to  act  without  bias,
prejudice, or ulterior motives, to guarantee that all
civil servants receive equitable treatment. Any failure
to adhere to this principle constitutes a breach of the
duty of fairness and invites judicial scrutiny.
……
W.P.  No. 17 945  of 202 5 .  6
12.  After it is determined whether the civil servant
was  employed  anywhere  during  the  intervening
period or not, the next stage  is to determine how
back  pay  should  be  granted.  The  question  of
awarding back pay in cases where a civil servant
has been unfairly dismissed is guided by a general
principle: such pay should ordinarily be granted to
those  civil  servants  who  have  been  unlawfully
deprived of their positions. However, this principle
is not absolute and is subject to various conditions.
The decision could grant back pay in full or in part,
or it could deny back pay altogether. There is no
rigid  formula  that  dictates  under  what  specific
circumstances back pay should be authorised. The
determination depends heavily on the unique facts
and  circumstances  of  each  case.  It  would  be
erroneous to  assume that entitlement to back pay is
warranted solely based on technical legalities that
invalidate a dismissal order. Instead, the awarding of
back pay — whether in whole or in part, or none at all
—  requires a nuanced evaluation of several factors.
These factors may include the nature of the civil
servant’s appointment, the length and quality of his
service,  any  proven  misconduct,  his  financial
situation,  and  other  relevant  considerations.  This
careful  analysis  seeks  to  balance  the  competing
interests at  play. Due to the diverse nature of such
cases,  there  is  no  one - size - fits - all  approach  for
providing  relief  in  the  form  of  back  pay.  Each
situation must be assessed on its own merits, and this
evaluation is often entrusted to the discretion of the
relevant  authority.  However,  it  needs  to  be
emphasised that while granting full back pay is
generally the standard, it falls upon the authority to
demonstrate compelling reasons that would justify a
deviation from this norm. At this stage, the authority
is tasked  with exercising its discretion judiciously
and consistently with established legal standards.
This  discretion  must  be  exercised  thoughtfully ,
based on a clear and convincing rationale that is
evident  from  the  official  record.  The  exercise  of
discretion should adhere to the principles of reason
and justice — based on law rather than whim — and
must avoid arbitrary or capricious decisions . ”
14.*****  This  deprivation  connects  directly  to
Article 9 of the Constitution, which guarantees the
right to life. The term ‘life’ encompasses a broader
interpretation, encapsulating not only mere existence
but also the means to sustain it. ”
… …
(emphasis provided)
W.P.  No. 17 945  of 202 5 .  7
7 .  The question of reinstatement of an employee came under
consideration before the Hon’ble Supreme Court of Pakistan in  the
case of  The Province of Sindh through Chief Secretary, Government
of Sindh  and  others  (supra) .  The Hon’ble Supreme Court, while
considering  the  said  question,  settled  the  matter  by,  inter  alia,
observing as follows:  -
“ 10.  In unison, a quick look to the glossary of the
expression Reinstatement communicates the meaning
of restoring something to a former, original, normal,
or  unimpaired  condition.  If  we  gaze  it  into  the
framework  of  employment,  it  characteristically
ensues  when  a  terminated,  dismissed  or  laid  off
employee is sent back to his former occupation status
which includes the reestablishing and protection of
his seniority rights, privileges or other benefits that
were previously lost. The doctrine of reinstatement
also affirms a legal precept that allows a party to
bring back a contract or agreement to its original
terms after it has been terminated or breached. For
all intent and purposes, this doctrine applies with
diversity  and  miscellany  that  includes  different
situations including the reinstatement of an employee
in the labour and/or civil service laws to his job after
an  unjust/wrongful  termination  or  dismissal  from
service and also the renaissance or reactivation of a
contract/agreement  etc.  Furthermore,  this  also
facilitates to rectify errors, settle disputes, and make
sure  that  person  concerned  is  not  unjustifiably
penalized which is in fact a fundamental perception
of guaranteeing justice and upholding unprejudiced
relationship in the legal and contractual agreements
or terms and conditions of employment/service . ”
8 .  Moreover,  the  question  of  grant  of  back  benefits  upon
reinstatement of an employee has been elaborately considered and
settled by the Hon’ble Supreme Court of Pakistan in  Tahir Kazmi and
another  (supra), wherein the Hon’ble Court, inter alia, observed as
follows:  -
W.P.  No. 17 945  of 202 5 .  8
“ 4.  The  core  issue  is  the  entitlement  to  back
benefits. Grant of back benefits while not expressly
defined in the service laws of Pakistan is a well -
established  remedy  in  Pakistan  as  well  as  other
jurisdictions. As defined in Black's Law Dictionary,
"back pay" refers to the salary an employee should
have  received  but  was  denied  due  to  unlawful
employer action.  A "back pay award" restores the
economic status quo ante, compensating for the loss
incurred.  In our jurisdiction, the term "back benefits"
typically  includes  arrears  of  salary  and  related
service entitlements.  Although broader meanings may
exist,  this  discussion  confines  "back  benefits"  to
arrears of pay and other emoluments denied due to
wrongful dismissal or removal.
… …
11.  This  constitutional  foundation  finds
affirmation in judicial precedent, most notably in
Tariq Mahmood  and Muhammad Sharif  where this
Court reaffirmed that the grant of back benefits is
the norm and denial of service benefits to such
reinstated employee is an exception.  Any deviation
from this must be justified by clear evidence that the
employee was gainfully employed elsewhere during
the intervening period . The logic is straightforward  -
when a court declares an official action unlawful, it
must undo not only the decision but the resulting
harm. Restitution is therefore integral to corrective
justice, not an exercise of discretion. ”
9 .  In the present case, petitioner’s absence was occasioned by
his incarceration in a criminal case in which he was ultimately
acquitted.  More  importantly,  the  departmental  appellate  authority
expressly found his absence to be beyond his control and did not find
any misconduct surviving against him. The respondents have also not
produced  any material showing  that  the petitioner was  gainfully
employed elsewhere or earned any income during the intervening
period. In t hese peculiar circumstances,  mere fact that the petitioner
did not physically perform his duties cannot constitute sufficient
justification for denying him the back benefits  upon  reinstatement .
W.P.  No. 17 945  of 202 5 .  9
1 0 .  That  regardless  of the extent of financial benefits granted, the
intervening period between a civil servant's removal and subsequent
reinstatement  must  be  regularized  to  maintain  the  continuity  of
service. This is, however, a separate theme from back benefits. The
reason for grant of extraordinary leave without pay or "leave of the
kind due" is to ensure that the service record of the civil servant
remains  unbroken ,  thereby  safeguarding  eligibility  for  pension,
promotion, seniority, and other ancillary service benefits. As held by
the Hon ’ble Supreme Court,  any failure to recognize such intervening
periods ,  results in an unlawful forfeiture of rights .
1 1 .  The direction to treat the intervening period as EOL does not
answer the question of back benefits. EOL may regulate the manner in
which an absence is accounted for in the service record  but it cannot,
without  lawful  basis,  be  used  to  defeat  the  consequences  of
reinstatement where the competent authority has it self recognized that
the  absence  was  beyond  the  employee’s  control  and  that  no
misconduct remained against him .
1 2 .  The judgment relied upon by the respondents in  Shafique
Ahmed & three others  (supra), instead of lending support to the
respondents, goes otherwise, as in the said judgment it has been held
that grant of back benefits be determined  justly  and  fairly  in light of
the circumstances attending the employee’s a bsence, nature and basis
of reinstatement, findings recorded by the competent authority and the
guidelines provided by the superior courts on the subject .
W.P.  No. 17 945  of 202 5 .  10
1 3 .  For  the  foregoing  reasons,  it  is  held  that  the  question
regarding grant of back benefits has not been dealt with in accordance
with the guidelines provided by the superior courts  and law  on the
subject. Consequently, I deem it appropriate to remit the matter back
for decision afresh on the point of back benefit s.
1 4 .  Accordingly, this petition is  allowed . The  impugned order s
dated 13.12.2024  and 10.01.2025 , to the extent of denial of back
benefits ,  are  set aside and the matter is remitted to  respondent No.2  for
decision  afresh ,  in  view  of  the  observations  made  hereinabove,
especially the observations of the Hon’ble Supreme Court of Pakistan,
contained in paragraphs No. 5,  7, 8, 11, 12 and 14 of  Shafique Ahmed
&  three  others  (supra) ,  The  Province  of  Sindh  through  Chief
Secretary, Government of Sindh and others  (supra)  and  Tahir Kazmi
and another  (supra) .  This shall be done  within a peri od of  two  (02)
months ,  from the date of  receipt of  a  certified copy  of this  judgment .
(SHIREEN IMRAN)
JUDGE
Approved for reporting.
JUDGE
U mar

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_002]
Case: Crl. Misc. 43416/26 (Ali Shahzad alias Shan Ali Vs The State etc).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Crl. Misc. 43416/26. Title: Crl. Misc. 43416/26 (Ali Shahzad alias Shan Ali Vs The State etc). Official Ref: 2026LHC5543. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Ghulam Sarwar Nihung. Judgment Date (as displayed): 03-09-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5543.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 11 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Form No: HCJD/C-121
ORDER SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT, LAHORE.
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
Crl. Misc. No. 43416-B of 2026
Ali Shahzad alias Shan Ali  Versus  The State etc.
Sr. No. of order/
Proceeding
Date of
order/
Proceeding
Order with signature of  Judge, and that of parties or counsel, where
necessary.
31.08.2026  Malik Ali Ahmad Noul, Advocate for the petitioner.
Hafiz Asghar Ali, DPG with Afzal, SI for the State.
Syed Qalb Abbas, Advocate for the complainant.
Through  the  instant  petition  under  section  497,
Cr.P.C., the petitioner, Ali Shahzad alias Shan Ali, seeks post-
arrest  bail  in  case  FIR  No.135  of  2025,  dated  25.02.2025,
registered under Sections 324, 148 and 149, PPC, at Police
Station Syed Wala, District Nankana Sahib.
2.  The Complainant has alleged that the Petitioner, while
armed  with  a  pistol,  fired  at  Farooq  Ahmad,  son  of  the
complainant, which allegedly struck the latter on the left side of
his chest.
3.  Learned counsel for the petitioner submits that the
petitioner has been arrested since 03.03.2025. The challan was
submitted before the learned Trial Court after significant delay,
whereas  the  charge  was  framed  only  on  02.03.2026.  The
petitioner contends that the prosecution has failed to conclude the
trial  within  the  statutory  period  despite  the  petitioner’s
continuous imprisonment and that the delay is not attributable to
any act or omission on his part. Learned counsel, therefore, prays
that the petitioner be released on bail on the statutory ground of
delay in conclusion of the trial.
4.  Conversely,  learned  Deputy  Prosecutor  General
assisted  by  the  learned  counsel  for  the  complainant,  while
disputing the petition, submits that the petitioner is specifically
nominated in the FIR with a direct role of causing a firearm
injury  to  the  injured  witness.  He  further  submits  that  the
Crl. Misc. No.43416-B of 2026  2
prosecution witnesses were produced before the learned Trial
Court but could not be examined or cross-examined due to  the
conduct of the defence. It is, therefore, maintained that the
petitioner is unable to claim the benefit of a delay which he
himself contributed to.
5.  Arguments  heard.  Available  record,  including  the
report furnished by the learned Trial Court, has been examined
with the able assistance of learned counsel for the parties.
6.  The petitioner seeks post-arrest bail on the ground of
statutory delay under the third proviso to Section 497(1), Cr.P.C.
The history of the earlier bail proceedings has also been taken
into account. The first post-arrest bail petition, bearing Crl. Misc.
No.47874-B of 2025, was dismissed for non-prosecution on
08.09.2025. The second petition, bearing Crl. Misc. No.52358-B
of  2025,  was  dismissed  on  merits  on  29.09.2025.  Another
petition for the same relief, bearing Crl. Misc. No.42342-B of
2026, was not pressed and was disposed of accordingly on
10.07.2026. The applications moved before the courts below on
the statutory ground were also dismissed.
7.  It is a settled principle that a successive bail petition is
maintainable only upon the availability of a fresh ground. This
principle is elaborately enunciated in  “ Muhammad Usman v. The
State and another ”  ( 2024 SCMR 28 ), relevant extract of which is
reproduced as under:-
"It is settled law that a second bail petition repeating the same
grounds that were earlier taken is not competent. Moreover,
the  grounds  raised  by  an  accused  in  a  subsequent  bail
application which were available at the time of filing of the
earlier petition could also not be treated as fresh grounds nor
urged for the purposes of seeking the same relief. This Court
has  already  highlighted  the  principles  regarding
maintainability of a subsequent bail petition. If the ground on
which bail has been sought subsists when a bail petition is
withdrawn then such a ground can also not be taken again.
However,  the  exception  to  this  rule  is  in  the  case  of
entitlement of bail on statutory grounds as has been held by
this Court. If any act or omission of the accused has hindered
the conclusion of trial within the period specified in the third
proviso of section 497(1) of the Code of Criminal Procedure,
1898 (Cr.P.C.) then a right, as contemplated thereunder, will
not accrue in the latter's favour and, therefore, he or she, as
the case may be, would not become entitled to be released on
bail on the statutory ground of delay in conclusion of the trial.
Nonetheless, if after the rejection of the plea of bail on
Crl. Misc. No.43416-B of 2026  3
statutory grounds, the accused has subsequently corrected
himself/herself and has abstained from doing any act or
omission in the following period specified under the third
proviso, then a fresh ground would accrue to the accused to
invoke the jurisdiction of the court for grant of bail. The third
proviso to section 497(1) of Cr.P.C. would thus become
operative as and when the period specified therein has expired
but the trial has not concluded without any fault on part of the
accused".
In the present case, the petition is competent because a
fresh  ground  of  delay  in  conclusion  of  trial  had  become
available to the petitioner.
8.  The third proviso to Section 497(1), Cr.P.C. reads as
under:
"497(1). When bail may be taken in cases of non-bailable
offence. —
3rd Proviso:-
Provided further that the Court shall, except where it is
of the opinion that the delay in the trial of the accused
has  been  occasioned  by  an  act  or  omission  of  the
accused or any other person acting on his behalf, direct
that any person shall be released on bail
(a)  who, being accused of any offence not punishable with
death,  has  been  detained  for  such  offence  for  a
continuous period exceeding one year, or in case of a
woman exceeding six months, and whose trial for such
offence has not concluded; or
(b)  who, being accused of an offence punishable with death,
has been detained for such offence for a continuous
period exceeding two years, and in case of a woman
exceeding one year, and whose trial for such offence has
not concluded:
Provided further that the provisions of the foregoing
proviso  shall  not  apply  to  a  previously  convicted
offender  for  an  offence  punishable  with  death  or
imprisonment for life or to a person who, in the opinion
of the Court, is a hardened, desperate or dangerous
criminal or is accused of an act of terrorism punishable
with death or imprisonment for life."
Under the third proviso to section 497(1), Cr.P.C., an
accused facing an offence not punishable with death becomes
entitled to the concession of bail where he has remained in
continuous detention for a period exceeding one year and the
trial has not concluded, unless the delay has been occasioned by
an act or omission attributable to the accused or his counsel. The
benefit may also be withheld where the accused falls within any
of  the  statutory  exceptions  prescribed  therein.  The  Hon'ble
Supreme  Court,  in  Muhammad  Usman  case  ( supra ),  has
Crl. Misc. No.43416-B of 2026  4
authoritatively held that the right recognized under the third
proviso to Section 497(1), Cr.P.C. cannot be denied merely on
the basis of discretionary powers of the Court.
"It has been held by this Court that the right recognized under
the third proviso of section 497(1) Cr.P.C. cannot be denied to
an accused on the basis of discretionary powers of the court to
grant bail. The right has not been left to the discretion of the
court, rather, its accrual is subject to the fulfillment of the
conditions mentioned under the third proviso of section 497(1)
Cr.P.C."
9.  The statutory scheme has to be read alongside Section
173, Cr.P.C., which casts an obligation upon the investigating
agency to complete the investigation without unnecessary delay
and to submit the police report/challan in accordance with law.
The prosecution cannot first allow the investigation to remain
incomplete beyond the prescribed period and thereafter seek to
rely upon the resulting delay against the accused. In  “ Hakim
Mumtaz Ahmad and another v. The State ”  ( PLD 2002 SC 590 ),
the Hon'ble Supreme Court took serious notice of delay in
submission  of  challan  and  emphasized  the  duty  of  the
Investigating Officer to complete the investigation within the
prescribed period and furnish justification where the challan has
not been submitted.
"It is not only this case in which it has been noticed that the
Investigating Agency as well as District Attorneys have failed to
submit challan of the case registered against accused within the
stipulated  period  but  in  majority  of  the  case  the  police
report/challan under section 173, Cr.P.C. is not filed despite
expiry  of  stipulated  period  due  to  which  accused  persons
involved in criminal case remain languishing in custody without
trial "
10.  In the present case, the record shows that the challan
was not submitted within the period as provided by Section 173,
Cr.P.C. The FIR was lodged on 25.02.2025, the accused was
arrested on 03.03.2025, whereas the report under Section 173,
Cr.P.C. was submitted on 13.11.2025, resulting in a delay of
approximately eight months and ten days from the date of arrest.
Nothing  has  been  brought  on  record  to  establish  that  the
petitioner either caused or contributed to such delay. The lapse,
Crl. Misc. No.43416-B of 2026  5
therefore,  was  on  the  part  of  the  investigating/prosecuting
machinery and cannot be converted into a ground for depriving
the petitioner of the statutory protection available under Section
497(1), Cr.P.C.
11.  The matter, however, does not end with submission of
the  challan.  The  subsequent  period  during  which  the  trial
remained inconclusive must also be examined to determine
whether any part thereof is legally attributable to the petitioner.
In  Muhammad Usman  (supra), the Hon'ble Supreme Court
further held that,
"While ascertaining the delay, the cumulative effect in disposal
of the case has to be considered and its assessment cannot be
determined  on  the  basis  of  mathematical  calculations  by
excluding those dates for which adjournments had been sought
by the accused or the latter's counsel. The main factor for
consideration is the attendance of the witnesses and whether,
despite the matter having become ripe for the recording of
evidence, whether the delay was caused by the defence. The
recording of the statement of a last witness would also not
defeat the right recognized under the third proviso and it would
be unreasonable to conclude that the trial has been completed."
The principal consideration is whether the matter had
become ripe for recording of evidence and whether the delay
was actually caused by the defence. The same principle is
equally reflected in  “ Haq Nawaz v. The State and another ”
( 2025 YLR 673 ), wherein, while relying upon  Muhammad
Usman  (supra),  “ Shakeel Shah v. The State and others ”  ( 2022
SCMR 1 ),  “ Nadeem Samson v. The State and others ”  ( PLD
2022 SC 112 ) and  “ Rohan Ahmad v. The State and others ”
( PLD 2024 SC 492 ), the Court reiterated that the prosecution
must establish that the accused intentionally prolonged the trial
through unjustified adjournments or other conduct having the
effect of delaying the proceedings.
12.  A bare perusal of the report furnished by the learned
Trial Court reveals that, after framing of the charge, prosecution
witnesses were present on certain dates, yet their evidence could
not be recorded or completed on account of the absence or non-
availability of defence counsel. However, such circumstance,
Crl. Misc. No.43416-B of 2026  6
without a corresponding finding as to the actual effect of those
adjournments on the progress of the trial, cannot  ipso facto
render  the  entire  period  attributable  to  the  petitioner.  The
relevant consideration is whether, on the dates sought to be
counted against him, the prosecution witnesses were available
and ready to depose, the prosecution was prepared to examine
them, and the recording of their evidence was nevertheless
frustrated by a specific and unjustifiable act or omission of the
petitioner or a person acting on his behalf. In the present case,
admittedly, no evidence of any prosecution witness was actually
recorded during the period in question. The order sheets do not
disclose any specific instance where a prosecution witness,
being present and ready, could not be examined solely because
of  a  deliberate  or  unjustified  act  of  the  petitioner.  The
prosecution was required not merely to produce its witnesses
before the Court but to demonstrate that their evidence was ripe
for recording and that the petitioner was responsible for its non-
recording. The mandate of  Muhammad Usman case  ( supra )
equally requires the Court to assess the cumulative effect of the
proceedings and the actual impact of the alleged adjournments,
rather  than  mechanically  treating  every  date  on  which  the
defence sought an adjournment as delay attributable to the
accused.
13.  The  impact  of  returning  unrecorded  prosecution
witnesses on a bail application based on statutory delay (Section
497, Cr.P.C.) is significant, particularly when the Trial Court
fails to record a speaking order. Under Pakistani jurisprudence,
the right to an expeditious trial is fundamental, and any delay
not directly attributable to the accused such as the court's failure
to record present witnesses operates in favor of the accused for
the purposes of statutory bail. Section 344 Cr.P.C relates to the
adjournment  of  proceedings  in  criminal  cases,  which  is
reproduced herein for ready reference:
"344. Power to postpone or adjourn proceedings. (1) If, from the
absence of a witness, or any other reasonable cause, it becomes
necessary or advisable to postpone the commencement of, or
Crl. Misc. No.43416-B of 2026  7
adjourn any inquiry or trial, the Court may, if it thinks fit, by
order in writing, stating the reasons therefor, from time to time,
postpone or adjourn the same on such terms as it thinks fit, for
such time as it considers reasonable, and may by a warrant
remand the accused if in custody..."
Under Section 344, Cr.P.C., a Trial Court is mandated
to record reasons in writing for any adjournment. If prosecution
witnesses  (PWs)  are  present  but  returned  without  being
examined, and the Trial Court fails to provide a "speaking order"
justifying this delay, such periods are generally not excluded
from the calculation  of the statutory period  for bail.  Such
omissions by the court or the prosecution are not considered
"acts or omissions of the accused" under the third proviso to
Section 497(1), Cr.P.C.
Similarly, the  Rules & Orders of Lahore High Court
(Vol. III, Chapter I, Part-A) Rule 4 :
"4. Speedy disposal of cases.- Magistrates shall give priority to
criminal cases when an accused person is in custody. A criminal
case  shall  be  proceeded  with  from  day  to  day  as  far  as
practicable and disposed of quickly. Witnesses, who are present,
should be examined promptly and shall not be detained longer
than  may  be  absolutely  necessary.  Adjournments,  when
necessary, shall be as short as the circumstances permit."
Part-E, Rule-14 of the Chapter 1  speaks in following
words:-
“ 14. Record to contain a brief note of all material orders
passed.-Each record or memorandum of evidence should be
dated and the record of a case made by a Magistrate or Sessions
Judge should not only contain depositions or memoranda of
evidence, according as the evidence is or is not recorded by him
in full, but also, in its proper place, a short note of every
material order made during the inquiry or trial, with the date on
which such order was made. Every order of adjournment must
be entered, and the date on which the inquiry was resumed
should be apparent.
Notes- Orders to be written by the Magistrate in his own hand.-
All notes and orders recorded by Presiding Officer (e.g., orders
of adjournment, notes regarding the presence of witnesses) other
than depositions, orders deciding any matter in dispute and the
final judgment, should be written by the Presiding Officer in his
own handwriting or dictated by him and be dated and appended
to the record. Each "order" or "note" should be clearly marked
as such."
Crl. Misc. No.43416-B of 2026  8
Chapter-11,  Part-B,  Rule  10  makes  a distinction
between police remand and judicial remand and that too requires
recording of reasons for adjournment to judicial remand:-
"10. Procedure when a remand for more than 15 days is required
for completion of the case.- If the limit of 15 days has elapsed,
and there is still need for further investigation by the Police, the
procedure to be adopted is that laid down in section 344,
Criminal  Procedure  Code.  The  case  is  brought  on  to  the
Magistrate's file and the accused, if detention is necessary, will
remain in magisterial custody. The case may be postponed or
adjourned from time to time for periods of not more than 15
days each, and as each adjournment expires the accused must be
produced before the Magistrate, and the order of adjournment
must show good reasons for making the order ."
Chapter  24,  Part-B,  Rule-2  also  requires  the
recording of reasons for adjournment in Sessions trial other than
day to day.
"2. Reservation of several days for possible Sessions cases.- The
trial of a Sessions case shall, as a rule, be held from day to day
and unless there be compelling reasons for adjournment, there
should be no adjournment."
14.  The  Hon'ble  Superior  courts  have  clarified  the
consequences of unreasoned adjournments and the return of
present witnesses in following ways:-
The august Supreme Court of Pakistan in  “ ABDUR RASHID
V. THE STATE ”  ( 1998 SCMR 897 ) held for the right of
accused to bail in case of any slackness or incapacity of
prosecution  to  conclude  trial  within  the  time  frame
mentioned  in  clauses  (a)  &  (b)  of  section  497  CrPC  .
However, if the delay occasioned  is  due to  any act  or
omission on the part of the accused, it may cause exception
to the said general rule. The apex court ruled as:
"Any slackness or incapacity of prosecution to conclude the
prosecution side within the time frame mentioned in clauses (a)
and (b), supra, will provide all advantage to the accused for
being released on bail. However, exception contained in third
proviso  unambiguously  imposes  a  responsibility  upon  the
accused for ensuring that delay in disposal is not occasioned or
caused on account of him or any person acting on his behalf.
Therefore, it is crystal clear that if for any reason accused or his
authorized  agent  which  necessarily  includes  the  advocate
engaged for defence causes delay, then in such eventuality
protection contained in the third proviso cannot be invoked. It
may further be noticed that while ascertaining cumulative effect
of ultimate delay in disposal of the case, it would not be merely
Crl. Misc. No.43416-B of 2026  9
mathematical calculation of excluding such days for which
adjournment was obtained by the accused or his counsel."
15.  The circumstances of the present case become even
more significant when it is noticed that the charge was framed
almost  one  year  after  the  petitioner's  arrest,  whereas  the
substantial period preceding the framing of charge has neither
been explained by the prosecution nor attributed, through any
specific act or omission, to the petitioner. A careful examination
of the order sheets, particularly the proceedings after framing of
charge, further belies the assertion that the delay in conclusion
of the trial was attributable to the petitioner. On several dates,
the  complete  prosecution  evidence  was  not  available  and
adjournments were sought from the complainant/prosecution
side for producing the complete evidence. On one occasion,
even  the  learned  Presiding  Officer  was  not  available.
Conversely, the record reflects that adjournment was sought on
behalf of the accused only on two occasions. Thus, the order
sheets,  when  examined  cumulatively  and  in  their  true
perspective, do not demonstrate that the petitioner or anyone
acting on his behalf repeatedly or deliberately obstructed the
recording of prosecution evidence. Rather, the record indicates
that on several dates the prosecution itself was not in a position
to proceed with its complete evidence. The subsequent report of
the learned Magistrate merely records, in general terms, that the
delay in conclusion of the trial was attributable to the accused,
but identifies neither the particular dates nor the proceedings nor
any specific conduct of the petitioner which occasioned such
delay. Such conclusion is not borne out by the contemporaneous
order sheets. The statutory exception contemplated by the third
proviso to Section 497(1), Cr.P.C. cannot be invoked on the
basis of a generalized assertion of attribution; the alleged delay
must be demonstrably traceable to an act or omission of the
accused or a person acting on his behalf. Had the record shown
that, despite the presence and readiness of a prosecution witness
and  the  prosecution's  preparedness  to  examine  him,  the
Crl. Misc. No.43416-B of 2026  10
petitioner had deliberately and without justification prevented
the recording of his evidence, or had failed to cross-examine
after his examination-in-chief was recorded despite adequate
opportunity, the period so occasioned could legitimately have
been taken into account. That is not the case here. In the absence
of any such specific finding or supporting material in the order
sheets, the petitioner cannot be saddled with responsibility for a
period during which the prosecution evidence was, in fact, not
recorded. The statutory right available under the third proviso to
Section 497(1), Cr.P.C. thus cannot be defeated merely on the
strength  of  an  unsupported  conclusion  that  the  delay  was
attributable to the petitioner.
16.  It is equally substantial that the prosecution has not
asserted that the petitioner is a previous convict, a hardened or
desperate criminal, or that he poses a serious threat and danger
to the community if released on bail. Nothing has been placed
on  record  to  bring  his  case  within  any  of  the  exceptions
contemplated by the third proviso to Section 497(1), Cr.P.C. The
principles laid down in  “ Noor Agha v. The State and another ”
( 2025  SCMR  1679 )  and  “ Adnan  Shafai  v.  The  State  and
another ”  ( 2024 SCMR 1543 ) may also be relied upon in this
context.
17.  The right to a fair and expeditious trial is an essential
component  of  due  process.  Pre-trial  detention  cannot  be
permitted to assume the character of punishment, particularly
where  the  accused  has  remained  incarcerated  beyond  the
statutory period and the trial is not likely to conclude in the
immediate future. The prosecution's allegations may be grave,
but the gravity of an accusation, by itself, is unable to defeat a
statutory  entitlement  where  the  prescribed  conditions  stand
satisfied.
18.  In the present case, the delay in submission of the
challan is attributable to the prosecution. No material has been
brought  on  record  showing  that  the  petitioner  caused  or
contributed to that delay. As regards the subsequent period, the
Crl. Misc. No.43416-B of 2026  11
report of the learned Magistrate does not identify any particular
act or omission of the petitioner which materially obstructed the
progress of the trial. The order sheets likewise do not disclose
that, on the dates relied upon, the prosecution was ready to
record its evidence and the petitioner deliberately prevented
such recording. The mere presence of prosecution witnesses in
Court, without recording of their evidence and without a specific
order-sheet explanation as to why such evidence could not be
recorded, is insufficient to deprive the petitioner of his statutory
right.
19.  Consequently, without commenting upon the merits of
the case, lest it prejudice either party during the trial, the instant
petition is  allowed . The petitioner, Ali Shahzad alias Shan Ali,
is admitted to post-arrest bail, subject to furnishing bail bonds in
the sum of Rs. 1,00,000/- (one lac) with one surety in the like
amount to the satisfaction of the learned Trial Court.
20.  The  observations  recorded  herein  are  tentative  in
nature and shall not influence the learned Trial Court, which
shall decide the case strictly on the basis of the evidence
produced before it.
(GHULAM SARWAR NIHUNG)
JUDGE
Announced, dictated, prepared
and signed on 31.08.2026.
Approved for reporting .
JUDGE
Iftikhar

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_003]
Case: Writ Petition 17594/20 (All Pakistan Markazi Union Pan Vs Federation of Pakistan etc).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Writ Petition 17594/20. Title: Writ Petition 17594/20 (All Pakistan Markazi Union Pan Vs Federation of Pakistan etc). Official Ref: 2026LHC5508. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Raheel Kamran. Judgment Date (as displayed): 02-09-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5508.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 35 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Stereo HCJDA 38
JUDGMENT SHEET
LAHORE HIGH COURT LAHORE
( JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT)
Writ Petition No.17594 of 2020
All Pakistan Markazi Union Pan, Beri, Cigarette, Mashroob
Retailers  through its President
V ersus
Federation of Pakistan  through Secretary National Health Services
Regulation & Coordination and others
Petitioners by:-  Khawaja Haris Ahmad, Sr. ASC.
M/s  Syed  Tassadaq  Mustafa  Naqvi,  Syed
Tassadaq  Murtaza  Naqvi,  Barrister  Naqsh
Fatima  Naqvi  and  Muhammad  Usman,
Advocates  (W.P. No17595 of 2020)
Respondents by :  Mr.  Nauman  Khalid,  Assistant  Attorney
General for the Federation of Pakistan.
Mr. Salman Asif Warraich and Umar Farooq
Khan,  Assistant  Advocates  General,  Punjab
with Dr. Hafiz Asim Hussain, Addl. Director
office  of  D.G.  Health  &  Population
Department, Punjab.
Dates of hearing: -  04.06.2026, 08.06.2026, 15.06.2026,
19.06.2026, 23.06.2026, 29.06.2026
JUDGMENT
RAHEEL KAMRAN, J.:  Through  the titled writ petition
under Article 199 of the Constitution of the Islamic Republic of Pakistan,
1973  ( “ the Constitution ” ) , the petitioner has called in question the  vires
of S.R.O.72(I)/2020 dated 30.01.2020  ( “ the impugned Notification ” ) ,
issued by the  Ministry of National Health Services, Regulations and
Coordination, whereby the advertisement, promotion and sponsorship of
tobacco  and  tobacco  products  has  been  comprehensively  prohibited
through the various media and other modes specified therein. As id entical
-2-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
challenge arises in Writ Petition No.17595 of 2020, both petitions were
heard together and are disposed of through this common judgment.
2 .  Learned counsel for the petitioners, adopting substantially
common grounds of challenge,  contended that the impugned Notification
dated 30.01.2020 is  ultra vires  the Prohibition of Smoking and Protection
of Non - Smokers Health Ordinance, 2002  (“the Ordinance of 2002”) .
According to  them , Section 7 contemplates only the regulation of tobacco
adv ertisements  through  guidelines  prescribed  by  the  Committee
constituted thereunder and does not authorize a complete prohibition. It
was  argued  that  the  expression  “ if  such  advertisement  is  not  in
accordance with guidelines ”  necessarily presupposes that som e form of
advertisement must remain permissible ,  therefore, the Committee lacked
authority to recommend a blanket prohibition. It was further contended
that no guidelines, as contemplated by Section 7, were ever framed by the
Committee itself and that the  impugned Notification merely gives effect
to  recommendations  of  a  sub - committee,  thereby  lacking  statutory
foundation. Learned counsel also submitted that Section 7 is confined to
“ advertisement ”  and  does  not  extend  to  promotion,  sponsorship  or
restriction s relating to the display and visibility of tobacco products at
points of sale, with the result that the impugned Notification travels
beyond  the  parent  statute.  Learned  counsel  further  relied  upon the
expression “except as provided by the Federal Excise R ules, 2005”
occurring in clause (a) of the  Guidelines  2013  as well as  the impugned
Notification  , contending that the said exception itself demonstrated that
the competent authority understood the statutory scheme as permitting at
least some form of tobacc o advertisement. According to learned counsel,
the existence of such an exception was inconsistent with the respondents’
assertion that Section 7 authorized a complete prohibition.
Much emphasis has been laid by the learned counsel for the
petitioners that the impugned Notification violates Articles 4 and 18 of
the  Constitution  by  imposing  what  is,  in  substance,  a  complete
prohibition  upon  a  lawful  commercial  activity  under  the  guise  of
-3-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
regulation. According to them, although a lawful trade may be subjected
to reasonable restrictions, regulation cannot be stretched to the point of
extinction. It was also contended that the Notification is based upon an
excessive  delegation  of  legislative  power  and  is  arbitrary,
disproportionate and violative of Articles 9, 10-A, 14, 18, 25, 37 and 38
of the Constitution. Learned counsel further submitted that the exclusion
of  any  representative  of  the  tobacco  industry  from  the  Committee
offended the principles of natural justice as well as Section 24-A of the
General Clauses Act, 1897.
Developing the challenge further, learned counsel contended
that the Committee travelled beyond its statutory mandate by seeking to
implement Article 13 of the WHO Framework Convention on Tobacco
Control  ( “ WHO FCTC ” ) , although the Convention has never been
incorporated into municipal law. According to learned counsel, Pakistan
follows the dualist theory of international law and treaty obligations
cannot be enforced domestically without legislative incorporation. It was
also argued that after the Eighteenth Constitutional Amendment, the
subject of health stands devolved to the Provinces and the Federation
consequently lacked constitutional competence to issue the impugned
Notification. In this context, considerable emphasis was laid upon the
historical merger of the Tobacco Control Cell with the Health Services
Academy under the Inter-Provincial Coordination Division, from which
it  was  argued that implementation  of tobacco-control  measures fell
within the domain of the Council of Common Interests rather than the
Federal Government.
3 .  Notice under  Order  XXVIIA of the Code of Civil Procedure,
1908, was issued to the Attorney General for Pakistan, in response to
which parawise  comments were submitted on behalf of the Federation  in
Writ Petition No.17594 of 2020 .
4 .  L earned Assistant Attorney General for Pakistan  opposed
the petitions principally on the basis of the parawise comments. He also
raised a preliminary objection to t he maintainability of the petitions on
-4-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
the grounds stated therein. On merits, he maintained that the impugned
Notification neither prohibits nor regulates the manufacture, import,
distribution  or  sale  of  tobacco  products  but  merely  restricts  their
advertisement,  promotion  and  sponsorship  in  furtherance  of  the
legislative policy embodied in the Ordinance of 2002 for the protection
of public health. According to him, Article 18 does not confer an
independent constitutional right to advertise tobacco products; Section 7
authorizes  the  Committee  to  prescribe  appropriate  guidelines  in
furtherance of the statutory object; the Committee acted strictly within
the authority conferred by the Ordinance; and the WHO FCTC was relied
upon only as a legitimate guide in exercising powers independently
conferred by domestic law. He further submitted that the continuing
operation of the Ordinance after the Eighteenth Amendment is protected
by Article 270AA(6) of the Constitution and that the Federation remained
constitutionally  competent  to  administer  the  statutory  framework
established thereunder.
5 .  The learned Assistant Advocate General, Punjab remained
present  during  the  hearing  but  did  not  advance  any  independent
submissions either supporting or opposing the challenge.
6 .  In  support  of  their  respective  submissions,  the  learned
counsel for the petitioners as well as the learned Assistant Attorney
General  placed  reliance  upon  numerous  statutory  provisions,
constitutional provisions and judicial precedents, which shall be referred
to, to the extent necessary, while examining the respective questions
arising for determination.
7 .  Having heard learned counsel for the parties and perused the
record with their able assistance, it is appropriate first to dispose of the
preliminary objection to maintainability raised by the learned Assistant
Attorney General. The objection is two-fold: first, that the petitioners,
being retailers rather than manufacturers of tobacco products, are not
persons aggrieved; and secondly, that the petitions are misconceived
because  neither  the  Ordinance  of  2002  nor  the  notifications  dated
-5-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
26.09.2018 and 31.05.2019 have been challenged. Neither objection
merits acceptance. The petitioners are associations representing retailers
dealing  in  tobacco  products.  The  impugned  Notification  expressly
regulates various aspects of retail sale, including the display and visibility
of tobacco products at points of sale and certain dealings between
suppliers and retailers. Its provisions are thus directly applicable to the
members of the petitioner associations, whose conduct is governed by the
impugned instrument and who may be affected by its enforcement. They,
therefore, possess a sufficient and direct interest to question its legality
under Article 199 of the Constitution. Reliance in this regard is placed on
the judgment of the Supreme Court in the case of  “Mian Fazal Din v.
Lahore Improvement Trust, Lahore and another”  (PLD 1969 SC 223) ,
wherein it has been held:
“The  right  considered  sufficient  for  maintaining  a
proceeding in writ jurisdiction is not necessarily a right
in the strict juristic sense but it is enough if the applicant
discloses  that  he  had  a  personal  interest  in  the
performance of the legal duty which if not performed or
performed in a manner not permitted by law would result
in the loss of some personal benefit or advantage or the
curtailment of a privilege or liberty or franchise.”
The preliminary objection is, therefore, overruled and the petitions are
held to be maintainable.
8 .  Turning now to the merits of the case, it is convenient to
address first the petitioners’ principal constitutional challenge, as the
determination of the remaining questions substantially depends upon its
resolution.  The foundation of the petitioners’ case is that the impugned
Notification,  by  comprehensively  prohibiting  the  advertisement,
promotion and sponsorship of tobacco products, impermissibly abridges
their fundamental right to carry on a lawful trade and business guaranteed
by Article 18 of the Constitution. The said Article undoubtedly accords
constitutional protection to the freedom of every citizen to enter upon a
lawful profession, occupation, trade or business. Economic liberty is an
-6-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
important facet of individual autonomy and a recognized constitutional
value. The guarantee, however, is not absolute, unqualified or unfettered
but subject to regulation and reasonable restrictions which may be
imposed by law in the larger interest of the society or for public welfare
.
The opening words of the Article 18 expressly make its operation
“subject to such qualifications, if any, as may be prescribed by law” . The
word “qualification” has been used to confer a right upon a citizen to
enter upon any lawful profession or occupation and not to conduct and
unlawful trade  or business. The word “lawful” qualifies the right of the
citizens in the relevant field and envisages that the State can by law ban a
profession, occupation, trade or business by declaring it to be unlawful
which in common parlance means anything forbidden by law
.
It  is not the  petitioners’  case that the impugned  Notification
prohibits  the  manufacture,  import,  distribution  or  sale  of  tobacco
products. The tobacco trade  in this country is lawful and  as such,
continues to remain  independently regulated .  The  petitioners’  grievance
is  confined to  the prohibition of the advertisement, promotion and
sponsorship  of  tobacco  products.  It  is  well  settled  that  reasonable
restriction s  which bear true relation to business or trade can be validly
imposed for the purp ose of promoting general welfare . By qualifying the
right to business and trade, the Constitution makers wanted to create a
balance between the societal needs and the right of an individual
.
9 .  It may be noted that n umerous lawful trades and  professions ,
within Pakistan and across comparative jurisdictions ,  are subjected to
stringent  restrictions  or  even  complete  prohibitions,  upon  public
advertisement without the legality of the underlying business being
doubted.  A  useful  illustration  is  pres cription  medicines:  their
manufacture, import, distribution and sale are entirely lawful, yet many
jurisdictions prohibit or severely restrict direct - to - consumer advertising,
“Mohammad Imran and others v. Province of Sindh through Chief Secretary and
others”  (2019 SCMR 1753)  Para 46.
ibid
-7-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
given  the  public-health  risks  of  indiscriminate  self-medication
.
Comparable restrictions apply to narcotic
and psychotropic substances
used  for  legitimate  medical  purposes,  certain  hazardous  chemicals,
infant-formula  products
in  healthcare  settings  —  in  each  case the
underlying  commerce  remains  lawful  while  public  advertising  is
prohibited or tightly controlled. These examples show that the distinction
between regulating a business and regulating the manner in which it is
promoted is a long-recognized and legitimate legislative choice. The
rationale  is  neither  novel  nor  difficult  to  appreciate.  Commercial
advertisements  are designed  not  merely  to  inform but  to  stimulate
demand. Where a product poses serious risks to public health or safety,
the Legislature may legitimately conclude that unrestricted promotion
would undermine the very regulatory objectives it seeks to achieve;
restrictions on advertising then serve not to prohibit the business but to
behavior the social harm arising from increased consumption of a product
acknowledged to be injurious. That distinction assumes particular force
for tobacco, which is not an ordinary consumer commodity. Tobacco
products are inherently addictive and scientifically established to cause
serious disease, affecting not only users but also those involuntarily
exposed  to  second-hand  smoke
.  The  object  of  restricting  tobacco
advertising is therefore not the suppression of commercial competition
but the protection of public health by reducing the inducements to
initiation and continued consumption, particularly among children and
young  persons.  Article  18  accordingly  does  not,  by  necessary
implication, confer a constitutional right to advertise tobacco products,
nor does the commercial promotion of such products enjoy immunity
from reasonable  statutory  regulation  merely  because  it  involves  an
element of expression. The conclusion reached above is also consistent
Clause 10 of the Advertisement of Therapeutic Goods Guidelines (Edition 01) issued by the
Drug Regulatory Authority of Pakistan. Therapeutic Goods Advertising Code 2021 issued by
the Therapeutic Goods Administration in Australia.
Morphine, fentanyl, methadone
The Protection of Breast-feeding and Child Nutrition Ordinance, 2002 (Pakistan), WHO
International Code on Marketing of Breast-milk Substitutes.
Preamble to the WHO Framework Convention on Tobacco Control
-8-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
with the broader constitutional principle that the freedom guaranteed by
Article 18 is inherently subject to the State’s regulatory power. In the
case of  “ Ghulam Rasool v. Muhammad Hayat”  (PLD 1984 SC 385) , the
Supreme Court explained that the State’s power to regulate lawful trades
and occupations is an essential attribute of sovereignty, exercisable to
secure public order, safety, health, morals and the general welfare. It was
observed  that  businesses  and  occupations  remain  amenable to such
regulation,  a  distinction  expressly  recognized  by  Article  18  of  the
Constitution.  The  impugned  Notification  is  a  manifestation  of  that
regulatory authority.
10 .  Advertisement  is  a  form  of  expression  that  facilitates
commerce and may be an effective means of expanding a business, but it
is only one of the several incidents of a trade. Whether undertaken
incidentally to any trade or business or pursued as a business in itself,
advertisement is permissible only to the extent that it is lawful and is not
subject  to  any  restriction  imposed  by  law  within  the  parameters
prescribed under Article 19 of the Constitution. The aforementioned
Articles  guarantees  freedom  of  speech  and  expression  subject  to
reasonable restrictions imposed by law in the interest of the glory of
Islam or the integrity, security or defence of Pakistan or any part thereof,
friendly relations with foreign States, public order, decency or morality,
or in relation to contempt of Court, commission of or incitement to an
offence. The impugned Notification merely restricts the commercial
advertisement,  promotion  and  sponsorship  of  tobacco  products  in
furtherance  of  a  statutory  public-health  objective.  Such  a  measure
regulates the manner in which a hazardous product may be commercially
promoted;  the  impugned  restrictions  do  not  impair  the  essential
constitutional freedom of expression for reasonable restrictions can be
imposed to maintain decency and morality. The challenge, therefore,
cannot succeed even if examined from the standpoint of Article 19 of the
Constitution.
-9-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
11 .  Having reached the conclusion that neither Article 18 nor
Article 19 has been violated by the issuance of Impugned Notification,
the inquiry thereafter is whether the impugned Notification represents a
lawful exercise of the authority conferred by the Ordinance of 2002 and
bears a rational relationship with the legislative object sought to be
achieved. The conclusion reached above is further reinforced by the
nature of the product with which the present case is concerned. Tobacco
products cannot be equated with ordinary articles of commerce. Their
manufacture  and  sale  may  be  lawful,  yet  their  consumption  is
scientifically established to be injurious to health and a leading cause of
preventable disease and premature mortality. Those consequences are not
confined  to  smokers,  rather  persons  involuntarily  exposed  to
environmental tobacco smoke, including children, also suffer serious
harm despite never having chosen to consume tobacco. Modern tobacco-
control policy is directed not merely at discouraging existing users but
principally at preventing initiation, especially among adolescents and
young  adults.  Research  consistently  shows  that  nicotine  is  highly
addictive and that the overwhelming majority of habitual users begin
during  adolescence  or  early  adulthood.  Once  addiction  takes  hold,
cessation becomes far more difficult, exposing individuals to lifelong
risk.  Contemporary  strategy  therefore  focuses  on  reducing  the
environmental  and  commercial  influences  that  encourage
experimentation by the young before addiction becomes established
.
Unlike conventional consumer goods, the commercial  promotion of
tobacco  products  does  not  merely  facilitate  competition  in  the
marketplace; it directly influences patterns of consumption, particularly
amongst  adolescents  and  young  persons,  thereby  increasing  the
likelihood of addiction and the consequent burden of disease. It is this
distinctive  public-health  dimension  that  has  consistently  justified  a
The 31st Surgeon General’s report on tobacco, issued in 2012 by the U.S. Department of
Health and Human Services, titled  “ Preventing Tobacco Use Among Youth and Young
Adults ”,  WHO Fact Sheet: Tobacco and Nicotine (updated June 2026) .
-10-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
regulatory approach fundamentally different from that applicable to the
advertisement of ordinary commercial products.
12 .  The legislative policy underlying the Ordinance of 2002 is
required to be appreciated in the above context. The object of the
legislation is neither to suppress a lawful trade nor to penalize those
engaged in it. Rather, it seeks to reduce the demand for tobacco products
by restricting commercial practices which encourage or normalize their
consumption.  The  prohibition  of  advertisement,  promotion  and
sponsorship  constitutes  one  of  the principal  means  adopted  by  the
legislature to achieve that objective. The legislative choice reflects a
judgment that reducing the commercial visibility and attractiveness of
tobacco products serves the larger public interest in protecting health,
particularly that of younger and more vulnerable segments of society
.
Such  policy  choices,  when  founded  upon  legitimate  public-health
considerations and implemented through law, ordinarily fall within the
legislative domain and warrant judicial deference unless shown to be
manifestly arbitrary or constitutionally impermissible.
13 .  The constitutional significance of protecting public health
has repeatedly been emphasized by the courts of this country. The right
to life guaranteed by Article 9 of the Constitution is not confined to mere
animal existence but embraces the right to live  in an environment
conducive  to  human  health  and  dignity
.  Consequently,  where  the
legislature adopts measures reasonably directed towards safeguarding
public health, the Court must examine such measures with due regard to
that constitutional objective. Restrictions designed to discourage the
consumption of products universally recognized as hazardous to human
health cannot be assessed solely from the perspective of commercial
convenience or economic advantage; they must equally be evaluated in
Article 13 of WHO FCTC
“ Mst. Shehla Zia and others v. WAPDA”  (PLD 1994 SC 693) ,  “ Syed Mansoor  Ali Shah
and 4 others v. Government of Punjab, through Housing, Physical and Environmental
Planning Department, and 3 others”  (2007 C L D 533 Lahore)
-11-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
the light of the State ’ s constitutional obligation to protect life and public
health.
14 .  Turning now to  the controversy  qua  the source and extent of
the statutory authority under which the impugned Notification has been
issued , i t is a settled principle that delegat ed legislation derives its
legitimacy entirely from the parent statute and can neither travel beyond,
nor exist independently of, the authority conferred by the Legislature
.
The validity of the impugned Notification, therefore,  is required to  be
examined  with reference to the scheme, object and language of the
Prohibition  of  Smoking  and  Protection  of  Non - Smokers’  Health
Ordinance, 2002. The preamble to the Ordinance reads: -
“WHEREAS it is expedient to provide for the prohibition of smoking
and protection of non-smokers health and for matters connected
therewith and ancillary thereto.”
Although  a  preamble  does  not  itself  confer  substantive
power, it identifies the legislative purpose and furnishes an important
guide to the construction of the operative provisions
. It is manifest from
the  preamble  of  the  Ordinance  of  2002  that  the  Legislature  was
concerned, not with advancing or regulating the commercial interests of
the tobacco industry, but with discouraging smoking and protecting
public health. The expression  “and for matters connected therewith and
ancillary thereto”  as used in the preamble further indicates the legislative
intention to establish a comprehensive regulatory framework capable of
effectively advancing those objectives
. Accordingly, every substantive
provision of the Ordinance, including Section 7, must be construed in a
manner that promotes, rather than defeats, that legislative purpose
. At
this stage, i t is also necessary to notice the definition of “advertisement”
“Khawaja Ahmad Hassaan v. Government of Punjab and others”  ( 2005 SCMR 186) .
“Director General, FIA and others v. Kamran Iqbal and others”  (2016 SCMR 447) ,  “ The
Additional Registrar Company v. Al - Qaim Textile Mills Limited ”  ( 2021 CLC 931 Lahore ) ,
“ Hafiz Muhammad Amir Hussain v. Securities and Exchange Commission of Pakistan and
oth ers ”  (20 25  CLD  1323 Islamabad ) .
“Collector of Customs and others v. Sheikh Spinning Mills”  (1999 SCMR 1402) .
“ Dr. Raja Aamer Zaman v. Omar Ayub Khan and others”  (2015 SCMR 1303) .
-12-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
as provided in Section 2(a) of the Ordinance of 2002, which reads as
under:-
“„advertisement‟ means and includes any  notice, circular, wall
paper,  pamphlet,  display  on  any  board  of  hoarding,  through
internet, or any visible representation made by any form of media
whether  mechanical,  electronic,  audio,  visual  or  any  other
technological  means  of  any  light,  sound,  smoke,  gas,  writing
instruments, stickers, symbol, color, logo, trade marks, display on
articles like T-shirts, shoes, sports wear, sports gears, caps, carry
bags, telephone booths or by any other mean, direct or indirect,
which have the effect of promoting smoking and other tobacco use
and the expression „advertise‟ shall be construed accordingly.”
The  above  definition  is  remarkably  expansive.  Rather  than
confining advertisement to conventional forms of commercial publicity
such as newspapers, television or radio, the Legislature adopted an
inclusive formulation embracing virtually every direct or indirect form of
communication  capable  of  encouraging  tobacco  consumption.  Its
emphasis  is  not  upon  the  medium  employed  but  upon  the  effect
produced. Accordingly, the statutory concept extends beyond traditional
advertisements  to  logos,  trademarks,  colours,  apparel,  promotional
articles and every other visible representation which has the effect of
promoting  smoking  or  tobacco  use.  The  breadth  of  this  definition
provides  the  statutory  context  within  which  Section  7  falls  to  be
interpreted.
15 .  Section 7 of the Ordinance reads as under:
“7.  Prohibition  on  advertisement  of  cigarettes,  etc. —
Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time
being in force, no person or company shall advertise tobacco and
tobacco products on any media or in any place and any public
service vehicle, if such advertisement is not in accordance with
guidelines prescribed for this purpose by a committee which the
Federal Government may, by notification in the official Gazette,
constitute.”
A plain reading of the above provision leaves little room for doubt that
the Legislature itself declared the governing policy. The opening  non-
obstante  clause accords overriding effect to the prohibition and prohibits
-13-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
tobacco advertisements in every medium and place unless permitted
under guidelines prescribed by the committee constituted by the Federal
Government. The Legislature thus determined the legislative policy while
leaving to a specialized expert body (committee) the task of prescribing,
consistently with that policy, the extent  and  manner in  which any
advertisement, if at all permissible, may be allowed.
16 .  The petitioners’ principal submission is that  in Section 7 the
qualifying words  “if such advertisement is not in accordance with
guidelines”  necessarily  imply  that  the  Legislature  intended  some
category of tobacco advertisement always to remain permissible, with the
consequence that the Committee lacked authority to prescribe guidelines
amounting to a comprehensive prohibition. In this respect, it is to be
noted that Section 7 does not operate in isolation. It must be construed in
the context of the Ordinance as a whole, particularly its preamble and the
expansive definition of “advertisement” contained in Section 2(a). The
Legislature has itself declared the governing policy of discouraging
tobacco use in the interest of public health and has entrusted to the
Committee the responsibility of prescribing guidelines consistent with
that policy. The expression “guidelines” regulates the manner in w hich
the statutory policy is to be implemented; it does not itself impose a
limitation upon the amplitude of the authority conferred. Nothing in the
language of Section 7 obliges the Committee to permit any minimum
category of tobacco advertisement irrespective of prevailing public-health
considerations. Had the Legislature intended to reserve any particular
mode or category of advertisement from prohibition, it could readily have
said so. The absence of any such limitation is indicative of a legislative
intention to leave the formulation of appropriate regulatory measures to
the specialized body constituted for that purpose, subject always to the
policy and object declared by Legislature.
17 .  Equally untenable is the contention that the Committee’s
authority to regulate tobacco advertisements necessarily excludes the
-14-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
power to prohibit them altogether. The distinction sought to be drawn
between regulation and prohibition is one of degree rather than of
principle. Where the Legislature has identified the mischief sought to be
remedied and has declared the governing policy, the regulatory measures
adopted by the delegate must be assessed by reference to their rational
relationship  with  that  statutory  purpose
.  The  decisive  question,
therefore, is not whether the impugned guidelines impose partial or
comprehensive  restrictions,  but  whether  they  remain  within  the
legislative policy embodied in the parent statute. In the present case, the
Ordinance seeks to discourage tobacco consumption and protect public
health,  while Section 2(a) deliberately defines “advertisement” in the
broadest terms so as to encompass virtually every direct or indirect
means of promoting tobacco use. Read together with Section 7, these
provisions disclose a legislative intention to suppress the promotional
influence of tobacco advertising in all its manifestations, leaving the
Committee  to  determine,  in  the  light  of  prevailing  public-health
considerations,  the  extent  of  restrictions  necessary  to  achieve  that
objective. If, in the exercise of that statutory discretion, the Committee
concluded that the legislative purpose could effectively be secured only
through a comprehensive prohibition of tobacco advertising, promotion
and sponsorship, such a determination cannot be regarded as travelling
beyond the authority conferred by Section 7 merely because it adopts the
most stringent regulatory measure. The width of the restriction, by itself,
does not determine its legality; what is material is whether it bears a
rational nexus to the object which Legislature intended to achieve. For
the reasons already recorded, that requirement stands fully satisfied.
18 .  The above  view  also finds considerable support from the
contemporaneous  administrative  understanding  of  Section  7  of  the
Ordinance of 2002 . The  impugned Notification dated 30.01.2020 was not
the  first  occasion  on  which  the  Committee  constituted  under  the
Ordinance  prescribed  comprehensive  restrictions  upon  tobacco
“Khawaja Ahmad Hassaan v. Government of Punjab and others”  ( 2005 SCMR  186)
-15-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
advertising. Nearly seven years earlier, the Ministry of National Health
Services, Regulations and Coordination had, in exercise of the same
statutory power under Section 7, issued the Tobacco Advertisement
Guidelines through S.R.O.1086(I)/2013 dated 31.12.2013. The relevant
portion thereof is reproduced below:
“ S.R.O. 1086 (I)/2013.---In exercise of the powers
conferred by section 7 of the Prohibition of Smoking and
Protection of Non-smokers Health Ordinance, 2002 (LXXIV
of 2002) and in supersession of its previous notifications or

1005(I)/2013  dated  21

(a)  in  print  media,  including  a  newspaper,  magazine,

(d)  through depiction of a human or animal image or

(f)  through a poster or banner made out of paper, plastic,

from 31
st
May 2014. ”
A plain reading of the above Guidelines shows that they prohibited
tobacco advertisements through virtually every recognized medium of
commercial communication, including the print media, cinema, theatre,
television, radio, branding on garments, posters, banners, shop fascia and
outdoor billboards. Thus, long before the issuance of the impugned
-16-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
Notification,  the  competent  statutory  authority  had  consistently
understood  Section  7  as  empowering  it  to  prescribe  guidelines
amounting, in substance, to a comprehensive prohibition of tobacco
advertising  wherever  such  a  measure  was  considered  necessary  to
advance the legislative object of protecting public health. Although an
executive  interpretation  cannot  control  the  meaning  of  a  statute,  a
contemporaneous and consistently applied administrative construction is
entitled to due persuasive weight where it accords with the language and
purpose of the enactment. The construction adopted by the authorities
since 2013 is entirely consistent with the broad terms employed in
Sections 2(a) and 7 of the Ordinance and lends further support to the
conclusion already reached by this Court that the statutory scheme does
not  require  the  Committee  to  preserve  any  minimum  category  of
permissible tobacco advertisement.
