The  comparison  between  the  2013  Guidelines  and  the  impugned
Notification demonstrates that the latter did not introduce an altogether
new legislative policy. The earlier Guidelines had already prohibited
tobacco advertisements across virtually every recognized medium of
commercial  communication.  The  principal  additions  made  by  the
impugned Notification relate to comparatively newer forms of indirect
promotion including displays within points of sale, direct promotional
communications and commercial arrangements intended to secure the
preferential display or exclusive sale of particular tobacco products. The
impugned  Notification  thus  represents  an  evolution  of  the  existing
-18-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
regulatory  framework  in  response  to  changing  methods  of  tobacco
promotion rather than a departure from the legislative policy consistently
pursued under Section 7 since 2013. This continuity in administrative
practice  further  fortifies  the  conclusion  that  the  comprehensive
restrictions contained in the impugned Notification fall well within the
scope of the authority conferred by the Ordinance.
20 .  Learned counsel for the petitioners nevertheless sought to
distinguish the 2013  as well as the impugned  Guidelines by placing
reliance upon clause (a)  of both the notifications , which prohibited
t obacco advertisements in the print media  by adding exceptions as
follows:  “ except  as  provided  by  the  Federal  Excise  Rules,  2005. ”
According to the learned counsel, this exception demonstrates that the
competent  authority  itself  understood  Section  7  to  requ ire  the
preservation of some category of permissible advertisements. A careful
examination of the Federal Excise Rules, 2005 reveals that they neither
authori z e nor recogni z e any form of tobacco advertisement. Chapter VI
of the Rules is concerned with matt ers relating to the manufacture,
packing, clearance, excise stamps, banderoles and other fiscal incidents
of tobacco products, while Chapter XVI deals with records and allied
procedural matters. Neither Chapter  in the said Rules  contains any
provision perm itting or regulating tobacco advertisements. Consequently,
the expression  “ except as provided by the Federal Excise Rules, 2005 ”
cannot reasonably be construed as preserving a class of permissible
advertisements.  It  merely  acknowledges  the  existence  of  ano ther
regulatory regime governing tobacco products, without creating any
substantive exception to the prohibition imposed under the Guidelines .
21 .  Indeed, the Federal Excise Rules reinforce rather than dilute
the regulatory policy embodied in the Ordinance. Rule 31(2) of the said
Rules expressly requires every manufacturer of cigarettes and other
tobacco products to comply with such conditions and restrictions as may
be  imposed  by  the  Federal  Government  in  implementation  of  the
-19-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
Framework  Convention  on  Tobacco  Control  (FCTC).  The  Rule
provides: -
“ From  such  date  as  may  be  specified  by  the  Federal
Government or the Board, every manufacturer of cigarettes
or other tobacco products shall meet all the conditions and
restrictions  which  may  be  imposed  by  th e  Federal
Government in compliance of the Framework Convention for
Tobacco Control (FCTC) adopted on the 23rd May, 2003 .”
(Emphasis supplied by this Court).
Far from preserving any right to advertise tobacco products, the Federal
Excise Rules contemplate that additional restrictions may from time to
time be imposed in furtherance of Pakistan ’ s tobacco-control obligations.
Read in that context, the reference to the Federal Excise Rules in the
2013 as well as the impugned  Guidelines cannot be interpreted as
recognizing a statutory entitlement to advertise. Rather, it reflects the
Legislature ’ s intention that the various regulatory instruments governing
tobacco  products  should  operate  harmoniously  as  components  of  a
unified public-health regime. The petitioners ’  reliance upon clause (a) of
the 2013 Guidelines is, therefore, misplaced.
22 .  Adverting now to  the petitioners ’  further contention that the
impugned Notification nevertheless lacks statutory foundation because
no guidelines were ever prescrib ed by the Committee contemplated by
Section 7 and that the impugned Notification rests merely upon the
recommendations of a sub - committee. The submission is not borne out
by the record. Section 7 requires that any permissible advertising regime
must confor m to guidelines prescribed by the Committee constituted by
the  Federal  Government.  The  material  placed  before  the  Court
demonstrates  that  the  statutory requirement  was  duly fulfilled.  The
Committee was validly constituted through Notification dated 26.09.2 018
and in its meeting held on 12.02.2019, considered the subject and
approved the policy decisions which ultimately formed the substance of
the impugned Guidelines. The sub - committee subsequently constituted
through  Notification  dated  31.05.2019  was  entru sted  only  with  the
-20-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
limited task of examining the approved decision items and formulating
recommendations  for  their  implementation.  It  neither  exercised  the
statutory  power  vested  in  the  Committee  nor  substituted  its  own
judgment for that of the Committee. Its role remained purely auxiliary
and facilitative, operating within the parameters already settled by the
Committee  itself.  The  statutory  decision  thus  remained  that  of  the
Committee whereas the sub-committee merely assisted in giving practical
shape to that decision.
23 .  Equally devoid of substance is the argument that, since the
Committee did not publish a separate document formally described as
“ Guidelines ” , the statutory requirement contained in Section 7 remained
unfulfilled. The Ordinance prescribes no particular form or nomenclature
in which the Committee ’ s guidelines are to be expressed. It requires only
that the applicable advertising regime should be based upon guidelines
prescribed  by  the  Committee.  Once  the  Committee  approved  the
governing  policy  decisions  and  those  decisions  were  thereafter
implemented  through  the  impugned  Notification  issued  under  the
authority of Section 7, the statutory requirement stood fully satisfied. The
law is concerned with the substance of the statutory exercise rather than
the label attached to it. To insist upon a separate document bearing the
heading  “ Guidelines ” , notwithstanding that the Committee had already
settled the governing principles and those principles were faithfully
embodied  in  the  impugned  Notification,  would  elevate  form  over
substance and attribute to the Legislature a procedural requirement which
it never chose to prescribe. The petitioners ’  challenge on this score must,
therefore, also fail.
24 .  It  was  also  contended  by  the  learned  counsel  for  the
petitioners  that,  even  if  Section  7  authorizes  restrictions  upon
“ advertisement ” ,  it  does  not  empower  the  Committee  to  prohibit
promotion, sponsorship, or the display of tobacco products at points of
sale, with the result that the corresponding provisions of the impugned
-21-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
Notification travel beyond the parent statute. This submission requires
each of the above referred categories to be examined separately. In so far
as promotion is concerned, the answer lies in the statutory definition
itself.  Section  2(a)  of  the  Ordinance  of  2002  does  not  confine
“ advertisement ”  to conventional commercial publicity but extends it to
“ any  visible  representation ”  made  through  any  medium,  direct  or
indirect,  “ which have the effect of promoting smoking and other tobacco
use. ”  The Legislature has thus chosen effect, rather than form, as the
governing criterion. Any activity which, by its nature or operation,
promotes  the  consumption  of  tobacco  falls  within  the  statutory
conception of advertisement, irrespective of the label attached to it.
Consequently, the Committee did not enlarge the scope of Section 7 by
addressing  promotional  activities;  it  merely  applied  the  statutory
definition existing in the Ordinance.
25 .  As  regards  the  challenge  to  the  restrictions  relating  to
point-of-sale display, the same stands on substantially the same footing.
Learned counsel sought to distinguish the mere display of tobacco
products within retail premises from an advertisement intended to solicit
customers. The distinction cannot be accepted having regard to the
language employed by the Legislature. Section 2(a) expressly includes
within  the  concept  of  advertisement  any  direct  or  indirect  visible
representation having the effect of promoting smoking or tobacco use and
specifically refers to logos, trade marks, colours and other identifying
features commonly associated with tobacco products. Retail display is
not an isolated act separate from commercial promotion. In modern
marketing  practice,  particularly  where  conventional  advertising  is
restricted, the display of branded tobacco products at the point of sale
itself becomes a significant promotional device by repeatedly exposing
consumers  to  the  brand  and  encouraging  impulse  purchases,  brand
recognition and continued consumption. The statutory inquiry, therefore,
is not whether the display occurs inside or outside retail premises, but
whether, viewed objectively, it has the effect of promoting tobacco use.
-22-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
Once that statutory test is satisfied, the display falls within the ambit of
Section 2(a) of the Ordinance and the Committee was fully competent to
regulate or prohibit it while exercising the authority conferred by Section
7.  The  impugned  restrictions  relating  to  point-of-sale  display  are,
therefore,  firmly  anchored  in  the  parent  statute  and  cannot  be
characterized as travelling beyond the legislative mandate.
26 .  So far as sponsorship is concerned, the same however,
stands on a somewhat different footing. Unlike promotion, the expression
“ sponsorship ”  does not expressly occur either in Section 7 or in the
definition of  “ advertisement ”  contained in Section 2(a). The petitioners
therefore argued that the Committee travelled beyond the parent statute
by prohibiting sponsorship altogether. The contention, however, proceeds
upon  an  unduly  literal  reading  of  the  Ordinance.  The  Legislature
deliberately  refrained  from  defining  advertisement  by  reference  to
particular  commercial  techniques.  Instead,  it  adopted  a  functional
definition embracing every direct or indirect means  “ which have the
effect of promoting smoking and other tobacco use. ”  The statutory
emphasis is thus upon the promotional effect of the activity rather than
the nomenclature by which it is described. A sponsorship arrangement
which  promotes  a tobacco  product  or  brand  by securing  publicity,
enhancing brand recognition or associating the product with a sporting,
cultural or other public event is, by its very nature, an indirect means of
promoting tobacco use and therefore falls within the statutory conception
of  advertisement.  Conversely,  a  sponsorship  wholly  devoid  of  any
promotional element would not answer the statutory description merely
because it bears the label of sponsorship. Properly construed, therefore,
the  impugned  Notification  does  not  prohibit  sponsorship  as  an
independent  commercial  activity;  it  operates  only upon  sponsorship
employed as a tool for the promotion of tobacco products. So understood,
the sponsorship-related provisions remain firmly rooted in Section 2(a)
read with Section 7 and cannot be characterized as  ultra vires  the
Ordinance.
-23-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
27 .  Coming now to t he petitioners ’  contention that  if Section 7
is construed as authori z ing a comprehensive prohibition of tobacco
advertising, promotion and sponsorship, it would amount to an excessive
delegation of legislative powe r  as the  Legislature  did not  prescribe any
intelligible  standards  to  govern  the  exercise  of  the  Committee ’ s
discretion ,  t he  submission  is  equally  without  substance.  The
constitutional objection of excessive delegation arises only where the
Legislature abdi cates its essential legislative function by conferring an
uncanali z ed  and unguided discretion upon the delegate without declaring
the  legislative  policy  or  prescribing  the  principles  by  which  that
discretion is to be exercised
. It is, however, well settle d that while the
Legislature must determine the policy of the law and lay down the
governing standards, it may legitimately entrust to speciali z ed authorities
the task of working out the details necessary for implementing that
policy. Such delegation is no t only constitutionally permissible but, in
fields involving scientific, technical or speciali z ed expertise, is frequently
indispensable  for  the  effective  administration  of  modern  regulatory
legislation.  In  the  case  in  hand ,  the  Legislature  has  discharged  its
essential legislative function. Through the preamble to the Ordinance and
the substantive provisions contained in Sections 2(a) and 7, it has
unequivocally declared the legislative policy of protecting public health
by discouraging smoking and regulati ng the means by which tobacco
products are promoted. It has defined the scope of  “ advertisement ”  in
deliberately expansive terms and has confined the Committee ’ s role to
prescribing  guidelines  consistent  with  that  declared  policy.  The
Committee is not at liberty to formulate an independent legislative policy
or to legislate at large; its authority is circumscribed by the object,
purpose and standards embodied in  the Ordinance itself. The impugned
Guidelines therefore represent the implementation of a legislative policy
already settled by  Legislature  rather than the creation of a new policy by
“Khawaja Ahmad Hassaan v. Government of Punjab and others”  ( 2005 SCMR 186) ,
“Nestle Pakistan v. Director PESSI and others”  (PLD 2019 Lahore 515)
-24-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
the  delegate.  The  challenge  founded  upon  excessive  delegation  is,
accordingly, repelled.
28 .  Learned counsel for the  petitioners  emphatically  contended
that the impugned Notification derives its legal foundation not from
Section 7 of the Ordinance but from Article 13 of the WHO FCTC,
notwithstanding  that  the  Convention  has  ne ver  been  expressly
incorporated into  Pakistan’s  municipal law. According to the learned
counsel, Pakistan adheres to the dualist theory of international law, with
the result that treaty obligations do not, merely by virtue of ratification,
become enforceab le within the domestic legal order. It was argued that,
since the notification constituting the Committee required it to frame
guidelines compliant with Article 13 of the WHO FCTC, the Committee
effectively exercised a power derived from an unincorporated  treaty
rather  than  from  the  Legislature ,  thereby  rendering  the  impugned
Notification legally unsustainable. The submission raises an important
question concerning the relationship between international obligations
and municipal law. It, however, proceeds u pon a misconception as to the
true source of the Committee ’ s authority. The issue is not whether the
WHO FCTC forms part of the law of Pakistan ,  rather, it is whether, while
exercising  a  statutory  discretion  already  conferred  by  domestic
legislation, the C ommittee could legitimately draw guidance from an
international convention to which Pakistan is a party. That distinction is
fundamental and lies at the heart of the present controversy.
29 .  The distinction as noted above, becomes apparent upon a
brief notice of the two principal theories governing the relationship
between international law and municipal law. Under the monist theory,
international and domestic law are regarded as forming a single legal
order, with the consequence that a treaty, once validly ratified, ordinarily
becomes capable of direct application within the municipal legal system,
subject always to the constitutional framework of the State concerned.
The dualist theory, on the other hand, proceeds upon the premise that
-25-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
international law and municipal law constitute two distinct legal systems.
Under that approach, a treaty binds the State upon the international plane
by virtue of ratification, but does not, merely for that reason, become
enforceable within domestic courts. For a treaty to create rights or
obligations  enforceable  under  municipal  law,  its  provisions  must
ordinarily be incorporated through legislation enacted by the competent
law-making authority. The controversy in the present case falls to be
examined  against  the  backdrop  of  the  latter  principle,  which  has
consistently  informed  the  jurisprudence  of  the  superior  Courts  of
Pakistan.
30 .  Pakistan has consistently followed the dualist approach
.
The superior Courts have repeatedly held that, unless incorporated into
municipal law through legislation or otherwise reflected in an existing
statutory framework, the provisions of an international convention do
not, merely by virtue of ratification, become independently enforceable
in domestic courts. At the same time, the jurisprudence has drawn a clear
distinction between enforcing a treaty as an autonomous source of
municipal rights and obligations, on the one hand, and referring to it as
an interpretative aid while construing domestic legislation, on the other.
Where a municipal statute is reasonably capable of more than one
interpretation,  or  confers  a  statutory  discretion  to  be  exercised
consistently with its legislative object, courts and public authorities may
legitimately have regard to an international convention to which Pakistan
is a party, provided such reliance neither conflicts with municipal law nor
treats the treaty itself as the source of the legal power being exercised. In
that limited but important sense, international conventions perform a
persuasive and interpretative function without acquiring the force of
municipal law.  The above  view  finds support in the jurisprudence of the
superior Courts. In  the case of “ Mst. Shehla Zia and others v. WAPDA ”
(PLD 1994 SC 693) , while considering the relevance of international
“Societe Generale De Surveillance S.A. v. Pakistan through Secretary, Ministry of Finance
Revenue Div ision, Islamabad”  ( 2002 SCMR 1694) .
-26-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
environmental instruments, the  Supreme Court of Pakistan held that an
international  agreement,  though  binding  upon  the  State  at  the
international plane, does not become enforceable as municipal law unless
incorporated through legislation. At the same time, the Court recognised
that such instruments possess persuasive value. Relevant extract from the
said judgment is as under:-
“ An international agreement between the nations if signed by
any country is always subject to ratification, but it can be
enforced as a law only when legislation is made by the
country through its legislature. Without framing a law in
terms of the international agreement the covenants of such
agreement cannot be implemented as a law nor do they bind
down any party. This is the legal position of such documents,
but the fact remains that they have a persuasive value and
command respect. ”
(Emphasis supplied by this Court).
The same principle was reiterated by the Peshawar High Court in the case
of  “ Haji  Lal  Muhammad  v.  Federation  of  Pakistan  through  Secretary,
Ministry of Interior, Islamabad ”  (PLD 2014 Peshawar 199) , wherein it
was held that  international law  unless in direct conflict with municipal
law ought to be applied and respected by municipal courts in deciding
matters arising therefrom . More recently, the Islamabad High Court, in
the case of  “ Yassa  Khan  v.  Federation  of  Pakistan  through  Secretary,
Minis try of National Health Services, Regulations and Coordination ”  (2023
CLC 744) , while dealing with issues relating to tobacco control under the
Ordinance of 2002, likewise referred to the WHO  FCTC  and its guiding
principles in issuing directions for the eff ective enforcement of domestic
law. These authorities thus illustrate a consistent judicial approach  that
international conventions do not operate as an independent source of
enforceable municipal law, yet they may legitimately be consulted as
persuasive i nterpretative aids  where such  use advances, rather than
contradicts, the purpose of the governing domestic legislation.
31 .  Applying the above principles, the petitioners ’  objection
cannot be sustained. The impugned Notification does not owe its legal
-27-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
existence to the WHO FCTC, nor does it purport to enforce Article 13
thereof  as an independent source of municipal law. The authority to
constitute the Committee, to prescribe tobacco-advertisement guidelines
and to issue the impugned Notification flows exclusively from Section 7
of the Ordinance of 2002. The notification dated 26.09.2018, whereby the
Committee was reconstituted, merely required the Committee, while
discharging the statutory function entrusted to it by the Legislature, to
take appropriate steps to make the guidelines compliant with Pakistan ’ s
obligations under Article 13 of the WHO FCTC and to review them
periodically in the light of national and international commitments. In
doing so, the Federal Government neither purported to incorporate the
Convention into municipal law nor delegated legislative authority to an
international instrument. It simply identified the normative considerations
which the Committee was expected to bear in mind while exercising a
statutory  discretion  already  conferred  by  domestic  legislation.  Such
recourse to an international convention as an interpretative and policy
guide  is  entirely  consistent  with  the  dualist  doctrine  recognised  in
Pakistan and does not transform the Convention into the source of the
Committee ’ s  legal  authority.  The  impugned  Notification,  therefore,
derives its validity from Section 7 of the Ordinance, whereas the WHO
FCTC merely informed the manner in which that statutory power was
exercised.
32 .  The petitioners nevertheless sought to derive suppor t from
the language of Article 13 of the WHO FCTC itself by contending that
the Convention contemplates a comprehensive prohibition of tobacco
advertising  only  “ in  accordance  with  a  Party ’ s  constitution  or
constitutional principles ”  and, therefore, does  not invariably mandate a
complete ban. The submission proceeds on a mistaken premise. The
qualification contained in Article 13 defines the extent of a Contracting
State ’ s obligation under international law; it neither confers legislative
authority  upon  d omestic  institutions  nor  circumscribes  the  powers
already conferred by municipal law. The present controversy is not
-28-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
concerned with the minimum obligations imposed by the Convention
upon Pakistan, but with the extent of the authority delegated by the
Legislature through Section 7 of the Ordinance. As already held, that
provision authorizes the Committee to prescribe guidelines consistent
with the legislative policy of protecting public health and contains no
statutory  limitation  requiring  that  some  category  of  tobacco
advertisement must invariably remain permissible. Whether Article 13
obliged  Pakistan  to  adopt  a  comprehensive  prohibition,  or  merely
permitted such a course consistently with its constitutional framework, is
therefore of no legal  consequence to the validity of the impugned
Notification. Once the measure is found to be independently authorized
by Section 7 of the Ordinance of 2002, its validity cannot be made to
depend upon the precise content of Pakistan ’ s treaty obligations under the
WHO FCTC. Indeed, the flexibility recognized by Article 13 itself — that
implementation is to be undertaken in accordance with each State ’ s
constitutional  order — reinforces  the  conclusion  that  the  Convention
leaves the choice of domestic regulatory measures to municipal law
rather than itself prescribing their legal source.
33 .  The  view expressed in the preceding paragraphs  that the
Committee merely employed the WHO FCTC as an interpretative guide,
while exercising powers independently conferred by Section 7 of the
Or dinance  of  2002 ,  is  further  borne  out  by  the  notification  dated
26.09.2018  whereby  the  Federal  Government  reconstituted  the
Committee contemplated by that provision. Significantly, the notification
expressly records that it was issued  “ in exercise of power s conferred by
section 7 of the Prohibition of Smoking and Protection of Non - Smokers
Health Ordinance, 2002 ” , thereby identifying the Ordinance — not the
Convention — as the sole legal source of the Committee ’ s authority. The
functions  assigned  to  the  Committe e  likewise  demonstrate  that
compliance with Article 13 of the WHO FCTC was intended to guide the
exercise of the statutory discretion conferred by  the Legislature , rather
than to substitute or enlarge it.  The reference to Article 13  in the
-29-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
impugned Notification appears only while describing the considerations
to be borne in mind in framing and periodically reviewing the guidelines.
The Convention thus informed the content of the guidelines, whereas the
authority to formulate and enforce them continued to flow exclusively
from the Ordinance.
34 .  The petitioners next assailed the impugned Notification on
the ground that, even if Section 7 of the Ordinance of 2002 authorized the
measures embodied therein, the Federal Government ceased to possess
constitutional competence to exercise that authority after the Constitution
(Eighteenth Amendment) Act, 2010. It was contended that, upon the
abolition of the Concurrent Legislative List and the devolution of the
subject of health to the Provinces, the regulatory power under Section 7
could no longer be exercised by the Federation. Learned counsel further
referred to the Rules of Business framed by the Government of the
Punjab,  whereby the  administration  of the  Ordinance of  2002  was
assigned  to  the  Provincial  Health  Department,  and  argued  that  the
continued constitution of the Committee by the Federal Government and
the issuance of the impugned Notification in exercise of Section 7 were
constitutionally incompetent. The plea is misconceived. International
treaties, conventions, etc and their implementations are federal legislative
subjects in terms of entries 3 and 32 of the Fourth Schedule read with
Article  70(6)  of  the  Constitution.  In  terms  of  Article  97  of  the
Constitution, executive authority of the Federation extends to the matters
with respect to which the Parliament has power to make laws. Once the
Federal  Government  signed  WHO  FCTC  prior  to  the  Constitution
(Eighteenth  Amendment)  Act,  2010, it  remained  a  Federal  subject.
Whether the provisions of existing law i.e., the Ordinance of 2002 were
sufficient to give effect to obligation under WHO FCTC or otherwise,
was exclusively for the Federal Government to consider. At the time of
passing of the Constitution (Eighteenth Amendment) Act, 2010, the
subject matter in question was never a concurrent subject to be devolved
upon  the  Provinces  as  canvassed  by  the  learned  counsel  for  the
-30-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
petitioners  covered  by  Article  270AA(6)  of  the  Constitution.
Furthermore, the constitutional validity of the Ordinance of 2002 has not
been  questioned  before  this  Court.  Consequently,  the  statutory
framework established thereunder — including the authority conferred by
Section 7 to constitute a Committee and prescribe guidelines regulating
tobacco advertisements — likewise continued to remain operative after the
Eighteenth Amendment.
35 .  It  may  also  be  noted  that  following  the  Eighteenth
Amendment,  the  Federal  Government  continued  to  administer  the
Ordinance of 2002 as a subsisting federal enactment and, in exercise of
the same power under Section 7, notified the Tobacco Advertisement
Guidelines  through  S.R.O.1086(I)/2013  dated  31.12.2013.  Those
Guidelines, as already noticed, imposed restrictions substantially similar
to those embodied in the impugned Notification. Significantly, neither
the Provincial Governments nor any person affected thereby questioned
the  constitutional  competence  of  the  Federation  to  constitute  the
Committee or to issue the Guidelines under Section 7. Likewise, when
the  Committee  was  reconstituted  through  the  notification  dated
26.09.2018, it included nominated representatives of the Governments of
Punjab, Sindh, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa and Balochistan, thereby ensuring
provincial  participation  in  the  formulation  of  the  recommendations
ultimately culminating in the impugned Notification. There is nothing on
the record to suggest that any Province objected either to the constitution
of the Committee or to the exercise of powers under Section 7 on the
ground that such authority had ceased to vest in the Federation after the
Eighteenth  Amendment.  Although  the  absence  of  a  constitutional
challenge  cannot,  by  itself,  determine  the  existence  of  legislative
competence, it is nevertheless a relevant circumstance demonstrating the
contemporaneous constitutional understanding of the Federation and the
Provinces alike that the Ordinance of 2002 continued to operate as a
subsisting federal law until altered by the competent legislature. That
understanding is entirely consistent with the constitutional scheme.
-31-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
36 .  An allied consti tutional objection advanced by the learned
counsel for the petitioners was founded upon the role of the Council of
Common Interests (CCI). It was contended that the Tobacco Control
Cell, originally established in 2007 as an attached department of the then
Ministry of Health for implementation of Pakistan ’ s obligations under the
WHO  FCTC , ceased to remain under the Federal Ministry of Health after
the  Eighteenth  Amendment.  Learned  counsel  pointed  out  that,  by
notification  dated  20.06.2011  issued  by  the  Cabin et  Division,  the
Tobacco Control Cell was merged with the Health Services Academy,
which was thereafter placed under the Inter - Provincial Coordination
Division. Since inter - provincial coordination is a matter falling within
Part II of the Federal Legislati ve List, it was argued that the formulation
and implementation of tobacco - control measures thereafter became a
matter requiring consideration by, or falling within the constitutional
domain of, the Council of Common Interests. According to the learned
coun sel, the Committee constituted under Section 7 and the impugned
Notification, having been issued without recourse to the Council of
Common Interests, were therefore constitutionally incompetent.  In this
regard, it is pointed out that t he Tobacco Control Ce ll owes its existence
to an executive decision of the Federal Government and not to the
Ordinance of 2002. Neither Section 7 nor any other provision of the
Ordinance assigns to that Cell any statutory function in relation to the
constitution  of  the  Committ ee  or  the  prescription  of  tobacco -
advertisement guidelines. The impugned Notification was issued by the
Federal Government in exercise of the delegated authority expressly
conferred by Section 7 through the Committee constituted thereunder,
and not by the  Tobacco Control Cell in exercise of any independent
statutory  power.  Consequently,  the  administrative  placement  of  the
Tobacco Control Cell under one Division or another, or its merger with
any other institution, cannot determine the constitutional validit y of the
exercise of powers under Section 7  of the Ordinance of 2002 .  It may also
be noted  that  the Council  of Common  interests  is  concerned  with
-32-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
formulation  and  regulation  of  policies  in  relation  to  the  matters
enumerated in Part II of the Federal Legislative List
, whereas t he source
of the impugned Notification remains  under entries in Part I of the
Federal Legislative List, the Federal statute and  the authority delegated
thereunder,  not  the  executive  location  of  an  administrative  unit
established to fac ilitate tobacco - control policy.
37 .  Adverting now to the remaining constitutional objections,
learned  counsel  for  the  petitioners  contended  that  the  impugned
Notification offends Article 4 of the Constitution on the premise that
Section 7 of the Ordinance of 2002 contemplates regulation of tobacco
advertisements rather than their complete prohibition and that, where a
statutory  provision  admits  of  two  possible  constructions,  the
interpretation advancing a fundamental right ought to be preferred. In this
regard, it may be pointed out that as held in the preceding paragraphs,
neither Article 18 nor Article 19 confers an independent constitutional
right to advertise tobacco products, and Section 7 of the Ordinance of
2002, when construed in the light of the legislative policy embodied in
the Ordinance as a whole, authorizes the prescription of such guidelines
as are reasonably necessary to advance its object of protecting public
health. As such no competing interpretation favourable to the petitioners
survives so as to attract the principle of adopting a rights-consistent
construction. On the contrary, where welfare legislation enacted for the
protection of public health and preservation of healthy environment as
guaranteed under Article 9A of the Constitution is reasonably capable of
a construction that furthers its legislative purpose, the Court must prefer
an interpretation that gives full effect to that purpose rather than one
which defeats or substantially impairs it
. The challenge founded upon
Article 4 is, therefore, without substance.
“Privatization  Commission  through  Secretary  and  others  v.  Aftab  Hussain  and
others” (PLD 2022 SC 267) .
“Director General, FIA and others v. Kamran Iqbal and others”  (2016 SCMR 447) ,  “ Saif -
Ur - Rehman  vs. Additional District Judge, Toba Tek Singh and 2 others”  (2018 SCMR 1885) .
-33-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
38 .  Coming  now  to  the  petitioners’  contention  that  no
representative of the tobacco industry was included in the Committee
constituted under Section 7  of the Ordinance of 2002 , thereby offending
the rule of  audi alteram p artem  as well as Section 24 - A of the General
Clauses Act, 1897 , it may be noted that  the Committee contemplated by
Section 7 is an expert statutory body constituted to assist the Federal
Government in furthering the public - health objectives embodied in the
Ordinance.  The  Legislature  neither  prescribed  any  representative
composition for the Committee nor required the inclusion of every
commercial or other interest likely to be affected by the guidelines
framed thereunder. In these circumstances, no legal rig ht accrued to the
petitioners to claim representation  in  the Committee. Likewise, Section
24 - A  of  the  General  Clauses  Act  does  not  engraft  an  inflexible
requirement  of  prior  hearing  before  every  exercise  of  delegated
legislative or policy - making authority.  It requires statutory discretion to
be exercised fairly, justly, reasonably and in furtherance of the purpose
for which it has been conferred. There is nothing on the record to suggest
that the Committee acted arbitrarily, in bad faith or for any purpose  alien
to the Ordinance  of 2002 . On the contrary,  it is manifest  that  the
committee  acted within the limits of the authority conferred by Section 7
and in furtherance of the legislative objective of protecting public health.
The challenge founded upon the p rinciples of natural justice and Section
24 - A of the General Clauses Act is, therefore, without merit.
39 .  The petitioners also invoked Articles 9, 10 - A, 14, 25, 37 and
38 of the Constitution and contended that the impugned Notification
would encourage  illicit trade in tobacco products. These submissions
were, however, advanced only in general terms and were not supported
by any independent or particulari z ed argument demonstrating how the
impugned  Notification  infringes  any  of  the  aforesaid  constitutiona l
provisions. Article 10 - A, which guarantees the right to a fair trial and due
process, has no application to the framing of subordinate legislation of
general application. Likewise, no material has been placed before the
-34-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
Court to establish any infringement of Articles 9, 14 or 25 beyond the
contentions  already  examined  and  rejected  while  dealing  with  the
principal constitutional challenge. As regards Articles 37 and 38, they
embody the Principles of Policy which, by virtue of Article 30 of the
Constitution, are not enforceable by any court, though they undoubtedly
reinforce the constitutional commitment to measures advancing public
welfare and  the protection  of public health.  The  apprehension  that
comprehensive restrictions on tobacco advertising may stimulate illicit
trade is equally speculative. Such a consequence, even if assumed,
concerns the efficacy or enforcement of the regulatory regime rather than
the constitutional or statutory validity of the impugned Notification. The
possibility of regulatory evasion cannot furnish a ground for invalidating
an  otherwise  lawful  exercise  of  delegated  legislative  power.  These
residual objections are, therefore, repelled.
40 .  At this stage,  it is appropriate to notice the  citations  relied
upon by the learned counsel for the petitioners. The propositions laid
down therein,  such as  delegated legislation must remain within the limits
of the parent statute and that restrictions upon a lawful trade must satisfy
constitutional scrutiny, ar e well settled and admit of no controversy.
Those principles have been kept in view throughout the judgment  in
hand . The  relied upon  precedents, however, arose in materially different
statutory  and  factual  contexts.  They  were  concerned  either  with
restrict ions directly imposed upon the carrying on of a lawful trade or
with subordinate legislation found to have transgressed the limits of the
enabling statute. The case  in hand  stands on a fundamentally different
footing. As already held, the impugned Notifica tion neither prohibits nor
regulates  the  petitioners ’  underlying  trade  in  tobacco  products;  it
regulates only the advertisement, promotion and sponsorship of such
products in furtherance of the legislative policy expressly embodied in
the Ordinance of 2002 . Equally, for the reasons already recorded, the
Committee acted within the authority conferred upon it by Section 7 and
the impugned Notification does not travel beyond the scope of the parent
-35-
W.P. No.17594 of 2020
statute. The authorities relied upon by the petitioners are, therefore,
distinguishable and do not advance their case.
41 .  For the foregoing reasons,  both petitions are found to be
devoid of merit and are  dismissed . There shall be no order as to costs .
Before parting with the judgment, this Court places on
record its appreciation for the valuable assistance rendered by the learned
counsel for the parties as well as learned Law Officer, whose able
submissions and assistance have greatly facilitated the Court in arriving
at the conclusions recorded herein.
(RAHEEL KAMRAN)
