Stereo HCJDA 38
JUDGMENT SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT LAHORE
( JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT)
Writ Petition No.45728 of 2024
Azim uddin Zahid
V s .
Government of the Punjab  through Secretary Law and Parliamentary
Affairs, etc.
Petitioner by:-  Barrister Muhammad Umer Riaz and
Rana Rehan, Advocates.
Respondents by :  Mr.  Imran  Khan,  Assistant  Advocate
General, Punjab.
Dates of hearing: -  09.06.2026 ,  30.06.2026
JUDGMENT
RAHEEL KAMRAN, J:-  Through  the instant petition filed
under Article 199 of the Constitution of the Islamic Republic of Pakistan,
1973  (“the Constitution”) , the petitioner has called in question the  vires
of the Punjab Healthcare Commission (Amendment) Act, 2024 (VI of
2024)  dated  14.06.2024  (“the  impugned  a mendment ”) ,  whereby
sub - section (1a) was inserted in Section 6 of the Punjab Healthcare
Commission  Act,  2010  (“the  Act  of  2010”)  providing  that  a
Comm issioner  of  the  Punjab  Healthcare  Commission  (“the
Commission”)  shall serve during the pleasure of the Government. The
petitioner  has  also  assailed  the  notification  dated  18.07.2024  (“the
impugned  notification”) ,  whereby  he,  along  with  the  other
Commission ers, was removed from the Commission pursuant to the
impugned  a mendment. The principal challenge is founded upon the
contention that vesting the Government with an unfettered power to
remove  Commissioners  at  its  pleasure  undermines  the  statutory
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
independence of the Commission and is contrary to the Constitution and
the scheme and object of the Act of 2010.
2 .  As per the contents of the petition, the Commission was
established under section 3 of the Act of 2010 as an autonomous
regulatory body for regulating healthcare services in the Province. Its
functions include,  inter alia , registration and licensing of hea lthcare
providers, regulation of quality and standards, accreditation, inspection
and investigation, issuance of regulatory directions and imposition of
penalties and sanctions. Under the unamended section 6, a Commissioner
was to hold office for a term of  three years, subject to resignation, death
or disqualification in terms of section 7. In October 2023, an amendment
was introduced through an Ordinance empowering the Government to
remove a Commissioner before expiry of his term without assigning any
reas on. The said amendment was challenged before this Court and
interim protection was granted. A similar provision was subsequently
introduced through another Ordinance in February 2024, which was also
challenged and interim relief was granted. After the seco nd Ordinance
lapsed, the Provincial Assembly enacted the impugned amendment on
14.06.2024,  whereby  sub - section  (1a)  was  inserted  in  section  6,
providing that a Commissioner shall serve during the pleasure of the
Government.  Pursuant  thereto,  the  petitioner  and  the  other
Commissioners were removed vide notification dated 18.07.2024.
3 .  Learned counsel for the petitioner contended that the
Commission is an autonomous regulatory body established to regulate
healthcare services in both the public and private sectors and that its
extensive  regulatory,  investigative  and  enforcement  powers  require
institutional independence from governmental control. According to him,
making the tenure of Commissioners subject to the pleasure of the
Government enables the executive to exercise control over the very
regulator which may be required to regulate governmental healthcare
institutions, thereby defeating the object and scheme of the Act of 2010.
He submitted that the impugned provision confers an unguided and
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
unfettered  discretion  to  terminate  a  fixed  statutory  tenure  without
assigning  reasons  or  following  any  prescribed  procedure  and  is
consequently  inconsistent  with  the  constitutional  requirements  of
fairness, non-arbitrariness and lawful exercise of public power. Reliance
was placed,  inter alia , upon  “Muhammad Yasin v. Federation of Pakistan
through Secretary, Establishment Division, Islamabad and others”  ( PLD
2012 SC 132),  “ Aman Ullah Khan and others v. The Federal Government of
Pakistan through Secretary, Ministry of F inance, Islamabad and others”
(PLD 1990 SC 1092),  “ Baz Muhammad Kakar v. Federation of Pakistan
through Ministry of Law and Justice, Islamabad”  (PLD 2012 SC 870) ,  “Ms.
Imrana Tiwana and others v. Province of Punjab and others”  (PLD 2015
Lahore 522),  “Barrister Sardar Muhammad v. Federation of Pakistan
and others”  (PLD 2013 Lahore 343), and  “Babar Sattar v. Federation of
Pakistan through Secretary Ministry of Water and Power and 4 others”  (2016
CLD 134) .
Learned  counsel  further  contended  that  the  impugned
amendment amounted to regulatory capture and was person-specific,
having been preceded by two unsuccessful attempts through Ordinances
to secure the removal of the existing Commissioners. He also referred to
the  statutory  regimes  governing  other  regulatory  bodies,  including
OGRA, NEPRA and PEMRA, to submit that autonomous regulators are
ordinarily protected against arbitrary removal through defined grounds
and procedural safeguards. He maintained that the impugned notification
was  independently  liable  to  be  set  aside  for  having  removed  the
Commissioners collectively without assigning reasons. He additionally
invoked Articles 4, 9, 10-A, 14 and 25 of the Constitution, submitting
that the independence of the Commission was necessary for effective
protection of the public ‟ s right to healthcare. In the alternative, he prayed
that  the  impugned  provision  be  read  down  so  as  to  preserve  the
independence of the Commission.
4 .  Learned  Assistant  Advocate  General,  Punjab,
controverted  the  submissions  and  contended  that  the  impugned
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
amendment neither compromised the autonomy of the Commission nor
amounted to regulatory capture. He emphasized that section 5 provides a
broad-based mechanism for constitution of the Board, under which seven
Commissioners are nominated on the recommendation of a committee
comprising  representatives  of  various  stakeholders,  while  two
Commissioners are nominated by the Government. Relying upon the case
of  “ LDA v. Imrana Tiwana ”  (2015 SCMR 1739) , he submitted that
governmental participation in appointments to a regulatory body does
not,  by  itself,  establish  regulatory  capture,  particularly  where  the
Government itself falls within the regulatory sphere. He further argued
that the concept of regulatory capture could not furnish a ground for
striking down legislation in the absence of conflict with a constitutional
provision.
Learned Law Officer further submitted that the allegation of
person-specific legislation was misconceived and amounted to attributing
mala fide  to the legislature. Relying upon  “Baz Muhammad Kakar v.
Federation of Pakistan through Ministry of Law and Justice, Islamabad”
(PLD 2012 SC 870)  he contended that the validity of legislation is to be
tested with reference to legislative competence and conformity with the
Constitution, rather than the alleged motive behind its enactment. He
maintained that the Provincial Assembly was competent to amend the
Act and that the petitioner had failed to identify any constitutional
provision expressly prohibiting the impugned arrangement. According to
him, the doctrine of pleasure could not be rendered inapplicable merely
because the office was previously subject to a fixed tenure, nor could the
possibility  of  its  arbitrary  exercise  render  the  legislation  itself
unconstitutional.
Learned Assistant Advocate General also distinguished the case
of  “M uhammad Yasin v. Federation of Pakistan ”  (PLD 2012 SC 132)  on
the ground that it concerned the legality of appointment to the office of
Chairman, OGRA, for non-compliance with statutory qualifications and
did not directly involve removal under a statutory pleasure clause.
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
Finally, he submitted that none of the petitioner ‟ s fundamental rights had
been infringed and that the asserted effect upon the right to healthcare
was speculative. He also contended that the petitioner ‟ s tenure had, in
any event, expired and, therefore, no effective relief survived, relying
upon the case of  “ Munawar Iqbal Gondal v. Mrs. Nasira Iqbal and others ”
(2014 SCMR 860) .
5 .  In rebuttal, learned counsel for the petitioner submitted
that  the  challenge  to  the  vires  of  the  impugned  amendment  was
maintainable under Article  199  and  could  not  be  defeated  by  any
alternate  statutory  mechanism.  He  reiterated  that  the  Commission
regulates both public and private healthcare establishments and that its
licensing,  inspection,  investigation  and  enforcement  powers  require
Commissioners  to  act  independently,  including  when  dealing  with
governmental institutions. He contended that a tenure terminable at the
unfettered pleasure of the Government would inevitably impair such
independence and that the  non-obstante  clause contained in the impugned
provision could not override the Constitution or the fundamental scheme
of the Act of 2010. He further maintained that collective removal of the
Commissioners without reasons or hearing was independently unlawful
for being violative of Article 10A of the Constitution and that premature
removal from a fixed tenure could also attract constitutional protection
where it carries stigma. He distinguished the judgment relied upon by the
respondents concerning the effect of an overriding statutory provision
and reiterated that, in the alternative, the impugned provision could be
read  down  to  preserve  the  independence  and  effectiveness  of  the
Commission.
6 .  Since  vires  of Section 6(1a) of the Act of 2010 was
assailed in this petition for alleged violation of fundamental rights and
constitutional guarantees, therefore, notice under Order XXVIIA of the
CPC was issued and reply thereto was also filed by the office of the
Advocate  General,  Punjab  in  this  and  the  connected  Writ  Petition
No.50857 of 2024. The said writ petition filed by the Pakistan Medical
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
Association, was fixed and heard along with the petition in hand till
09.06.2026  when  that  was  consigned  to  record  room  for  want  of
instructions to the counsel.
7 .  L earned counsel for the parties  have been heard and
record examined . Before adverting to the constitutional challenge, it
would  be  appropriate  to  notice  an  objection  raised  by  the  learned
Assist ant Advocate General that the petitioner‟s tenure has, in any event,
expired and, therefore, no live controversy survives for adjudication.
Reliance in this regard has been placed upon  the case of  “M unawar Iqbal
Gondal v. Mrs. Nasira Iqbal and others ”  (2014 SCMR 860) , wherein the
Supreme  Court  declined  to  examine  an  issue  which  had  become
academic after expiry of the relevant term, while leaving it open to the
parties to agitate the substantive question in a future live controversy.
The objection, thoug h attractive at first sight, does not  warrant disposal
of the  instant  petition in its entirety. The petition  in hand  assails two
distinct acts: first, the constitutional validity of sub - section (1a) inserted
in Section 6 of the Act of 2010 through the  impu gned amendment ; and
secondly, the notification dated 18.07.2024 whereby the petitioner was
removed from office pursuant thereto.  It is correct that with expiry of the
petitioner‟s original tenure,  relief qua restoration of the petitioner to the
office of t he Commissioner for the unexpired portion of that tenure  has
lost its practical significance .  However, t he challenge to the  vires  of the
impugned  amendment, however, stands  on  a different  footing.  The
impugned  provision  continues  to  remain  on  the  statute  b ook  and
constitutes  a  threat  of  removal  of  every  Commissioner  of  the
Commission before expiry of his/her tenure .  Its constitutional validity is,
therefore, not a question confined to the petitioner ‟ s individual grievance.
The Supreme Court has observed tha t a constitutional question cannot be
regarded  as  a  mere  academic  exercise  where  its  determination  is
necessary for clarification of the relevant constitutional provisions. In  the
case titled  “ Sh. Riaz - ul - Haq and another v. Federation of Pakistan ”  (PLD
2013 SC 501) , while considering questions relating to the constitutional
scheme concerning the judiciary, the Supreme Court declined to treat the
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
interpretation of the relevant constitutional provisions as academic and
observed  that  such exercise was  „very  essential  and  necessary‟ for
making the constitutional position clear. The same principle is attracted
here because the question concerns a continuing statutory provision
governing the tenure and removal of Commissioners of an important
institution of the healthcare regulatory body.
8 .  The next question is whether a statutory provision enacted
by the Provincial Assembly conferring upon the Government the power
to remove a Commissioner “during the pleasure of the Government” is
immune  from  judicial  revie w  merely  because  the  legislature  was
competent to enact the amendment. The answer, in  the  considered view
of this Court ,  is  in the negative. The legislative competence of the
Provincial Assembly and the constitutional validity of the legislation are
two se parate inquiries. The fact that the Assembly possesses legislative
competence over the subject does not mean that every legislative choice
made in exercise of that competence is beyond constitutional scrutiny.
Legislation, like every other exercise of Stat e power, remains subject to
the Constitution and to the fundamental principles of law which inform
the exercise of public power under our constitutional order.  The question
is whether, in its legal operation and effect, the impugned provision is
consistent  with the constitutional guarantees and limitations applicable to
the exercise of legislative and public power. In the present case, as shall
presently be examined, the impugned provision falls to be tested,  inter
alia , against the guarantees contained in  Article 10A of the Constitution.
9 .  The Commission is not an ordinary departmental office
nor is  a Commissioner  merely an  incumbent  of  an  executive post
terminable at the will of the appointing authority. The statutory scheme
of the Act of 2010 reveals that the Commission was conceived as a
distinct institutional and regulatory body, entrusted with functions of
regulating and improving the quality and standards of healthcare services
in the Province. Section 3(2) gives the Commission a separate corporate
personality by declaring it to be a  bo dy corporate having perpetual
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
succession and a common seal, with powers to enter into contract, sue
and be sued by its name . The regulatory character of the institution is
further evident from section 4, which is reproduced below for reference:
“4.  Functio ns  and  powers  of  the  Commission . –  (1)  The
Commission  shall  perform  such  functions  and  exercise  such
powers as may be required to improve the quality of healthcare
services and clinical governance and to ban quackery.
(2) Without prejudice to the generality of the provisions of sub-
section (1), the Commission shall –
(a) maintain register of all healthcare service providers;
(b) grant, revoke and renew licenses to persons involved
in the provision of the healthcare services and to vary
terms and conditions and purposes of the licenses;
(c) monitor and regulate the quality and standards;
(d) operate accreditation programmers in respect of the
healthcare services and to grant accreditation to such
healthcare service providers who meet the standards;
(e)  enquire  and  investigate  into  maladministration,
malpractice and failures in the provision of healthcare
services and issue consequential advice and orders;
(f) impose and collect fees and charges on registration,
licensing and accreditation under this Act;
(g) impose and collect penalties on violation, breach or
non-compliance of the provisions of the rules, regulations,
standing orders and instructions issued under this Act;
(h) advocate rights and responsibilities of recipients and
providers of the healthcare services;
(i) hold seminars, conferences and meetings on developing
awareness  about  provision  of  high  quality  healthcare
services;
(j) enter into agreement or arrangement with the Federal
Government or a Provincial Government, any authority,
board, council or entity, nongovernmental organization,
domestic or international institution or agency for the
purposes of this Act;
(k) coordinate, liaise and network with any person, agency
or institution;
(l) take on lease land, buildings for the purpose of offices
or premises of the Commission at such price and on such
term as may be necessary;
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
(m) appoint, engage, authorize and terminate employees,
consultants,  advisors,  attorneys,  inspection  teams,
contractors,  agents  and  experts  on  such  terms  and
conditions as deemed fit and assign, delegate or entrust
them with such functions and powers as are expedient for
the performance of functions of the Commission;
(n) take measures for the welfare of the present and past
employees of the Commission as well as its all registered
members;
(o)  issue  regulations,  guidelines,  instructions  and
directives  to  persons  involved  in  the  provision  of
Healthcare services;
(p) grading of the healthcare establishments; and
(q) take necessary steps to ban quackery.
(3) The Commission may assign any of its functions to a person
on such terms and conditions as may be agreed between the
Commission and the person.
(4) In the performance of its functions, the Commission shall –
(a)  take  into  consideration  the  policy  advice  of  the
Technical Advisory Committee; and (b) co-ordinate with
the Government.
(5) Subject to sub-section (6), the Commission shall conduct third
party  evaluation  through  independent  performance  audit  of
healthcare establishments in a phased manner.
(6) The Commission shall not conduct third party evaluation
through  independent  performance  audit  of  healthcare
establishments in the  private sector other  than tertiary  care
hospitals.
(7) Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law, the
Commission may –
(a) on a complaint by any aggrieved person; or
(b) on a complaint by any aggrieved healthcare service
provider; and shall –
(c) on a reference by the Government or the Provincial
Assembly of the Punjab; or
(d) on a motion of the Supreme Court of Pakistan or the
Lahore  High  Court  made  during  the  course  of  any
proceedings  before  it,  undertake  investigation  into
allegations of maladministration, malpractice or failures
on  the  part  of  a  healthcare  service  provider,  or  any
employee of the healthcare service provider.
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
(8)  The  Commission  shall  take  cognizance  of  any  case  of
harassment of a healthcare service provider or damage to a
healthcare  establishment  and  may  refer  such  a  case  to  the
competent forum.
(9) The Commission shall take measures and devise a strategy to
counter sale of drugs without prescription.
(10) The Commission may exercise the same powers as are vested
in a civil court under the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908 (V of
1908), in respect of the following matters: –
(a) summoning and enforcing the attendance of any person
and examining him on oath;
(b) compelling the production of documents;
(c) receiving evidence on affidavits; and
(d) issuing commission for the examination of witnesses.
(11) The Commission shall not investigate or inquire into any
matter subjudice before a Court of competent jurisdiction on the
date of the receipt of a complaint, reference or motion.
(12) The Commission may authorize members of the staff to
administer oaths and to attest various affidavits, affirmations or
declarations  which  shall  be  admitted  in  evidence  in  all
proceedings under this Act without proof of the signature or seal
or official character of such person. ”
The  perusal  of  above  section  4  shows  that  the  statutory  functions
assigned to the Commission are regulatory in nature and extend well
beyond  the  performance  of  ordinary  administrative  or  departmental
functions. Section 4 ibid empowers and requires the Commission to
maintain a register of healthcare service providers; grant, revoke and
renew licences and regulate their terms and conditions; monitor and
regulate  the  quality  and  standards  of  healthcare  services;  operate
accreditation  programmes;  enquire  and  investigate  into
maladministration, malpractice and failures in the provision of healthcare
services and issue consequential advice and orders; impose and collect
penalties  for  violations  and  non-compliance;  issue  regulations,
guidelines,  instructions  and  directives  to  persons  involved  in  the
provision of healthcare services; grade healthcare establishments; and
take necessary measures to ban quackery.
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
10 .  The  regulatory  character  of  the  Commission  is
further  reinforced  by  the  institutional  structure  prescribed  for  its
governance  under  section  5  of  th e  Act,  which  vests  the  general
superintendence, direction and management of its affairs, as well as
overall policy - making in respect of its operations, in the Board of
Commissioners.  For reference, section 5 is reproduced hereunder: -
“ 5. Constitution of the Board . –  (1) The general superintendence,
direction and management of the affairs of the Commission and
overall policy making in respect of its operations shall vest in the
Board which may exercise all such powers and do all such acts,
deeds  and  things  that  may  be  exercised  or  done  by  the
Commission under this Act.
(2) The Government shall notify the Board which shall consist of
nine Commissioners, seven of whom shall be nominated on the
recommendation of the committee.
(3)  A  person  shall  not  be  eligible  to  be  nominated  as
Commissioner unless he possesses a minimum of fifteen years
experience in public or private sector.
(4) The committee shall recommend to the Government a panel of
two persons for each vacant post of the seven Commissioners to
be nominated on the recommendation of the committee.
(5) The Government shall nominate a former Judge of the Lahore
High Court and a financial expert as Commissioners.
(6) The Board shall regulate the conduct of its meetings, quorum
and minutes of the meetings.
(7) The Government shall prescribe the remuneration payable to
a Commissioner for attending a meeting of the Board.
(8) A decision of the Board shall not be valid if decided in a
meeting without quorum.
(9)  The  committee,  for  the  purpose  of  recommendation  for
nomination of Commissioners under this section, shall consist of
the following:
(a) Secretary to the Government, Health Department;  Chairman
(b) Vice Chancellor of a health sector University
nominated by the Government;  Member
(c) a representative of the Pakistan Medical Association;  Member
(d) a representative of the private healthcare
establishments nominated by the Government;  Member
(e) a representative of the general practitioners
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
nominated by the Government;  Member
(f) a representative of the Council for Tibb;  Member
(g) a representative of the Council for Homeopathy.  Member ”
From perusal of the section 5 it is vivid that the Board is constituted as
the principal governing organ of the Commission and is empowered to
exercise all such powers and perform all such acts, deeds and things as
may be exercised or done by the Commission under the Act.  The
composition  of  the  Board  is  also  significant.  It  consists  of  nine
Commissioners, seven of whom are nominated on the recommendation of
a committee comprising representatives of the Government, the health
sector,  the  Pakistan  Medical  Association,  private  healthcare
establishments,  general  practitioners,  the  Council  for  Tibb  and  the
Council for Homeopathy. A person is not eligible to be nominated as a
Commissioner  unless  he  possesses  a  minimum  of  fifteen  years ‟
experience in the public or private sector. In addition, the Government is
required to nominate a former Judge of the Lahore High Court and a
financial expert as Commissioners. The statutory scheme, therefore,
contemplates  a  multi-member  governing  body  comprising  persons
possessing professional, institutional and specialized experience, rather
than a conventional departmental hierarchy in which the relevant office-
holder functions as a subordinate of the executive Government. The
legislature has deliberately placed the general superintendence, direction,
management and overall policy-making of the Commission in the Board
and has prescribed a structured mechanism for the nomination of its
members. This structural arrangement assumes that the Commissioners
are  to  discharge  their  statutory  responsibilities  as  members  of  an
institutional regulatory body, rather than merely execute the policy or
directions of the Government. This aspect becomes particularly relevant
when the tenure of a Commissioner is examined. The original scheme of
section 6(1) provided that a Commissioner  “shall hold office for a term of
three years”  and was eligible for re-nomination in accordance with
section 5. The statutory conferral of a defined term of office, read
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
together with the nature of the functions vested in the Commission and
the governing authority assigned to its Board, indicates that the office of
Commissioner was conceived as a tenure-based statutory office rather
than an ordinary executive appointment. The distinction is material. A
regulator is required to apply the law even when the result may be
inconvenient to the Government, a governmental institution, a politically
influential  person  or  any  other  entity  falling  within  its  regulatory
jurisdiction. The effectiveness of such regulation necessarily depends
upon the ability of the regulator to act without apprehension that an
adverse regulatory decision may result in the loss of office of the person
who made it.
11 .  It is in this context that  the institutional  independence  of
the  Commission  assumes  substantive  significance.  Such  autonomy
cannot be treated as a mere matter of nomenclature, but must be gathered
from the statutory scheme, the nature of the functions entrusted to the
Commission and the field over which those fun ctions are to be exercised.
Significantly, subsection (4) of section 1 provides that the Act of 2010
shall apply to all healthcare establishments, public or private hospitals,
non - profit  organizations,  charitable  hospitals,  trust  hospitals,  semi -
government  and  autonomous  healthcare  organizations.  Thus,  the
regulatory jurisdiction of the Commission extends not only to privately
operated  healthcare  establishments  but  also  to  public  and  semi -
government healthcare institutions. The Commission may, therefore, be
required in the discharge of its statutory functions to exercise regulatory,
investigative and enforcement powers even in respect of healthcare
institutions falling within or associated with the governmental sphere.
An institution entrusted with regulating entities falling within
the sphere of the executive Government cannot meaningfully discharge
that function if the same executive authority retains an unrestricted power
to remove, at any time and for no ascertainable reason, the persons
constituting  the regulatory body. Security of tenure is  thus  not an
incidental privilege of a Commissioner. In the context of an autonomous
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
regulator, it constitutes an important structural safeguard designed to
enable the office-holder to  perform the statutory mandate  with the
requisite  independence.  The  significance  of  the  independence  of  a
regulatory body finds support in the observations of the Supreme Court
in  the  case  of  “ Muhammad  Yasin  v.  Federation  of  Pakistan  through
Secretary, Establishment Division, Islamabad and others ”  (PLD 2012 SC
132) . In that case, while examining the doctrine of pleasure in its
historical  and  constitutional  context,  the  Supreme  Court  traced  the
doctrine to the British constitutional scheme, under which important State
functionaries held office during the pleasure of the Crown in its absolute
and unfettered discretion. The Court observed that Pakistan, having
adopted a democratic Constitution founded upon the rule of law, had
retained  the  doctrine  in  its  broad  form  only  in  respect  of  a  few
constitutionally  identified  offices,  and  significantly  held  that  such
discretionary powers do not exist in statutes relating to autonomous
regulatory bodies such as OGRA.
12 .  Of particular significance  are the observations of the
Supreme Court in  Muhammad Yasin  case regarding the importance of
institutional autonomy in the functioning of regulatory bodies. While
examining the regulatory framework governing OGRA, the Supreme
Court observed:
“Vital  autonomous  institutions  such  as  OGRA  can  function
„effectively and efficiently‟ only if their autonomy is respected.
This is the letter as well as the spirit of the law. Such autonomy is
only  possible  when  appointments  to  key  positions  in  these
regulators are made in a demonstrably transparent manner; that
is,  by  ensuring  the  implementation  of  the  checks  which  the
Ordinance lays down for such appointments.”
The Court further explained the rationale underlying such institutional
autonomy in the following terms:
“These bodies have explicitly been made autonom ous to ensure
that they remain free from political or other interference and thus
remain focused on the objectives of their parent statutes.”
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
The principle emerging from these observations is of wider application.
Independence of a regulatory institution is not merely a formal attribute;
it has to be understood in the context of the statutory safeguards intended
to preserve the institution ‟ s capacity to discharge its mandate without
improper  interference.  While  the  case  of  Muhammad  Yasin  was
concerned,  in  particular,  with  the  statutory  safeguards  governing
appointment to key positions in OGRA, the underlying rationale is
equally relevant when examining the effect of a statutory power of
removal upon the independence of a regulatory body. A transparent or
participatory process of appointment may protect the initial constitution
of a regulator; it does not, by itself, ensure the subsequent independence
of its office-holders if their tenure remains liable to be terminated at the
unfettered pleasure of the executive. The manner of appointment and
security of tenure, though distinct safeguards, may operate together to
secure the institutional independence necessary for the discharge of
regulatory  functions.  In  the  case  in  hand,  this  distinction  assumes
particular importance because the Commission regulates provision of
healthcare services which constitute an essential and integral facet of the
right to light and human dignity as guaranteed under Articles 9 and 14 of
the Constitution. The legislature prescribed a structured mechanism for
the constitution of the Board and, in the original enactment, conferred
upon a Commissioner a defined term of three years. The subsequent
insertion of subsection (1a) in section 6, providing that a Commissioner
shall serve during the pleasure of the Government, therefore cannot be
examined in isolation from the nature of the institution, the functions
entrusted to it and the statutory scheme governing its constitution and
tenure. The doctrine of pleasure cannot be treated as a talisman which,
merely by its use in statutory language, places the resulting power
beyond judicial scrutiny. Indeed, the Supreme Court in  Muhammad Yasin
case itself observed that, even where appointments are to be made in the
exercise  of  discretionary  powers,  such  powers  are  required  to  be
employed reasonably and their exercise is amenable to judicial review.
Whether  a  statutory  power  of  removal  is  legally  sustainable  must,
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
therefore, depend upon the nature of the office, the statutory scheme, the
purpose for which the institution was created and the effect which the
power of removal is capable of producing upon the discharge of its
statutory functions.
13 .  The precise difficulty lies not in the existence of a power
of removal as such, but in the nature and extent of the power conferred
by subsection (1a) of section 6. The impugned provision does not make
premature  termination  of  a  Commissioner‟s  tenure  dependent  upon
misconduct, incapacity, conflict of interest, failure to discharge statutory
functions, or any other ascertainable circumstance mentioned in section 7
of the Act of 2010. Nor does it prescribe any procedure for exercise of
the power or require the Government to disclose the basis upon which the
tenure is brought to an end. The power is thus vested without any
discernible statutory standard governing its exercise. This is materially
different from a scheme under which a Commissioner may be removed
for specified causes and in accordance with a defined procedure. In the
latter case, the tenure remains subject to accountability within a legal
framework; in the former, its continuation is made dependent solely upon
the continuing satisfaction of the executive authority. The constitutional
question must, therefore, be examined not in the abstract, but in the
context of the nature of the office, the regulatory functions entrusted to
the Commission and the constitutional guarantees governing the exercise
of a power having such consequences for the holder of the office. The
fact that an actual instance of governmental interference may not have
been demonstrated does not conclude the matter. The validity of an
institutional arrangement is capable of being examined by reference to
the legal power which the arrangement itself creates and the manner in
which that power is capable of affecting the discharge of statutory
functions.
14 .  The  foregoing  statutory  position  must,  however,  be
examined in the light of the constitutional guarantees governing the
exercise of public power. Article 10A guarantees to every person the
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
right to a fair trial and due process for the determination of his civil rights
and obligations. The constitutional guarantee of due process is not
confined to proceedings before ordinary courts or tribunals. In the case of
“Naveed Asghar and 2 others v. The State”  (PLD 2021 SC 600)  it has been
held that principles of fair trial guaranteed by Article 10A are to be read
as an integral part of every sub-constitutional legislative instrument that
dealt with determination of civil rights and obligations of any person.
15 .  The expression “civil rights and obligations” employed in
Article 10A is of particular significance in the present context. The
impugned amendment concerns a statutory office carrying a defined
tenure, while the power conferred thereby, when exercised, results in
premature termination of that tenure and thereby produces direct and
adverse civil consequences for the incumbent. The mere fact that the
power is conferred by legislation does not, by itself, place its exercise
beyond the discipline of due process. As noticed above, the constitutional
guarantee of fair trial and due process under Article 10A extends not only
to judicial proceedings but also to administrative proceedings involving
the determination of civil rights and obligations. Likewise, in the case of
“Justice Qazi Faez Isa and others v. President of Pakistan and others”  (PLD
2022 SC 119) , the Supreme Court explained the wide amplitude of the
expression “civil rights and obligations” and recognized the right of
hearing as an essential component of fair trial and due process. The
Supreme Court in the case of  “ Commissioner Inland Revenue v. Messrs RYK
Mills ”  ( 2023  SCMR  1856 ),  while  considering  the  constitutional
guarantees  contained  in  Articles  4  and  10A  in  the  context  of
administrative adjudication, emphasized that the principles of natural
justice require a fair and meaningful opportunity to respond before an
adverse  order  affecting  rights  or  interests  is  passed.  The  Court
specifically recognized the importance of notice, opportunity to present
one ‟ s case, impartiality and reasons in securing a fair and transparent
decision-making process.
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
16 .  Tested against these constitutional standards, subsection
(1a) of  section  6  presents  a  difficulty of  a  fundamental  nature.  It
authorizes the premature termination of a Commissioner‟s defined tenure
at the pleasure of the Government, yet prescribes neither any substantive
ground or ascertainable standard for such termination nor any procedure
regulating the exercise of the power. The provision does not require the
Government to disclose the basis upon which the tenure is proposed to be
brought to an end, afford the Commissioner an opportunity to respond, or
record reasons for the adverse decision. The vice, therefore, is not merely
that a power of removal has been conferred upon the executive; rather, it
is that a power carrying serious civil consequences has been conferred
without any discernible legal standard or procedural safeguard. The
exercise of such an unstructured power, which permits adverse action
against the statutorily secured tenure of a healthcare regulator without
affording the safeguard of due process, cannot be reconciled with the
guarantee enshrined in Article 10A of the Constitution.
17 .  It  is  necessary  to  emphasize  that  the  constitutional
objection is not directed against the existence of a power of removal as
such. A legislature may, consistently with the constitutional scheme,
provide for premature termination of a statutory tenure upon specified
grounds  and  through  an  appropriate  procedure.  The  constitutional
difficulty arises where a defined tenure, conferred as part of the statutory
architecture of an autonomous regulatory institution, is made terminable
at the unstructured pleasure of the executive, without any ascertainable
standard or process by which the exercise of that power can be tested.
The expression “during the pleasure of the Government” may identify the
source of the power, but it cannot, merely by its use, immunize the
exercise of that power from the constitutional requirements of lawful and
fair process. The statutory discretion of the executive remains subject to
the Constitution and to judicial review.
18 .  The  foregoing  constitutional  considerations  acquire
particular significance when examined in the context of the pleasure
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
doctrine itself. The Supreme Court has recognized that, under the present
constitutional order, the doctrine is not a general reservoir of executive
power  but  an  exceptional  constitutional  arrangement  confined  to
specified offices. In  Muhammad Yasin  case  (supra)  the Supreme Court,
while considering the independence of an autonomous regulatory body,
explained the constitutional position in the following terms:
“ 29.  Much  has  changed  since  then.  Pakistan  now  has  a
democratic Constitution which provides for the government of
laws and not of men. It is for this reason that in our Constitution
there remain few positions where the incumbents "hold office
during the pleasure" of someone else based on broad discretion.
In its undiluted form this convention exists only in Article 100(2),
Article 101(3) and Article 140(3) which relate to the appointments
of a Governor, the Attorney General and the Advocates General
respectively. Similarly, such discretionary powers do not exist in
those statutes which relate to autonomous regulatory bodies like
OGRA. ”
(Emphasis supplied by this Court).
The above pronouncement is of particular relevance to the controversy
before this Court. The Supreme Court drew a clear distinction between
the limited constitutional offices in respect of which the Constitution
itself employs the language of pleasure and statutory offices forming part
of autonomous regulatory bodies. The observation that such discretionary
powers do not exist in statutes establishing autonomous regulatory bodies
is directly germane to the present case, where the impugned provision has
introduced an unrestricted pleasure-based removal power in relation to a
Commissioner of an autonomous healthcare regulator. The mere use of
the expression “during the pleasure of the Government” in a statute,
therefore, cannot by itself be treated as sufficient to confer upon the
executive the same broad discretionary authority which the Constitution
has expressly recognized in respect of the specified offices.
19 .  Comparative legislative practice within Pakistan
also does not support the proposition that an executive pleasure
power is an inherent or necessary incident of the regulation of
healthcare establishments. The Sindh Healthcare Commission
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
Act, 2013 provides that a Commissioner shall hold office for a
term of three years and specifies statutory disqualifications,
while reserving the pleasure provision in section 8(2) to the
office  of  Chairperson,  whose  continuation  in  that  capacity
depends upon the pleasure of the majority of Commissioners.
Likewise,  the  Islamabad  Healthcare  Regulation  Act,  2018
establishes the Islamabad Healthcare Regulatory Authority as a
body corporate and provides that a member of its Board shall
hold office for three years, eligible for one further term, while
prescribing  specific  disqualifications;  it  contains  no
corresponding provision authorizing the Federal Government to
terminate  a  Board  member  merely  at  its  pleasure.  These
legislative models are significant because they demonstrate that
healthcare regulation can be entrusted to statutory bodies whose
governing members enjoy a defined tenure, while accountability
is  secured  through  specified  statutory  qualifications  and
disqualifications rather than through an unrestricted executive
power of removal.  Furthermore, neither the impugned amendment
shows nor the learned Law Officer has been able to point out  any
legislative necessity for an autonomous healthcare regulator to
be subjected to an unrestricted executive pleasure power.
20 .  Furthermore,  the  significance  of  due  process  and
procedural fairness in the exercise of executive removal powers, as well
as  the  relationship  between  security  of  tenure  and  institutional
independence, is also illustrated by comparative jurisprudence. Although
the constitutional framework of the United States is materially different
from our own, the decision of the Supreme Court of the United States in
Trump v. Cook, 609 U.S. ___ (2026),  is instructive in both respects.
The case concerned the attempted removal of a Governor of the Federal
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
Reserve Board, whose office was held for a defined term and from which
she could be removed only for cause. The Court emphasized that the
Federal Reserve operates at a deliberate remove from the ordinary
political process and that not only the fact of its independence but also
the appearance of independence is integral to its design. In that context,
the  Court  held  that  the  statutory  expression  “cause”  could  not  be
construed as conferring an unrestricted power of removal and that the
cause relied upon must genuinely relate to unfitness for office rather than
serve as a pretext for securing a more congenial replacement. The Court
further held that the fixed statutory tenure and for-cause protection
carried with them a right to notice and an opportunity to respond before
removal; as the requisite procedural safeguards had not been afforded to
the office-holder, the attempted removal could not, at that stage, be
sustained.
21 .  The relevance of  Trump v. Cook  (supra)  to the present
controversy lies not in importing the constitutional law of the United
States into our constitutional framework, but in the manner in which the
Court approached the relationship between defined tenure, grounds of
removal, procedural fairness and institutional independence. The decision
demonstrates that these considerations are not necessarily separate or
competing  concerns.  Where  an  office  is  entrusted  with  functions
requiring independence from ordinary political influence, security of
tenure serves not merely to protect the individual office-holder but also to
preserve the institutional character of the body. At the same time, where
premature removal is sought on the basis of adverse allegations or
considerations, procedural fairness requires that the affected office-holder
be  informed  of  the  basis  of  the  proposed  action  and  afforded  an
opportunity to respond before the decision is finalized.
22 .  This conclusion finds support in the reasoning adopted by
this Court in the case titled  “Nabila Hakim Ali Khan v. Government of the
Punjab, etc.” ,  (PLD 2025 Lahore 759) , wherein, while examining a
statutory office carrying a defined tenure, the importance of security of
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
tenure to the independent and fearless discharge of statutory functions
was recognized, particularly where the office-holder was required to deal
with the Government or persons wielding influence.  It was further
noticed that permitting termination without a defined legal process could
impair  the  independence  and  impartiality  expected  of  the  office.
Although  the  statutory  office  considered  therein  was  different,  the
principle concerning the relationship between defined tenure, institutional
independence and accountability through law is directly apposite to the
controversy in the case in hand.
23 .  The contention that the impugned provision cannot be
subjected to constitutional scrutiny merely because no provision of the
Constitution expressly declares that a Commissioner of a provincial
healthcare regulator shall not hold office during the pleasure of the
Government also cannot be accepted. The constitutional challenge in the
present case does not rest upon the existence of an express constitutional
provision prescribing a particular tenure for Commissioners. Rather, the
impugned provision is challenged on the ground that the power which it
confers,  in  its  legal  operation  and  effect,  is  inconsistent  with  the
constitutional guarantees of lawful treatment and due process embodied
in Articles 4 and 10A of the Constitution, read with the constitutional
consequence prescribed by Article 8. Constitutional adjudication is not
confined to identifying a textual provision which reproduces, in identical
terms, the precise prohibition sought to be enforced. The exercise of
legislative power remains subject to the Constitution, while the exercise
of public power created by legislation remains subject to the requirement
that such power be exercised within the limits and for the purposes for
which  it  has  been  conferred.  The  Supreme  Court‟s  decision  in
Muhammad Yasin  case  (supra)  is instructive in this regard. The relevance
of  Muhammad Yasin  (supra)  to the present controversy, therefore, lies in
the constitutional principle that the independence and autonomy of a
statutory  regulator  cannot  be  divorced  from  the  legal  framework
governing the exercise of public power in relation to its office-holders.
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
24 .  The same conclusion follows from the settled principle
that statutory discretion is not synonymous with arbitrary power. Even
where legislation confers a broad discretion upon a public authority, the
power must remain connected with the object of the enactment and must
be exercised within the legal and constitutional framework applicable to
that authority. Article 4 of the Constitution, in particular, requires that
every action detrimental to a person be taken in accordance with law;
statutory conferment of a power cannot, therefore, be construed as an
authorization  to  exercise  it  arbitrarily  or  without  regard  to  the
constitutional  requirements  governing  its  exercise.  The  expression
“during the pleasure of the Government” may identify the source and
character of the power conferred, but it does not, by itself, answer the
question whether the power is subject to constitutional limitations. The
Court  is  consequently  required  to  examine  the  legal  effect  of  the
provision within the statutory scheme as a whole. Here, the legislature
established a specialized regulatory institution, vested its Board with
extensive regulatory and coercive functions, prescribed qualifications and
a structured mechanism for constitution of the Board, and originally
secured the office of Commissioner through a defined term of three
years. The question is whether that statutory design is compatible with a
subsequent  provision  which  makes  the  continuation  of  that  tenure
dependent  upon  the  unstructured  pleasure  of  the  executive.  In  the
considered view of this Court, the two features cannot be reconciled
without substantially diminishing the institutional protection which the
original scheme was designed to provide.
25 .  The nature of the field regulated by the Commission
further  reinforces  this  conclusion.  As  discussed  in  the  preceding
paragraphs, the Commission regulates provision of  healthcare services
which constitute an essential and integral facet of the right to light and
human dignity as guaranteed under Articles 9 and 14 of the Constitution.
Jurisdiction of the Commission extends to public as well as private
healthcare establishments and its statutory powers include licensing,
monitoring, investigation, inspection, issuance of regulatory directions
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
and  imposition  of  penalties.  Its  decisions  may,  therefore,  affect
governmental institutions as well as private healthcare providers and
other  entities  operating  within  the  healthcare  sector.  The  statutory
mandate is consequently not one of ordinary departmental administration
but  one  requiring  the  application  of  regulatory  standards  across
institutional interests that may, at times, include those of the Government
itself. In such a setting, the ability of the regulator to take decisions
according to the statute, rather than according to the perceived wishes of
the executive, assumes particular importance. The constitutional values
embodied in Articles 9 and 14, insofar as those protect life and dignity of
the citizens, provide an additional contextual reason for insisting upon an
effective regulatory mechanism. The conclusion reached herein rests
upon the statutory character of the Commission, the functions entrusted
to it and the constitutional requirements governing the exercise of public
power.
26 .  The contention raised by the learned counsel for the
petitioner that the impugned amendment constitutes persons-specific
legislation also requires consideration. This Court does not consider it
necessary to determine the subjective motive of the legislature as an
independent ground for invalidating the enactment.
27 .  The argument concerning stigma does not require separate
determination as a basis for the present decision. Learned Assistant
Advocate  General  has  correctly  distinguished  between  removal  at
pleasure, which does not necessarily carry any stigma, expiry of tenure,
which likewise carries none, and removal founded upon an allegation of
misconduct, which may have stigmatic consequences. The Court accepts
that distinction for the purposes of the submission. The conclusion
reached herein does not depend upon  the proposition that the petitioner‟s
removal was stigmatic or that Article 14 is attracted on that account.
28 .  At the same time, it is necessary to make clear what this
judgment does not hold. An autonomous regulatory body does not
require that its Commissioners be placed beyond accountability, nor does
a defined tenure render an office-holder immune from removal in every
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
circumstance. Misconduct, incapacity, conflict of interest or other legally
recognized grounds may furnish a legitimate basis for premature removal
where the legislature so provides and the competent authority acts in
accordance  with  law.  Independence  and  accountability  are
complementary,  rather  than  competing,  requirements  of  a  sound
regulatory framework .  The constitutional difficulty arises where the law
dispenses altogether with the standard by reference to which premature
termination may be justified and leaves the continuation of office solely
to the unfettered will of the executive. Such an arrangement does not
merely provide a mechanism for accountability; it alters the character of
a  defined  statutory  tenure  by  making  it  inherently  precarious.  The
distinction  is  therefore between  lawful  accountability and  executive
control at will. The former is compatible with institutional independence;
the latter may substantially impair it.
29 .  For the foregoing reasons, subsection (1a) inserted in
section 6 of the Punjab Healthcare Commission Act, 2010, insofar as it
provides that a Commissioner shall serve during the pleasure of the
Government without prescribing any substantive ground or procedural
safeguard for premature termination of the defined statutory tenure,
cannot be sustained. In the context of the statutory scheme, the provision
places the continuation of the office- holder‟s tenure entirely at the
discretion  of  the  executive,  notwithstanding  the  autonomous  and
regulatory character of the institution and the nature of the functions
vested in it. It thereby creates a legal regime in which the independence
of the healthcare regulator is capable of being compromised through the
exercise of an unstructured removal power. More importantly, insofar as
the provision permits adverse action carrying serious civil consequences
without any ascertainable standard or procedural safeguard for fair and
lawful decision-making, it is inconsistent with the guarantees embodied
in Article 4 and 10A of the Constitution. Being inconsistent with a
fundamental right, the provision cannot be sustained in view of Article 8
of the Constitution. Therefore, the petition in hand is allowed and
consequently, the impugned subsection (1a) of the Punjab Healthcare
W.P.No.45728 of 2024
Commission  Act,  2010  is  declared  void  to  the  extent  of  such
inconsistency and of no legal effect.
30 .  In  consequence  of  the  declaration  made  above,  the
impugned  notification  dated  18.07.2024,  insofar  as  it  purported  to
remove the petitioner in exercise of the power conferred by subsection
(1a) of section 6, cannot be sustained.  The petitioner‟s  original three-year
tenure has, however, expired during the pendency of these proceedings.
No effective order for his restoration to an office the term of which has
already elapsed can, therefore, presently be made. The declaration made
herein concerning the invalidity of subsection (1a), being directed against
the statutory provision  itself and  not  merely against  the individual
notification of removal, shall nevertheless operate according to law.
(RAHEEL KAMRAN)
JUDGE
Announced in the Open Court on 02.09.2026.
JUDGE
Approved for reporting.
JUDGE
Azhar*

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_005]
Case: Misc. Writ 55244/25 (Farah Hassan & 5 others Vs Additional District & Sessions Judge etc.).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Misc. Writ 55244/25. Title: Misc. Writ 55244/25 (Farah Hassan & 5 others Vs Additional District & Sessions Judge etc.). Official Ref: 2026LHC5473. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Raheel Kamran. Judgment Date (as displayed): 02-09-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5473.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 9 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Stereo HCJDA 38
JUDGMENT SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT LAHORE
( JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT)
Writ Petition No.55244 of 2025
Farah Hassan,  etc .
V s .
Additional District & Sessions Judge  and others.
Petitioners by:-  Malik Kashif Raza Awan, Advocate
Respondent No.3 by :  Ch. Akbar Ali Shad, Advocate
Respondent No.4 by:  Malik  Muhammad  Azeem  Awan  and
Mr. Mazhar Ali Haider, Advocates.
Respondent No.6 by:  Ch. Fiaz Gujjar, Advocate
Date of hearing: -  21.05.2026
JUDGMENT
RAHEEL KAMRAN, J:-  Through the instant petition filed
under Article 199 of the Constitution of the Islamic Republic of Pakistan,
1973  (“the Constitution”) , the petitioners have assailed judgment dated
21.02.2025  passed  by  the  Civil  Judge,  Depalpur,  whereby  their
application under Section 12(2) of the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908
( “ CPC ” ) , seeking recall of judgment and decree dated 19.04.2022, was
dismissed,  as  well  as  judgment  dated  02.07.2025  passed  by  the
Additional District Judge, Depalpur, whereby the civil revision preferred
by them against the said dismissal was also dismissed.
2 .  The facts forming background to the filing of instant
petition, precisely, are that respondent No.3 Muhammad Arshad Iqbal
instituted a suit for declaration and permanent injunction against the
petitioners and respondents No.4 to 7, claiming ownership of agricultural
land measuring 450 Kanals, 18 Marlas, situated at Mauza Khairpur,
Tehsil Depalpur, District Okara (hereinafter referred to as “the suit
W.P.No.55244 of 2026
property”), on the basis of gift mutations, inheritance mutation and other
revenue  entries.  During  the  proceedings,  some  of  the  defendants
appeared and conceded the claim of respondent No.3, whereas the
remaining defendants were proceeded against  ex parte . Vide judgment
and decree dated 19.04.2022, the Civil Judge partly decreed the suit to
the extent of 258 Kanals, 2 Marlas and 18 Sarsahi, while declining the
remaining relief. The petitioners thereafter filed an application under
Section 12(2) CPC, seeking recall of the said judgment and decree. After
framing of issues and recording of evidence, the Civil Judge dismissed
the  application  vide  judgment  dated  21.02.2025.  The  civil  revision
preferred by the petitioners against the said judgment was also dismissed
by  the  Additional  District  Judge  on  02.07.2025,  hence  the  instant
petition.
3 .  Heard learned counsel for the parties and file has been
perused.
4 .  Since the petitioners ’  principal grievance is that they were
either not duly served or were otherwise not validly brought before the
trial Court, it would be appropriate, before examining the legal objection
regarding the remedy invoked by them, to first examine the manner in
which  the  proceedings  were  conducted  and  the  defendants  were
proceeded against.
5 .  The suit was instituted by respondent No.3 on 25.11.2019.
On 28.11.2019, summons were ordered to be issued to defendants No.2
to 8, whereas service upon the public at large/defendant No.1 was
ordered through publication in the newspaper. On 20.12.2019, on receipt
of publication, the public at large was proceeded against  ex parte . The
matter was thereafter adjourned to 06.01.2020, on which date defendant
No.2, Mst. Fehmeeda Begum and defendant No.5, Mst. Razia Begum,
appeared before the trial Court and their statements conceding the claim
of respondent No.3/plaintiff were recorded on the order sheet. The case
was then adjourned to 18.02.2020 for summoning of the remaining
defendants.
W.P.No.55244 of 2026
6 .  On  18.02.2020,  a  power  of  attorney  on  behalf  of
defendant No.3 Mst. Rasheeda Begum and defendant No.4, Mst. Safia
Begum was submitted before the trial Court. Significantly, the order
passed on that date does not record the personal appearance of either
defendant No.3 or defendant No.4, nor does it record that any statement
on their behalf was made or recorded. Rather, the case was adjourned to
25.02.2020 specifically for submission of written statement and for
summoning of the remaining defendants. The order dated 18.02.2020
reads as under:
- 18-02-2020 کونسل فریقین حبضر۔
مدعب علیہم 3 ، 4 کب وکبلت نبمہ داخل شد۔ ملتوی ہو کر برائے ادخبل جواة و طلبی بقیہ مدعب علیہم
بتقرر 0202 - 20 - 05 کے لئے پیش ہووے۔
However, in the documents annexed with the petition, there is available a
page whereupon statements of defendants No.3 and 4 are shown to have
been recorded on 18.02.2020 whereby they are shown to have conceded
the claim of the plaintiff/respondent No.3.
7.  The above order dated  18.02.2020  is of considerable
significance.  Had  defendants  No.3  and  4  personally  appeared  and
conceded the claim of respondent No.3 on 18.02.2020, there would
hardly have been any occasion for the trial Court to adjourn the matter
for  submission  of  written  statement  on  their  behalf.  The
contemporaneous order sheet, therefore, does not support the subsequent
position reflected in the record that statements of the said defendants
conceding the plaintiff ’ s claim had already been recorded on that date.
8.  The matter becomes more significant because separate
statements attributed to defendants No.3 and 4 do not bear the signatures
of the Presiding Officer contemporaneously with the proceedings dated
18.02.2020. The signatures of the Presiding Officer appearing thereon are
dated 25.01.2022 and have been made by way of  takmeel . Thus, there is a
material discrepancy between the contemporaneous order sheet dated
18.02.2020 and the separate statements subsequently forming part of the
record.  The  former  records  submission  of  power  of  attorney  and
W.P.No.55244 of 2026
adjournment for filing of written statement, whereas the latter purport to
show that the defendants had already appeared and conceded the claim.
Such discrepancy, at the very least, casts serious doubt upon the manner
in which the said statements came to be incorporated into the judicial
record and their evidentiary worth could not have been determined
merely by noticing their existence.
9.  The Civil Judge, while dealing with this aspect, appears to
have proceeded on the premise that the existence of the statements itself
established the appearance of defendants No.3 and 4 before the Court.
Such approach overlooks the significance of the contemporaneous order
sheet and the subsequent date appearing with the signatures of the
Presiding Officer on the statements. Where two parts of the same judicial
record disclose materially different positions regarding the appearance
and conduct of a defendant, the Court is required to reconcile the
discrepancy before placing reliance upon one version to the prejudice of
the other. In the present case, no satisfactory reconciliation of the two has
been brought to notice of this Court.
10.  The question of service of the remaining defendants also
presents a serious procedural infirmity. On 11.09.2020, the report of the
Ahlmad appearing on the margin of the order sheet recorded that Majida
Begum had died, that Aqsa Bibi had been personally served, whereas no
service had been effected upon the remaining defendants. Yet, on the
subsequent date, i.e. 26.10.2020, the trial Court made no determination
regarding service of the defendants and instead directed the plaintiff to
file an amended plaint. Thereafter, on 18.05.2021, the earlier direction
regarding the amended plaint was recalled and defendant No.6 and the
legal heirs of defendant No.7 were ordered to be summoned through
summons/notices along with registered envelope A.D. and courier for
08.06.2021.
11.  On  08.06.2021,  the  trial  Court  again  directed  that
defendant No.6 and the legal heirs of defendant No.7 be summoned
through registered envelope A.D., courier service and affixation, subject
W.P.No.55244 of 2026
to deposit of process fee, besides publication in the newspaper. The
margin of the same order, however, contains the report of the  Ahlmad
that the requisite process fee had not been deposited, although the
proclamation had been received. Thus, the specific direction of the trial
Court regarding issuance of summons through the prescribed modes had
admittedly not been complied with.
12.  Despite the above position, on 15.07.2021 the trial Court
proceeded against defendant No.6 and the legal heirs of defendant No.7
ex parte , apparently relying upon the publication in the newspaper. What
is conspicuously absent from the order is any reason as to why the earlier
direction for service through registered post, courier and affixation was
not complied with, or how publication alone came to be regarded as
sufficient service in the circumstances. The Court, having itself directed
service  through  particular  modes,  could  not  simply  dispense  with
compliance thereof without recording a judicial satisfaction, supported by
reasons, that such modes had failed or could not reasonably be adopted
and that substituted service had consequently become necessary.
13.  This defect assumes importance because the requirement
of service is not an empty procedural formality. Its purpose is to secure
the attendance of a defendant and to afford him an effective opportunity
of defending the claim. Where a defendant is proceeded against  ex parte
without  first  ensuring  compliance  with  the  Court ’ s  own  directions
concerning service, the resulting proceedings are rendered vulnerable,
particularly where the defendant subsequently asserts that he had no
knowledge of the proceedings.
14.  The position is further reinforced by the judgment of the
Supreme Court in the case of  “Messrs Mobiserve Palistan (Pvt. Limited. V.
Messrs V- Tech and others”  (2025 SCMR 1936) . In the said case, the
Supreme Court examined the legality of substituted service and found
that the trial Court had resorted to publication without first satisfying the
mandatory requirements relating to ordinary service. It was emphasized
in the said judgment that substituted service can be resorted to only when
W.P.No.55244 of 2026
the Court is satisfied that ordinary service cannot be effected or that the
defendant  is  deliberately  avoiding  service,  and  that  the  prescribed
procedure must be followed before such mode of service is adopted. The
Court further underscored the fundamental principle that a person should
not be condemned unheard and that procedural technicalities should not
be allowed to defeat a litigant ’ s right to a fair opportunity of hearing.
15.  The  facts  noticed  above  cannot,  therefore,  be
characterized as isolated or inconsequential irregularities. On one hand,
the  contemporaneous  order  dated  18.02.2020  does  not  record  the
personal appearance or any concession by defendants No.3 and 4, while
separate statements attributed to them appear on the record with the
signatures of the Presiding Officer subsequently made by way of  takmeel .
On the other hand, in respect of defendant No.6 and the legal heirs of
defendant No.7, the specific directions for service through registered
post, courier and affixation were not complied with, yet the defendants
were subsequently proceeded against  ex parte  on the basis of newspaper
publication without recording reasons for dispensing with the earlier
modes of service. These circumstances, when considered cumulatively,
materially undermine the regularity of the proceedings through which the
defendants were brought within the process of the Court and ultimately
deprived of an opportunity to contest the suit.
16.  It is at this stage that the objection raised by learned
counsel for the respondents assumes significance. Considerable emphasis
was placed upon the fact that the petitioners had invoked Section 12(2),
CPC, and had not independently sought the remedy provided under Order
IX Rule 13 CPC. Learned counsel relied upon the reasoning adopted by
the Civil Judge, who, while dismissing the  petitioners’  application under
Section 12(2) CPC also observed that the petitioners had not claimed
relief under Order IX Rule 13 CPC and that such remedy could not be
extended to them by the Court itself. The said objection, therefore,
requires examination.
W.P.No.55244 of 2026
17.  There is no cavil with the proposition that Section 12(2)
and Order IX Rule 13 CPC operate in distinct fields. Section 12(2)
provides a remedy where the validity of a judgment, decree or order is
challenged  on  the  ground  of  fraud,  misrepresentation  or  want  of
jurisdiction, whereas Order IX Rule 13 provides a specific remedy to a
defendant against an  ex parte  decree where the summons was not duly
served or the defendant was prevented by sufficient cause from appearing
when the suit was called for hearing. The two provisions should not
ordinarily be treated as interchangeable merely because the facts of a
particular case may overlap.
18.  The distinction, however, does not mean that a Court
should disregard the substance of an application merely because the
applicant has invoked one provision while his pleaded case also discloses
circumstances attracting the other. In the present case, the petitioners ’
application under Section 12(2) CPC was not confined to a bare assertion
that they had not been served. They specifically alleged that respondent
No.3  had  procured  the  ex  parte  proceedings  through  fraud  and
misrepresentation, including furnishing incorrect addresses, fraudulently
obtaining signatures and thumb impressions, and relying upon a fictitious
or unauthorized power of attorney. Non-service and the consequent
ex parte  proceedings were pleaded as part of a broader challenge to the
manner in which the decree had allegedly been procured.
19.  The  judgment  of  the  Supreme  Court  in  Mobiserve
Pakistan (Pvt.) Limited  (supra) is instructive in this regard. The Supreme
Court was confronted with an application styled under Section 12(2) CPC
in circumstances where the grievance substantially concerned defective
service and an  ex parte  decree. After examining the respective scopes of
Section 12(2) and Order IX Rule 13 CPC, the  Supreme Court did not
permit  the  technical  description  of  the  application  to  defeat  the
substantive grievance. Having found from the record that the prescribed
procedure for service had not been followed, the Court treated the matter
in accordance with the remedy warranted by the facts and ultimately
accepted the application by treating it as one under Order IX Rule 13
W.P.No.55244 of 2026
CPC. The judgment thus makes it clear that where the record establishes
that a party was deprived of an opportunity of hearing because of
defective or legally insufficient service, the Court should not allow
procedural nomenclature to stand in the way of doing substantial justice.
20.  The case in hand stands on an even more significant
footing inasmuch as the petitioners did not merely allege non-service in
the abstract. Their application specifically questioned the manner in
which the  ex parte  proceedings were procured and alleged fraud and
misrepresentation as the basis for invoking Section 12(2) CPC. The
record  examined  above,  moreover,  independently  discloses  material
procedural defects concerning service and the manner in which the
proceedings  were conducted. In such circumstances, the application
could not have been rejected merely on the ground that the petitioners
had not separately invoked Order IX Rule 13 CPC.
21.  This  Court  is  conscious  that  allegations  of  fraud  or
misrepresentation cannot be accepted merely because they have been
pleaded, and that the person invoking Section 12(2) CPC bears the
burden of establishing such allegations in accordance with law. So it is
not  considered  necessary,  in  the  present  proceedings,  to  record  a
definitive finding upon each allegation of fraudulent procurement of
signatures, thumb impressions or the alleged power of attorney. The
conclusion  reached  by  this  Court  does  not  rest  solely  upon  those
allegations. Rather, the conclusion is independently supported by the
deficiencies apparent from the judicial record itself, particularly the
failure to ensure compliance with the Court ’ s own orders concerning
service before proceeding against the concerned defendants  ex parte .
22.  The  cumulative effect  of the  circumstances  discussed
above is that the  ex parte  proceedings culminating in judgment and
decree dated 19.04.2022 cannot safely be sustained. The Civil Judge,
while  deciding  the  application  under  Section  12(2)  CPC,  failed  to
properly  appreciate  the  material  discrepancies  appearing  from  the
original record and placed undue reliance upon the mere existence of the
W.P.No.55244 of 2026
disputed statements, while also failing to adequately address the non-
compliance  with  the  orders  passed  for  service  of  the  remaining
defendants. The Additional District Judge, while dismissing the civil
revision, did not cure these material defects.
23.  For the foregoing reasons, the petition in hand is allowed.
The impugned judgment dated 21.02.2025 passed by the Civil Judge,
Depalpur, and judgment dated 02.07.2025 passed by the Additional
District Judge, Depalpur, are set aside. Consequently,  the petitioners’
application under Section 12(2) CPC is allowed and the judgment and
decree dated 19.04.2022 passed by the Civil Judge are also set aside. The
matter is remanded to the trial Court for decision afresh in accordance
with law, after affording the petitioners an opportunity to file their
written statements, framing appropriate issues and recording evidence of
the parties. The trial Court shall decide the suit strictly on its own merits,
uninfluenced by any observation made herein. Parties are directed to
appear before the trial court on 25.09.2026.
(RAHEEL KAMRAN)

Approved for reporting.
JUDGE
Azhar*

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_006]
Case: Misc. Writ 30572/26 (Hafiz Ghazanfar Ali Vs Province of Punjab etc).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Misc. Writ 30572/26. Title: Misc. Writ 30572/26 (Hafiz Ghazanfar Ali Vs Province of Punjab etc). Official Ref: 2026LHC5456. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Raheel Kamran. Judgment Date (as displayed): 02-09-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF).
