VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 17 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Stereo. H C J D A 38.
JUDGMENT SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT, LAHORE.
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT.
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026
Hafiz Ghazanfar Ali  Versus  Province of Punjab  and others
JUDGMENT
DATE OF HEARING  10.07.2026
PETITIONER BY  Ch. Ishtiaq A. Khan, M/s Jahangir Ahmed Bhatti and
Adnan Ahmed Chaudhary, Advocates.
RESPONDENTS BY  Mr.  Mohammad  Osman  Khan,  Assistant  Advocate
General, Punjab assisted by Mr. Ahsan Ashraf, Junior
Legal Consultant at the office of the Advocate General,
Punjab.
Mr. Muhammad Adil Chattha, Advocate for respondent
No.7.
RAHEEL KAMRAN, J.  –  Through this petition under Article
199 of the Constitution of the Islamic Republic of Pakistan, 1973,  (“the
Constitution”),  the petitioner has challenged the Notification No.SOR-
III(S&GAD)1-25/2025  dated  30.10.2025  issued  by  the  Secretary
(Regulations),  Services  and  General  Administration  Department,
Government of the Punjab, Lahore, whereby the Tourism, Archaeology
and Museums Department (Tourist Services) Rules, 2025 have been
framed while exercising powers under section 23 of the Punjab Civil
Servants Act, 1974. The prayer clause of the petition reads as under:
In view of above submissions it is very humbly prayed that the
instant petition may very kindly be allowed and the impugned
Rules made through notification dated 30-10-2025 only for
the post of Controller Tourism, Archaeology and Museums
Department (Tourist Services), Punjab in negation to Federal
Statute, and without statue by Provincial Government may
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  2
very kindly be set aside by declaring it ultra vires to the
statue  and  constitution  and  illegal/  unlawful,  biased,
discriminatory, void ab-initio and unconstitutional.
It is further prayed that the in the mean while the operation of
the impugned rules for the post of Controller may very kindly
be suspended in the interest of justice and the respondents
may very kindly be refrained to proceed further upon the
impugned rules till the final decision of the Honorable Court
on the instant petition.
It is further prayed that any other relief which this honorable
court deems fit may very kindly be awarded to meet the ends
of justice.
2.  The petitioner, a Deputy Controller in the Department of
Tourist Services, challenges the Tourism, Archaeology and Museums
Department (Tourist Services) Rules, 2025 on the ground that after the
Eighteenth  Constitutional  Amendment  the  Federal  statutory  regime
governing the Department continues to remain in force by virtue of
Articles 268 and 270AA of the Constitution until altered or repealed by
the competent legislature. It is alleged that, without first enacting or
adopting the requisite Provincial legislation, the Provincial Government
framed  the impugned  Rules  under section  23  of the Punjab  Civil
Servants Act, 1974, replacing the existing promotional channel for the
post of Controller with posting of a PAS/PMS Officer. According to the
petitioner, the Rules were framed to validate the transfer/posting of a
particular officer whose appointment had already been suspended by this
Court,  thereby  defeating  the  petitioner’s  legitimate  expectation  of
promotion  and  diluting  the  requirement  of  technical  experience
considered essential for heading the Department of Tourist Services.
3.  Learned  counsel  for  the  petitioner  contended  that  th e
impugned rules  are  ultra vires  the Pakistan Hotels and Restaurants Act,
1976, the Travel Agencies Act, 1976 and the Pakistan Tourist Guides
Act, 1976. According to him, in view of Articles 268 and 270AA(6) of
the Constitution, the aforesaid Federal statu tes and the rules framed
thereunder continue to remain in force until altered, amended or repealed
by the competent legislature. He submitted that although the Provincial
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  3
Legislature was competent to enact a law governing the devolved subject
and to confer rule-making power upon the Provincial Government, no
such  legislation  has  been  enacted.  Consequently,  the  Provincial
Government  lacked  the  authority to  frame  the  impugned  Rules  in
exercise of powers under section 23 of the Punjab Civil Servants Act,
1974. He further argued that the impugned Rules are also inconsistent
with Article 148 of the Constitution as the Province cannot, through
subordinate legislation, override the rules framed by the Federation
under the aforesaid enactments, the operation whereof continues to be
protected under the Constitution. Learned counsel further submitted that
the  impugned  Rules  fundamentally  alter  the  existing  method  of
recruitment to the post of Controller by providing for appointment
through posting of a PAS/PMS Officer, contrary to the Civil Servants
(Appointment, Promotion and Transfer) Rules, 1973, as amended vide
Notification  dated  14.01.2004  (S.R.O.128(I)/2004).  Under  the  said
notification, the post of Controller (BS-19) was required to be filled
100% by promotion, subject to prescribed academic qualifications and
twelve years ’  supervisory experience in the field of tourism, hotel
management or travel agency. According to him, the impugned Rules
dispense  with  these  essential  eligibility  requirements  and  thereby
undermine the specialized and technical character of the office. He
further  contended  that  the  impugned  Rules  are  arbitrary  and
discriminatory as they permit appointment of the head of the Department
through posting of a PAS/PMS Officer, without insisting upon the
specialized  experience  considered  indispensable  for  the  efficient
administration of the Department of Tourist Services. In support of his
contentions, reliance has been placed on  “ Contempt Proceedings against
Chief Secretary, Sindh and others”  (2013 SCM R 1752)  and  “ General Post
Office, Islamabad and others v. Muhammad Jalal”  (PLD 2024 SC 1276) .
4.  Notice  under  Order  XXVIIA  of  the  Code  of  Civil
Procedure,  1908,  was  issued  to  the  Advocate  General,  Punjab,  in
response to which parawise comments have been submitted.
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  4
5 .  Learned Assistant Advocate General, Punjab, assisted by
the learned counsel for respondent No.7, raised a preliminary objection
to the maintainability of the instant petition. He submitted that the
controversy essentially pertains to the terms and conditions of service of
the petitioner, who admittedly is a civil servant governed by the Punjab
Civil Servants Act, 1974 and the rules framed thereunder. Referring to
paragraph No.8 of the writ petition as well as the relief claimed therein,
he argued that the grievance falls within the exclusive jurisdiction of the
Punjab Service Tribunal and, therefore, the constitutional jurisdiction of
this Court is barred by Article 212 of the Constitution. He maintained
that the exclusive jurisdiction of the Service Tribunal extends even to
cases involving the  vires  of statutory rules or notifications or allegations
regarding violation of Fundamental Rights, provided the controversy, in
substance, relates to the terms and conditions of service. In support of
the said objection, reliance has been placed upon the judgments in the
case of  “Muhammad Hassanullah (OMG/B -18), Acting Additional Secretary,
Health  Department,  Balochistan  v.  Chief  Secretary,  Government  of
Balochistan, Quetta and another”  (2025 SCMR 134)  and  “Ahmad Ullah
and others v. District Education Officer (Male), Buner and others”  (2024
SCMR 2004) .
On merits, learned Assistant Advocate General submitted that the
impugned Notification has validly been issued in exercise of the rule-
making power conferred by section 23 of the Punjab Civil Servants Act,
1974. He further argued that the petitioner has adopted inconsistent and
mutually destructive positions by questioning the competence of the
Provincial Government to frame the impugned Rules while himself
having  been  appointed  under  the  Punjab  Youth  Affairs,  Sports,
Archaeology and Tourism Department (Tourist Services Dying Cadre)
Employees Service Rules, 2017, framed by the Provincial Government
under the very same statutory provision  through notification dated
27.04.2017. According to him, having accepted appointment under the
Provincial Rules, the petitioner cannot now challenge the Province’s
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  5
competence to frame similar rules regulating appointment to the post of
Controller. To substantiate his contention, reliance has been placed upon
“ Asrar Ahmed and others v. Chairman Pakistan Aeronactuical Complex
Board,  Kamra  and  others”  (2023  SCMR  1427) .  Learned  Assistant
Advocate  General  further  submitted  that  prior  to  the  Eighteenth
Constitutional Amendment, the subject of tourism formed part of Entry
No.42  of  the  Concurrent  Legislative  List.  Although  the  post  of
Controller was governed by the Federal service rules framed under the
Civil  Servants  Act,  1973  and  the  Civil  Servants  (Appointment,
Promotion and Transfer) Rules, 1973, the devolution brought about
through  Article  270AA  of  the  Constitution  conferred  legislative
competence upon the Province to regulate the service structure relating
to the devolved subject. According to him, Articles 268 and 270AA do
not freeze the pre-existing Federal legal regime, rather, they merely
preserve  the  continuity  of  existing  laws  until  altered,  amended  or
repealed  by the  competent  Provincial  authority.  In  support  of  this
proposition, reliance has  been  placed  upon  “ Taisei  Corporation  and
another  v.  A.M.  Construction  Company  (Pvt.)  Ltd  and  another”  (2024
SCMR 640),  “Messrs Azfar Laboratories Private Limited through Directors
and others v. Federation of Pakistan through Secretary Ministry of National
Health Services and 4 others ”  (PLD 2018 Sindh 448),  “ Syed Imran Ali
Shah v. Government of Pakistan ”  (2013 PLC 143) ,  “ Salim Javed Baig and
others v. Federal Ombudsman and others ”  (PLD 2016 Lahore 433)  and
“ Kamil  Khan  Mumtaz  and  others  v.  Province  of  Punjab ”  (PLD 2016
Lahore 699) . Learned Assistant Advocate General lastly contended that
the determination of qualifications, eligibility criteria and the mode of
recruitment for a particular post falls within the policy domain of the
Government and the competent Service Rules Committee. Such matters,
being administrative in nature, are ordinarily not amenable to judicial
review unless shown to be contrary to law. He submitted that no
Government servant possesses a vested right either to promotion or to
insist upon the continuance of a particular promotion channel and that
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  6
the Government is competent to amend the service rules in the interest of
administrative  exigency  or  to  remove  anomalies.  To  support  his
contention,  reliance  has  been  placed  upon  “ Government  of  Khyber
Pakhtunkhwa through Chief Secretary, Peshawar and others v. Hayat Hussain
and  others ”  (2016  SCMR  1021) ,  “ The  Central  Board  of  Revenue,
Government of Pakistan v. Asad Ahmad Khan ”  (PLD 1960 SC 81) .
6.  In rebuttal, learned counsel for the petitioner  submitted that
the  objection  r egarding  maintainability  proceeds  on  an  erroneous
characterization of the controversy as an individual service dispute. He
contended that the petitioner does not dispute the general competence of
the Provincial Government under the Punjab Civil Servants Ac t, 1974 to
regulate  the  provincial  services  or  to  frame  rules  in  that  regard.
According to him, the petitioner had accepted his appointment under the
2017 Rules, which regulated the subordinate posts and preserved the
then existing promotional structure; h is challenge is confined to the
impugned Rules of 2025, whereby the established promotion channel
from the feeder cadre to the post of Controller has been eliminated and
the post has instead been made available for appointment through
posting  of  a  PAS/PMS  Officer  without  the  prescribed  academic
qualifications  and  relevant  experience  in  the  field  of  tourism.  He
maintained that the present challenge is, therefore, directed against the
constitutional validity, arbitrariness and discriminatory character of the
2025  Rules  themselves  and  not  against  the  general  rule - making
competence of the Provincial Government. He further submitted that
acceptance of the earlier 2017 Rules, which according to him were
consistent with the statutory and constitutional framework an d did not
disturb the existing promotional structure, could not amount to waiver of
the right to challenge a subsequent rule which alters that structure in
violation of constitutional and statutory requirements. He, therefore,
contended that the authoritie s relied upon by the learned Law Officer,
concerning disputes founded upon the individual terms and conditions of
service of civil servants, are distinguishable from the present case.
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  7
7.  Arguments heard.
8 .  It is  trite  law that the question of jurisdi ction goes to the
root of the matter and must be decided at the very outset, for unless a
Court is satisfied that it possesses the requisite jurisdiction, it cannot
proceed to examine the merits of the dispute. In this regard, the Supreme
Court of Pakistan  in the case of  “ Muslim Commercial Bank Limited v.
Muhammad Anwar Mandokhel”  (2024 SCMR 298)  has reiterated that
every Court, before taking cognizance of and adjudicating upon any
controversy, is under a legal obligation to first satisfy itself regarding t he
assumption  of  jurisdiction  and  only  upon  recording  an  affirmative
conclusion in that behalf can it proceed to determine the issues raised
before it.  So, before adverting to the merits of the controversy, it is
imperative to determine the preliminary obj ection raised by the learned
Assistant  Advocate  General  regarding  the  maintainability  of  this
constitutional petition. It has been contended by the  learned  Law Officer
that  the petitioner admittedly is a civil servant and the impugned Rules
directly regula te the method of appointment to the promotional post of
Controller,  thereby  affecting  his  terms  and  conditions  of  service.
According to him, notwithstanding the constitutional grounds urged in
the petition, the controversy essentially pertains to a service  matter
falling within the exclusive jurisdiction of the Punjab Service Tribunal,
thus attracting the constitutional bar contained in Article 212 of the
Constitution.  However, the l earned counsel for the petitioner has argued
that  the  instant  petition  is  m aintainable  under  Article  199  of  the
Constitution as it calls in question the constitutional validity of the
impugned Rules.
9 .  The  jurisdictional  question  has  to  be  determined  with
reference to the true nature of the controversy and the relief sought. The
Federal Constitutional Court, in its recent pronouncement in the case
titled  “ Province of Sindh through Chief Secretary, Government of Sindh,
Karachi & others  v . Zulfiqar Khushk & others”  ( C.P.L.A No.2799 of
2023)  has  observed  that  “A  Service  Tribunal  do es  not  have  the
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  8
jurisdiction to determine the constitutionality of a Rule framed by a
competent authority, if the terms and conditions of a Civil Servant are
not disputed .”
10 .  In the case in hand, the petitioner, while serving as Deputy
Controller, was given only the additional charge of Controller for some
time when the post fell vacant. He does not claim an accrued or vested
right to promotion to the post of Controller, nor is any individual order
denying him promotion under challenge. His substantive challenge is
directed against the impugned Rules themselves, whereby the method of
appointment to the post of Controller has been altered, extinguishing the
promotional avenue from Deputy Controller to Controller. The relief
sought is to declare the impugned Rules  ultra vires  the statute and the
Constitution and to set them aside. Such relief, if granted, would not be
confined to the petitioner but would operate upon the rule itself and,
consequently, upon all persons presently serving or who may hereafter
serve as Deputy Controller and otherwise be eligible for consideration
for appointment to the post of Controller. The controversy, therefore, is
not confined to the petitioner’s individual terms and conditions of
service but essentially concerns the validity and legality of the impugned
Rules.
11 .  In these circumstances, the case falls within the principle
enunciated by the Federal Constitutional Court that where the terms and
conditions of the civil servant are not themselves in dispute, the Service
Tribunal lacks jurisdiction to determine the constitutionality of the Rule.
The objection raised by the learned Law Officer is, therefore, repelled,
and the petition is held to be maintainable under Article 199 of the
Constitution.
12 .  T he controversy  may now b e examined  on merits. The first
question is whether the impugned Rules were made by an authority
competent  to  frame  them ?  Undeniably,  prior  to  the  Constitution
( Eighteenth Amendment ) Act, 2010  the subject of tourism formed part
of Entry No.42 of the Concur rent Legislative List. Although the post of
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  9
Controller was governed by the Federal service rules framed under the
Civil  Servants  Act,  1973  and  the  Civil  Servants  (Appointment,
Promotion and Transfer) Rules, 1973, the devolution brought about
through  Article  270AA  of  the  Constitution  conferred  legislative
competence upon the Province to regulate the service structure relating
to the devolved subject. Co-extensive with such legislative power is the
executive authority of the Province in terms of Article 137 of the
Constitution. Articles 268 and 270AA of the Constitution do not freeze
the  pre-existing  Federal  legislative  authority,  rather,  they  merely
preserve the continuance in force of existing laws until altered, amended
or repealed by the provincial authority. Reliance in this regard is placed
on the judgment of this Court in the case of  “Salim Javed Baig and others
v.  Federal  Ombudsman  and  others”  (PLD  2016  Lahore  433) .
Additionally,  learned  counsel  for  the  petitioner,  in  rebuttal  to  the
objection  regarding  approbation  and  reprobation,  has  conceded  the
general  competence  of  the  Provincial  Government  to  regulate  the
provincial service and to frame rules in that behalf. Section 23 of the
said Act empowers the competent authority to make such rules as may
be necessary or expedient for carrying out the purposes of the Act. The
impugned notification having been issued by the Government of the
Punjab in exercise of the rule-making power available under the Act,
there is no substance in the challenge to its competence as such.
13 .  The determination of the method of appointment to a civil
post,  including  the  choice  between  initial  appointment,  promotion,
transfer or posting, is ordinarily a matter of governmental policy and
administrative judgment. Guidance is drawn from the cases of  “ Lt.-Col.
Farzand Ali and others v. Province of West Pakistan through the Secretary,
Department of Agriculture, Government of West Pakistan, Lahore ”  (PLD
1970 SC 98) ,  “ Syed Muhammad Arif and others v. University of Balochistan
and others ”  (PLD 2006 SC 564) ,  “ Wattan Party and another v. Federation
of Pakistan and others ”  (PLD 2013 SC 167) . Judicial review, however, is
not excluded merely because the impugned measure is described as a
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  10
matter of policy. Where the manner in which the policy has been
translated into a statutory rule is shown to be arbitrary, discriminatory,
unguided or unrelated to the object sought to be achieved, the rule
remains subject to constitutional scrutiny. Reliance may be placed on the
cases of  “Messrs Al -Raham Travels and Tours (Pvt.) Ltd. and others v.
Ministry of Religious Affairs, Hajj, Zakat and Ushr through Secretary and
others”  (2011 SCMR 1621) ,  “ Dossani Travels (Pvt.) Ltd. v. Messrs Travels
Shop (Pvt.) Ltd.”  ( PLD 2014 SC 1 ),  “Syed Azam Shah v. Federation of
Pakistan through Secretary Cabinet Division, Cabinet Secretariat, Islamabad
and another ”  (2022 SCMR 201)  and  “Sikandar Hayat  Maken v. Federation
of Pakistan through Secretary Establishment Division Government of Pakistan
and others ”  (2021 PLC (C.S.) 1450).
14 .  The  post  of  Controller  is  not  an  ordinary  general-
administration post. It is the apex post in the Department of Tourist
Services and carries statutory and regulatory responsibilities in relation
to  a  specialized  field  involving,  inter  alia ,  tourism,  hotels  and
restaurants,  travel  agencies  and  tourist  guides.  The  legislative  and
regulatory  scheme  itself  demonstrates  that  the  functions  performed
within this field require familiarity with its technical and professional
dimensions. This is also borne out by the service structure which existed
before the impugned Rules. Through Notification dated 14.01.2004,
issued by the Federal Government in exercise of the powers conferred by
sub-rule (2) of rule 3 of the Civil Servants (Appointment, Promotion &
Transfer) Rules, 1973, the post of Controller (BS-19) was prescribed to
be filled 100% by promotion and the eligible officers were required to
possess the prescribed academic qualifications together with twelve
years’ supervisory experience in tourism, hotel management or travel
agency. The significance of this requirement is not that the Provincial
Government is forever bound by the earlier federal rule, but that it
furnishes an objective indication of the nature of the office and the
relevance which the rule-making authorities have historically attached to
specialized experience for appointment to it.
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  11
15 .  The significance of specialized education and experience
assumes even greater importance because the Controller is not merely
required to perform routine administrative or coordinating functions; as
the head of the Department, he is expected to provide its professional
leadership, formulate and implement its vision, guide its direction and
exercise informed judgment in matters falling within its specialized
domain. Officers belonging to PAS/PMS are undoubtedly qualified
public administrators and may possess substantial experience in general
administration;  however,  such  general  administrative  competence
cannot, by itself, be regarded as a substitute for substantive knowledge
and practical experience in the field which the officer is required to lead
and regulate. Administrative competence may complement specialized
expertise, but where the office carries responsibility for the overall
direction of a specialized Department, relevant academic qualification
and practical experience bear a direct and rational relationship to the
effective discharge of its functions and, therefore, cannot reasonably be
treated as irrelevant to the suitability of the incumbent.
16 .  The same consideration is reflected in the service structure
below the post of Controller, where experience has been treated as an
essential eligibility criterion for progression within the Tourist Services
cadre. Under the Punjab Youth Affairs, Sports, Archaeology & Tourism
Department (Tourist Service Dying Cadre) Employees Service Rules,
2017, framed in exercise of the powers conferred by section 23 of the
Punjab Civil Servants Act, 1974, the post of Deputy Controller (BS-18),
which is immediately below the post of Controller, requires, in addition
to the prescribed academic qualification, five years’ experience as a
supervisor in the field of tourism, hotel management or travel agency.
The same Rules also provide for appointment by promotion against 50%
of the posts of Deputy Controller from amongst Assistant Controllers
(BS- 17) having five years’ service in BS -17. In this statutory context, the
complete dispensation with any requirement of relevant experience for
appointment to the higher and functionally more significant post of
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  12
Controller is difficult to reconcile with the service structure devised by
the Provincial Government itself. If the rule-making authority considered
five years’ relevant experience necessary even for appointment to the
post of Deputy Controller, there appears to be no discernible rational
basis for treating experience as wholly irrelevant to appointment as
Controller, who  heads the specialized Department. The executive’s
power  to  reform  the  service  structure  cannot  be  equated  with  an
unfettered and unbridled power to dispense with all rational criteria for
appointment to a specialized statutory office.
17 .  The authority of the Government to regulate its services and
determine the method of appointment to a civil post, though broad, is
subject to the constitutional guarantees governing equality, fairness and
opportunity in public employment. In this regard, Article 27 of the
Constitution is required to be read in conjunction with,  inter alia ,
Articles 2A, 18 and 25. In the case of  “Mushtaq Ahmad Mohal and others
v. The Honourable Lahore High Court, Lahore and others”  (1997 SCMR
1043) , the Supreme Court observed:
“We may observe that Article 27 of the Constitution is to be
read in conjunction with inter alia Articles 2A, 18 and 25 of
the  Constitution.  Aforesaid  Articles  2A  and  18  of  the
Constitution  have  already  been  referred  to  hereinabove.
Whereas above Article 25 of the Constitution guarantees that
all citizens are equal before law and are entitled to equal
protection and that they shall not be discriminated on the
basis  of  sex  alone.  Inter  alia  the  above  Articles  of  the
Constitution are designed, intended and directed to bring
about an egalitarian society based on Islamic concept of
social justice.”
The same constitutional conception has more recently been reaffirmed
by the Supreme Court in  “General Post Office, Islamabad and others v.
Muhammad Jalal”  (PLD 2024 SC 1276) . While examining the validity of
rules and policies governing appointments in the public service, the
Supreme  Court  observed  that  “The  Government  and  public  sector
employment cannot be allowed to be parceled out to the functionaries of
the Sta te”  and that the Constitution stipulates that equal employment and
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  13
economic opportunities must be provided to all citizens; economic
justice, being a component of social justice, focuses on creating equal
opportunities for all within society. The Supreme Court further held that
appointments which obstruct qualified citizens from competing for entry
into the service of Pakistan according to their ability and eligibility
negate equality of opportunity, competition and merit and defeat the
object of good governance. It was also emphasized that good governance
cannot be achieved by exercising discretionary powers unreasonably or
arbitrarily and that a law, policy or rule which is manifestly inconsistent
with constitutional commands and discriminatory is subject to judicial
review.  Significantly,  applying  these  constitutional  principles,  the
Supreme Court in the said case declared the policies and rules providing
appointments without open advertisement, competition and merit to be
discriminatory and ultra vires Articles 3, 4, 5(2), 18, 25(1) and 27 of the
Constitution, while emphasizing that equal employment opportunities,
transparency  and  merit  are  essential  to  good  governance  and  that
discretionary powers cannot be exercised unreasonably or arbitrarily.
18 .  The foregoing principles make it clear that although the
Government possesses considerable latitude in structuring its services
and determining the method of appointment to a civil post, such power is
not unconfined. The framework of public employment remains subject
to the constitutional requirements of equality, fairness, merit and non-
discrimination. The impugned Rule must, therefore, be tested against
these constitutional standards.
19 .  Within this constitutional framework, Article 25 does not
prohibit the State from making classifications. It does, however, require
that any differential treatment rest upon a reasonable basis. The Supreme
Court in  “I.A. Sherwani and others v. Government of Pakistan and others”
(1991 SCMR 1041)  authoritatively held that equal protection of law
does  not  envisage  that  every  person  must  be  treated  alike  in  all
circumstances; persons similarly situated or similarly placed are required
to be treated alike, while classification is permissible where it is founded
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  14
upon a reasonable distinction or basis. The same principle has been
reiterated in  “Syed Azam Shah v. Federation of Pakistan through Secretary
Cabinet  Division,  Cabinet  Secretariat,  Islamabad  and  another”  (2022
SCMR 201)  and  “Hadayat Ullah and others v. Federation of Pakistan  and
others”  (2022 SCMR 1691) . In  Syed Azam Shah  case it was explained
that the concept of “intelligible differentia” connotes a comprehensible
dissimilarity between those grouped together and those left out, and that
such  differentiation  or  categorization  must  have  a  logical  and
commonsensical nexus with the object sought to be achieved. Thus, the
constitutional requirement of equality does not prevent the Government
from prescribing different eligibility criteria for different posts or classes
of officers; what it prohibits is a classification which is arbitrary, lacks
an intelligible basis or bears no rational nexus with the object sought to
be achieved.
20 .  No such rational nexus is discernible from the impugned
Rule. It does not require the officer proposed to be posted as Controller
to possess any relevant academic qualification, specialized training or
experience. Nor does it prescribe any objective yardstick by which the
suitability of an otherwise eligible PAS/PMS officer is to be assessed
with reference to the specialized functions of the office. Consequently,
an officer may be selected solely by reason of belonging to the specified
service and grade, irrespective of whether he possesses any knowledge
or practical experience of the field which he would be required to
administer. Conversely, an officer possessing the relevant academic
qualification and experience considered significant by the earlier service
regimes is excluded from meaningful consideration merely because of
his service affiliation. The distinction is thus founded upon service
identity  rather  than  upon  any  characteristic  bearing  a  rational
relationship  with  the  functions  of  the  post.  Indeed,  the  very
considerations which are objectively relevant to the performance of the
office have been excluded from the eligibility framework, while an
otherwise unrelated characteristic has been made the decisive basis for
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  15
eligibility. Such a classification cannot be regarded as reasonable merely
because it has been incorporated in a statutory rule or is sought to be
justified  as  an  administrative  policy.  A  rule  governing  public
employment cannot, consistently with the constitutional guarantees of
equality  of  opportunity,  merit  and  fairness,  confer  an  exclusive
advantage  upon  a  particular  class  of  officers  without  prescribing
objective criteria having a rational nexus with the duties of the post.
21 .  The conclusion is further reinforced by the nature of the
statutory functions attached to the office. If the rule-making authority
itself considered five y ears’ relevant supervisory experience necessary
for  appointment  to  the  immediately  subordinate  post  of  Deputy
Controller  and  the  earlier  service  regime  considered  twelve  years’
supervisory experience in the relevant field necessary for appointment as
Controller, there must be some rational  explanation  for dispensing
altogether with relevant experience and qualification when prescribing
the  eligibility  of  the  Controller  who  is  to  head  the  specialized
Department. No such basis is disclosed by the impugned Rule. The
defect, therefore, does not lie in the Government’s decision to depart
from the earlier promotional arrangement; rather, it lies in the manner in
which that policy has been translated into a rule which confers eligibility
upon one class of officers solely on account of their service affiliation
while  excluding  persons  possessing  qualifications  and  experience
directly relevant to the functions of the post. The rule consequently
substitutes an objective and functionally relevant eligibility framework
with a discretion which is not guided by any discernible standard relating
to the suitability of the incumbent for the specialized office. As observed
by  the  Supreme  Court  in  General  Post  Office  case  (supra),  good
governance  cannot  be  achieved  by  exercising  discretionary  powers
unreasonably  or  arbitrarily;  rather,  constitutional  requirements  of
justness,  fairness  and  openness  must  govern  the  exercise  of  such
discretion.  The  discretion  conferred  by  the  impugned  Rule,  being
unsupported by intelligible standards and lacking a rational nexus with
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  16
the object sought to be achieved, therefore suffers from the vice of
arbitrariness and discriminatory classification in violation of Article 25
of  the  Constitution  and  is  inconsistent  with  the  constitutional
requirement of equality of opportunity in public employment.
22 .  This Court is conscious that it cannot substitute its own
administrative preference for that of the Executive. The constitutional
issue is not whether a Controller ought necessarily to be appointed by
promotion rather than posting, nor whether an officer belonging to
PAS/PMS is incapable of performing the functions of the post. Those are
matters falling within the executive domain. The narrower and decisive
question  is  whether,  while  exercising  its  rule-making  power,  the
Government could lawfully confine eligibility to PAS/PMS officers
without prescribing any educational qualification, specialized training or
relevant experience commensurate with the functions of a specialized
statutory office. The Constitution does not require the Government to
perpetuate a particular method of recruitment, but it does require that the
method ultimately prescribed for public employment operate within the
bounds of equality,  merit, fairness and non-discrimination. For the
reasons recorded above, the answer is in the negative. The impugned
Rule, to that extent, is arbitrary and discriminatory and cannot be
allowed to stand.
23 .  For the foregoing reasons, the Tourism, Archaeology and
Museums Department (Tourist Services) Rules, 2025, issued through
Notification No.SOR-III(S&GAD)1-25/2025 dated 30.10.2025, to the
extent they govern appointment to the post of Controller, are hereby set
aside.  The  competent  authority is  directed  to  reframe  the relevant
provision governing appointment to the post of Controller in accordance
with  the  constitutional  requirements  discussed  hereinabove,  by
prescribing reasonable, objective and functionally relevant educational
qualifications and experience and by providing an intelligible criterion
for selection. Upon reframing of the said provision, the appointment to
the post of Controller shall thereafter be made afresh in accordance with
Writ Petition No.30572 of 2026  17
the duly reframed Rules. The aforesaid exercise, including the making of
fresh appointment, shall be completed as expeditiously as possible and
preferably within three months from the receipt of a copy of this
judgment. A compliance report shall be submitted before this Court
through the Deputy Registrar (Judicial) within the aforesaid period.
24 .  The petition in hand is allowed in the above terms.
(RAHEEL KAMRAN)
JUDGE
Announced in the Open Court on 01.09.2026.
JUDGE
Approved for reporting.

in case he commi ts default in the payment of  Diyat  amount, a t

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_008]
Case: Crl. Appeal-Against Interim Order-CNSA 221-25 (MUHAMMAD ARSLAN VS THE STATE ETC.).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Crl. Appeal-Against Interim Order-CNSA 221-25. Title: Crl. Appeal-Against Interim Order-CNSA 221-25 (MUHAMMAD ARSLAN VS THE STATE ETC.). Official Ref: 2025LHC8715. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Tariq Saleem Sheikh. Judgment Date (as displayed): 02-09-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2025LHC8715.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 11 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Stereo. HCJDA 38
JUDGMENT SHEET
LAHORE HIGH COURT
BAHAWALPUR BENCH, BAHAWALPUR
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
Muhammad Arsalan  Vs.  The State and another
JUDGMENT
Date  of hearing :  30 . 0 6 .202 5
For  the  Appellant :  Malik Saeed Ejaz, Advocate.
For the  Respondent s :
Rao  Riaz  A hmad  Khan ,  Deputy  Prosecutor
General , with  Muhammad  Tayyab Ameer / T - SI .
Tariq Saleem Sheikh, J.  –  The Appellant is facing trial in
connection with FIR No. 147/2022 dated 12.03.2022, registered under
section 9(c) of the Control of Narcotic Substances Act, 1997 ( “ CNSA ” )
at Police Station Samma Satta, Bahawalpur. During the trial, the
prosecution produced six witnesses whose evidence was recorded.
Thereafter, the case was fixed for recording the Appellant ’ s statement
under section 342 Cr.P.C. On 26.08.2024, he filed an application under
Article 59 of the Qanun-e-Shahadat, 1984 (the  “ QSO ” ), read with
Article 10-A of the Constitution of the Islamic Republic of Pakistan,
1973 (the  “ Constitution ” ), praying that the Recovery Memo (Exh. PA),
complaint (Exh. PB) and the rough site plan (Exh. PC) be sent to a
handwriting  expert  for  comparison  with  the  handwriting  of  the
Complainant/Respondent  No.2,  Muhammad  Tayyab  Ameer/T-SI
(PW-3) who claimed that all those documents were in his handwriting.
The Additional Sessions Judge, Bahawalpur, dismissed the application
vide order dated 22.04.2025 (the  “ Impugned Order ” ). The Appellant
has challenged that order through this appeal filed under section 48 of
the CNSA.
The Impugned Order
2.  The  trial  court  observed  that  the  evidence of  all  the
prosecution witnesses, including the Complainant/Investigating Officer
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
- 2 -
Tayyab/T-SI (PW-3), had been recorded and that the defence had
already conducted detailed cross-examinations. The case was fixed for
the statement of the accused (Appellant) under section 342 Cr.P.C. If
there were any contradictions in the prosecution ’ s case, their effect
would be considered at the time of final arguments. The court added
that under section 29 of the CNSA, a presumption arises against the
accused unless proven otherwise. It further remarked that, according to
settled case law, expert opinions on handwriting are generally weak and
not conclusive, particularly when other direct evidence is available.
Referring to  Syed Shabbir Hussain v. The State  (1968 SCMR 1126),
the court concluded that seeking expert opinion at this stage would
serve no useful purpose and only prolong the proceedings. It therefore
dismissed the Appellant ’ s application.
The submissions
3.  The  Appellant ’ s  counsel  contends  that  the  Impugned
Order is perverse and not sustainable. He argues that the Appellant has
been denied the right to a fair trial guaranteed under Article 10A of the
Constitution. As such, it is liable to be set aside.
4.  The  Deputy  Prosecutor  General  has  controverted  this
appeal and supported the Impugned Order.
Opinion
5.  Muhammad  Tayyab  Ameer/T-SI  (PW-3)  is  the
Complainant as well as the Investigating Officer of this case. He
testified that on 12.03.2022, on a spy information, he along with other
police officials apprehended the Appellant from Mouza Rama and
recovered 1080 grams of crystal  ICE  from a polythene bag he was
carrying. PW Tayyab/T-SI further stated that he separated 54 grams
from the contraband and prepared a sealed sample parcel for chemical
analysis. Then, he sealed the remaining  ICE  in another sealed parcel
(P-1), affixed his stamp bearing the acronym  “ TA ”  on both the parcels
in the presence of Sajid Nazir 462/HC (PW-2) and Riaz Hussain
1833/C (PW-5) and secured them vide Recovery Memo Exh. PA. After
that, he drafted the complaint Exh. PB and sent it to the police station
through Riaz Hussain 1833/C (PW-5) for the registration of FIR
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
- 3 -
No.147/2022  (Exh.  PD).  He  further  stated  that  subsequently  the
investigation  of  the  case  was  also  entrusted  to  him,  which  he
conducted. He prepared a rough site plan (Exh. PC) and recorded the
statements  of  the  witnesses  (Exh.  DA,  Exh.  DB,  Exh.  DC,  and
Exh. DD).
6.  During cross-examination, the Appellant asked Tayyab/
T-SI  (PW-3)  various  questions  to  establish  that  the  documents
mentioned in paragraph 1 were not in his handwriting and that the
recovery proceedings were fabricated. PW Tayyab initially stated that
he could not replicate the handwriting on Exh. DA, Exh. DB, Exh. DC,
and Exh. DD because his index finger was swollen. Then, responding
to another question, he stated that the finger injury had occurred a year
earlier and that he had not been declared disabled or unfit. The relevant
portion of the cross-examination reads:
“ I  completed  the  investigation,  I  consumed  30  minutes  in
preparation of site plan. I reached the police station at 3:55 p.m.
Exh. DA, E xh. DB and Exh. DC are written by me … I cannot write
down the same writing of Exh. DA, Exh. DB and Exh. DC due to
the reason that my index finger is swelling condition … I cannot
write down the same writing [like in] Exh. DD (statement of Sajjad
Nazir under section 161 Cr.P.C.) as well as the hand writing on Exh.
PA, Exh. PB and Exh. PC due to the said reason. It is also incorrect
to suggest that the reason given is absolutely wrong and that no
index finger is swell (sic). At this stage, the witness again said that
my finger is not swell, however, it was injured about one year back.
I am not declared disable neither by any doctor nor anyone from my
police department. I was not declared unfit by my hand over other
organ/fingers. I can write down through this finger. ” (sic)
(emphasis added)
7.  Given the above, the question arises as to whether the
Additional Sessions Judge rightly declined the Appellant ’ s request to
refer the aforementioned documents to the handwriting expert.
Applicable  l aw
8.  Articles 59, 61, 78, 78-A, 79, and 84 of the QSO set out
the legal framework for proving a document and/or its handwriting or
any signature thereon. In  Mobarik Ali Ahmed v. State of Bombay
(AIR 1957 SC 857), the Supreme Court of India (SCI) explained that
the authorship of a document may be proved through various means,
including  direct  testimony,  handwriting  comparison,  internal
consistency with related documents, or expert opinion. It emphasized
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
- 4 -
that proof of authorship is simply proof of fact and may rest on direct
or  circumstantial  evidence.  Building  on  Mobarik  Ali ,  the  SCI  in
Gulzar Alishri Raj Mohammad v. State of Himachal Pradesh  (1998)
2 SCC 192, the SCI held that the evidentiary modes provided in
sections 45 and 47 of the Indian Evidence Act 1872  –  corresponding to
Articles 59 and 60 of the QSO  –  are not exhaustive for proving the
genuineness or authorship of a document.
9.  Article 59 of the QSO provides that when the court has to
form an opinion upon a point of foreign law, or of science or art, or as
to identity of handwriting or finger impressions or any other expert
matter, the opinions upon that point of persons specially skilled in such
foreign law, science or art, or in questions as to identity of handwriting
or finger impressions, are relevant facts. This provision acknowledges
that  certain  matters  lie  beyond  judges’  knowledge  or  experience.
Therefore, in such cases, expert input becomes necessary. The term
“ relevant fact ”  signifies that expert opinion is not merely ancillary
commentary but is admissible evidence that may assist the court in
resolving technically complex or disputed issues, such as handwriting
authenticity. However, such an opinion is not binding. Its evidentiary
weight depends on the expert ’ s qualifications, the methodology, and
reasoning. An unsupported or speculative opinion carries little value,
and where conflicting expert opinions are presented, the court must
determine which is more persuasive in light of the entire record.
10.  Article 84(1) of the  QSO  stipulates  that, in  order  to
ascertain whether a signature, writing, or seal is that of the person by
whom it purports to have been written or made, any signature, writing,
or seal admitted or proved to the satisfaction of the court to have been
written or made by that person may be compared with the one in
question, even if that signature, writing, or seal has not been produced
or proved for any other purpose. Article 84(2) provides that the court
may direct any person present in court to write any words or figures to
enable it to compare those words or figures with any alleged to have
been written by such person. Article 84(3) states that these provisions
also apply to finger impressions with any necessary modifications.
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
- 5 -
11.  In  Brindra  Kumar  Ghose  and  others  v.  Emperor
(37 C 467 : 7 IC 359 : 11 Cr LJ 453), Jenkins, C.J. stated that section
73 of the Indian Evidence Act, which corresponds to Article 84 of the
QSO, requires that the disputed writing must  “ purport ”  to have been
written by the person to whom it is attributed, that is to say, the writing
itself  must  state  or  indicate  that  it  was  written  by  that  person.
According to this interpretation of the section, it is not competent to a
court under the first paragraph of the section, to compare with an
admitted or proved writing another writing which does not  “ purport ”  to
have been written by a particular person, but is alleged to have been
written by that person. A disputed writing which does not  “ purport ” ,
but is alleged to be in a person ’ s handwriting, cannot be compared with
an admitted or proved writing, but only with the words or figures
which, under the second paragraph of the section, the alleged writer is
made to write in court. According to Monir, this narrow interpretation
of the section though followed in subsequent Calcutta cases and by the
Oudh Court in another case, has not found favour with the Bombay and
the Madras High Courts where it has, in substance, been held that the
word  “ purports ” , in the first paragraph of the section is equivalent to
“ is alleged ”  and that a court is competent to compare with an admitted
or proved writing not only a writing which  “ purports ”  to be in the
alleged writer ’ s handwriting but also a writing which is alleged to be in
his handwriting without expressly purporting to be so.
12.  Article 84 of the QSO is an enabling provision which is
indicated by the use of the word  “ may ” . It confers discretion on the
court. However, while this power exists, it should be exercised with
caution, particularly in cases involving technical complexity or genuine
dispute. Judges are not handwriting experts; relying solely on visual
comparison  may  lead  to  error.  In  Kessarbai  v.  Jethabhai  Jivan
(AIR 1928 PC 277), the Privy Council observed that it is not always
safe to stake a decision on the correct determination of the genuineness
of a signature by mere comparison with admitted signatures, especially
without the aid of microscopic enlargements or any expert evidence. In
Brindra  Kumar  Ghose,  the  Court  stated  that  comparison  of
M. Monir, Law of Evidence, Fourteenth Ed., Vol.2, p. 1316
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
- 6 -
handwriting is always a hazardous and inconclusive mode of proof,
particularly when undertaken by someone not conversant with the
subject and without the benefit of guidance from counsel ’ s arguments
or expert evidence. In  Galstaun v. Sanatan Pal  (AIR 1925 Cal. 485,
78 IC 668), it was held that it is undesirable for a judge to undertake the
task of comparing signatures to determine whether forgery has been
committed in a case. Courts in Pakistan have also held the same view.
13.  While Articles 59 and 84 of the QSO prescribe different
methods of proof, one involving expert assistance and the other judicial
scrutiny, they are not alternatives in the sense of being mutually
exclusive. They are rather complementary. Courts may resort to Article
84  in  clear-cut  or  straightforward  cases  where  the  similarity  or
dissimilarity is obvious. In more complex situations, where the writing
is distorted, disguised, or forged with sophistication, they may prefer or
permit recourse to expert opinion under Article 59.
14.  In addition to the provisions of the QSO, sections 510 and
540 Cr.P.C. are relevant in the present context. Section 510 governs the
admissibility  of  reports  by  certain  government  experts,  such  as
chemical examiners, serologists, and fingerprint analysts, and allows
the court to summon them if their oral examination is necessary.
Handwriting experts are not among the categories expressly covered by
this  provision.  Accordingly,  their  written  reports  are  not  per  se
admissible under section 510. The expert who authored the report must
ordinarily be summoned for examination unless both parties consent to
its admission without formal proof.
15.  Section 540 Cr.P.C. empowers the court at any stage of the
inquiry, trial, or other proceeding to summon any person as a witness,
or examine any person in attendance, if it considers his evidence
essential to the just decision of the case. This provision is couched in
the widest possible terms and is designed to enable the court to obtain
all necessary evidence for reaching a just conclusion, even if such
evidence is sought after the closure of the prosecution case. The
See:  Messrs  Waqas  Enterprises  and  others  v.  Allied  Bank  of  Pakistan  and  others
(1999  SCMR  85),  Mst.  Umatul  Waheed  and  others  v.  Mst.  Nasira  Kausar  and  others
(1985 SCMR 214),  Ghulam Rasool and others v. Sardarul Hassan and another  (1997 SCMR 976),
and  Rehmat Ali Ismailia v. Khalid Mehmood  (2004 SCMR 361).
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
- 7 -
discretionary power under section 540 may be exercised to summon or
refer matters to a handwriting expert when a material dispute arises and
the court finds that such evidence is necessary for resolving it. The
guiding principle is that the interest of justice must prevail over
procedural formality.
16.  Courts in Pakistan have consistently held that the opinion
of a handwriting expert is a weak type of evidence. In  Syed Shabbir
Hussain v. The State  (1968 SCMR 1126), the Supreme Court of
Pakistan held that  “ the evidence of the handwriting expert is neither the
only nor the best method of proving the handwriting or signature of a
person. It is at best only opinion evidence. ”  In  Ch. Abdul Hamid v.
Deputy Commissioner and others  (1985 SCMR 359), the Supreme
Court cited the following excerpt from  M. Monir Law of Evidence
(Vol. l, page 621):  “…  of all kinds of evidence admitted in a Court, this
is the most unsatisfactory. It is so weak and decrepit as scarcely to
deserve a place in our system of jurisprudence. In view of this infirmity
of expert testimony, it is the settled practice of courts not to base a
finding  merely  on  expert  opinion.  Conclusion  based  on  mere
comparison of handwriting must at best be indecisive, and yield to the
positive evidence in the case. ”  In  Mst. Saadat Sultan and others v.
Muhammad Zahur Khan and others  (2006 SCMR 193 : PLJ 2006 SC
1478), the Supreme Court reaffirmed that the opinion of a handwriting
expert, while relevant under the law, is a weak type of evidence and not
conclusive.  It  is  merely confirmatory  or  explanatory of  direct  or
circumstantial  evidence and cannot  prevail over testimony that is
confidence-inspiring and worthy of credence. Such opinion may be
rebutted by independent and overwhelming evidence to the contrary.
The Court observed that it is always risky to base findings regarding
the genuineness of handwriting solely on expert opinion. This form of
evidence must be received with great caution, particularly because an
expert, however impartial he may appear, may unconsciously develop a
bias in favour of the party who has called him. The Court further stated
that while such evidence must be critically examined, its rejection does
not imply that the expert has acted dishonestly. Although corroboration
is not a legal requirement, the degree of reliance that a court may place
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
- 8 -
on expert opinion in any case depends on the facts and circumstances
of that particular case.
17.  Indian  jurisprudence  adopts  a  more  nuanced  view.
In  Murari Lal v. State of Madhya Pradesh,  AIR 1980 SC 531 :
(1980) 1 SCC 704, the SCI observed that there is no justification for
equating the evidence of an expert with that of an accomplice or
requiring corroboration as a rule. While it may be hazardous to base a
conviction solely on the opinion of a handwriting expert, the risk arises
not because experts are inherently unreliable but because all human
judgment is fallible. An expert may err due to faulty observation,
mistaken premises, or incorrect reasoning. The more developed and
precise a scientific field is, the lower the risk of error. Fingerprint
identification is highly reliable, whereas handwriting analysis is less
exact and thus carries a greater margin of error. However, this does not
justify a blanket requirement of corroboration in every case. The SCI
emphasized that an expert deposes, not decides. His duty is to provide
the judge with the necessary scientific criteria to assess the issue,
enabling the judge to form an independent conclusion based on the
facts and the expert ’ s reasoning. The Court further clarified that a judge
is not precluded from comparing disputed and admitted writings under
section 73 of the Indian Evidence Act (which corresponds to Article 84
of the QSO), even in the absence of expert evidence. It held that where
expert testimony is available, it may assist the court, but where it is not,
the court must perform the task itself. The duty to compare cannot be
avoided on the ground that the court lacks expertise. Judges must
discharge this function with or without expert guidance, using such aids
as authoritative texts or personal experience where appropriate.
18.  However,  in  Magan  Bihari  Lal  vs  State  of  Punjab
(AIR 1977 SC 1091), the SCI held that expert opinion must always be
received with great caution, particularly the opinion of a handwriting
expert. It is considered unsafe to base a conviction solely on such
evidence without substantial corroboration. In that case, the Court
found it especially hazardous to convict the accused solely on the
strength of the expert ’ s opinion.
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
- 9 -
19.  In  Lalit  Popli  v.  Canara  Bank  and  others
(AIR 2003 SC 1796), the Supreme Court explained that handwriting
identification is governed by sections 45, 47, and 73 of the Indian
Evidence Act (which correspond to Articles 59, 61, and 84 of the QSO,
respectively). Sections 45 and 47 deal with opinion evidence  –  either
scientific or based on familiarity  –  while section 73 empowers the court
to make a direct comparison. The Court clarified that these provisions
are complementary. Although expert evidence need not be corroborated
in every case, its weight depends on the surrounding circumstances.
Even in the absence of expert assistance, the court may compare the
writings and reach its own conclusion.
20.  To conclude, Articles 59 and 84 of the QSO are not
mutually  exclusive.  Where  the  writing  is  complex,  disguised,  or
technically disputed, recourse to expert opinion under Article 59 is
generally more appropriate. Nevertheless, if a party requests that a
document be referred to a handwriting expert, such a request must be
duly considered. If it is declined, reasons must be recorded, particularly
where the authenticity of the document is material to the outcome of
the case. This obligation flows from section 24A of the General
Clauses Act, 1897, and the constitutional guarantee of a fair trial under
Article 10A of the Constitution.
Application of  l aw to the  p resent  c ase
21.  The Appellant alleges that the recovery proceedings were
fabricated and that key documents  –  Recovery Memo (Exh. PA),
complaint (Exh. PB), and site plan (Exh. PC)  –  were not written by
Tayyab/T-SI  (PW-3).  During  cross-examination,  PW  Tayyab  gave
contradictory statements regarding his ability to write. He initially
claimed that he could not reproduce the handwriting on the documents
due to a swollen index finger. However, he subsequently stated that the
injury had occurred about a year earlier and that he had never been
declared unfit or disabled. These contradictions cast doubt on his
credibility and give rise to a serious factual dispute regarding the
authorship of the documents. In these circumstances, referring them to
a handwriting expert under Article 59 of the QSO is necessary. The
court cannot undertake a comparison under Article 84 because no
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
- 10 -
admitted or proved writing of PW Tayyab/T-SI is available on the
record  for  that  purpose.  Expert  opinion  would  provide  relevant
assistance in determining whether PW Tayyab was the author of the
documents, which is central to the defence case.
22.  The trial court declined the request partly on the ground
that  handwriting  expert  opinions  are  generally  weak  and  not
conclusive. While this observation reflects settled jurisprudence,
it
does not justify the rejection of such evidence altogether. Article 59 of
the QSO expressly recognises expert opinion as a relevant fact. Its
admissibility is not precluded merely because it is rebuttable. The
evidentiary weight to be attached to the expert ’ s findings is a matter to
be assessed at trial. Where authorship of the documents is in dispute
and direct evidence is contested, the expert ’ s opinion may still offer
meaningful assistance to the court in arriving at a just conclusion.
23.  The trial court also relied on section 29 of the CNSA,
which stipulates that in trials under the Act, it may be presumed that the
accused has committed an offence unless the contrary is proved.
However, this reverse burden does not absolve the prosecution of its
initial  duty.  The  Supreme  Court  has  consistently  held  that  such
presumptions  are  not  automatic  but  are  triggered  only  after  the
prosecution establishes a  prima facie  case through credible evidence.
Where the defence contests the recovery process and disputes the
authorship of key documents, the presumption cannot be invoked to
circumvent that burden. The accused must be afforded a reasonable
opportunity to rebut the prosecution ’ s case through relevant evidence,
including expert opinion.
24.  The trial court’s  reasoning that obtaining expert opinion
would delay the trial is also not tenable. Procedural convenience cannot
override the need for fairness. The defence has specifically challenged
the  authenticity  of  critical  prosecution  documents.  That  challenge
would remain untested without expert analysis, and resultantly, the
adjudication  would  be  incomplete.  Procedural  convenience  cannot
See paragraph 16, above.
Pir  Mazharul  Haq  and  others  v.  The  State  through  Chief  Ehtesab  Commissioner
(PLD 2005 SC 63).
Criminal Appeal No. 221/2025
- 11 -
justify curtailing the accused ’ s right to lead evidence central to their
defence.  In  Mrs.  Kalyani  Baskar  vs  Mrs.  M.  S.  Sampoornam,
(2007) 2 SCC 258, the SCI held that where the accused seeks to rebut
the  prosecution ’ s  case  using  a  document  relied  upon  by  the
complainant (such as a cheque in a criminal proceeding), the court must
not  deny  the  opportunity  to  have  that  document  examined  by  a
handwriting expert. Refusing such a request deprives the accused of a
meaningful chance to contest the prosecution ’ s case. A conviction
rendered  without  affording  the  accused  an  opportunity to  present
material evidence amounts to a denial of a fair trial. A  “ fair trial ”
includes a fair and reasonable opportunity, guaranteed by law, to
establish one ’ s innocence. The right to adduce evidence in support of
the defence is fundamental, and its denial tantamounts to the denial of a
fair trial.
Similarly, in  Muhammad Tariq and others v. Asif Javed
and another  (2009 SCMR 264), the Supreme Court of Pakistan held
that a party is entitled to every possible assistance from the court to
prove its case.
Conclusion
25.  The authenticity of the disputed documents is a material
issue in this case, and the defence is entitled to properly contest it. The
trial court ’ s refusal to refer the documents for expert opinion, despite a
genuine factual dispute, curtailed the Appellant ’ s right to a fair trial
under Article 10A of the Constitution. Accordingly, this appeal is
allowed  and the Impugned Order dated 22.04.2025 is set aside. The
Appellant’s application under Article 59 of the QSO is accepted.
( Raja Ghazanfar Ali Khan)

harboured a grudge. On 29.09.2023, her father  went  to attend a gathering of

1 0 .  Arguments heard. Record perused.
1 1 .  According to the prosecution, on  29.09.2023 at 9:00 p.m.,  the
Appellant  ravished  the Complainant  in her house .  The prosecution examined
Crl. Appeal No. 572 / 20 2 4
-  5  -
Complainant  K.B.  (PW - 1)  and Muhammad Taj  (PW - 2 ) to pro ve the charge
against the Appellant.  At the very outset, we have noted that the Complainant
reported the incident to the police  through an application Exh. P A  on
08 . 10 .20 23 ,  i.e. ,  nine  days  later ,  whereupon  FIR  No.  741 /20 23
(Ex h .  CW - 4/A ) was registered.  However, it is settled law that  a  delay in
reporting  cases  of  sexual  violence  does  not  automatically  vitiate  the
prosecution ’ s  case.  Reference in this regard may be made to  Mehboob
Ahmed v. The State  (1999 SCMR 1102),  Irfan Ali Sher v. Th e State
(PLD 2020 SC 295),  Zahid and another v. The State  (2020 SCMR 590),
Mubeen Ahmed v. The State and another  (PLD 2021 Islamabad 431),
Zahid v. The State  (2022 SCMR 50) and  Imdad Ullah v. The State and
another  (PLD 2025 Lahore 512) .  In the  present case, t he Complainant
(PW ‑ 1), in her cross ‑ examination, stated that after the alleged occurrence ,
she and her family approached the police, but  “ they did not turn an ear to our
submission ” .  However, she did not specify when this alleged approach wa s
made, nor did she explain why she failed to submit an application to senior
police officers or the Ex - Officio Justice of Peace, or why no medico - legal
examination was sought during the intervening period. Therefore, in the
circumstances of this case, the  delay remains unexplained.
1 2 .  The Complainant  is the key witness for the prosecution. It is
well established that in cases of sexual offen c es, the victim ’ s testimony is
crucial, and unless there are compelling reasons to seek corroboration,  the
courts s hould have no difficulty  in  solely  rely ing  on the victim ’ s testimony
to convict the accused. However, this is contingent on the testimony being
independent, unbiased, and straightforward enough to substantiate the
accusation. If the court finds it difficul t to accept the victim ’ s account, it may
seek corroboration from other evidence that supports her version. Reliance
is placed on  Muhammad Aslam v. The State and others  (2023 SCMR 397)
and  Atta - ul - Mustafa v. The State and another  (2023 SCMR 1698).  I t is in
this legal context that the Complainant ’ s testimony must now be assessed.
1 3 .  The Complainant ’ s testimony  in court largely followed the one
set out in the private complaint (Exh. PA /CS ), but  she  introduced significant
embellishments and was at variance with her earlier version given to the
police.  During cross - examination, she admitted that she had not included key
facts in the private complaint. She  further  stated that she had earlier warned
Crl. Appeal No. 572 / 20 2 4
-  6  -
the  Appellant to desist from harassing her and that he committed the offence
in revenge. However, she acknowledged that this was not recorded in
Exh.  PA .  Similarly,  she  stated  in  her  examination - in - chief  that  the
eyewitnesses attempted to apprehend the accus ed ,  but they fled ,  brandishing
their weapons. This ,  too ,  was  not mentioned in the private complaint.  When
confronted with these discrepancies, she was unable to offer any explanation
and merely stated that she had narrated the full facts to her counsel  whe n he
drafted the private complaint .
1 4 .  The Complainant also conceded that there was a pending
dispute between her family and the Appellant regarding the drainage of water
from her house into a watercourse adjoining the Appellant ’ s land. She denied
that water flowed directly into his property but admitted that her family used
to sprinkle the drainage water in the street. She further acknowledged that
her family had forbidden the Appellant from using the street in front of their
hou se after a quarrel. When asked about an application allegedly moved by
the Appellant to the police on 03.10.2023, she admitted knowledge of it and
confirmed that  tensions had intensified  after  it .  These facts are extremely
relevant. Where animosity is admi tted, the possibility of false implication
cannot  be  ruled  out  lightly,  and  the  court  must  seek  independent
corroboration.
1 5 .  Muhammad  Taj  (PW ‑ 2)  claimed  that  on  the  night  of
29.09.2023, he was irrigating his fields when he heard cries and rushed to
the  house of the Complainant ’ s father. According to him, he saw the
Appellant committing rape  with  the Complainant while co - accused Shahzad
stood guard. He stated that he was accompanied by Ghulam Ali and that they
both attempted to apprehend the accused. The  Appellant allegedly raised a
lalkara  and  asked  his co - accused to fire at them,  upon which they  stepped
back . He further asserted that the accused  had fled, leaving behind a slipper,
and that the slipper was subsequently  secured  by the police .
1 6 .  Muhammad  Taj (PW ‑ 2) was confronted with  his statement
under  section  161  Cr.P.C.  (Exh.  DA)  during  cross - examination.  He
conceded that several core elements of his deposition were not recorded in
it . These included the claim that he witnessed the rape, the Appellant ’ s
alleged threat to shoot, the fact that the victim was left naked, and the
recovery of the slipper. His only explanation was that the police did not
Crl. Appeal No. 572 / 20 2 4
-  7  -
record his version accurately. While such a claim may occasionally be true,
it cannot override the settle d evidentiary rule that a witness who materially
improves upon his initial statement must be viewed with caution.
17.  The  Complainant  herself  admitted  during  her  cross -
examination that  “ prior to the arrival of Taj PW my uncle Ghulam
Muhammad (read Ghulam  Ali) had approached the place of occurrence. Taj
reached the place of occurrence after about 15 minutes of arrival of Ghulam
Muhammad (read Ghulam Ali). ”  This admission clearly shows that PW Taj
was not the eyewitness. The prosecution should have produced  Ghulam  Ali,
but he was given up. Under Illustration (g) of Article 129 of the Qanun - e -
Shahadat, 1984, the presumption would be that if he had been produced, he
would not have supported the prosecution. Reliance is placed on  Lal Khan
v. The State  (2006 SCMR  1846).
1 8 .  There  is another point: if PWs  Muhammad Taj , Ghulam Ali and
15 to 20 people , as claimed,  ha d gathered  at the crime scene,  why  did they
not  make any attempt to apprehend the Appellant and his co - accused. In
incidents of this nature, it is  commonly observed that the victim ’ s relatives
or bystanders, without regard  for their own safety, typically try to capture
the culprit to prevent their escape and to hold them accountable for their
misconduct.  The prosecution has not  given  any satisfactory  explanation in
this regard.
19.  During cross - examination, Muhammad Taj (PW ‑ 2) conceded
that he  was  a close relative of the Complainant. He described her as his niece
and confirmed that his own residence is approximately one acre away from
the scene of th e occurrence. He stated that his house is situated inside his
agricultural land and that he reached the Complainant ’ s house only after
hearing the cries. When asked about the timing of the incident and his
movements, he could not recall the time. He also a cknowledged that the
Complainant ’ s sister Nusrat resided in the same house, but was not cited as
a witness.
2 0 .  For the reasons discussed above, the testimony of Muhammad
Taj (PW - 2)  is unreliable and cannot be used to corroborate the Complainant.
2 1 .  It  is significant that both the Complainant and Muhammad Taj
(PW ‑ 2) stated that the Appellant left behind his  slippers  while fleeing the
Crl. Appeal No. 572 / 20 2 4
-  8  -
scene. However, those were neither  secured  during the investigation nor
produced in evidence at trial. Furthermore, the w eapon allegedly used to
threaten the Complainant was never recovered.
2 2 .  The Complainant was medically examined on 05.10.2023 by
Dr. Hadia Sarfraz (CW 6), who initially noted that the hymen was ruptured
and mild hyperemia was present at the introitus. H owever, she found no
signs of injury, resistance ,  or trauma on any part of the body. In her final
opinion, given after receipt of the PFSA report, she concluded that  “ nothing
can approve the act of sexual offence. ”  She confirmed that the absence of
seminal  material, coupled with the lack of clinical findings, did not support
the allegation. In her cross - examination, she clarified that hyperemia is not
necessarily a sign of rape and may result from infection. She also admitted
that there were no injuries on  the hips, thighs, knees, or genitals of the victim.
According to the  PFSA report (Exh. PB) ,  no seminal material was detected
on the vaginal swabs or clothing of the victim. The Agency did not conduct
DNA profiling due to this absence. Taken together, the m edical and forensic
material does not corroborate the allegation of rape.
2 3 .  It is a cardinal principle of criminal jurisprudence that the
accused is entitled to the benefit of doubt arising from any deficiency in the
prosecution case. In this instance, the evidence as a whole does not meet the
standard required  to sustain a convic tion under s ection 376 PPC. The finding
of guilt recorded by the Additional Sessions Judge appears to  be based on an
inadequate appraisal of the  evidence and cannot be maintained.  Accordingly,
we  accept  Crl. Appeal No.572/2024 and set aside the Appellant ’ s  conviction
and sentence. He shall be released from jail if not required to be detained in
another case.
( Muhammad Amjad Rafiq)  (Tariq Saleem Sheikh)
Judge  Judge
Naeem

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_010]

IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT
BAHAWALPUR BENCH BAHAWALPUR

Shahbaz Anwar  Vs  The Province of Punjab
etc.

order/
proceeding

order/
proceeding
Order with signature of Judge, and that of parties or
counsel, where necessary
24.08.2026  Mr. Muhammad Yaseen Ataal, Advocate for the
petitioner.
The petitioner has filed the instant  petition under
Article  199  of  the  Constitution  of  the  Islamic
Republic  of  Pakistan ,  1973  (‘ the  Constitution ’)
seeking grant of the  following  prayers :
i.  T he  impugned  Office  Order  No.SO
( E - III)WL / 3 - 3/2026 dated 16.08.2026, may
kindly be set aside as illegal, without lawful
authority,  coram  non  judice ,  mala  fide,
arbitrary and violative of the PEEDA Act,
2006 and the principl e s of natural justice.
ii.  I t is also humbly prayed that the operation
of  the  impugned  Office  Order  No.SO
(E - III)WL / 3 - 3/2026 dated 16.08.2026, may
kindly be suspended and the respondent
No.1 may kindly be restrained  for  tak ing
any further coercive/adverse action against
the petitioner on the basis of the impugned
proceedings.
iii.  A ny other relief deemed just and proper in
the circumstances of the case may kindly be
granted.
2.  The  necessary facts, as set out in the petition and
relevant  for  the  adjudication  of  the  present
controversy, are  that the petitioner  is  an  employee of
Pakistan  Museum  of  Natural  History,  Pakistan
Science  Foundation,  Ministry  of  Science  &
Technology,  Islamabad .  Since  10.04.2023,  the
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
petitioner  has been  with  the respondent department
and  presently  he  is posted as  the  Curator, Bahawalpur
Zoo, Bahawalpur, vide office order dated 19.08.2025 .
Petitioner  was  issued  a  letter  dated  15.08.2026
seeking his explanation as to why he failed to  report
the  death  of  330  chicks  of  different  species  to
respondent  No.4/Deputy  Chief  Wildlife  Ranger ,
Bahawalpur  Region,  Bahawalpur ,  which  he  had
already  replied .  Subsequently,  through  o rder dated
16.08.2026,  respondent No.1 has placed the petitioner
under  suspension  for  a  period  of  90  days  with
immediate effect (the ‘ Impugned Order ’)  under
Section  6  of  the  Punjab  Employees  Efficiency,
Discipline and Accountability Act, 2006 ( ‘ PEEDA  or
the  Act’ )  that has been challenged in this Writ
Petition.
3.  The  sole  argument  advanced by the  petitioner  is
that the Impugned  Order  has been  passed by the
B orrowing  Organization  without  taking
‘ prior  approval ’  f rom  the  L ending  O rganization ,
which  i s  mandatory  requirement  under  Section
15(1)(a)  &  (b)  of  the  Act  before  placing  a
deputationist,  like  Petitioner,  under  suspension .
Consequently , the  I mpugned  O rder  placing him under
suspen sion  is illegal,  unlawful,  without  jurisdiction
and  void ab initio , hence, liable to be set aside.  While
addressing the maintainability of this Writ Petition,
learned counsel  contends that  he is  on deputation  with
the  respondent department , so the bar contained in
Article 212(2)  of the  Constitution  does not apply  to
the  p etitioner  in view of  Section  2(1)(b)( i )  of the
Punjab Civil Servant s  Act, 1974 whereby a person on
deputation to the Province from the Federation or any
other Province or  A uthority does not fall in the
definition of civil servant .  A ccording to him,  this
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
Writ Petition is maintainable.  In these facts and
circumstances, the Petitioner prays for the acceptance
of this Writ Petition by setting aside the Impugned
Order of his suspension.
4.  At the very outset, learned counsel was confronted
qua  the maintainability of this Writ Petition. Besides ,
he  was  inquired  if  he  ha d  any  precedent  to
substantiate his submissions made at the bar. Learned
counsel sought time to furnish case law in support of
his petition and to refute the propositions of law
expounded by this Court ,  which  have  duly  been
formulated  and  reproduced  herein  below.
Subsequently ,  the case was taken up  at the end of the
cause list after conclusion of all  court  work, whe n  the
learned counsel for the petitioner frankly conceded
that he could not find any precedent favouring his
p etition.  However, he  quibbled  that he could also not
find anything supporting  the propositio n s of law  as
canvassed  by this Court.  He,  therefore,  insisted that
Writ Petition is maintainable and the  Impugned Order
be set aside being illegal and without jurisdiction.
5.  Be that as it may.  A rguments have been heard at
great  length and  the  record  of this  case  has been
perused with the able assistance of learned counsel for
the petitioner.
6.  For  complete  determination of the controversy
agitated in this petition ,  it is imperative  to  reproduce
the  following  relevant  provisions of PEED A :
Section  5. Initiation of proceedings. —  (1) If on
the basis of its own knowledge or information
placed before it, the  c ompetent  authority is of the
opinion  that  there  are  sufficient  grounds  for
initiating proceedings against an employee under
this Act, it shall either —
(a)  p roceed itself against the accused by issuing a
show cause notice under  Section  7 and , for
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
reasons to be recorded in writing,  dispense with
the  enquiry :
Provided that no opportunity of showing cause
or personal hearing shall be given where —
(i)  the  competent  authority is satisfied
that  in  the interest  of security of
Pakistan or any part thereof, it is not
expedient  to  give  such  an
opportunity;  or
(ii)  an employee has entered into plea
bargain under any law for the time
being in force or has been convicted
of the charges of corruption which
have led to a sentence of fine or
imprisonment;  or
(iii)  an  employee  is  involved  in
subversive activities;  or
(iv)  it is not reasonably practicable to give
such an opportunity to the accused;  or
(b)  get an  enquiry  conducted into the charge or
charges against the accused, by appointing an
enquiry  officer or an  enquiry  committee, as the
case may be, under  Section  10 :
Provided  that  the  competent  authority  shall
dispense with the  enquiry  where --
(i)  an employee has been convicted of
any offence other than corruption by
a court of law under any law for the
time being in force; or
(ii)  an employee is or has been absent
from duty without prior approval of
leave:
(Emphasis Added)
Section  6 .  Suspension. ---
An employee against whom action is  proposed  to
be initiated under  Section  5 may be placed under
suspension for a period of ninety days, if in the
opinion of the  competent  authority, suspension is
necessary  or  expedient,  and  if  the  period  of
suspension is not extended for a further period of
ninety days within thirty days of the expiry of
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
initial period of suspension, the employee shall be
deemed to be reinstated:
Provided that the continuation of the period of
suspension shall require the prior approval of the
competent  authority for each period of extension .
(Emphasis Added)
Section  15.  Procedure of  i nquiry against officers lent
to other government s ,  etc. —
(1)  Where the services of an employee are transferred
or lent to any other government , department,
corporation, corporate body , autonomous body,
authority,  statutory  body  or  any  other
organization or institution,  hereinafter referred to
as the borrowing organization, the  competent
authority  for  the  post  against  which  such
employee is posted in the borrowing organization
may --
(a)  suspend him under  Section  6; and
(b)  initiate proceedings against him under this Act :
Provided that the borrowing organization shall
forthwith inform the lending organization of the
circumstances  leading  to  the  order  of  his
suspension  and  the  commencement  of  th e
proceedings :
Provide d  further that the borrowing organization
shall  obtain  prior  approval  of  the  lending
organization before taking any action under this
Act, against an employee holding a post in Basic
