Pay Scale 17 or above.”
(Emphasis Added)
7.  Perusal of the above  basic provisions of law
reveals  the  scheme  of  PEEDA,  where under  the
Competent  Authority  has  two options available to it ,
if  and when  it is of the opinion that there are sufficient
grounds  for  initiating  proceedings  against  an
employee  under  PEEDA .  First ,  the  Competent
Authority  can  proceed itself against the employee
under  Section  7 of the Act by dispensing with the
regular  enquiry  OR  Second  the  Competent  Authority
can  get  an  enquiry  conducted  by  appointing  an
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
Enquiry  Officer or  Enquiry  Committee (as the case
may be) under  Section  10  ibid . It would not be out of
the place to  briefly  mention that there are procedural
safeguards in built by the legislature and  also those
introduced by the Courts, from time to time, for
structuring this discretionary power of the  Competent
Authority and  guiding as to when  a  regular  enquiry
can be dispensed with  or  under  what  circumstances a
regular  enquiry  is  mandatory  as  well  as  for
safeguarding  and protecting  the rights of  the  accused
employee, but since they are not the subject matter of
the instant writ petition, therefore, they are not being
referred to herein below.
8.  The first set of questions that need to be answered
are whether the Competent Authority under  the  Act
has the power or authority to place an employee under
suspension. At what point in time, this power can be
exercised by the Competent Authority ?  Are there any
pre - requisites or condition precedent for exercise of
power of suspension? Answers to all these questions
are found in Section 6 of the Act. This power of the
Competent Authority to issue a suspension order
against an employee is derived fr om Section 6  ibid
and is exercised  if the suspension is necessary or
expedient in the opinion of the Competent Authority
a t  the  time  when  the  Competent  Authority  is
proposing  or contemplating  to initiate any action
against an employee  under PEED A .  Except this  rider ,
there is no other  pre - requisite  for exercise of  such
power  by the Competent Authority  f or  issuance of a
suspension  order .  It is c lear  as day that  t he act of
placing an employee under suspension precedes the
initiation of proceedings under  Section  5 of  the  Act
and  the passing of any adverse order in consequence
thereof.  By the bare reading of this  Section , one is
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
constrained to note that it is a stand - alone provision
containing no reference, neither express nor implied,
to any other  s ection  of  the  Act.  In  Section  6, there is
no concept of seeking permission, prior or later, for
placing the name of an employee under suspension for
90 days.  Section  6, unlike  Section  15, does not have
any controlling proviso, which makes it obligatory
upon the  Competent A uthority to seek prior approval
of the  L ending  O rganization.  Section  6 i s  self -
contained  s ection  of law without having any pre -
requisites for exercise of such power, except the
opinion of the  Competent  Authority that suspension
is necessary or expedient.  This exercise of power
under  Section  6  has not been made subject to or
subservient to  Section  15  ibid .
9.  When  interpreting  different ,  apparently
incongruent,  provisions of a statu t e, the first and the
foremost duty of the Court is to read ,  explain  and
apply  th ose  provisions  harmoniously  in  such  a
manner that no  part  of law  made by the legislature  is
made  redundant , rendered surplus or made  otiose
.
By following this cardinal principle of law ,  when
these two provisions of  the  Act ,  Section s  6 & 15 ,  are
p ut  in  juxtaposition  and  read  harmoniously ,  it
cumulatively  follows  that  under  Section  6  the
Competent  Authority, before initiation of any action
as stated in  Section  5, can place an employee under
suspension for  90 days , if in the opinion of  Competent
Authority  suspension  is  necessary  or  expedient.
However, in case the employee in question has been
lent, transferred or sent on deputation  from one
government  to  other  government,  department,
corporation  or authority  etc., as explained in S ection
Aftab Shahban Mirani and others vs. Muhammad Ibrahim and others  ( PLD 2008 SC 779 )
Lucky Cement Ltd. vs. Commissioner Income Tax, Zone Companies, Circle - 5, Peshawar  ( 2015 SCMR 1494 ) ,
Haji tooti and another vs. Federal Board of Revenue, Islamabad and others  ( 2023 SCMR 1980 )
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
15, then the  Competent  Authority  in  the Borrowing
Organization can  (i)  suspend him under  Section  6,
meaning thereby that the procedure as set out in
Section  6  ibid  would apply i.e.  if  in opinion of the
Competent  Authority  suspension  is  necessary  or
expedient ;  and  (ii)  initiate proceedings against him
under the Act i.e.  either of the two actions stated in
Section  5  ibid  would take place .  T he  only  extra  step,
added in  the  case of an employee on deputation,  is  that
the  Borrowing Organization shall forthwith inform
the  Lending  Organization  of  the  circumstances
leading  to  the  order  of  suspension  and
commencement of proceedings .  To reach to this
conclusion, t his Court  has noted that  the word s  ‘prior
approval ’ ha ve  only been used in the 2
nd
proviso to
Section  15 stating  to the effect  that prior approval of
the  Lending  Organization  is  required  by  the
Borrowing Organization before taking any action
under this Act.  2
nd
proviso does not supersede,
overrides or control the 1
st
proviso. Rather these two
provisos address two different subjects.  1
st
proviso
casts obligation on the Competent Authority about
rel a ying the information to the Lending Organization
qua  the circumstances leading to the issuance or
passing of the order of suspension and the fact the
proceedings under PEEDA have been initiated or
commenced  or  begun  against  a  deputationist
employee.  It  is  a  Post  f acto  obligation
communicating of what has already transpired.  On
the other hand, 2
nd
proviso imposes a duty on the
Competent Authority  to obtain prior approval of the
Lending Organization before taking any action under
this Act against an employee on deputation holding a
post in BPS - 17 or above. This is an  ex - ante  or before
the  event  obligation .  Legislature’s  conscious
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
omission of  word s  ‘prior  consent ’ in  the  1
st
proviso  to
Section  15  ibid ,  is  instructive  of  legislature’s  true
intent that no  prior approval,  advance permission or
previous  consent of the Lending Organization is
needed for placing an employee under suspension and
for initiation of enquiry against a deputation ist.  Had
the legislature wanted  so,  it would have said so in the
st
proviso.
10.  Besides  i t  is  also  trite  law  that  while
interpreting a statute, the heading or title of  s ection  or
provision  is  also  useful  internal  aid  or  tool  for
interpretation
.  The title  / heading  of  Section  1 5  reads
‘ Procedure of enquiry against officers lent to other
governments, etc. ’ , so  prima facie  Section  15  only
relates to  the  procedure of  e nquiry not the procedure
of suspension.  As opined earlier,  the  initiation of
enquiry  has a separate and distinct meaning  in  the  Act
whereas  the passing of a  suspension  order  is not an
enquiry, it  is just preface  to  an  Enquiry .  Nowhere in
Section  15 , the passing of an order of suspension been
clogged or initiation of enquiry has been blocked.  2
nd
proviso to  Section  15  relates to taking of action under
this Act ,  which ,  prima facie ,  means  order(s) that  is  to
be passed by the  Competent  Authority  on receipt of
the  report from the Enquiry Officer or Committee (as
the case may be)  under  S ection  13 or where enquiry
has been dispensed with, before passing an order
under  Section  7.
11.  In  view  of the foregoing  facts  and the
applicable law, this Court is of the firm view that for
the suspension order issued under  Section  6, there is ,
as such , no  requirement of seeking prior approval or
consent  of  the  Lending  Organization  by  the
Mst. Rehmat Begum vs. Mehfooz Ahmed and others  ( PLD 2024 SC 1108 )
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
Borrowing Organization.  All that the Borrowing
Organization is required to do is to forthwith inform
the  Lending  Organization  of  the  circumstances
leading  to  the  order  of  the  suspension  and  the
commencement of the proceedings.  In other words,
what  has  led  to  the  suspension  of  the  officer
concerned and  beginning of an enquiry that is to be
conveyed  to  the  Lending  Organi z ation  by  the
Borrowing Organi z ation.
12.  At this juncture, it would not be out of the
place to  refer to,  although in passing,  the meaning and
purport of phrase ‘ forthwith ’ as used in 1
st
proviso to
the Section 15.  According to Black’s Law Dictionary
(12th Edition), edited by Bryan A. Garner  on 05
th
April, 2024  and published by Thomson Reuters, the
term “forthwith” is defined as  follows :
1. Without unnecessary delay.  2. Within a
reasonable time under the circumstances; with
all  convenient  dispatch.  The  U.S.  Supreme
Court has stated that “(t)here are many cases
which turn upon the question whether a person
was  not  too  late  in  complying  wit h  a
requirement  that  a  thing  must  be  done
forthwith.” Dickerman v. Northern Trust Co.,
176 U.S 181, 193, 20 S. Ct. 311, 315 (1900).
That was an anomalous case in which it was
argued  that  the  person  required  to  do
something forthwith did it too quickly. The
Court held there was no good argument that the
actor had “acted with too great haste.” Id. The
court  cited  with  approval,  however,  a
lexicographic authority stating that the word
“has a relative meaning, and will imply a
longer or shorter period, according to the
nature  of  the  thing  to  be  done.”  Id.  Cf.
IMMEDIATELY; PROMPTLY.
( Emphasis Added )
On the other hand, t he word  ‘ forthwith ’  in its ordinary
parlance , as opposed to legal,  means immediately,
instantly, at once and promptly.  Reference is made to
Oxford Advanced Learner’s Dictionary of Current
English (10th Edition) by A.S. Hornby  published by
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
Oxford University Press in the year  2020 ,  where  term
“forthwith” means immediately; at once .  This term
‘forthwith’ has come under judicial scrutiny more
than once, mostly, in the context of Order XVII Rule
3 CPC, where this term has been interpreted as ‘within
reasonable time’
, without delay
and was not equated
with words ‘at once’
.  So the judicial consensus is
that  the  word  ‘forthwith’  does  not  mean
‘immediately’ but ‘with all reasonable celerity’
.  It
has a relative meaning depending on the nature of the
thing to be done.  If the legislature wanted the
C ompetent  A uthority to straightaway communicate
the circumstances leading to the order of suspension
and commencement of proceedings from Borrowing
Organization to the Lending Organization, it would
have used the words like  ‘ immediately ’  or  ‘ promptly ’
that imply urgency .  The use of word  ‘ forthwith ’
leaves the Competent Authority with reasonable time
to do the needful.  What is reasonable  time is a fact
specific question that  will  invariably fluctuate  on case
to case basis  as it has, rightly, been pointed out in the
above  quoted  definition  from  the  Black’s  Law
Dictionary.
13.  At  this  juncture,  after having addressed the
merits of arguments and  submissions  made by the
learned counsel for the Petitioner,  it would not be out
of the place to  recall  that the Petitioner has only
challenged the order whereby he has been suspended.
To comment upon with the nature and effect of an
order of suspension, let us begin by remembering that
an order of placing an employee under suspension has
never been termed,  treated and accepted as a penalty
Maulvi Abdul Aziz Khan vs. Mst. Shah Jahan Begum and 2 others  ( PLD 1971 SC 434 )
Lutfullah Virk vs. Muhammad Aslam Sheikh  ( PLD 2024 SC 887 )
Muhammad Aslam vs. Nazir Ahmed  ( 2008 SCMR 942 )
Inspector General, Frontier Corps, Baluchistan, Quetta vs. The Superintendent, Central Jail, Mach, & 3
others  ( 1990 MLD 2258 )
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
by the superior courts.  Suspension is a temporary
measure,  effect whereof is to merely stop an official
from  performing  his  duties  for  the  time  being.
Suspended employee continues to hold his office,
receive pay or subsistence allowance, as the case may
be, but is  restrained  from performing  the actual
functions  of  his  office  during  the  period  of  his
suspension. It is not permanent removal from service,
which order may only be passed upon completion of
the enquiry. Thus, an order of suspension pending a
departmental enquiry is not a penalty as it does not
violat e  any  legal  right  vesting  in  the  suspended
officer.  Seminal  judgment  by  a  Full  Bench  of
Hon’ble  Supreme  Court  on  this  score  is  Mian
Muhammad Hayat v. The Province of West Pakistan
reported  as  ( PLD  1964  SC  321 )  that  was  later
approvingly followed in the case of  Abdul Khaliq
Bangash  v.  The  Secretary,  Government  of  West
Pakistan, Co - Operative Department, Lahore & 2
others  ( 1970  SCMR  103 ),  wherein  the  Hon’ble
Supreme Court, after referring to the above exposition
of law, was pleased to  make the following important
observation :
“The  petitioner  continues  to  remain  in
service in spite of his suspension and if he is
ultimately exonerated in the enquiry, he can
be re - instated and paid all the arrears of his
salary that may be found due to him”.
Subsequently,  the  above  principle  of  law  was
followed  in  the  case  titled  The  Divisional
Superintendent,  Pakistan  Railways,  Lahore  v.
Muhammad Halim through Legal Representatives
and others  ( PLD 2000 SC 55 ).
14.  As  a  necessary corollary of the  discussion in
the  foregoing  paragraph s ,  since  this writ petition  is
aimed against an interim order  of suspension that is
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
not a penalty under the service laws, so  this  writ
petition against an order of interlocutory nature is
premature,  non - maintainable  and  liable  to  be
dismissed on this score alone.  In this respect,
r eliance is  placed on  case s  titled  Muhammad Sadiq
Khokhar v. Engineer - in - Chief, Pakistan Army, GHQ
and another  reported as  ( 1981 PLC (C.S.) 123 ) ,
Professor Dr. Zafar Iqbal Chaudhry v. Province of
Punjab and others  reported as  ( 2017 PLC (C.S.)
583 ) ,  Ahmed Raza Sultan etc. v. Senior Member
Board of Revenue  reported as ( PLJ 2014 Lahore
265 )  and  Muhammad Amin Mughal v. Secretary
Local  Government  and  Rural  Development
Department/Chairman  Punjab  Local  G overnment
Board, Government of the Punjab, Civil Secretariat,
Lahore and 4 others  reported as  ( 2002 PLC (C.S . )
816 ) , where this Court was pleased to observe as
follows :
“The suspension order or the holding of
inquiry is step towards passing of the final
order and no writ petition is maintainable
against interim order. As if the inquiry the
petitioner is found guilty of the charge and
thereupon some action is taken against  him,
the petitioner has the remedy to file an
appeal before the Service Tribunal. The
High Court cannot interfere in such like
interim orders in view of dictum laid down
by the Karachi High Court in Abdul Bari's
case (PLD 1981 Karachi 290, 303 and 309),
M uhammad  Mahmood  Ali's  case  (1984
CLC 142 at page 145) and Nazir Ahmad
Sheikh's case (1998 PLC (C.S.) page 607).
In the light of the above discussion the writ
petition is not maintainable and the same is
dismissed.
15.  Since  the petitioner has  only  challenged  the
order of his  suspen sion  under  Section  6 of  the Act ,
whereas the  Competent  Authority is yet to initiate
proceedings against him either through a regular
W.P. No. 4833 - 2026
enquiry  under  Section  10 or by dispensing with it
under  Section  7 , let alone conclude  the same ,  and the
petitioner  has other remed ies  available to him under
the  hierarchy of  the  Act  as and when the enquiry
proceeds, therefore, i n the  view  of  the  aforementioned
legal  position ,  this petition  is held to be  premature,
devoid  of  any  merit  and  not  deserv ing  any
interference by this Court at this stage.  Hence , this
Writ Petition  is  dismissed  in limine .
(Muhammad Usman Ghani Rashid Cheema)
Judge
Approved for reporting
Judge
*M.Rizwan*

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_011]
Case: Writ Petition 33829/26 (LESCO Through Secretary Vs Federation of Pakistan etc.).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Writ Petition 33829/26. Title: Writ Petition 33829/26 (LESCO Through Secretary Vs Federation of Pakistan etc.). Official Ref: 2026LHC5409. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Raheel Kamran. Judgment Date (as displayed): 01-09-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5409.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 13 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Stereo HCJDA 38
JUDGMENT SHEET
LAHORE HIGH COURT LAHORE
( JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT)
Writ Petition No.33829 of 2026
Lahore Electric Supply Company  (LESCO) through its
Company Secretary
V ersus
Federation of Pakistan  through Secretary Ministry of Information
and Broadcast, and others
Petitioner by:-  Barrister Muhammad Umer Riaz, Rana
Rehan,  Barrister  Ch.  Abubakar,  Ch.
Haseeb  Tariq  and  Dahhar  Hussain,
Advocates with Ch. Imtiaz Ilahi, C.O.
LESCO.
Respondents by :  Mr. Nauman Khalid, Assistant Attorney
General for the Federation of Pakistan
with  Laeeq  Ahmad  Bajwa,  Deputy
Director  Ministry  of  Information  &
Broadcasting, Islamabad.
Barrister  Mian  Adnan  Tariq  and
Muhammad  Sheraz  Sultan,  Advocates
for respondent No.3  (Ateeq Ahmad Khan) .
Date of hearing: -  24.06.2026
JUDGMENT
RAHEEL KAMRAN, J.:  Th rough this petition filed under
Article 199 of the Constitution of the Islamic Republic of Pakistan, 1973
( “the  Constitution” ),  the petitioner has called  in question the order dated
04.05.2026  passed  by  the  Chief  Information  Commissioner,  Camp
Office, Lahore, whereby Appeal No.5240 - 12/25 filed by respondent No.3
Ateeq Ahmad Khan, was allowed and the petitioner - Lahor e Electric
-2-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
Supply Company ( “LESCO” ), was directed to furnish to the applicant as
well as the Information Commission the information sought by him
within ten days.
2 .  The controversy emanates from an information request made
by respondent No.3 Ateeq Ahmad Khan seeking certified copies of the
minutes of the 144
th
HR Meeting held on 27.02.2023 and the 261
st
Board
of Directors Meeting held on 04.05.2025 concerning the promotion of
Muhammad Ramzan Butt, who, at the relevant time, was serving as an
officer of LESCO. The requisite information was not furnished by
LESCO, whereafter respondent No.3 preferred an appeal before the
Pakistan Information Commission under section 17 of the Right of
Access to Information Act, 2017 ( “the Act of 2017” ). The petitioner-
LESCO  contested  the  appeal  by  filing  a  written  reply,  principally
maintaining that the requested record was excluded from public record
under section 7(a), (b), (c) and (g) of the Act of 2017 and was otherwise
exempt from disclosure under section 16 thereof. The Commission,
however, allowed the appeal through the impugned order, holding that
the final decision regarding the promotion had already been taken and,
consequently,  the  minutes  of  the  meetings  no  longer  enjoyed  the
exclusion contemplated by section 7(b) of the Act of 2017. Hence, these
petitions.
3 .  Learned counsel for the petitioner contended that the impugned
order is contrary to the statutory scheme governing the right of access to
information. It was submitted that the minutes sought by respondent No.3
relate to the promotion and service matters of identifiable employees and
contain,  inter  alia ,  information  from  their  service  records,  ACRs,
remarks  and  observations,  disclosure  of  which  would  invade  their
personal privacy and is therefore excluded under section 7(g) of the Act
of 2017. Learned counsel further contended that the fact that a final
decision had been taken by LESCO did not render the deliberations
leading to such decision open to disclosure, as sections 7(a), (b) and (c)
expressly protect noting on files, minutes of meetings and intermediary
opinions or recommendations subject to a final decision by the public
-3-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
body. According to learned counsel, section 6(d) makes final orders and
decisions public record, but does not convert the minutes or deliberations
preceding such decision into public record. Reliance was also placed
upon section 16(1)(c), relating to invasion of personal privacy, and
section 16(1)(i)(iii), which exempts information the disclosure of which
is likely to undermine the deliberative process in a public body by
inhibiting the free and frank provision of advice or exchange of views.
Learned counsel also referred to the judgment of the Islamabad High
Court  reported  as  “ Pakistan  Telecommunication  Authority  v.  Pakistan
Information Commission ”  (2024 YLR 929 Islamabad) , contending that
the protection available to deliberative material does not cease merely
because a final decision has subsequently been made. It was further
argued that the Information Commission had acted beyond its lawful
mandate by keeping the matter for implementation after passing the
impugned order and directing LESCO to furnish the information within a
specified period. To substantiate his contentions, learned counsel has also
placed reliance on cases reported as  “Muhammad Nawaz v. Additional
District and Sessions Judge and others”  (PLD 2023 SC 461) ,  “Akhtar Ali v.
Muhammad Shoaib  Ahmad”  (2011 CLD 1 Lahore)  and  “Shahbaz Akmal
Jandran  v.  Province  of  Punjab  through  Director  General,  Excise  and
Taxation, Lahore and another”  (PLD 2025 Lahore 649) . Learned counsel
also took the Court through case law from Indian jurisdiction such as
“ Girish Ramchandra Deshpande v. Central Information Commissioner and
others ”  (2013) 1 Supreme Court Cases 212  and  “ R.K. Jain v. Union of
India and another ”  (2013) 14 Supreme Court Cases 794 .
4 .  Learned Assistant Attorney General for the Federation, as well
as learned counsel appearing for respondents No.2 and 3, supported the
impugned order. Learned counsel for respondent No.3 submitted that
section 7(b) does not confer an absolute or perpetual exclusion upon
minutes of meetings; rather, the exclusion is expressly made subject to a
final decision by the public body. Since the decision concerning the
promotion in question had already been finalized and acted upon, the
statutory  condition  attached  to  the  exclusion  no  longer  remained
-4-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
operative. It was further contended that the information sought relates to
the functioning and decision-making of a public body concerning an
official matter and cannot be withheld merely by invoking personal
privacy. Learned counsel maintained that the petitioner ’ s reliance upon
the exemptions contained in section 16 of the Act of 2017 was equally
misconceived and that the impugned order correctly applied the statutory
scheme.  The  objection  regarding  the  Commission ’ s  direction  for
furnishing  the  information  within  a  specified  period  was  also
controverted on the ground that the Commission is expressly empowered
under the Act of 2017 to order disclosure and ensure implementation of
its decisions.
5 .  I have heard learned counsel for the parties and examined
the record as well as the relevant provisions of the  Right of Access to
Information Act, 2017 (“the Act of 2017”).
6 .  Before  examining  the  specific  objections  raised  by  the
petitioner, it would be appropriate to appreciate the constitutional and
statutory scheme governing the right of access to information.  Article
19A of the Constitution provides the foundational guarantee in the
following terms: —
“19A . Every  citizen shall have the right to have access to
information in all matters of public importance subject to
regulation and reasonable restrictions imposed by law. ”
The  language  of  Article  19A  makes  access  to  information  a
constitutionally guaranteed right of every citizen, albeit subject to such
regulation and reasonable restrictions as may be imposed by law. The
constitutional  guarantee,  therefore,  does  not  render  every  piece  of
information held by a public body unconditionally accessible; at the same
time, any restriction upon the exercise of the right must have the sanction
of law and remain within the parameters prescribed by the law. The right
is intended to facilitate access to information concerning matters of
public importance and thereby promote transparency and accountability
in the functioning of public bodies.
-5-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
7 .  The Right of Access to Information Act, 2017 was enacted
to give effect to the aforesaid constitutional guarantee. The legislative
purpose underlying the enactment is expressly set out in  its Preamble,
which, insofar as relevant, provides as follows: —
“WHEREAS Government believes in transparency and
the right to have access to information to ensure that the
people of the Islamic Republic of Pakistan have improved
access to records held by public authorities and promote
the  purposes  of  making  the  Government  more
accountable to its people, of improving participation by
the people in public affairs, of reducing corruption and
inefficiency  in  Government,  of  promoting  sound
economic growth, or promoting good governance and
respect for human rights;
AND WHEREAS it is expedient to provide for a
law which gives effect to the fundamental right of access
to information, as guaranteed under Article 19A of the
Constitution of the Islamic Republic of Pakistan and
international law, whereby everyone shall have the right
to have access to all information held by public bodies
subject only to reasonable restrictions imposed by law
and  for  matters  connected  therewith  or  incidental
thereto;”
The Preamble thus makes it vivid that the legislation is not merely
procedural in character; rather, it constitutes the statutory mechanism for
giving  effect  to  a  fundamental  right  and  is  designed  to  advance
transparency, governmental  accountability, public participation, good
governance and respect for human rights. The expr ession “subject only to
reasonable restrictions imposed by law” is also significant, as it reflects
the constitutional balance between the citizen ’ s right of access and the
legitimate restrictions which the Legislature may prescribe.
8 .  The general rule governing access to information is then
expressly enacted in section 3 of the Act of 2017, which provides: —
“ 3.  Right  to  have  access  to  information  not  to  be
denied .---(1) Subject to the provisions of this Act, no
applicant shall be denied access to information or record
held by a public body.
(2) This Act shall be interpreted so as to advance
its purposes as set out in the preamble and to---
-6-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
(a) promote the right of access to information; and
(b)  facilitate  and  encourage  promptly  the
disclosure of the information at the lowest and
reasonable cost.”
Section  3  thus  establishes  the  basic  statutory  rule  that  access  to
information or record held by a public body shall not be denied, while
expressly making that right subject to the provisions of the Act itself.
More importantly, subsection (2) prescribes the interpretive approach to
be adopted in applying the Act: its provisions are to be construed so as to
advance the purposes stated in the Preamble, promote the right of access
to information and facilitate its prompt disclosure. Consequently, the
exclusions and exemptions contained elsewhere in the Act cannot be
construed independently of this overarching legislative policy. They must
be applied according to their language, purpose and statutory limits,
without either defeating the right of access or enlarging an exception
beyond the scope intended by the Legislature.
9 .  Against the above constitutional and statutory framework,
the controversy may now be examined with reference to the particular
record sought by respondent No.3 and the exclusions and exemptions
relied upon by the petitioner. The first question which arises is whether
the minutes of the meetings in question continued to enjoy the exclusion
contemplated by section 7(b) of the Act after the decision concerning the
promotion had been finally taken by the public body.
10 .  The petitioner does not dispute that the requested minutes
are in possession of the LESCO, which being a body falling within the
defi nition of “public body” contained in section 2(ix) of the Act of 2017,
is consequently subject to the obligations imposed by the Act. The
controversy, therefore, is not whether the petitioner-LESCO is amenable
to the Act, but whether the particular record sought by respondent No.3
falls within any subsisting exclusion under section 7 or exemption under
section 16 thereof.
11 .  To determine whether the minutes sought by respondent
No.3 are presently accessible under the Act, it is necessary to examine
-7-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
the statutory scheme governing what constitutes public record and in
particular, the treatment  accorded by the Legislature to minutes of
meetings  and  other  deliberative  material.  Section  6,  subject  to  the
exclusions contained in section 7, declares specified categories of record
to be public record. The relevant provisions read as follows: —
“ 6.Declaration of public record .---Subject to the provisions
of section 7, the following record of all public bodies is
hereby declared to be the public record, namely;---
(a)  - - - - -
(b)  - - - - -
(c)  - - - - -
(d)  Final orders and decisions, including decisions relating to
members of public- - -  ”
The  Legislature  has,  however,  expressly  qualified  the  operation  of
section 6 by enacting section 7, which provides, insofar as relevant to the
controversy before this Court: —
“ 7.  Exclusion  of  certain  record .---Nothing  contained  in
section 6 shall apply to the following record of all public
bodies, namely:---
(a)  Noting on the files, subject to a final decision by the
public body;
(b)  Minutes of meetings, subject to a final decision by the
public body;
(c)  Any intermediary opinion or recommendation, subject to a
final decision by the public body;
(d)  - - - - -
(e)  - - - - -
(f)  - - - - -
(g)  Record relating to the personal privacy of any individual-
- - - ”
12 .  The  statutory  language  reproduced  above  is  significant.
Section 6 declares,  inter alia , final orders and decisions to be public
record, but does so expressly subject to section 7. Section 7, in turn,
creates specific exclusions from the operation of section 6. Insofar as
minutes of meetings are concerned, however, the Legislature has not
created an absolute or perpetual exclusion; rather, it has expressly made
the exclusion “subject to a final decision by the public body”. The same
qualification has been employed in respect of noting on files under clause
(a) and intermediary opinion or recommendation under clause (c). The
-8-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
duration  of  the  exclusion  is,  therefore,  controlled  by  the  statutory
condition expressly attached to it.
13 .  In the present case, the minutes sought by respondent No.3
concern  the  consideration  and  decision  regarding  promotion  of
Muhammad Ramzan Butt. The Information Commission examined the
record and found that the decision concerning his promotion had already
been finalized and acted upon through the office order referred to in the
impugned order. Once the public body had taken its final decision on the
matter which was the subject of the deliberations, the statutory condition
upon which the exclusion under section 7(b) rests ceased to operate. The
petitioner ’ s contention that the minutes continue to remain excluded
merely because they contain deliberations which preceded the final
decision would render the words “subject to a final decision by the public
body”  redundant, which cannot be attributed to the Legislature. Reliance
in this regard may be made to the cases of  “Messrs Pakistan Television
Corporation  Limited  v.  Commissioner  Inland  Revenue  (Legal),  LTU,
Islamabad and others”  (2017 SCMR 1136),  “ Searle IV Solution (Pvt.) Ltd.
and others v. Federation of Pakistan and others”  ( 2018 SCMR 1444) ,
“ Justice Shaukat Aziz Siddiqui  and others v. Federation of Pakistan through
Secretary Law and Justice, Islamabad and others”  (PLD 2018 SC 538)  and
“ Directorate of Intelligence and Investigation - FBR, through Director and
others v. Taj International (Pvt.) Ltd. and others”  (2025 PTD 1270 ) .
14 .  The  submission  that  section  6(d)  makes  only  the  final
decision public and consequently preserves the confidentiality of the
deliberations leading thereto even after the decision has been made, does
not withstand the plain language of section 7(b). Sections 6 and 7 have to
be read harmoniously. Section 6 identifies the record declared to be
public  record,  whereas  section  7  specifies  the  record  which,
notwithstanding section 6, remains excluded and, in the case of minutes
of meetings, expressly makes such exclusion subject to a final decision
by the public body. To accept the petitioner’ s interpretation would be to
render the qualifying words “subject to a final decision by the public
body” practically meaningless. Such an interpretation cannot be adop ted
-9-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
when  the  Legislature  has  consciously  attached  a  condition  to  the
exclusion.
15 .  The petitioner, however, seeks to sustain withholding of the
requested record independently of section 7 by invoking the exemptions
contained in section 16 of the Act. It is, therefore, necessary to examine
the relevant provisions thereof. Section 16, insofar as material to the
controversy, provides: —
“ 16. Information exempt from disclosure .---(1) Subject to
the provisions of this Act,---
(a)  A public body shall not be required to disclose exempt
information,---
(i) provided that where only part of a record or the
information  falls  within  the  scope  of  the  exceptions
provided for in this Act, that part shall be severed and the
residual record or information shall be provided to the
applicant; and
(ii) - - - - -
(b)  - - - - -
(c)  Information is exempt if its disclosure under this Act
would  involve  invasion  of  privacy  of  an  indentifiable
individual, including a deceased individual, other than the
applicant. This exception shall not apply where----
(i) - - - - -
(ii) - - - - -
(iii) the third party is or was an official of a public body
and the information relates to his functions as a public
official;
(d)  - - - - -
(e)  - - - - -
(f)  - - - - -
(g)  - - - - -
(h)  - - - - -
(i)  Information may be exempt if its disclosure is likely to---
(i)  cause  prejudice  to  the  effective  formulation  or
development of government policy;
(ii)  frustrate  the  success  of  a  policy,  by  premature
disclosure of that policy;
(iii) undermine the deliberative process in a public body
or inhibiting the free and frank provision of advice or
exchange of views------ ”
The above provisions make it clear that the exemptions contemplated by
section 16 operate on a basis distinct from the conditional exclusion
-10-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
contained in section 7(b). While the latter ceases to operate upon the
occurrence of the statutory event specified therein, i.e., a final decision by
the public body, a claim under section 16 must independently satisfy the
particular  statutory  requirements  of  the  exemption  invoked.  The
petitioner’ s reliance upon the deliberative nature of the meetings may,
therefore, be examined on that footing.
16 .  Section 16(1)(h)(iii) permits exemption where disclosure is
likely  to  undermine  the  deliberative  process  in  a  public  body  by
inhibiting the free and frank provision of advice or exchange of views.
The exemption, therefore, is not attracted  merely because a record
contains deliberations; the statutory test as to the likelihood of the
specified  prejudice must  also  be satisfied. In the present case, the
petitioner has not demonstrated how disclosure of the minutes, after the
decision concerning the promotion had been finalized and acted upon, is
likely to  undermine any continuing  deliberative process. A  general
apprehension that disclosure of deliberations may inhibit frankness in
future decision-making, without demonstrating the statutory prejudice
contemplated by section 16(1)(h)(iii), cannot by itself justify withholding
the record.
17 .  The plea founded upon personal privacy also does not justify
withholding the entire record. The petitioner has principally relied upon
section 7(g), which excludes from public record “record relating to the
personal privacy of any individual”, as well as section 16(1)(c), which
permits exemption in respect of information the disclosure of which
would  constitute  an  unwarranted  invasion  of  the  privacy  of  an
identifiable  individual.  The  mere  fact  that  the  minutes  concern  an
identifiable officer and relate to his promotion does not, however, make
the entire record a thing of his personal privacy. The information sought
concerns the decision-making process of a public body in relation to
promotion to a public office and, in that sense, bears upon an official
matter. Moreover, section 16(1)(c) itself recognizes an exception where
the third party is or was an official of a public body and the information
relates to his functions as a public official. The petitioner has not
-11-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
established that disclosure of the requested minutes, as a whole, would
constitute  an  unwarranted  invasion  of  personal  privacy  within  the
meaning of the Act.
18 .  This Court is conscious that the minutes of a meeting may,
depending upon their contents, contain information which is genuinely
personal or otherwise falls within a statutory exemption. However, that
possibility cannot justify withholding the entire record. The Legislature
has expressly provided in section 16(1)(a)(i) that where only part of a
record falls within the scope of an exception, that part shall be severed
and the residual record or information shall be provided to the applicant.
Therefore,  if  any  specific  portion  of  the  minutes  is  demonstrably
protected by a valid statutory exemption, the same may be severed in
accordance with law; such possibility, however, does not revive the
otherwise expired exclusion under section 7(b) or entitle the petitioner-
LESCO to withhold the minutes in their entirety.
19 .  The argument that respondent No.3 failed to disclose any
public interest or reasons for seeking the information is also without
substance. Section 11(5) of the Act of 2017 expressly provides that in no
case shall an applicant be required to provide reasons for his request. The
petitioner, therefore,  could not  lawfully require respondent No.3 to
disclose  the purpose or justification for which  he  sought the requested
record. The statutory right  is thus not made contingent upon the applicant
first  establishing  his  personal  interest  or  demonstrating  a particular
purpose for seeking information.
20 .  The  petitioner’ s  further  objection  that  the  Information
Commission lacked jurisdiction to keep the matter for implementation is
also  misconceived.  The  statutory  scheme  itself  contemplates
implementation of decisions of the Commission. Section 19(2)(e) assigns
to the Commission the responsibility to ensure implementation of the Act
and the record to be made public under it, while section 20(1)(e)
empowers the Commission to order a public body to disclose information
to an applicant or to take such other reasonable measures as it may deem
necessary to remedy any failure to implement the Act. The direction
-12-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
requiring LESCO to furnish the information within a specified period
was, therefore, consequential to the decision allowing the appeal and fell
within  the  statutory  framework  governing  enforcement  of  the
Commission’s decision. The mere fact that the matter was fixed for
implementation could not, by itself, render the impugned order without
lawful authority.
21 .  It is also pertinent that, in an appeal under section 17, the
public body bears the burden of proof to establish that it acted in
accordance  with  the  Act.  The  petitioner,  despite  raising  several
objections, has not established that the requested minutes fall within any
subsisting exclusion under section 7 or satisfy the requirements of any
exemption under section 16 so as to justify their complete withholding.
The impugned order, therefore, cannot be characterized as having been
passed  in  disregard  of  the  statutory  scheme.  On  the  contrary,  the
Information Commission correctly appreciated that the final decision
having already been taken, the conditional exclusion contained in section
7(b) was no longer available to the petitioner-LESCO.
22 .  The  authorities  relied  upon  by  learned  counsel  for  the
petitioner do not alter the  above conclusion, as the questions considered
therein are materially distinguishable from the precise controversy arising
in the present case. In  the case of  Pakistan Telecommunication Authority ,
the Islamabad High Court was concerned with a request for t he minutes
of a meeting relating to a licence and while noticing section 7 of the Act
of 2017, observed that the protection afforded to minutes was subject to a
final decision by the public body and directed that the final decision be
furnished. The judgme nt, however, did not examine the precise question
whether the statutory exclusion of minutes under section 7(b) continues
after the public body has taken the final decision to which the statutory
qualification expressly relates. The said judgment, therefor e, does not lay
down a contrary principle so as to displace the interpretation which
follows from the plain language of section 7(b). Likewise,  the cases of
Muhammad  Nawaz ,  Girish  Ramchandra  Deshpande  and  R.K.  Jain
principally  concern  the  protection  of  per sonal  information,  service
-13-
W.P. No.33829 of 2026
records, ACRs and the right to privacy, rather than the duration or
operation of the conditional exclusion relating to minutes of meetings
under section 7(b) of the Act of 2017. The judgment in the case of
Shahbaz Akmal Jandran  likewise dealt with the scope of the right to
information  vis-à-vis  the  privacy  of  individuals  under  the  Punjab
Transparency and Right to Information Act, 2013 and not with the
statutory consequence of the words “subject to a final decision by the
public bo dy” occurring in section 7(b) of the federal Act of 2017.  The
remaining authorities cited by learned counsel also do not address the
specific statutory question presently before the Court and, therefore,
cannot be regarded as controlling the interpretation of section 7(b) in the
facts of the present case.
23 .  For the foregoing reasons, this Court finds no illegality,
jurisdictional defect or misreading of the provisions of the Act of 2017 in
the impugned order dated 04.05.2026. The petitioner ’ s objections seek to
sustain exclusions and exemptions beyond the scope prescribed by the
Legislature and, therefore, do not warrant interference in constitutional
jurisdiction. Consequently, the petition in hand is dismissed. There shall,
however, be no order as to costs.
(RAHEEL KAMRAN)
JUDGE
Announced in the Open Court on 01.09.2026.
JUDGE
Approved for reporting.
JUDGE
Azhar*

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_012]
Case: Service 44193/22 (Muhammad Akram Vs Province of Punjab etc).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Service 44193/22. Title: Service 44193/22 (Muhammad Akram Vs Province of Punjab etc). Official Ref: 2026LHC5401. Author Judge: Justice Ms. Shireen Imran. Judgment Date (as displayed): 01-09-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5401.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 8 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Stereo .  H  C  J  D  A 38
JUDGMENT SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT ,  LAHORE
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
Writ Petition No.  44193  of 20 2 2 .
Muhammad  Akram  Vs.  Province of Punjab  & others
JUDGMENT
Date of hearing:  31 .08.2026 .
Petitioner  by:  Mr.  Kashif Ali Chaudhry , Advocate.
Respondents  by:  Mr.  Qamar  Zaman  Qureshi,  Additional
Advocate General.
SHIREEN IMRAN,  J :  Through  this  petition, petitioner has
challenged  order  dated 24.06.2022  (impugned order)  passed by the
Divisional  Forest  Officer,  Faisalabad  Forest  Division,  Faisalabad
(respondent No.4) , whereby  earlier order  dated 30.06.2021,  qua his
retirement  on  medical  grounds ,  with  effect  from  18.06.2021 ,
along with  encashment of 365 days  in lieu of  Leave Preparatory to
Retirement (LPR), was withdrawn.
2.  W hile assailing  impugned order ,  dated 24.06.2022  passed by
respondent No.4 , learned counsel for the  petitioner  contended  that  the
petitioner was initially retired from service on medical grounds  vide
order  dated 30.06.2021, with effect from 18.06.2021, after having
been declared medically incapacitated in Category “B”. It is submitted
that  pursuant to the said order, a vested and legitimate right had
accrued in favour  of the petitioner with regard to his retirement
W.P . No. 44193  of 202 2 .  2
benefits, including the benefit of  LPR  and encashment of 365 days’
leave in lieu thereof.  Learned counsel further submits that  order  dated
30.06.2021  was  valid and operative  and had attained legal efficacy
much prior to  the  subsequent clarification  issued  by the Finance
Department, Government of the P u njab ,  dated 24.08.2021 ,  after which
department passed impugned order ,  dated 24.06.2022.  According to
learned counsel, once the petitioner had already been retired and the
consequential  benefits  had  accrued  to  him,  same  could  not
subsequently be withdrawn or nullified retrospectively ,  merely on the
basis of a later clarification .  It is further argued that the impugned
order has been passed in clear violation of the principles of natural
justice  as before  withdrawing the earlier retirement order,  petitioner
was  neither  issued  any  show  cause  notice  nor  afforded  any
opportunity of hearing.  Learned counsel has also questioned the
competence and  authority of the department to pass the impugned
order on the basis of recommendations allegedly made by the Finance
Department. It is contended that the question relating to the mode and
manner of  petitioner’s retirement falls within the domain of the
Services & General Administration Department  ( S&GAD )  and that
the  Finance  Department  has  no  substantive  role  in  determining
whether the petitioner was liable to be retired on medical grounds or
otherwise ,  which was  duly proceeded .  Learned counsel ,  thus submits
that  impugned order ,  dated 24.06.2022  is without lawful justification,
contrary to the settled principles of natural justice  and liable to be set
aside, especially  when  no prior notice or opportunity of hearing was
W.P . No. 44193  of 202 2 .  3
afforded to the petitioner before taking  any  adverse action  against
him.
3.  Conversely,  learned  Law  Officer,  while  supporting  the
impugned order submits that the authority  empowered  to pass an order
can also  recall /withdraw  the same ,  t hus no exception can be taken to
the  impugned  order,  which  has  been  passed  on  the  basis  of
clarification dated 24.08.2021 issued by the Finance Department .
Further submits that  the  impugned order has caused  no prejudice to
the petitioner as only the nature of his reti rement has been changed.
Adds that  if the argument of learned counsel for the petitioner  qua  his
retirement falls within the domain of S&GAD is accepted as correct ,
then  S&GAD  having  not been impleaded as party, th is  petition is
liable to be dismissed for non - joinder of necessary party.
4.  Arguments  heard. Record perused.
5.  Admittedly,  petitioner stood retired from government service
w.e.f. 18.06.2021  vide  order dated 30.06.2021 passed by respondent
No.4 on medical grounds. Later on, the said order w as withdrawn  vide
order dated 24.06.2022 ,  about one year of passing of earlier order  on
the basis of clarification issued by the Finance Department dated
24.08.2021 , which reads as under:  -
“ In  order  to  discourage  the  tendency  of  getting
declaration of incapacitated /invitation on the veige of
retirement  as  a  matter  of  routine  the  benefit  in
question may not be allowed beyond the date of when
the LPR is rule. ”
W.P . No. 44193  of 202 2 .  4
After passing of order  dated 30.06.2021, reasonable rights accrued in
favour of the petitioner ,  which cannot be withdrawn later on. There is
no cavil with the fact that an authority having power to pass an order
can also recall /withdraw  the same but the same is subject to the
condition that no decisive step has been taken. So far as the case in
hand is  concerned, after passing of order dated 30.06.2021, certain
decisive steps , including appointment of son of the petitioner under
Rule 17 - A of  the Punjab Civil Servants (Appointment and Conditions
of Service) Rules, 1974  were taken ,  consequently  the rights  accrued in
favour of the petitioner cannot be withdrawn  in view of  the  principle
of  locus poenitentiae .  The basic judgment on the principle of  locus
poenitentiae  has been rendered in the case of  Pakistan, through the
Secretary, Ministry of Finance v. Muhammad  Himayatullah Farukhi
( PLD 1969 SC 407 ), wherein the  H o n ’ ble Supreme Court of  Pakistan
elaborated the scope of  said principle  as under:  -
“ There can hardly be any dispute with the rule as
laid  down,  in  these  cases  that  apart  from  the
provisions of section 21 of the General Clauses Act,
locus poenitentiae, i.e., the power of receding till a
decisive step is taken, is available to the Government
or the relevant authorities. In fact, the existence of
such  a  power  is  necessary  in  the  case  of  all
authorities empowered to pass orders to retrace the
wrong steps taken by them. The authority that has the
power to make an order has also the power to undo
it. But this is subject to the exception that where the
order has taken legal effect, and in pursuance thereof
certain rights have been created in favour of any
individual, such an order cannot be withdrawn or
rescinded to the detriment of those rights. ”
W.P . No. 44193  of 202 2 .  5
6.  Further, the aforesaid view was followed by the Hon’ble
Supreme Court of Pakistan in the case s  of  Pakistan Railways through
Chief Executive Officer/ Senior General Manager, Lahore and another
v. Muhammad Aslam  ( 2024 SCMR 97 ) and  Shams Ur Rehman v.
Military Accountant General, Rawalpindi and another  ( 2020 SCMR
188 ) . In the latter case, the Hon’ble Supreme Court of Pakistan has
inter alia  observ ed  as under:  -
“…  The  case  would,  thus,  be  governed  by  the
principle of  locus  poenitentiae and, in our view, the
Department cannot retrace the steps already taken
and lawfully acted upon by the petitioner."
Foregoing  the  above  discussion,  after  passing  of  order  da ted
30.06.2021, certain decisive steps were  taken and  valuable  rights
accrued including appointment of son of the petitioner ,  which cannot
be taken back in view of the principle of  locus poenitentiae .
7.  Another important aspect of the matter is that whether the
clarification of the Finance Department can operate retrospectively.
The impugned clarification does not  mention its retrospective or
prospective effect  and  it is trite law that any policy/rules  does not
operative retrospectively until and unless the same has  been given
retrospective effect by the Legislator. Reliance in this regard can be
made to the  case of  Secretary to Government of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa
Communication and Works Department, Civil Secretariat, Peshawar
and others v. Inayat Ullah and another  ( 2026 SCMR 558 ) , in which it
was  held as under:  -
“The  fundamental  principle  governing  the
application of amended rules is that such amendment
W.P . No. 44193  of 202 2 .  6
operates prospectively unless a contrary intimation is
manifestly evident from the statute.”
Likewise, in the case o f  Controller General of Accounts, Government
of Pakistan, Islamabad and others v. Abdul Waheed and others
( 2023 SCMR 111 ) , it was held that :  -
“ 6.*****  The insertion or deletion of any provision
in the rules or the law, if merely  procedural  in nature
would  apply  retrospectively  but  not  if  it  affects
substantial rights which already stood accrued at the
time when the un - amended rule or provision was in
vogue …. ”
As there was no mention of retrospective application of the subject
clarification, the same was  to operate prospectively.
8.  Interestingly, before withdrawal of  order dated 30.06.2021,
neither an opportunity of  hearing  was granted to the petitioner nor any
show cause  notice  whatsoever  in that regard was ever issued , which
approach being against the principle of  audi alteram partem  cannot be
authenticated.  No one should be condemned unheard would be enough
to vitiate even the most solemn proceedings. This principle has
originated from the Islamic principles of justice. Reliance in  this
regard is placed  in  the case of  Hyderabad Chamber of Commerce and
Industry v. M/o Commerce Govt. of Pakistan.  ( PLD 2024 Islamabad
353 ).  Likewise, in the case of  Central Development Authority through
Chairman, Islamabad and others v. Shabir Hussain and others  ( 2022
SCMR 627 ),  it was held that:  -
“7***** Another shortcoming and unlawfulness is
that no prior notice or justification was even shown
in the cancellation letter which is flagrant violation
of well settled principle of natural justice which is
W.P . No. 44193  of 202 2 .  7
firmly established and  deep rooted in the judicial
conscience to be entrenched and embedded in every
decision  making  function  either  judicial,  quasi -
judicial or administrative which is a fundamental
rule of law that no decision must be taken affecting
the right of any person without first being informed of
the  case  and  affording  an  ample  opportunity  of
defence…”
9.  The requirement of  hearing the parties  is not an empty
formality ,  rather  it constitutes an essential component of the principles
of natural justice, due process and fair trial. The authority proposing to
take an adverse action ,  must  before reaching any conclusion against
the person concerned ,  disclose the substance of the allegations and
provide an effective opportunity to submit a response or defence.
10.  Where  an  adverse  or  punitive  action  is  taken  without
following the prescribed procedure of law  or without affording the
person concerned a n  adequate opportunity of hearing, such action is
rendered vulnerable to judicial scrutiny. The violation of the principles
of natural justice strikes at the very root of the proceeding s  and any
consequential order founded upon such defective proceedings cannot
ordinarily  be  sustained  merely  on  the  ground  that  the  authority
otherwise possessed jurisdiction  to take  action.
1 1 .  The  requirement  of  observance  of  due  process  assumes
greater  significance  where  the  proposed  action  carries  civil
consequences,  affects  the  rights,  status,  employment,  benefits,
reputation or other legitimate interests of a person. An authority
cannot bypa ss the requirement of providing a reasonable opportunity
of defence and thereafter seek to validate the action on the basis of
W.P . No. 44193  of 202 2 .  8
subsequent explanations or proceedings. The safeguards of natural
justice are intended to prevent arbitrary exercise of power and to
ensure fairness, transparency and impartiality in administrative and
disciplinary decision - making.  Reliance in this regard  is  placed  o n the
case of  The Province of Sindh through Chief Secretary Government of
Sindh and others v. Raj Kumar Lohana and others  ( 2026 SCMR 278 ).
1 2 .  In view of the foregoing discussion  and having examined the
matter in the light of the applicable law and the principles of natural
justice, this Court is of the considered opinion that  impugned order
dated 24.06.2022 cannot be sustained  i n the eye of  law , having been
passed without due observance of  requisite procedure . Consequently,
this  petition is  allowed  and  impugned  order dated 24.06.2022  is set
aside .  As  a result,  order dated 30.06.2021  shall  hold the  field.
(SHIREEN IMRAN)
JUDGE
Approved for reporting.
JUDGE
U mar

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_013]
Case: Criminal Proceedings 46868/26 (Muhammad Aslam Vs Additional Sessions Judge etc).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Criminal Proceedings 46868/26. Title: Criminal Proceedings 46868/26 (Muhammad Aslam Vs Additional Sessions Judge etc). Official Ref: 2026LHC5390. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Munawar Iqbal Duggal. Judgment Date (as displayed): 31-08-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5390.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 11 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Stereo. H C J D A 38.
Judgment Sheet
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT AT LAHORE.
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
W.P. No. of 46868 of 2026
Muhammad Aslam
VERSUS
Addl. Sessions Judge/Ex-Officio Justice of Peace etc.
JUDGEMENT
Date of Hearing:  27.8.2026
Petitioner by:  Muhammad Afzaal, Advocate along with petitioner
Muhammad Aslam.
Respondent by:  Mr. Anwaar Hussain, Assistant Advocate General
for respondent Nos.2 to 4, 9 and 10 along with
Waheed, Station House Officer.
Mian H. M. Asim Mehmood and Malik Muhammad
Atif, Advocates along with respondent Nos.5 and 6
and Mst. Hina Aslam (daughter of the petitioner)
under the escort of Sana Nawaz 32407/C.
Ms.  Samra  Yar  Muhammad,  Law  Officer  Child
Protection and Welfare Bureau, Lahore
MUNAWAR  IQBAL  DUGGAL,  J.  Through  the  instant
constitutional petition under Article 199 of the Constitution of the Islamic
Republic  of  Pakistan,  1973  ( hereinafter  to  be  referred  as  “the
Constitution” ), the petitioner has called in question the legality and
propriety of the order dated 06.08.2026 passed by the learned Additional
Sessions  Judge/Ex-Officio  Justice  of  Peace,  Kasur,  whereby  the
application filed by the petitioner under Sections 22-A and 22-B of the
Code of Criminal Procedure, 1898 ( hereinafter to be called as “Cr.P.C.”)
seeking  registration  of  a  criminal  case  against  respondent
No.5/Muhammad Luqman and others was dismissed.
W.P. No.46868 of 2026  -2-
2.  The facts necessary for adjudication of the instant petition are that
the daughter of the petitioner, namely Mst. Hina Aslam, went missing,
whereupon  the  petitioner  got  registered  FIR  No.1121/2026  dated
20.05.2026 under Section 365-B of Pakistan Penal Code (Act XLV of
1860), ( he reinafter to be referred as “PPC” ), at Police Station Sattokatla,
Lahore, alleging that his daughter had been abducted by unknown persons
for the purpose of illicit intercourse. During the course of investigation,
however, Mst. Hina Aslam appeared before the learned Area Magistrate
and got her statement recorded under Section 164, Cr.P.C., on 29.05.2026,
wherein she stated that she had neither been abducted nor subjected to
zina by anyone and that she had contracted marriage with respondent
No.5/Muhammad Luqman of her own free will and consent. She further
stated that her father had lodged a false and frivolous FIR and expressed
no objection to its cancellation. On the basis of the aforesaid statement,
the said FIR is stated to be under the process of cancellation.
3.  Subsequently, upon coming to know about the alleged Nikah of his
daughter, the petitioner approached the Municipal Committee, Kasur, for
verification of the Nikahnama. The Municipal Committee, Kasur, vide
letter dated 23.06.2026, informed the petitioner that no record of the
alleged  Nikah  was  available  in  its  office.  The  petitioner  thereafter
approached the Station House Officer, Police Station B-Division, Kasur,
seeking registration of an FIR against respondent Nos. 5 to 9, alleging,
inter alia, that a marriage had been solemnized with a minor girl in
violation of the Punjab Child Marriage Restraint Act, 2026 and that the
purported Nikahnama was forged and fictitious. As no action was taken
on the application, the petitioner invoked the jurisdiction of the learned
Ex-Officio Justice of Peace under Sections 22-A and 22-B, Cr.P.C.
4.  The learned Additional Sessions Judge/Ex-Officio Justice of Peace,
Kasur, after hearing the parties and perusing the police report, dismissed
the application vide order dated 06.08.2026 and suggested the petitioner
W.P. No.46868 of 2026  -3-
to approach the Investigating Officer for addition of the relevant offences
in FIR No.1121/2026. Hence, the instant petition.
5.  Upon preliminary examination of the material available on record,
this Court, vide order dated 17.08.2026, directed respondents Nos.3 and 4
to produce Mst. Hina Aslam before the Court. In compliance therewith,
she  was  produced  before  the  Court.  Respondent  No.5/Muhammad
Luqman and his father, namely Muhammad Ilyas, also appeared along
with their learned counsel.
6.  Learned counsel for respondent Nos.5 and 6 was confronted with
the Child Registration Certificate (CRC No.10818545) available on the
record, issued by NADRA on 09.03.2017, wherein the date of birth of
Mst. Hina Aslam is recorded as 01.03.2011. Thus, according to the
document available on record, she was approximately fifteen years and
two months old on the date of the alleged Nikah, i.e. 18.05.2026. Learned
counsel for the respondents could not produce any contemporaneous and
reliable document establishing that Mst. Hina Aslam had attained the age
of eighteen years on the date of the alleged marriage. The respondents,
however, submitted that she had represented herself to be nineteen years
of  age and  that, relying  upon  her representation, the  marriage  was
solemnized. Reliance was also placed upon her statement recorded under
Section 164, Cr.P.C., wherein she stated that she was above eighteen
years of age and had contracted marriage with respondent No.5 of her
own free will and consent.
7.  Learned counsel for petitioner has reiterated his earlier contentions
recorded in the interim order dated 17.08.2026. For sake of completion
and brevity, the same are reproduced for ease of reference as under:
“ Learned counsel for the petitioner submits that the petitioner’s
daughter, namely Hina Aslam, is about fifteen years of age and is
W.P. No.46868 of 2026  -4-
stated to have contracted marriage with respondent No.5. In this
regard, learned counsel refers to the Nikahnama, according to
which the marriage was solemnized on 18.05.2026 by a Nikah
Khawan within the territorial limits of District Kasur. Learned
counsel further relies upon a birth certificate issued by NADRA,
wherein Hina Aslam is mentioned at Serial No.7 and her date of
birth is recorded as 01.03.2011.
Learned counsel further submits that the petitioner approached the
Municipal  Committee,  Kasur,  for  verification  of  the  aforesaid
Nikahnama , whereupon the Chief Officer, Municipal Committee,
Kasur, vide report dated 23.06.2026, reported that no record of the
said  Nikah  was available in the office. He further contends that the
alleged  Nikah  was solemnized and the purported  Nikahnama  was
prepared  in  violation  of  the  provisions  of  the  Punjab  Child
Marriage Restraint Act, 2026. The petitioner thereafter approached
the  Station  House  Officer,  Police  Station  B-Division,  Kasur,
seeking registration of an FIR, but to no avail, whereupon he was
constrained to invoke the jurisdiction of the learned Ex-Officio
Justice of Peace under Sections 22-A & 22-B, Cr.P.C ”
8.  Heard, the record perused.
9.  At the outset, it is necessary to appreciate the legal effect of the
Punjab Child Marriage Restraint Act, 2026 published in official gazette
on 11
th
of May, 2026 with its commencement at once ( hereinafter to be
called as “the Act , 2026 ”) . The Act defines a “child” as a person, male or
female, who is under eighteen years of age, and defines “child marriage”
to include an act of Nikah or solemnization of marriage where either of
the contracting parties is a child. The statute further prohibits registration
of a child marriage and creates penal consequences for an adult who
contracts marriage with a child. More significantly, Section 5 declares that
any form of cohabitation, before attainment of eighteen years of age, with
or without consent, resulting from a child marriage, constitutes “child
abuse” under the Act , ibid. The statutory scheme thus represents a
material departure from the earlier legislative framework.
W.P. No.46868 of 2026  -5-
10.  It is worthy to note that quite recently the Federal Constitutional
Court,  in  “ Shahbaz  Masih  v.  Additional  Sessions  Judge,  Lahore  &
others ” , F.C.P.L.A. No.536 of 2025, decided on 03.02.2026, considered
the earlier legal regime applicable in Punjab and, after examining the
judgments in  “ Mst. Bakhshi v. Bashir Ahmad ”  (PLD 1970 SC 323) and
“ Mauj Ali v. Syed Safdar Hussain Shah ”  (1970 SCMR 437), observed
that  the  Child  Marriage  Restraint  Act,  1929,  as  then  applicable,
criminalized the solemnization of child marriage but did not expressly
declare such marriage void or voidable. The Federal Constitutional Court
consequently held that, in the absence of an express statutory provision
invalidating the marriage, its validity was not affected merely by reason of
the contravention of the penal provisions.
11.  The aforesaid principle, however, may not be applicable to the
controversy before this Court. The alleged Nikah in the instant case was
solemnized on 18.05.2026, i.e. after the enactment and coming into force
of the Act, 2026. The new enactment has materially altered the statutory
landscape by prescribing eighteen years as the minimum age for both
male and female persons, criminalizing an adult's marriage with a child,
and, most importantly, expressly characterizing cohabitation with a child
arising from such marriage as “child abuse”, irrespective of consent. The
present case must, therefore, be examined in the light of the law actually
in force on the date of the alleged occurrence.
12.  The  Court  is  conscious  that  the  question  of  age  may,  in  an
appropriate  case,  require  determination  upon  evidence  and  that
constitutional jurisdiction is ordinarily not the forum for conclusively
adjudicating disputed questions of fact. Nevertheless, at the stage of
considering an application under Sections 22-A and 22-B, Cr.P.C., the
Court is not required to finally determine the guilt or innocence of the
proposed  accused.  The  limited  question  is  whether  the  information
furnished to the police discloses commission of a cognizable offence
W.P. No.46868 of 2026  -6-
warranting registration and investigation. The evidentiary value of the
documents and veracity of the defence version are matters to be examined
during investigation and, where necessary, determined at trial.
13.  In the present case, the CRC issued by NADRA in 2017 records the
date of birth of Mst. Hina Aslam as 01.03.2011. The alleged Nikah took
place on 18.05.2026. Prima facie, therefore, she was below eighteen years
of age on the date of the alleged marriage. The respondents have not
produced,  at  this  stage,  any  equally  reliable  contemporaneous
documentary material rebutting the said entry. Consequently, the material
placed before the Court gives rise to a reasonable basis for treating the
alleged occurrence as falling within the statutory framework of the Punjab
Child Marriage Restraint Act, 2026.
14.  The statement of Mst. Hina Aslam recorded under Section 164,
Cr.P.C., undoubtedly constitutes relevant material and her views cannot
be disregarded merely because she is a child. At the same time, under the
express statutory scheme introduced by Section 13 of the Act, 2026, her
purported consent cannot, by itself, be treated as determinative of her
residence, custody or protection where the Court is confronted with a
prima facie case of child marriage and the consequent risk of child abuse.
The statute requires the Court to make an independent assessment of her
safety, welfare and best interests.
15.  This  approach  is  also  consistent  with  the  contemporary
jurisprudence concerning the welfare of child, according to which the
wishes of a child capable of forming an intelligent preference are a
relevant  consideration,  but  the  paramount  consideration  remains  the
welfare  and  best  interests  of  the  child.  The  Supreme  Court  has
emphasized that custody proceedings require a holistic assessment of the
child's circumstances and that the best interests of the child, rather than
W.P. No.46868 of 2026  -7-
the  competing  interests  of  the  parents  or  other  adults,  remain  the
controlling consideration.
16.  It is, therefore, not necessary for this Court, at the present stage, to
pronounce conclusively upon the validity or otherwise of the alleged
Nikah. Such a determination may involve questions of fact and law which
are appropriately left to the competent forum. What is material for the
present proceedings is that there exists prima facie material disclosing an
occurrence which, if established, may attract penal consequences under
the Act, 2026. The police cannot decline to set the criminal law into
motion merely because the alleged child has expressed willingness to
reside with the adult contracting party.
17.  The next question is whether registration of a separate FIR is barred
by the legal principle laid down in  “ Mst. Sughran Bibi v. The State ”  (PLD
2018 SC 595). The answer depends upon the nature of the occurrence
alleged in the proposed FIR. The law laid down in  Sughran Bibi  is that a
second FIR is not permissible merely to place before the police another
version of the same occurrence already reported. At the same time, the
investigating agency remains under a duty to investigate all aspects of the
occurrence and all versions brought before it.
18.  The facts of the instant case, however, prima facie disclose a
distinction between the occurrence forming the subject matter of FIR
No.1121/2026 and the subsequent allegations now made by the petitioner.
The earlier FIR concerned the alleged abduction of Mst. Hina Aslam by
unknown  persons.  The  present  complaint  concerns  the  subsequent
discovery and alleged solemnization of a child marriage on 18.05.2026,
the alleged preparation/use of a purported Nikahnama and the alleged
involvement of persons in solemnizing or facilitating the marriage of a
child. The latter allegations are not merely a counter-version of the
W.P. No.46868 of 2026  -8-
alleged abduction; rather, they relate to an identifiable statutory offence
arising from a distinct transaction.
19.  The distinction is further reinforced by the fact that the alleged
Nikah came to light during the investigation of the earlier case and that
the  Municipal  Committee,  Kasur,  has  reportedly  stated  that  no
corresponding record of the alleged Nikah exists in its office. Whether the
Nikahnama is forged, whether the marriage was in fact solemnized, who
participated in or facilitated it, and what offences, if any, were committed
are  matters  requiring  investigation.  They  cannot  appropriately  be
foreclosed at the threshold by treating the complaint as merely a second
version of the earlier allegation of abduction.
20.  The principle enunciated in  Sughran Bibi , therefore, does not, in the
peculiar circumstances of the instant case, furnish a lawful basis for
refusing to entertain the petitioner's complaint altogether. The police shall,
however, ensure that the investigation of the earlier FIR and the newly
reported occurrence is investigated objectively and strictly in accordance
with law, without any no duplication of proceedings or prejudice to any
person.
21.  It follows that the learned Additional Sessions Judge/Ex-Officio
Justice of Peace fell in error in declining to exercise jurisdiction on the
sole premise that the petitioner should seek addition of offences in the
earlier FIR. The impugned order has failed to take into consideration the
subsequent and distinct allegation of child marriage and the statutory
consequences flowing from the Act, 2026.
22.  For what has been discussed above, the instant petition is  allowed .
The impugned order dated 06.08.2026 passed by the learned Additional
Sessions  Judge/Ex-Officio  Justice  of  Peace,  Kasur,  is  set  aside.
Respondent No.4/SHO, Police Station B-Division, Kasur, is directed to
proceed in accordance with Section 154, Cr.P.C., on the basis of the
W.P. No.46868 of 2026  -9-
petitioner's application and, if the information discloses commission of a
cognizable  offence, record  the  same  and  proceed  with  investigation
strictly in accordance with law. Needless to observe that the registration
of the case shall not be construed as an expression of opinion regarding
the guilt of any person.
23.  As regards the custody and protection of Mst. Hina Aslam, the
Court cannot lose sight of the fact that, prima facie, she is below eighteen
years of age and that the alleged marriage was solemnized after the
coming into force of the Act, 2026. Therefore, despite her statement that
she wishes to go with respondent No.5, Mst. Hina Aslam cannot be
allowed to go with respondent No. 5/Muhammad Luqman as any form of
cohabitation, before attaining the age of eighteen years, with or without
consent has been treated as child abuse under Section 5 of the Act, 2026
and punishment of seven years has been prescribed for any person who
induces, forces, persuades, entices or coerces, a child bride to engage in
child abuse.  For ease of ready reference, the section 5 the Act, ibid is
reproduced as under:-
“5.  Child  abuse  and  its  punishment-  (1)  Any  form  of
cohabitation, before attaining the age of eighteen years, with or
without consent, resulting from a child marriage shall be the
child abuse under the Act.
(2)  Any person who induces, forces, persuades, entices or
coerces any child bride or child groom to engage in child abuse
or commits child abuse shall be punished with imprisonment for
a term which may extend to seven years but shall not be less
than five years and with fine which shall not be less than one
million rupees.”
24.  Section 13 of the Act, 2026 expressly requires the best interests of
the child to remain a primary consideration and contemplates immediate
protective measures, including safe shelter and supervised custody where
required. The statutory scheme further provides that the child's purported
W.P. No.46868 of 2026  -10-
consent, by itself, shall not determine questions of custody or residence in
proceedings under the Act, ibid.
25.  At the same time, the Court must not disregard the expressed
wishes and dignity of Mst. Hina Aslam. Her statement before this Court
indicates that she does not wish to return to her parental home and
apprehends  ill-treatment  at  the  hands  of  family  members.  In  these
circumstances, neither immediate restoration to the parental home nor
handing over of her custody to respondent No.5 would adequately address
the  competing  considerations  of  safety,  welfare,  protection  and  her
expressed wishes.
26.  Consequently, and purely as a protective measure, Mst. Hina Aslam
shall remain in the safe custody of the Child Protection & Welfare
Bureau, Government of the Punjab, through its duly authorized officer,
pending further orders of the competent forum. The Bureau shall ensure
her safety, dignity, education, psychological well-being and access to
appropriate counselling and shall not permit any person to exercise
coercion or undue influence upon her.
27.  The learned Guardian Judge, Lahore, shall have jurisdiction to
determine the question of custody in appropriate proceedings under the
Guardians and Wards Act, 1890, upon an independent assessment of the
welfare  and  best  interests  of  the  minor.  Nothing  contained  in  this
judgment  shall  prejudice  the  determination  of  the  competent  Court
regarding permanent custody, guardianship or the legal status of the
alleged Nikah.
28.  In  view  of  the  apprehension  expressed  by  Mst.  Hina  Aslam
regarding her safety at her parental home, her interaction with her parents
shall, for the present, be arranged only in a supervised environment by an
authorized officer of the Child Protection & Welfare Bureau. Likewise, if
W.P. No.46868 of 2026  -11-
Mst. Hina Aslam expresses a desire to meet respondent No.5 or his
parents, such interaction may be arranged by the Bureau in a safe and
supervised environment, subject to the assessment of the child's welfare
and without permitting any private or unsupervised meeting.
29.  The Child Protection & Welfare Bureau shall submit a brief report
before this Court regarding the safety and welfare of Mst. Hina Aslam
after an interval of four weeks and thereafter as and when required by this
Court.
30.  Before  parting  with  the  judgment,  it  is  clarified  that  the
observations made herein are confined to the prima facie assessment
necessary for deciding the petition under Article 199 of the Constitution
and Sections 22-A and 22-B, Cr.P.C. The Investigating Officer shall
conduct  an  impartial  investigation  without  being  influenced  by  any
observation contained herein, and the competent criminal Court shall
decide the matter strictly in accordance with the evidence and law.
31.  The instant Writ Petition is allowed in the above terms.
( MUNAWAR IQBAL DUGGAL)
JUDGE
ANNOUNCED IN OPEN COURT on 31.08.2026.
JUDGE.
APPROVED FOR REPORTNG.
JUDGE.
Nadeem *

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_014]
Case: Crl. Misc.-Post-arrest Bail 1313-B-26 (MUHAMMAD FAZIL VS STATE ETC ).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Crl. Misc.-Post-arrest Bail 1313-B-26. Title: Crl. Misc.-Post-arrest Bail 1313-B-26 (MUHAMMAD FAZIL VS STATE ETC ). Official Ref: 2026LHC5385. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Syed Farhad Ali Shah. Judgment Date (as displayed): 31-08-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5385.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 5 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Form No. HCJD/C-121
ORDER SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT
MULTAN BENCH MULTAN
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
Crl. Misc. No.1313-B of 2026
Muhammad Fazil. Vs. The State etc.
Sr. No. of
order /
proceeding s
Date of
order /
proceeding s
Order with signature s  of Judge, and that of  p arties o r
counsel, where necessary
