24.08.2026  Mr.  Muhammad  Usman  Sharif  Khosa,  Advocate  for
petitioner.
Mr. Muhammad Abdul Wadood, Additional Prosecutor
General with Muhammad Younas SHO.
Mr. Khizar Ali Khan Wadani, Advocate for complainant.
Muhammad Fazil (petitioner) seeks post arrest bail in
case FIR No.175 dated 17.04.2015, offences under sections
302, 324, 365-B, 496-A, 337-A(i), 337-F(i), 148, 149, 201
P.P.C. registered at Police Station Saddar, District, Dera
Ghazi Khan.
2.  Succinctly stated, the case of prosecution as unfolded
from the crime report is to the effect that on 17.04.2015
Muhammad  Sajjad  (complainant)  along  with  his  family
members was present at his home; that in the meanwhile, at
about 04:30 p.m., Muhammad Fazil (petitioner) along with 14
co-accused while riding 05 motorcycles and armed with their
respective weapons reached there and started indiscriminate
firing; that Muhammad Fazil (petitioner) exhorted to teach a
lesson to complainant party and fired a shot from his pistol
which hit Muhammad Riaz on his right shin; that thereafter
Muhammad  Ismail  (co-accused)  inflicted  six  successive
hatchet blows on the head and face of Muhammad Riaz; that
co-accused Hanif and Masso inflicted injuries to complainant
with their respective weapons and while leaving the place of
occurrence the accused forcibly abducted Mst. Zulaikha Bibi
Crl. Misc. No.1313-B of 2026
wife of sabir Hussain and took her to some unknown place,
hence, this case.
3.  Arguments heard. Record perused.
4.  It is discernible from the tentative review of record that
instant case is arising out of an incident having occurred on
17 . 04 .201 5  at about  04 :30  p .m. during which one  Muhammad
Riaz  lost his life aft er the receipt of  multiple  injuries, besides
that  complainant  also  endured  traumas  inflicted  by  the
assailants . The burden of committing this crime was pointed
towards a bunch of perpetrators comprising upon  1 5  persons
including  Muhammad  Fazil  (petitioner).
5.  It is informed by learned law officer that the petitioner
applied for his post arrest bail on merits before Honorable
Supreme Court of Pakistan, which was dismissed as withdrawn
vide  order  dated  29.04.2025  passed  in  Crl.  P.L.A.
No.259/2025. The grant of instant post arrest bail is solely
urged on the ground of delay in the conclusion of trial in terms
of third proviso of Section 497 Cr.P.C., thus I intend to restrict
myself only to this aspect. It is observed from the record that
admittedly the petitioner initially remained fugitive from the
process  of  law  and  was  apprehended  after  a  long  and
noticeable abscondance on 02.01.2024, whereas, the report
under section 173 Cr.P.C. to the extent of petitioner was
submitted  in  the  Court  on  07.05.2024.  In  pursuance  of
aforementioned police report, the petitioner was indicted by
the trial court on 24.06.2024 and subsequent thereto the case
was fixed for the recording of prosecution evidence. It is
noticed that though approximately a period of 02-years, 07-
months and 23-days has elapsed but till now not even the
statement of a single prosecution witness is recorded. I have
carefully scanned the report submitted by learned trial judge
and record of the case, from which it evinces that the pending
proceedings were delayed mainly due to the reasons attributed
Crl. Misc. No.1313-B of 2026
to the prosecution and complainant. According to record, the
file of the case was placed before the trial court for the
recording of prosecution evidence on 04.07.2024, 15.07.2024,
15.08.2024, 29.08.2024, 11.09.2024, 26.09.2024, 10.10.2024,
22.10.2024, 05.11.2024, 19.11.2024, 03.12.2024, 16.12.2024,
04.01.2025, 14.01.2025, 27.01.2025, 10.02.2025, 24.02.2025,
11.03.2025, 24.03.2025, 12.04.2025, 26.04.2025, 19.05.2025,
14.06.2025, 03.07.2025, 16.07.2025, 04.08.2025, 09.09.2025,
24.09.2025, 11.10.2025, 11.11.2025, 12.12.2025, 08.01.2026
and  09.02.2026.  Out  of  the  aforementioned  dates,  on
15.07.2024, accused was not produced from jail, whereas, on
15.08.2024,  29.08.2024,  11.09.2024  and  26.09.2024  the
presiding officer was not available. On 10.02.2025, 24.02.2025
and 11.03.2025 the case was adjourned on the joint request of
both the sides and except the aforesaid dates no adjournment
was sought on behalf of accused/petitioner.
6.  The 3
rd
proviso of section 497 Cr.P.C. unfolds that if the
trial of a person accused of having committed an offence
punishable with death is not concluded within two years of his
detention, he becomes entitled to the concession of post-arrest
bail. He can be held disentitled from the relief of post arrest
bail under 3
rd
proviso of section 497 Cr.P.C. only if it is
demonstrated that such delay has been caused or occasioned by
an act or omission of the accused seeking bail or by any other
person acting on his behalf. It manifests from the report of the
learned trial court and record that the delay in the conclusion
of trial can in no manner be attributed to the petitioner or any
person acting on his behalf. This is envisaged in the 4
th
proviso
that the benefit of 3
rd
proviso of section 497 Cr.P.C will not be
extended  to  a  person,  who  is  convicted  in  an  offence
punishable  with  death  or  imprisonment  for  life  or  is  a
hardened, desperate or dangerous criminal or a person, who is
involved  in  an  act of terrorism punishable  with death or
Crl. Misc. No.1313-B of 2026
imprisonment for life. Needless to mention here that in order to
bring the case of an accused within the 4
th
proviso of section
497  Cr.P.C.,  the  prosecution  has  to  display  its  cards  in
reference to some material. In the instant case, no such stance
is found present on the record, which may bring the case of the
petitioner  within  the  4
th
proviso  of  section  497  Cr.P.C.
Reliance in this regard is placed upon the case reported as
Noor Agha v. The state and another  (2025 SCMR 1679) ,
wherein the Honorable Supreme Court held as under:
“The 3rd proviso to subsection (1) of section 497 of the
Cr.P.C. governs the statutory right to claim release on bail
on  the  ground  of  delay  in  the  conclusion  of  trial.  The
legislature has explicitly used expression 'shall' in the 3rd
proviso and it creates a right in favour of an accused to be
released  on  bail  provided  the  conditions  stipulated  for
claiming such a right have been fulfilled. The 3rd proviso
sets out the conditions for being released on bail on the
ground  of  statutory  delay.  While  the  4th  proviso  has
described three distinct exceptions. Even if the conditions
specified in the 3rd proviso are met but the case falls in one
of the exceptions set out in the 4th proviso, the accused
would not be entitled to claim bail on statutory ground as of
right. The right would come into existence if the conditions
explicitly set out in the 3rd proviso have been met and the
exceptions in the 4th proviso are not attracted.
Further reliance is placed upon the  case reported as
Fayaz Khan v. Mohsin Shah and another  (2026 SCMR 1076) .
7.  In the given circumstances, the provisions of 3
rd
proviso
of Section 497 Cr.P.C. are inflexibly applicable in the instant
case and petitioner is entitled to the concession of post arrest
bail. While holding so, this Court is enlightened from the cases
reported as  Zahid Hussain Shah v. The State  (PLD 1995 SC
49)  and  Muhammad Iqbal v. The State  (1999 PCr.LJ 948)
Further  reliance  is  placed  upon  the  case  reported  as
Muhammad Usman v. The State and another,  (2024 SCMR
28) ,  wherein the  Honorable Supreme Court of Pakistan granted
bail to an accused on the ground of statutory delay in the
conclusion of trial with following observations:
“ On the touchstone of the principles highlighted herein
above, a right has accrued in favour of the petitioner
Crl. Misc. No.1313-B of 2026
under the third proviso of section 4 97(1) of the Cr.P.C., to
be released on bail because the conditions have been met
and the prosecution was not able to persuade us that the
delay  in  conclusion  of  trial  can  be  attributed  to  the
petitioner nor that the mischief contemplated under the
fourth  proviso is attracted.”
8.  The dismissal of instant post arrest bail is vociferously
urged by the learned Additional Prosecutor General on the
ground  that  noticeable  abscondance  is  attributed  to  the
petitioner. Suffice it to say in this regard that if the case of an
accused comes within the ambit of further inquiry, his previous
abscondance cannot be used as hurdle for the grant of post
arrest bail. Reliance in this regard is placed upon the case
reported as  Muhammad Aslam v. The State and others  (2016
SCMR 1520) , an extract of which is reproduced hereunder:
“ It  may  be  true  that  a  person  absconding  after  an
occurrence and declared as a Proclaimed Offender may lose
his claim to exercise of discretion in his favour by a court of
law on the basis of proprie ty but at the same time it is
equally true that an accused person the case against whom
calls for further inquiry is to be admitted to bail as a matter
of right. It goes without saying that whenever a question of
propriety is confronted with a question of  right the latter
must prevail.”
Further  reliance  is  place  upon  the  case  reported  as
Hidayat Khan v. The State and another  (2023 SCMR 172) .
9.  For what has been discussed above, instant petition is
allowed  and  petitioner  who  is  found  incarcerated  since
02.01.2024 and that too without any material progress in trial
is ordered to be released on bail subject to his furnishing bail
bonds in the sum of Rs.10,00,000/- with two sureties in the like
amount to the satisfaction of the learned trial court.
(SYED FARHAD ALI SHAH)
JUDGE
Approved for reporting.
JUDGE
Umer

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_015]
Case: Crl. Misc.-Pre-arrest Bail-Under Section 498 Cr.PC 2930-B-26 (SYED TARIQ HUSSAIN BUKHARI VS THE STATE ETC).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Crl. Misc.-Pre-arrest Bail-Under Section 498 Cr.PC 2930-B-26. Title: Crl. Misc.-Pre-arrest Bail-Under Section 498 Cr.PC 2930-B-26 (SYED TARIQ HUSSAIN BUKHARI VS THE STATE ETC). Official Ref: 2026LHC5381. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Muhammad Usman Ghani Rashid Cheema. Judgment Date (as displayed): 31-08-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5381.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 4 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Form No.HCJD/C - 121
ORDER SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT ,
BAHAWALPUR  BENCH,  BAHAWALPUR .
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
Crl.Misc.No. 2930 - B of  2026
Syed Tariq Hussain Bukhari  v ersus  The State , etc.
S.No.of order /
Proceeding
Date of  Order/
Proceeding
Order with  signature of Judge, and that of parties’
counsel, where necessary
20 . 08 . 2026  Mr.  Muhammad  Naeem  Bhatti , Advocate with  the
petitioner .
Mr. Javed Iqbal Bhayya, Assistant District Public
Prosecutor alongwith  Amanullah ,  S . I .
Through this  petition ,  under  s ection 498 Cr.P.C. ,
the  petitioner  seeks  pre - arrest  bail in case FIR No. 503/2026
dated  26 . 06 . 2026 , under  s ection s  341, 188  PPC  and Section
16 of the Punjab Maintenance of Public Order Ordinance,
1960  registered  at  Police  Station  Kotwali,  District
Bahawalpur .
2 .  Succinctly ,  the  facts  of  the  case  are  that  on
26.06.2026 at about 11:00 p.m., the petitioner/accused, in his
capacity as administrator, took out a procession of  Noha
Khawani  and  Matam Dari  in violation of the approved route,
thereby causing obstruction  on  the road a nd inconvenience to
the  general  public,  in  contravention  of  the  relevant
notification and applicable law. Hence, the instant  FIR  on
26.06.2026 .
3 .  Contentions  of the learned counsel for the petitioner
and  learned Assistant District Public Prosecutor have been
heard and record  of the case  has been  perused  with their able
assistance.
Crl.Misc.No. 2930 - B of 20 2 6  2
4 .  Although, it is a pre - arrest bail application  but the
Hon’ble Supreme Court of Pakistan has held, a number of
times,  whi le granting pre - arrest bail even merits of the case
can be touched upon , or looked at and discussed .  Reference
is made to  “Sajid Husain alias Joji v. The State”  ( PLD 2021
SC 898),  “ Javed Iqbal v. The State through Prosecutor
General Punjab and another”  ( 20 22 SCMR 1424)  and
“Muhammad Imran Nasrullah v. The State and others”
( 2022 SCMR 1946) .
5 .  FIR  and  the  material  collected  by  police  and
presented before this Court  reveals that  the  petitioner  is
nominated in  FIR  with  the allegation  of  violation of the
approved route while conducting the procession of  Noha
Khawani  and  Matam Dari  by compl ainant/Sajid Nazir, S.I.,
Police Station Samma Satta . However, a  copy of  Certificate
of Appreciation dated 09.07.2026  issued by the District
Police  Officer, Bahawalpur, h a s  been  submitted by the
petitioner ,  the  petitioner  ha s  been  appreciated  for  his
cooperation with the police and  especially  his efforts towards
maintaining peace and law  &  order during Muharram - ul -
Haram, particularly in connection wit h the peaceful conduct
of  Majalis, Noha Khawani  and  Matam Dari ,  were lauded .
Significantly, the said certificate was issued after the alleged
occurrence dated 26.06.2026. The subsequent appreciation of
the petitioner by the District Police Officer for his  role in
maintaining peace during the religious  proceedings  does not ,
prima facie ,  reconcile with the prosecution allegation that the
petitioner had acted in a manner causing obstruction and
disturbance  that resulted in public nuisance . This peculiar
circumstance  casts  serious  doubt  upon  the  prosecution
version and makes the culpability  of the petitioner a matter
requiring further probe, to be determined by the  learned  trial
Court after recording and evaluating the evidence.  Hence, i n
Crl.Misc.No. 2930 - B of 20 2 6  3
the facts and circumstances of this case, the possibility of
false implication  of the petitioner  cannot  be ruled out.
Reliance is  respectfully placed on the case of  “ Shahid Abbas
v. The State and others ” ( 2021 SCMR 2082 ) .
6 .  Learned  ADPP has  argued with vehemence that the
petitioner has  failed to point out any  mala fide  or ulterior
motive on the part of  complainant or the police party , which
is  sine qua non  for confirmation of pre - arrest bail. It  is  well
settled by now that it is not possible in each and every case to
prove the  mala fide  and  the  same can be  gleaned  from the
facts and circumstances of the  case.  I  am  fortif ied  by  the
dictum laid down  by the Hon’ble Supreme Court  of Pakistan
in case s  titled as  “ Khalil Ahmed Soomro v. The State”  (PLD
2017 SC 730)  and  “ Shahzada Qaiser Arfat  alias Qaiser v.
The State and another”  (PLD 2021 SC 708).
7 .  Another aspect  worth noticing  is  that out of the
offences  mentioned  in the FIR, Sections 341 and 188  of  PPC
are  bailable in  nature. Though the offence under Section 16
of the Punjab Maintenance of Public Order Ordinance, 1960
is non - bailable by virtue of Section 22  ibid ,  but  the maximum
punishment provided  for  is upto  three years’ imprisonment,
or fine, or both. Thus, the said  offence does not fall within the
prohibitory  clause  of  Section  497(1),  Cr.P.C.  This
circumstance,  when  considered  alongside  the  material
discussed above, further weighs in favour of the petitioner for
the purposes of bail.
8 .  Yet another  facet  of the nature of the offences
mentioned in the FIR , not immaterial for  decision  of this  bail
application ,  is  that  no recovery of any  sort  is to be effected
from  the  petitioner .  learned ADPP has also not been able to
substantiate as to what lawful purpose,  the intended arrest of
the  present  petitioner,  would  serve  in  furtherance  of
Crl.Misc.No. 2930 - B of 20 2 6  4
prosecution’s case.  T herefore, no useful purpose shall be
served by causing  the petitioner’s  arrest.  It is trite law that
denial of bail in such like cases will amount to  pre - tri al
punishment,  which  is not permissible.  Reference is made to
the Hon’ble Supreme Court in the case of  “ Naeem Qadir
Sheikh and another v. The State and others ” ( 2022 SCMR
2068 ) .
9 .  The  epitome  of  above  discussion  is  that  the
petitioner  has  succeeded  in  making  out  a  case  for
confirmation of pre - arrest bail, hence, this petition is  allowed
and ad - interim pre - arrest bail already granted to the  petitioner
vide  order dated  17 . 07 . 2026  is  confirmed ,  subject to  his
furnishing of fresh bail bonds in the sum of Rs. 1 00,000/ -
(Rupees  one  hundred thousand only) with one surety in the
like amount to the satisfaction of the learned trial court.
10.  Before parting, it is clarified that the observat ions
made hereinabove are tentative in nature and the Trial Court
shall be free to decide the case, on merits, without being
influenced  in  any  manner  from  the  same,  strictly  in
accordance with law .
( Muhammad  Usman Ghani Rashid Cheema )
Judge
Waqas*
Approved for reporting
Judge

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_016]
Case: First Appeal Against Order(F.A.O.) 244285/18 (Mr. Ghulam Ali Zahid Chaudhry etc. Vs. Damas Jewellary LLC).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: First Appeal Against Order. Title: First Appeal Against Order(F.A.O.) 244285/18 (Mr. Ghulam Ali Zahid Chaudhry etc. Vs. Damas Jewellary LLC). Official Ref: 2025LHC8701. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Raheel Kamran. Judgment Date (as displayed): 31-08-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2025LHC8701.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 6 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Stereo. H C J D A 38.
Judgment Sheet
LAHORE HIGH COURT LAHORE
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
F.A.O. No.244285 of 2018
Ghulam Ali Zahid Ch. And another
Versus
Damas Jewellary LLC
J U D G M E N T
Date of Hearing  13 .10.2025
For the  appellants  M/s Saqib Asghar and Mudassar Hassan, Advocates.
For the  respondent  Mr. Haseeb Zafar, Advocate.
Raheel Kamran J : - The instant First Appeal from Order
(FAO) has been filed under Order XLIII Rule 1 of the Code of Civil
Procedure, 1908  (CPC)  read with section 19 of the Intellectual Property
Organization of Pakistan Act, 2012  challenging the  vires  of the order dated
01.08.2018 passed by the Intellectual Property Tribunal, Lahore  (hereinafter
„the Tribunal‟) .
2 .  By the impugned order, the Tribunal dismissed the application
filed by the present appellants  (who were the defendants in the suit)  under Order
VII Rule 10 CPC, seeking the return of the plaint to the respondent-plaintiff
on the premise that the Tribunal lacked jurisdiction to adjudicate the dispute,
as the matter was governed by the exclusive jurisdiction clause (Clause 6) of a
prior  Settlement  Agreement  dated  28.09.2010  (hereinafter,  „Settlement
Agreement‟) .
3 .  Factual  matrix  of  the  matter  is  that  the  respondent  Damas
Jewellary LLC, instituted a suit against the appellants seeking declaration,
injunction and damages under section 117 of the Trademarks Ordinance, 2001
for the alleged infringement of their registered trademark  “DAMAS.” I t was the
case of the respondent  (hereinafter,  „plaintiff‟)  that the parties had a prior
F.A.O. No.244285 of 2018  2
commercial relationship which was amicably settled through a comprehensive
Settlement Agreement dated 28.09.2010. The agreement covered the use of the
mark, the winding up of a joint venture company (Damas Toomban (Pvt.)
Limited) and financial considerations. It was the stance of the plaintiff that the
clause 2 of the Settlement Agreement allowed the defendant No.1  (appellant
No.1 herein)  to use the  “ DAMAS ”  trademark only till the completion date,
however, following the  failure of defendant No.1 to meet the original terms,
the plaintiff granted an extension to the defendant No.1 for payment and
trademark use. It was claimed in the plaint that during the grace period, the
defendant No.1 struck off Damas Toomban (Pvt.) Limited on 27.07.2011 and
misused the permission and got the plaintiff‟s trademark registered in  his name
through concealment and misrepresentation. It was further stated by the plaintiff
that all obligations under the Settlement Agreement including the final payment
were  fully  and  finally  discharged  on  26.01.2017,  thereby  bringing  the
contractual relationship to an end; consequently, the right to the exclusive use
of the trademark  “Damas”  reverted to the plaintiff as its rightful owner. The
subsequent use of the trademark in Lahore by defendant No.1 constitutes a fresh
act of infringement of the  plaintiff‟s  statutory intellectual property rights, thus
necessitating the institution of the suit before the Intellectual Property Tribunal,
which is the court of exclusive jurisdiction under the relevant statute.
4 .  The  appellants-defendants,  in  their  written  statement  and
subsequent application under Order VII Rule 10 CPC, asserted that the dispute
regarding the use of the trademark was one of the subjects covered by the
Settlement Agreement. They contended that any controversy relating to the
mark‟s use must be resolved under Clause 6 of the said agreement, arguing that
the true  lis  is contractual, not statutory, thus demanding the return of the plaint.
The appellants further pleaded that they had registered the mark in their name
and that the terms of the settlement were never acted upon, or that the plaintiff
was estopped due to acquiescence.
5 .  The Tribunal dismissed the application under Order VII Rule 10
CPC by way of order dated 01.08.2018 by concluding that the dispute qua
alleged infringement under the Trademarks Ordinance, 2001 is within its
F.A.O. No.244285 of 2018  3
purview as the Settlement Agreement had reached its logical conclusion and
born fruit. The dismissal of their application has been assailed by the appellants
in this appeal.
6 .  Learned counsel for the appellants contended that the Tribunal
erred in dismissing the application. It was argued that the very subject matter of
the suit i.e. the right to use the trademark, is directly linked to and flows from
the Settlement Agreement dated 28.09.2010. Therefore, any dispute concerning
this subject matter is mandatorily covered by Clause 6 of the Settlement
Agreement, which governs the venue and mechanism for resolving contractual
disputes. Learned counsel emphasized that the appellants have specifically
pleaded in paragraph 10 of their written statement that the Agreement was
never acted upon and alternatively, that the respondent had acquiesced in the
a ppellants‟ continued use, thus demonstrating that the controversy necessarily
involves the interpretation and enforcement of the Settlement Agreement.
7 .  Conversely, learned counsel for the respondent fully supported the
reasoning adopted by the Tribunal, submitting that the Settlement Agreement
was a time-bound, executory contract, which has been fully performed, with all
obligations discharged, including the final payment made in January 2017.
Once an agreement is fully performed and the company formed under it is
wound  up,  the  jurisdiction  clause  relating  to  disputes  arising  from  its
performance or validity ceases to apply to fresh commercial torts. He added that
the present suit is based on a distinct statutory cause of action i.e. trademark
infringement.  Learned  counsel  maintained  that  the  Intellectual  Property
Tribunal, having been established under the law, is the court of exclusive and
specialized jurisdiction to try suits for infringement under the Trademarks
Ordinance, 2001.
8 .  Having considered the submissions of the learned counsel for the
parties  and  examined  the  record,  the  fundamental  question  requiring
determination is whether the  plaintiff‟s  suit qua infringement of the trademark
“Damas”  is a contractual dispute arising from the Settlement Agreement falling
within the jurisdiction of foreign courts, or a distinct cause of action under the
F.A.O. No.244285 of 2018  4
Trademarks Ordinance, 2001 falling squarely within the jurisdiction of the
Intellectual Property Tribunal.
9 .  The narrow inquiry at the stage of Order VII Rule 10 CPC is
jurisdiction, not the merits of the defence. For a plaint to be returned, the court
must find that it lacks the competence to try the suit as framed by the Plaintiff.
The plaintiff specifically asserted in paragraph No.23 of the plaint that final
payment in pursuance of Settlement Agreement had been made by the appellant
No.1  on  26.01.2017,  as  such  the  Settlement  Agreement  has  been  fully
performed. In their written statement, the appellants did not specifically deny
the  plaintiff‟s  assertion qua final payment in pursuance of the Settlement
Agreement on 26.01.2017, rather they claimed that the plaintiff being fully
aware of the use of trademark by the appellants acquiesced by not taking any
legal action against them from 2012 to 2017. This plea of acquiescence while
not specifically controverting the pl aintiff‟s assertion qua  full performance of
the Settlement Agreement reacts against the appellants. The jurisdiction clause
of  a  private  contract  is  meant  to  govern  disputes  concerning  the  terms,
implementation, interpretation or validity of that contract. Where a contract, by
its own terms, has been fully performed, its life as an executory instrument
terminates. The subsequent alleged unauthorized use of a trademark is not a
dispute over the breach of the completed contract; rather, it constitutes a new,
independent  tort  of  infringement,  which  is  governed  by  the  Trademarks
Ordinance, 2001. The Tribunal rightly observed that the dispute no longer
relates to the existence, validity, or termination of the Settlement Agreement,
but is a dispute simpliciter for the enforcement of statutory trademark rights. To
hold otherwise would imply that a jurisdiction clause in a settled contract
operates in perpetuity, governing every future commercial dispute between the
parties, regardless of the cause of action, which is an absurd proposition.
10 .  The  Intellectual  Property  Tribunal  is  a  court  of  specialized
jurisdiction, explicitly empowered under the Trademarks Ordinance, 2001 to
deal with infringement suits. The respondent ‟ s plaint, on its face, pleads a cause
of action for trademark infringement based on the a ppellants‟ conduct after
January 2017. This is a purely statutory claim. The a ppellants‟ attempt to use
F.A.O. No.244285 of 2018  5
the Settlement Agreement to divest the Tribunal of jurisdiction is misplaced
because the new commercial activity creates a separate, statutory cause of
action. Furthermore, the Tribunal derives its jurisdiction from a special statute.
Where a statutory right  is sought to be enforced, the special court constituted
for  that  purpose  maintains  jurisdiction,  notwithstanding  prior  contractual
dealings between the parties, unless the contract explicitly and validly bars
future statutory claims, which the Settlement Agreement does not appear to do.
11 .  Even assuming, arguendo, the claims of the appellants to the effect
that the agreement was never acted upon or that the respondent is estopped due
to  acquiescence,  are  matters  of  evidence  and  trial.  These  involve  mixed
questions of fact and law which require the Tribunal to record evidence and
interpret the parties ‟  conduct  vis-à-vis  the Settlement Agreement. The Tribunal
cannot, at the interlocutory stage while deciding application under Order VII
Rule 10 CPC, return the plaint merely because the defendant has raised
complex factual defences. The power to return a plaint is based only on the
ground that the court is not competent to entertain the suit, which is not the case
here.
12 .  The appellants raised several points which, while critical to the
ultimate outcome of the suit, are defences on  the merits and  cannot be
considered at the stage of deciding application under Order VII Rule 10 CPC.
The  a ppellants‟  assertion  that  they  have  registered  the  trademark ,  is  a
formidable defence against the infringement claim, as it raises a question of
concurrent rights or the validity of the r espondent‟s registration. However, the
Tribunal is perfectly competent to decide whether an infringement has occurred,
taking into account the a ppellants‟ claimed registration. The existence of a
strong defence does not automatically extinguish the Tribunal‟s jurisdiction to
hear the suit.
13 .  In view of the foregoing detailed discussion, this Court finds no
infirmity,  illegality  or  jurisdictional  error  in  the  impugned  order  dated
01.08.2018 passed by the Intellectual Property Tribunal, Lahore. Consequently,
the appeal stands dismissed. No order as to costs.
F.A.O. No.244285 of 2018  6
Before parting with this order, it has been noticed that the civil suit
from which instant appeal has arisen out, was filed in the year 2017. To prevent
further delay in decision of the matter, the Intellectual Property Tribunal is
directed to conclude the trial of the suit expeditiously, preferably within a
period of six months.
(RAHEEL KAMRAN)
JUDGE
Azhar*

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_017]
Case: Family 21804/20 (Dr. Rukhsana Kausar etc Vs Shahid Nazeer etc).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Family 21804/20. Title: Family 21804/20 (Dr. Rukhsana Kausar etc Vs Shahid Nazeer etc). Official Ref: 2026LHC5363. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Raheel Kamran. Judgment Date (as displayed): 31-08-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5363.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 18 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Stereo H C J D A 38.
Judgment Sheet
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT, LAHORE
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
Writ Petition No.21804 of 2020
Dr. Rukhsana Kausar  and another
Versus
Shahid Nazeer  and others
Date of hearing  15.06.2026
Petitioner  by :  M /s  Shaukat Umar  Pirzada  and Shujaat Umer
Pirzada , Advocate s .
Respondent  No. 1 by :  Mr. Ijaz Feroz Kashmiri, Advocate.
RAHEEL KAMRAN , J.:  This  single judgment shall govern the
titled petition as well as Writ Petition No. 26320 of 2020 (Shahid Nazir
versus Dr. Rukhsana  Kausar, etc), Writ Petition No. 23651 of 2026 (Shahid
Nazir versus Addl. District Judge, Muzaffargarh, etc.) and Civil Revision
No.23652 of 2026  (Shahid Naz ee r versus Fazal Hussain)  as all these matte r s
arise out of the same dispute and between  the same pa rties.
2 .  Facts  relevant for decision of the  controversy, precisely are that
on 23.12.2011  Dr. Rukhsana Kausar  (hereinafter to be referred  to  as  “the
petitioner” )  instituted  a suit  against  Shahid  Nazeer  (hereinafter to be
referred  to  as  “the respondent” )  for dissolution of marriage, recovery of
maintenance allowance for herself as well as her minor son namely Rohan
Shahid  (hereinafter to be referred  to  as  “the minor” ) , recovery of dowry
articles and delivery expenses.  The petitioner’s  case was that, durin g the
negotiations  preceding  the  marriage,  the  respondent  and  his  family
represented that a residential house and gold ornaments weighing 11 tolas
would be given to her as dower. According to the petitioner, the respondent
and his family lacked the financi al means to fulfil l  the said commitment,
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  2
whereupon, at their request and on the assurance that the amount would be
repaid, her parents financed the purchase of the residential house as well as
the 11 tolas of gold, although both were entered in the Nikahna ma as dower.
She further asserted that her parents also gave her dowry articles, including
gold ornaments weighing 22 tolas, and that, owing to the respondent ’ s
persistent neglect, failure to maintain her, and cruel conduct, she was
ultimately compelled to  leave the matrimonial home along with the minor
child and return to her parental house. Besides dissolution of marriage, she
sought recovery of maintenance, dowry articles, delivery expenses and the
gold ornaments, while also disputing the respondent ’ s en titlement to claim
restoration of the 11 tolas of gold or the house as  Badl - e - Khula .
The respondent contested the suit by denying the material
allegations. He maintained that the marriage was arranged by the elders of
the families and that the suit house  had been purchased by him from funds
arranged  through  his  brother  before  the  marriage  and  was  thereafter
transferred in the petitioner ’ s favour as dower. He denied having borrowed
any amount from the petitioner ’ s parents for purchase of either the house o r
the jewellery and asserted that the entries relating to maintenance in the
Nikahnama had been made by the petitioner herself. According to the
respondent, he regularly maintained the petitioner and bore the expenses
relating to the birth of the minor. He  further pleaded that the petitioner
voluntarily left the matrimonial home after taking with her all the gold
ornaments, including the 11 tolas entered in the Nikahnama as dower,
whereas only ordinary dowry articles remained in the house. He also
questione d the validity of the subsequent gift  ( Tamleek )  executed by the
petitioner in favour of her father in respect of the suit house. On these
premises, he prayed for dismissal of the suit.
3 .  During the pendency of the suit, the Judge Family Court, by
order d ated 28.04.2012, dissolved the marriage between the parties by way
of  Khula  and observed that the petitioner was obliged to return the dower
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  3
received by her as  Badl - e - Khula , while keeping the remaining controversies
pending for adjudication. Consequently,  following  issues were framed :
ISSUES .
1.  Whether the plaintiffs are entitled for recovery of maintenance
allowance, if so for which quantum and for which period? OPP
2.  Whether the plaintiff No.1 is entitled for recovery of dowry articles
as per list annexed w ith the plaint? OPP
3.  Whether the suit of the plaintiffs is liable to be dismissed? OPD
4.  Whether the defendant has paid/given Haq Mehar as mentioned in
column No.14, 15 and 16 of Nikahnama? OPD
5.  Whether the defendant is entitled for recovery of Haq Mehar as
Za r - e - Khula? OPD
6.  Whether the plaintiff No.1 has not received dower as mentioned in
column No.14, 15 and 16 of Nikahnama? OPP
7.  Whether the plaintiff have not come into the court with clean
hands? OPD
8.  Whether the plaintiffs have no cause of action? OPD
9.  Relief.
ADDITIONAL ISSUE NO.2A .
Whether the plaintiff No.1 is entitled for recovery of Rs.63,000/ -  as
delivery expenses from the defendant? OPP
After recording the evidence of the parties, the Family Court, through
judgment and decree dated 31.10.2018, partly decr eed the suit by awarding
maintenance to the petitioner for the period of  iddat  and to the minor son,
directing recovery of dowry articles (excluding the gold ornaments) and
requiring the petitioner to return 11 tolas  of gold or its value to the
respondent as  Badl - e - Khula . The Family Court, however, declined the
respondent ’ s claim for restoration of the suit house, holding that the same
had not been proved.
Feeling  aggrieved,  both  parties  preferred  separate  appeals
before  the  Additional  District  Judge.  By  judgment  and  decree  dated
20.02.2020, both  appeals  were substantially dismissed, except that the
annual enhancement in the maintenance  allowance  awarded to the minor
was reduced from 15% to 10%. Still dissatisfied,  the petitioner has instituted
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  4
Writ  Petition  No.21804  of  2020  challenging  the  concurrent  findings
whereby her claims relating to the gold ornaments, delivery expenses and
certain ancillary reliefs were declined and she was directed to return 11 tolas
of gol d or its value as  Badl - e - Khula . Conversely, the respondent has filed
Writ Petition No.26320 of 2020 questioning,  inter alia , the findings relating
to the suit house, maintenance and dowry articles.
4 .  The controversy relating to the suit house did not end with the
family litigation. After executing the Tamleek deed in favour of her father
Fazal Hussain Asim, the petitioner gave rise to two independent civil
proceedings  before  the  Civil  Court  at  Muzaffar garh.  The  respondent
instituted a suit seeking declaration and cancellation of the Tamleek deed on
the ground that the transfer was void and ineffective in law, whereas
Fazal Hussain Asim instituted a separate suit seeking possession of the suit
house and  mesne profits on the strength of the said Tamleek. Since both
suits involved common questions relating to the title and possession of the
suit house, they were consolidated for trial. However, in view of the
pendency of  the  family litigation, both  suits  w ere  adjourned  sine  die
awaiting the final determination of the issues arising therein. After the
Family Court finally decided the controversy, the civil proceedings were
revived. Ultimately, the suit instituted by the respondent for cancellation of
the  Tam leek  deed  was  dismissed ,  whereas  the  suit  filed  by
Fazal Hussain Asim was decreed and the respondent was directed to deliver
possession of the suit house. Appeals preferred against both decisions were
dismissed by the Additional District Judge, Muzaffarga rh. The respondent
has assailed those concurrent decisions through Writ Petition No.23651 of
2026 and Civil Revision No.23652 of 2026.
Although  the  aforesaid  proceedings  originated  within  the
territorial jurisdiction of the Multan Bench of this Court, bo th matters were
ordered to be heard at the Principal Seat as they arise directly out of the
same  matrimonial  dispute,  involve  substantially  overlapping  questions
concerning the ownership and legal character of the suit house, and their
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  5
adjudication is depe ndent, to  a considerable extent, upon  the findings
recorded in the family litigation. To avoid the possibility of conflicting
judicial determinations and in the interest of consistency, all the above
matters have, therefore, been heard together and are bei ng disposed of by
this common judgment .
5 .  Learned  counsel  for  the  petitioner  assailed  the  impugned
judgments and decrees on the ground that they suffer from misreading and
non - reading of material evidence, resulting in a manifest miscarriage of
justice.  He submitted that the respondent had failed to discharge the burden
of proving that the dower entered in the Nikahnama, particularly the suit
house and the 11 tolas of gold ornaments, had been financed or paid by him
from his own resources. According to th e learned counsel, the evidence
produced by the petitioner unequivocally established that both the suit house
and the 11 tolas of gold had been purchased from funds provided by the
petitioner ’ s father at the request of the respondent and his family, and  once
the Courts below accepted that position in relation to the suit house, they
committed a patent inconsistency in declining to extend the same conclusion
to the gold ornaments despite the absence of any evidence demonstrating the
respondent ’ s financial  capacity.  Learned counsel further contended that the
concurrent findings whereby the petitioner was denied recovery of the 22
tolas of gold ornaments given to her by her parents as dowry are likewise
unsustainable, as the evidence on record sufficiently es tablished that the said
ornaments remained in the respondent ’ s possession. It was argued that the
Family Court and the Appellate Court adopted an erroneous approach in
appreciating the evidence and ignored material circumstances favourable to
the petitione r ’ s case. He also challenged the dismissal of the petitioner ’ s
claim  for  delivery  expenses  and  assailed  the  reduction  made  by  the
Appellate Court in the annual enhancement of maintenance awarded to the
minor.
Insofar as the connected civil proceedings are  concerned, learned
counsel submitted that once the petitioner had validly transferred the suit
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  6
house to her father by way of  Tamleek , the respondent ceased to have any
subsisting right, title or interest therein. Consequently, the suit instituted by
the r espondent for cancellation of the  Tamleek  deed was rightly rejected,
whereas the suit filed by the petitioner ’ s father for possession was rightly
decreed. It was, therefore, argued that the impugned judgments passed in the
connected civil proceedings call  for no interference.  On these premises,
learned counsel prayed that Writ Petition No.21804 of 2020 be allowed,
whereas Writ Petition No.26320 of 2020, Writ Petition No.23651 of 2026
and Civil Revision No.23652 of 2026 be dismissed.
6 .  Conversely, learned  counsel for the respondent  submitted  that
the petitioner had failed to substantiate her plea that the suit house and the
11 tolas of gold entered in the Nikahnama  had been financed by her father.
According to him, the evidence produced by the petitioner was insufficient
to  displace  the  presumption  arising  from  the  entries  recorded  in  the
Nikahnama, whereas the respondent had satisfactorily established that the
suit  house formed part of the dower and that the petitioner was consequently
obliged to restore the dower received by her as  Badl - e - Khula . He further
submitted that the petitioner also failed to prove that the 22 tolas of gold
ornaments allegedly gifted by her  parents remained in the respondent ’ s
possession and, therefore, the concurrent findings declining recovery thereof
call  for  no  interference.  Learned  counsel  further  submitted  that  the
respondent ’ s own challenge to the impugned judgments, insofar as they
r elate to the suit house, the award of maintenance in favour of the minor and
other reliefs granted to the petitioner, is equally well - founded and deserves
acceptance.
I n relation to the connected civil proceedings, learned counsel argued
that the Tamleek  deed executed by the petitioner in favour of her father did
not confer any valid title, as she herself had acquired no lawful ownership in
the suit house. He further contended that, in any event, the alleged gift was
legally defective for want of delivery  of possession, which, according to the
learned counsel, constitutes an essential ingredient of a valid gift under the
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  7
applicable  principles  of  law.  Consequently,  it  was  submitted  that  the
respondent ’ s suit seeking declaration and cancellation of the Tamlee k deed
was maintainable, whereas the suit instituted by the petitioner ’ s father for
possession was liable to dismissal. He, therefore, prayed that Writ Petition
No.21804 of 2020 be dismissed, whereas Writ Petition No.26320 of 2020,
Writ Petition No.23651 o f 2026 and Civil Revision No.23652 of 2026 be
allowed.
7 .  Having heard the learned counsel for the parties and gone
through the record, it has been noticed that t he concurrent findings recorded
by the Courts below regarding maintenance do not call for  interference. The
evidence  on  record  unmistakably  establishes  that  the  respondent  is  a
professionally qualified engineer who, after initially serving in the private
sector, joined the  Government Service  and was drawing a substantial salary.
The Family Cour t rightly observed that despite being in possession of the
best evidence, the respondent withheld his complete salary record and
produced only a certificate reflecting his basic pay, thereby entitling the
Court to draw an adverse inference while assessing  his actual financial
capacity. The maintenance awarded to petitioner No.1 was confined only to
the period during which she remained legally entitled thereto, whereas
maintenance of the minor was determined keeping in view his growing
educational  and  other  day - to - day  needs.  The  Appellate  Court  further
moderated  the  respondent’s  financial  burden  by  reducing  the  annual
enhancement from 15% to 10%.  This Court finds  the quantum so determined
to be fair, reasonable and commensurate with the circumstances brought  on
record.
8 .  It has been noted that qua his financial capacity, the respondent
took inconsistent stance. While asserting his claim over the suit house, he
maintained that he had sufficient financial resources to purchase a house
worth Rs.19,50,000/- in the year 2006, claiming that although part of the
sale consideration had initially been arranged through financial assistance
from his brother, the same was repaid by him within a period of two to three
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  8
years.  During  his  cross-examination,  he  also  asserted  that  he  himself
possessed sufficient funds to purchase the said property. Likewise, he
claimed to have purchased 11 tolas of gold for the petitioner at the time of
marriage. During cross-examination, he also referred to sources of income
apart from his salary, including investments in business. Yet, when called
upon to discharge his legal obligation of maintaining his own child, he
projected himself as a person of meagre means. The inconsistency becomes
even more apparent from the testimony of DW-3, the respondent ’ s own
brother, who stated during cross-examination that the respondent owned a
Honda Civic car. These circumstances, when viewed cumulatively, lend
support to the conclusion reached by the Courts below that the respondent ’ s
plea regarding his limited financial capacity did not inspire confidence. It
scarcely needs reiteration that maintenance of a minor child is not merely a
statutory liability but also a legal, moral and social obligation of the father.
The law does not countenance an attempt by a financially capable parent to
understate his means with a view to reducing or avoiding his obligation
towards  a  child  who,  owing  to  the  breakdown  of  the  matrimonial
relationship, is being brought up by the other parent. Such pleas, if accepted
without careful scrutiny, would defeat the very object of the beneficial
provisions governing maintenance and would expose minor children to
financial hardship for no fault of their own. The Courts are, therefore,
required to examine such assertions in the light of the entire evidence rather
than the income figures selectively projected by the obligor. Learned counsel
for the respondent failed to point out any legal infirmity, misreading or non-
reading of evidence or jurisdictional defect in the concurrent findings
whereby maintenance has been awarded in favour of the petitioners.
9 .  Adverting  now  to  the  petitioner ’ s  claim  for  recovery  of
maternity  expenses  amounting  to  Rs.63,000/-,  which  too  has  been
concurrently declined by the Courts below. Having examined the findings
recorded by the Courts below on the said issue, no valid reason is found for
taking a different view. The petitioner ’ s case was that the delivery expenses
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  9
were initially borne by her parents and that at the respondent ’ s request, the
receipts were prepared in his name on the representation that he intended to
seek reimbursement from his employer and thereafter repay the amount to
them. The respondent, however, categorically denied the said assertion.
Admittedly, the receipts pertaining to the delivery expenses stood in the
respondent ’ s name. In the absence of any documentary evidence showing
payment by the petitioner ’ s parents or any independent evidence, such as the
testimony of the concerned medical practitioner, hospital official or any
other  person  associated  with  the  medical  facility  to  substantiate  the
explanation furnished by the petitioner regarding issuance of the receipts in
the respondent ’ s name, the Family Court rightly concluded that the claim
had remained unsubstantiated. The Appellate Court, upon reappraisal of the
record, affirmed the said finding. The concurrent conclusion is based upon a
proper appreciation of the evidence available on record and suffers from
neither misreading nor non-reading of evidence so as to warrant interference
by this Court.
10 .  Turning now to the controversy relating to the suit house, no
legal infirmity is found in the concurrent findings recorded by the Courts
below. The principal question before the Family Court was whether the
respondent had in fact paid the sale consideration of the house, which
admittedly stood transferred in the name of petitioner No.1 prior to the
solemnization of marriage, or whether the entire purchase price and the
ancillary transfer expenses had been borne by the petitioner ’ s father, as
asserted by the petitioners. Upon an exhaustive appraisal of the oral as well
as documentary evidence, the Family Court returned a categorical finding
that the respondent had failed to establish that the property had been
acquired from his own resources, whereas the petitioner had successfully
proved that the entire consideration for the purchase of the house, together
with the transfer expenses, had been provided by the petitioner ’ s father. The
Appellate Court independently reappraised the evidence and affirmed the
said conclusion.
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  10
11 .  The record reveals that this finding does not rest merely upon
oral assertions. Rather, the petitioner succeeded in establishing a coherent
and unbroken chain of evidence tracing the source and movement of the
funds utilized for the purchase of the property. The evidence showed that the
petitioner ’ s father, who was serving abroad at the relevant time, arranged the
requisite funds through his foreign currency account. Those funds were
converted  into  Pakistani  currency,  whereafter,  through  his  authorised
representative, a demand draft was obtained and deposited into the bank
account  of  the  respondent ’ s  brother,  from  whose  account  the  sale
consideration was ultimately transmitted to the vendor. The said transactions
were substantiated not only through the testimony of the person who had
acted  on  behalf  of  the  petitioner ’ s  father  in  arranging  the  financial
transaction, but also by the relevant banking officials (PW-4, PW-5 and
PW-6), who produced and verified the corresponding banking record before
the Family Court. Significantly, material aspects of this chain of evidence
remained substantially unchallenged during cross-examination. The Family
Court also noticed that the respondent could not furnish any satisfactory
explanation as to why the petitioner ’ s representative had participated in the
registration process and executed the necessary documents on behalf of
petitioner No.1 if, in fact, the respondent himself had financed the entire
transaction. Conversely, the respondent ’ s plea that the property had been
purchased from his own resources, or through financial assistance obtained
from his brother, did not withstand judicial scrutiny. The evidence produced
in support of that plea was found to be internally inconsistent and deficient
in material particulars, particularly regarding the alleged source of funds, the
financial capacity of the respondent and his brother to make such payment,
and the manner in which the alleged financial arrangement had been made.
No documentary evidence was produced to substantiate the respondent ’ s
assertion that the purchase price had been arranged by him or on his behalf.
Both the Courts below have discussed these aspects of the evidence in
considerable  detail  and  assigned  cogent  reasons  for  preferring  the
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  11
petitioner ’s  version over that of the respondent. Learned counsel for the
respondent has been unable to point out any instance of misreading or non-
reading  of  evidence,  much  less  any  perversity,  which  may  justify
interference with the concurrent findings of fact recorded by the Courts
below in exercise of constitutional jurisdiction. The conclusion that the
house had not been provided by the respondent as paid dower, and that no
occasion therefore arose to direct its restoration as  Badl-e-Khula , is thus
fully borne out by the record.
12 .  As regards the objection raised by the respondent pertaining to
the validity of the Tamleek executed by petitioner in favour of her father, it
is an admitted position that at the time when the Tamleek was executed, the
suit house stood validly transferred and registered in the name of petitioner,
who was the registered and lawful owner thereof. There existed no statutory
or legal impediment restraining her from transferring the property in favour
of  any  person  of  her  choice,  including  her  father,  through  a  legally
recognized mode of transfer. The respondent has, however, argued that the
Tamleek was ineffective for want of delivery of possession, contending that
actual possession of the house had never been handed over to the petitioner’s
father. The argument overlooks the peculiar factual matrix of the case. The
petitioner was admittedly the owner of the property and, by virtue of the
subsisting marital relationship, continued to reside in the suit house as the
legally wedded wife of the respondent. Her possession, though shared with
or enjoyed through the marital relationship, was nonetheless lawful and
referable to her title. In such circumstances, the absence of a formal physical
change  of  possession  does  not,  by  itself,  invalidate  the  transfer.  The
petitioner’s possession was juridical and lawful, though referable to the
marital  relationship  and  in  the  circumstances  amounted  to  sufficient
dominion over the property for the purpose of the transfer, which continued
till  the  matrimonial  relationship  deteriorated.  Therefore,  when  the
transaction  is  appreciated  in  the  backdrop  of  the  attending  facts  and
circumstances, the respondent’s objection regarding want of actual delivery
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  12
of possession does not advance his case. No illegality or jurisdictional defect
is found in the concurrent findings whereby the Courts below upheld the
petitioner ’ s title in the suit house and the subsequent transfer thereof in
favour of her father.
13 .  The findings recorded hereinabove conclusively determine the
challenge laid to the decree passed by the Civil Court in favour of the
petitioner ’ s father for poss ession of the suit property .  Once it has been found
that the petitioner was the lawful owner of the suit house and that the
subsequent Tamleek in favour of her father was legally valid, the latter ’ s
entitlement to seek possession from the respondent, who c eased to have any
lawful  claim  over  the  property,  necessarily  follows.  The  decree  for
possession, being a natural consequence of the lawful transfer of title and the
findings concurrently recorded by the Courts below, suffers from no legal
infirmity warran ting interference.
14 .  Coming now to the controversy relating to the gold ornaments,
b efore adverting to the rival claims, it would be advantageous to notice that
although, by order dated 28.04.2012, the Judge Family Court dissolved the
marriage between the parties by way of  khula  and observed that  “ Plaintiff
No.1 has an obligation to retur n the dower, received at the eve of marriage
as Badl - e - Khula ” , the remaining disputes between the parties were kept
pending for adjudication. Consequently, while proceeding with the suit, the
Family Court framed,  inter alia , Issues No.4, 5 and 6 specifical ly relating to
payment  of dower, entitlement  of  the respondent  to  its  restoration as
Badl - e - Khula  and the petitioner ’ s plea that the dower mentioned in the
Nikahnama  had, in fact, never been paid. The onus to prove payment of
dower was placed upon the respondent, whereas the petitioner was required
to establish her plea that she had not received the dower mentioned in
columns No.14 to 16 of the  Nikahnama . This course  adopted by the Family
Court was consistent with the law as it then stood, since subsections (5) and
(6) were inserted in Section 10 of the Family Courts Act, 1964 only
subsequently  (through  the Punjab Family Courts (Amendment) Act 2015)
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  13
and were not retrospectively in operation. Therefore, the contention of the
learned  counsel  for  the  respondent  that  after  passing  the  decree  for
dissolution of marriage the Family Court had become  functus officio  and
lacked jurisdiction to determine the controversy regarding dower, is without
substance.
15 .  There is no dispute between the parties that the petitioner
received gold ornaments weighing approximately 22 tolas from her parents
at the time of marriage. Likewise, it is an admitted position that 11 tolas of
gold were entered in the Nikahnama as dower and that the said ornaments
were delivered to the petitioner. The principal controversy relating to the 11
tolas of gold entered in the Nikahnama, therefore, is to the source from
which it was purchased. From the very inception of the litigation, the
petitioner ’ s consistent case has been that the respondent lacked the financial
means to provide either the suit house or the gold mentioned as dower and
that, at the request of the respondent and his family, the petitioner ’ s father
arranged the necessary funds for both. According to the petitioner, the house
and the 11 tolas of gold were entered in the Nikahnama as dower merely to
preserve the honour and social standing of her father within the family and
the brotherhood, although the respondent had himself contributed nothing
towards their purchase. This plea was reiterated not only by the petitioner
herself but also by PW-1, PW-2 and PW-3, whose evidence remained
materially consistent throughout. It is true that, unlike the purchase of the
suit house, no documentary evidence or banking record was produced
regarding the payment made for the purchase of the 11 tolas of gold.
Nevertheless, absence of documentary evidence is not by itself conclusive in
civil proceedings where the rights of the parties are to be determined on the
touchstone of balance of probabilities. The Court is required to appreciate
the entire evidence cumulatively rather than in isolation. In the case in hand,
the respondent completely failed to establish through any documentary or
reliable oral evidence that he possessed the financial capacity to purchase 11
tolas of gold at the relevant time. The evidence available on the record
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  14
sufficiently demonstrates the meagre financial resources of the respondent
and his family and the improbability of their having independently financed
both the house and the jewellery. An aspect of considerable significance is
the suggestion put by the  respondent’s counsel  itself during the cross-
examination of PW-3, the petitioner ’ s father, that:
“ یہ درست ہے کہ میں نے مدعا علیہ کو جو رقم ادھار دی اس کا لین دار ہوں۔ ”
By  putting  such  suggestion,  the  defence  itself  acknowledged  the
advancement of money by PW-3 to the respondent, the only endeavour
being  to  treat  the  transaction  as  a  loan.  This  circumstance  materially
corroborates the petitioner ’ s consistent plea that the funds utilized for
purchase  of  the  jewellery  had  in  fact  been  provided  by  her  father.
Significantly, apart from a bare denial, the respondent neither produced any
evidence  regarding  availability  of  his  own  funds  nor  furnished  any
satisfactory explanation regarding the source from which the purchase price
of the jewellery was arranged.
16 .  When  the  entire  evidence  is  appreciated  cumulatively,  the
consistent testimony of the petitioner and her witnesses, the established
financial  incapacity  of  the  respondent,  the  absence  of  any  evidence
demonstrating availability of funds with the respondent, and the above
admission emerging from the cross-examination of PW-3 collectively render
the petitioner ’ s version more probable. Therefore, like the suit house, the
amount utilized for purchase of the 11 tolas of gold had also been provided
by the petitioner ’ s father. Consequently, the respondent cannot claim return
of the said ornaments as  Badl-e-Khula , for the simple reason that they did
not represent dower financed or paid by him from his own resources.
17 .  It is well s ettled that this Court while exercising constitutional
jurisdiction  under Article 199  of the Constitution, does  not  ordinarily
interfere with concurrent findings of fact  recorded by the courts below
merely because another view of the evidence is possible. Such restraint,
however, is not an inflexible rule. Where the findings suffer from misreading
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  15
or non-reading of material evidence, omission to consider circumstances
having  a  decisive  bearing  upon  the  controversy,  or  are  otherwise
unsupported by the record, the constitutional jurisdiction of this Court may
legitimately be invoked to prevent miscarriage of justice. In the case in hand,
the error committed by the Courts below lies not in their appreciation of any
individual piece of evidence but in their failure to evaluate the entire
evidentiary material in its cumulative effect. By treating the absence of
documentary proof regarding payment of Rs.150,000/- as determinative,
while  overlooking  other  material  circumstances  which  substantially
corroborated the petitioner ’ s case, the Courts below arrived at a conclusion
which is not borne out by the record as a whole. The concurrent findings
relating to the 11 tolas of gold, therefore, cannot sustain.
18 .  T he  above  conclusion,  however,  determines  only  the
controversy relating to the source of funds utili z ed for the purchase of the 11
tolas of gold and, consequently, the respondent ’ s entitlement to claim its
restoration as  Badl - e - Khula . A distinct question  re mains  regarding the
possession of the gold ornaments, particularly the petitioner ’ s claim for
recovery thereof from the respondent. On this aspect,  this Court  find s  no
sufficient ground to differ from the concurrent findings recorded by the
Courts below.  T he petitioner ’ s consistent case was that when she finally left
the matrimonial home, she departed merely in her wearing apparel, leaving
behind not only the 11 tolas of gold entered in the Nikahnama but also the
remaining 22 tolas of gold claimed to have b een given to her by her parents
at the time of marriage. It was, however, never her case that the respondent
or any member of his family had forcibly removed the jewellery from her
person. The record further reveals material discrepancies regarding the
cir cumstances in which she finally left the matrimonial home. While the
petitioner maintained that she left permanently in July 2011, some of her
own witnesses stated during cross - examination that she continued residing
with  the  respondent  until  September/Oct ober  2011.  Although  this
discrepancy may not, by itself, be decisive, it does impair the reliability of
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  16
her version regarding the circumstances in which she allegedly left without
the jewellery.  Equally significant is the fact that the petitioner never ple aded
nor did the evidence disclose that her final departure from the matrimonial
home took place amidst any sudden or extraordinary circumstances which
could reasonably explain her leaving behind ornaments of such considerable
value. It was not her case th at immediately before her departure any
altercation or violent incident had occurred, that she had been physically
assaulted or forcibly expelled from the house, or that the respondent or any
member of his family had prevented her from taking her personal  belongings
or had snatched the jewellery from her possession. On the contrary, her own
case merely was that she left the matrimonial home along with her minor son
wearing the clothes she had on. In the ordinary course of human conduct,
where no such compel ling or coercive circumstances are shown to exist, it
becomes  difficult  to  accept,  on  the  touchstone  of  preponderance  of
probabilities, that a person would voluntarily leave behind valuable gold
ornaments  constituting  her  personal  property  without  making  a ny
contemporaneous  attempt  to  retrieve  them  or  without  alleging  any
impediment which prevented her from doing so. The absence of any such
surrounding circumstances, therefore, constitutes an important factor while
assessing the probability of the petitione r ’ s version.  Furthermore,  greater
significance  attaches  to  the  evidence  relating  to  the  marriage  of  the
petitioner ’ s  sister  which  was  solemnized  after  the  petitioner  left  the
matrimonial home  for the last time . The petitioner ’ s father  (PW - 3)  admitted
during cross - examination that the petitioner attended the marriage wearing
jewellery. Likewise, when the petitioner herself was specifically asked
whether  she  wished  to  produce  the  video  recording  of  the  marriage
ceremony, she declined to do so.  A suggestion was then put to her that the
reason for withholding the video was that it would depict her wearing the
disputed jewellery. Had the petitioner not been wearing the ornaments
claimed in the present proceedings, the production of the video would  have
furnished the best available evidence to dispel the controversy. Her refusal
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  17
to produce such evidence entitled the Family Court to draw an adverse
inference in accordance with the settled principles governing appreciation of
evidence.
19 .  When the evidence in the case in hand is examined on the
touchstone of preponderance of probabilities, the concurrent findings of the
Courts below that the petitioner failed to establish that the disputed jewellery
remained in the respondent ’ s possession do not suffer from any misreading
or non-reading of evidence, nor has any jurisdictional infirmity been pointed
out  warranting  interference  in  exercise  of  constitutional  jurisdiction.
Accordingly, while the findings relating to the source of funds for purchase
of the 11 tolas of gold are liable to be modified for the reasons already
recorded, the concurrent findings relating to possession of the jewellery call
for no interference.
20 .  For the  foregoing reasons,  Writ Petition No.21804 of 2020
succeeds partly and is accordingly allowed to the extent that the findings
recorded by the Family Court and affirmed by the Appellate Court directing
the petitioner to  return 11  tolas of gold/jewellery or its  value to the
res pondent as  Badl - e - Khula  are set aside  and  the respondent is  held  not
entitled to claim restoration of the said 11 tolas of gold or its value as
consideration of Khula. The remaining findings recorded in the impugned
judgments  and  decrees  are  maintained.  T h e  judgments  and  decrees
impugned in Writ Petition No.21804 of 2020 stand modified  accordingly .
Decree sheet be drawn.  Writ Petition No.26320 of 2020  filed by the
respondent  is dismissed.
21 .  The controversy involved in Writ Petition No.23651 of 2026
and Civil Revision No.23652 of 2026 substantially emanates from the
respondent ’ s challenge to the petitioner ’ s title in the suit house and the
validity of the Tamleek executed in favour of her father. Those very
questions  have  been  examined  hereinabove  and  answered  against  the
respondent. Once it has been held that the petitioner validly acquired title in
W.P . No.21804 of 2020  18
the suit house, that the property had not been provided by the respondent as
paid dower, and that the subsequent transfer in favour of Fazal Hussain
Asim was legally valid, the very foundation of the respondent ’ s challenge to
the rejection of his suit as well as to the decree passed in favour of Fazal
Hussain Asim for possession ceases to survive. The findings recorded by the
Civil Court and affirmed by the Appellate Court are fully consistent with the
conclusions  reached  herein  and  disclose  no  legal  infirmity  warranting
interference.  Consequently,  Writ  Petition  No.23651  of 2026  and  Civil
Revision No.23652 of 2026 are also dismissed. The judgments, decrees and
orders impugned therein are affirmed. There shall, however, be no order as
to costs.
( RAHEEL KAMRAN )
JUDGE
Announced in the Open Court on 31.08.2026.
JUDGE
Approved for reporting.
JUDGE
Azhar*

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_018]
Case: Crl. Misc.-Post-arrest Bail 5377-B-26 (RIAZ HUSSAIN VS STATE & 1 OTHER ).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Crl. Misc.-Post-arrest Bail 5377-B-26. Title: Crl. Misc.-Post-arrest Bail 5377-B-26 (RIAZ HUSSAIN VS STATE & 1 OTHER ). Official Ref: 2026LHC5349. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Tanveer Ahmad Sheikh. Judgment Date (as displayed): 28-08-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5349.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 7 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Form No: HCJD/C - 121
ORDER SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT,
MULTAN  BENCH,  MULTAN
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
Cr iminal  Misc ellaneous  No.  5377 - B of 202 6
Riaz Hussain .  Versus  The State and  another .
S.No. of
order/
proceeding.
Date of order/
proceeding.
Order with signatures of Judge, and that of parties
or counsel, where necessary.
20 . 0 8 .202 6 .  Sardar  Muhammad  Akhtar  Khan
Leghari ,  Advocate  for  the petitioner .
Mr. Jamshaid  Safdar, Advocate for the
complainant.
Malik  Ashfaq  Ahmad,  Deputy
Prosecutor General with  Khalid , S.I.
The  petitioner  ( Riaz  Hussain ),  being
arrayed as an accused in case F.I.R. No. 1101
of  2025,  dated  15.11.2025,  registered  with
Police  Station  Saddar  Shujaabad,  District
Multan, for offence under Section 322 of PPC,
seeks his post arrest bail, after the same was
refused  to  him  by  the  court  of  learned
Magistrate Section 30, Shujaabad and then by
the  court  of  learned  Additional  Sessions
Judge,  Shujaabad,  vide  order  dated
24.06.2026.
2.  According  to  prosecution  case as set
forth in the F.I.R. sons of Muhammad Shareef
(complainant) namely Muhammad Abu Bakar
and Ali Hamza were proceeding on motorcycle
bearing registration No.3937-MNX along with
their  cousin  namely  Yasir  and  a  friend
Muhammad  Amjad.  Marriage  of  said  Yasir
was  scheduled  for  the  very  next  day.  They
Crl. Misc. No. 5377-B of 2026  2
were going on their left at Adda Nasir Mor at
about  07.00  p.m.  that  a  bus  bearing
registration  No.6478-DGI  coming  negligently
and  rashly  on  wrong  side  collided  with  the
motorcycle. All the four persons succumbed to
the injuries at the very spot.
3.  Learned  counsel  for  petitioner  ‘ while
canvassing for the bail ’  mainly focused on the
points that petitioner was not nominated in
the F.I.R. registered  on 15.11.2025; he was
introduced through supplementary statement
got recorded by complainant on 23.12.2025,
as such said infirmity alone was sufficient to
bring  the  case  with  the  purview  of  ‘further
inquiry’;  offence  under  Section  322  of  PPC
was not attracted to the case; from the bear
perusal  of  the  contents  of  FIR,  the  offence
under  Section  320  of  PPC  was  made  out,
which  was  bailable;  even  otherwise  offence
under  Section  322  of  PPC  was  inviting  the
punishment only of Diyat as such was not hit
by embargo contained under Section 497 of
Cr.P.C.,  therefore,  petitioner  claims  his
release on bail as of right.
4.  Conversely,  the  learned  Deputy
Prosecutor  General  assisted  by  the  learned
counsel  for  complainant  opposed  present
petition  vehemently  and  rigorously  on
multiple  grounds.  Further  added  that  since
petitioner  was  not  holding  public  service
vehicle license, as such he was not authorized
to  drive  the  Bus,  hence  his  act  of  driving,
Crl. Misc. No. 5377-B of 2026  3
being illegal and unlawful, was fully covered
by the mischief of Section 322 of PPC.
5.  I  have  adhered  to  the  submissions
made by all the concerned with utmost care.
In order to determine as to which of the penal
provision  of  Section  320  PPC  or  322  PPC
would be attracted to the case, the combined
study and critical analysis of the provisions of
Sections  318,  319,  320,  321  and  322  is
required.  All  the  above  said  provisions  are
being  reproduced  below  for  the  facility  of
reference:-
Section 318.  Qatl-i-khata.  Whoever, without
any  intention  to  cause  death  of,  or  cause
harm  to,  a  person,  causes  death  of  such
person either by mistake of act or by mistake
of fact, is said to commit  qatl-i-khata.
Section 319.  Punishment for qatl-i-Khata.
Whoever commits  qatl-i-khata  shall be liable
to diyat:
Provided  that,  where  qatl-i-khata  is
committed by any rash or negligent act, other
than rash or negligent driving, the offender
may, in addition to  diyat , also be punished
with imprisonment of either description for a
term which may extend to five years as  ta’zir .
Section  320.  Punishment  for  qatl-i-khata
by  rash  or  negligent  driving.  Whoever
commits  qatl-i-khata  by  rash  or  negligent
driving shall, having regard to the facts and
circumstances  of  the  case,  in  addition  to
diyat ,  be  punished  with  imprisonment  of
either  description  for  a  term  which  may
extend to ten years.
Section  321.  Qatl-bis-sabab.  Whoever,
without any intention to cause death of, or
cause  harm  to,  any  person,  does  any
unlawful act which becomes a cause for the
death of another person, is said to commit
qatl-bis-sabab .
Section  322.  Punishment  for  qatl-bis-
sabab.  Whoever commits  qatl-bis-sabab  shall
be liable to  diyat.
Crl. Misc. No. 5377-B of 2026  4
6.  Offence  of  qatl-i-khata  has  been
defined in Section 318 of PPC, which applies
to a person, who causes the death of a person
without any intention to kill or cause harm
either by mistake of the act or mistake of the
fact.  Meaning  thereby  that  the  offence  is
committed  inadvertently,  without  any
intention or malice, but out of  khata .
Offence  of  qatl-bis-sabab  has  been
elaborated in Section 321 of PPC. It applies to
a  person  who  causes  the  death  of  other
without  any  intention  to  kill  by  doing  an
unlawful  act.
Intention or knowledge is not a “sine
qua non” for the applicability of  both the said
offences.
7.  The word  “ unlawful  act ”  is of  much
significance,  which  draws  a  line  of
demarcation between above said two offences.
I am of the confirmed view that when a person
carrying a valid and effective license drives a
vehicle and causes the death of a person by
rash and negligent driving, his act would be
covered by the offence of  qatl-i-khata . He has
no intention to kill, but his act of driving is
lawful.
On the other hand where a person, not
holding a license, or a valid/effective driving
license causes the death of somebody by his
rash  or  negligent  driving,  although  he  has
also no intention to kill, but his act of driving
is  illegal  and  unlawful,  therefore,  his  act
Crl. Misc. No. 5377-B of 2026  5
squarely falls within the mischief of offence of
“ qatl-bis-sabab ”  and  penal  provisions  of
Section 322 of PPC shall be applicable to him.
8.  It  was  borne  out  of  the  record  that
petitioner was not named in the FIR registered
on 15.11.2025. Certain persons Shakeel and
Liaqat, owners of the Bus were joined in the
investigation on 20.11.2025. They revealed in
their statement under Section 161 of Cr.P.C.
that petitioner was their driver and bus was
handed  over  to  him  for  driving.
Supplementary statement of complainant was
recorded  accordingly  on  23.12.2025,
nominating  the  petitioner.  Name  of  the
petitioner floated on the surface in the natural
course of investigational steps. Circumstances
were  pointing  accusing  finger  towards  the
petitioner. He failed to assign any malice or
grudge on the part of complainant or owners
of  the  vehicle  to  falsely  involve  him.  Above
delay  in  recording  the  statement  of  said
persons  cannot  be  resolved  in  favour  of
petitioner in any manner whatsoever.
9.  In  the  case  in  hand,  although
petitioner  was  holding  HTV  license,  but  he
was  not  issued  public  service  vehicle  (PSV)
license, which is required for driving the Bus.
“ Public service vehicle license ”  is a special
driving  endorsement  required  to  drive
commercial  passenger  transport  like  van,
coaster and bus. It is issued at least one year
after  holding  LTV  or  HTV  license  with  the
minimum requirement of the age of 22-years.
Crl. Misc. No. 5377-B of 2026  6
10.  It  is  matter  of  the  record  that
petitioner  was  not  a  holder  of  PSV  license,
therefore, he was not authorized to drive any
public transport vehicle. A person driving the
vehicle illegally/unlawfully puts not only his
own life, but also life of the persons travelling
with him as well as life of the public at risk.
Act of petitioner of driving the Bus could only
be  regarded  as  illegal  and  unlawful.  It  was
also in flagrant violation of the provisions of
Punjab Motor Vehicle Ordinance, 1965. Act of
the  petitioner  triggered  a  crash  causing  a
fatality  and  resulted  into  the  death  of  four
young boys, out of which marriage of a boy
was scheduled for following day. Every sane
person  is  presumed  to  know  the
consequences  of  his  illegal/unlawful  act  or
omission.
11.  Offence  of  “ qatl-bis-sabab ”
punishable  under  Section  322  of  PPC  was
inviting the punishment only of Diyat, but as
per second schedule appended with Code of
Criminal  Procedure  it  was  shown  to  be
cognizable  and  non-bailable.  Said  provision
was  introduced  following  the  amendment  of
Chapter XVI of the Pakistan Penal Code (Of
offences affecting the human body) in 1990,
which  is  manifesting  the  intention  of
legislature. The fact that it is punishable only
with Diyat is not sufficient by itself for the
release of an accused on bail, because each
and every case has to be scrutinized in the
light of its own surrounding circumstances. I
Crl. Misc. No. 5377-B of 2026  7
have sought guidance from a case reported as
‘ Majid  Nadeem  Vs.  The  State  and  another ’
( 2011  SCMR  1227 ),  wherein  five  persons
were  crushed  and  done  away  with  by  rash
and negligent driving of a car and post arrest
bail was refused by apex Court.
12.  Prima  facie  involvement  of  the
petitioner in the crime could not be ruled out.
There was even  not an  iota  of the material
suggesting  that  the  case  against  him  was
requiring  “ further  inquiry ”.  Circumstances
appearing  on  the  surface  are  presenting  a
dreadful picture. Act of petitioner was that of
“extreme  rash  and  negligent  driving”,  which
neither  can  be  condoned,  nor  discretionary
powers can be exercised in his favour. I do not
think it appropriate to allow this petition.
13.  For  what  has  been  discussed  above,
present  petition  has  no  force,  hence
dismissed .
14.  Needless  to  mention  that  any
observation made in this order is tentative in
nature  and  shall  not  influence  the  learned
trial court in any manner.
(TANVEER AHMAD SHEIKH)
