[SECTION: LHC_CASE_019]
Case: Crl. Misc. 35608/26 (Muhammad Sarwar Vs The State etc).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Crl. Misc. 35608/26. Title: Crl. Misc. 35608/26 (Muhammad Sarwar Vs The State etc). Official Ref: 2026LHC5345. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Muhammad Amjad Pervaiz. Judgment Date (as displayed): 27-08-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5345.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 4 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Form No. HCJD/C - 121
ORDER SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT  LAHORE
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
Crl.Misc.No.35608-B of 2026
Muhammad Sarwar vs. The State and another.
Sr. No. of
order/
proceedings
Date of
order/
proceedings.
Order with signatures of Judge, and that of parties or
counsel, where necessary.
19.08.2026  Mr.Muhammad Abbas Ali Ansari, Advocate for the
petitioner.
Ch.Usman Ghani, Assistant Attorney General with
Irfan SHO/FIA.
Through the instant petition under section 497 of
the  Code  of  Criminal  Procedure,  1898  (Cr.P.C),  the
petitioner seeks bail after arrest in case FIR No.CCC-LHR-
20/2026 dated 02.03.2026 for offences under sections 489-
B, 489-C P.P.C. registered at Police Station Corporate
Crime Circle, Lahore (CCC).
2.  As per contents of the F.I.R., the prosecution story,
precisely, is that pursuant to secret information regarding the
involvement of a gang in the illegal business, possession and
transportation  of  counterfeit  Pakistani  currency  notes,  a
raiding team comprising officials of the F.I.A. apprehended
the petitioner. Upon his personal search, counterfeit Pakistani
currency  notes  amounting  to  Rs.200,000/-  (200  notes  of
Rs.1,000/- denomination each) were allegedly recovered from
his possession. It is further alleged that, at the time of his
arrest, the petitioner confessed to counterfeiting of currency
notes and sale thereof since last one year.
3.  Arguments heard. Record perused.
4.  At this stage of tentative assessment, it has been
observed that the raid has been conducted in pursuance of
secret information and the petitioner was arrested at a busy
public place yet no independent witness was associated
during the proceedings. No evidence against the petitioner
Crl.Misc.No.35608-B of 2026
is available on the record to support the allegations of
counterfeiting currency notes or sale thereof in the market,
except  the  above  so-called  confession  which  is
inadmissible.  There  is  no  evidence  as  to  any  past  or
prospective buyer/customer. As of now, material available
on record is not sufficient to attract the mischief of section
489-B P.P.C and the available evidence, at the most, makes
out a case in terms of section 489-C P.P.C. In the peculiar
facts and circumstances of the case, the question of guilt of
the petitioner qua the offence calls for further inquiry
within the ambit of section 497(2) Cr.P.C, as per principles
enunciated in various cases which are reproduced below.
Possession simpliciter of a counterfeit currency note does
not constitute ingredients of section 489-B, P.P.C.
5.  It is settled legal position that at the bail stage
deeper appreciation of the record cannot be gone into, but
only a tentative assessment is to be made just to find out as
to whether present applicants/accused are connected with
the commission of offence or not. Applying the above
settled legal position to the case in hand, it will be seen that
prima facie ingredients of section 489-B, P.P.C. are not met
in the circumstances of the case, but at best it can be a case
of section 489-C, P.P.C. for which maximum punishment is
prescribed to the extent of seven years or with fine or with
both, which does not fall within the prohibitory clause of
section 497, Cr.P.C.
6.  It is for the learned trial Court to determine finally
whether provisions of sections 489-B or 489-C, P.P.C. are
attracted in this case, of course, after recording evidence.
As of now, case against the petitioner calls for further
enquiry falling within the ambit of section 497(2), Code of
Criminal Procedure.).
1995, SCMR, 48, 1996 P Cr.LJ 815.
2012 YLR 1228
2021 SCMR 729
Crl.Misc.No.35608-B of 2026
7.  It is since settled law that the benefit of doubt goes
to the accused even at bail stage.
The petitioner is behind
the bars since his arrest.  Nothing is to be recovered from
him. In this case, evidence is documentary in nature which
is already in possession of the prosecution and the question
of tampering may not arise.
8.  It is important to remember that bail is not to be
withheld  as  a  punishment.  There  is  no  legal  or  moral
compulsion to keep people in jail merely on the allegation that
they  have  committed  offences  punishable  with  death  or
transportation, unless reasonable grounds appear to exist to
disclose  their  complicity.  The  ultimate  conviction  and
incarceration of a guilty person can repair the wrong caused
by a mistaken relief of interim bail granted to him, but no
satisfactory reparation can be offered to an innocent man for
his unjustified incarceration at any stage of the case albeit his
acquittal in the long run.
9.  While dealing with the question of whether or not
bail is to be granted to an accused, it has to be kept in mind
that the object of bail is to secure the attendance of the
accused at the trial. The object is neither punitive nor
preventive. Imprisonment of a person and deprivation of
his  liberty,  cannot  be  described  other  than,  being  a
punishment,  unless  no  less  restrictive  alternative  is
available to ensure that the accused will stand his trial
when called upon to do so. All civilized societies recognize
the principle that punishment comes only after conviction,
and the presumption of innocence subsist with the accused,
till he is handed down punishment after trial.
10.  Learned Law Officer has urged for refusal of bail to
the petitioner on the ground that further evidence may
surface in the case of arrest of co-accused Muhammad Asif
PLD 1995 SC 34
2026 SCP 116
PLD 1972 SC 81
PLD 2020 SC 456
Crl.Misc.No.35608-B of 2026
yet to be arrested. Suffice it to say that surfacing of new
facts and material tending to establish guilt of an accused is
a valid ground to seek cancellation of bail.
11.  For what has been discussed above, the instant
petition is accepted and the petitioner is admitted to post
arrest bail subject to his furnishing bail bonds in the sum of
Rs.1,00,000/- with one surety  in the like amount to the
satisfaction of the learned trial court.
12.  Before parting with this order, it is clarified that if
the petitioner or anyone else acting on his behalf, in any
way, delays the conclusion of the trial, the other side (State
or the complainant) shall be at liberty to file a petition for
cancellation of bail.
13.  It is also elucidated that the above observations are
tentative in nature only for the purpose of decision of this
bail application and the same shall not influence the fate of
the trial.
(  Muhammad Amjad Pervaiz )
Judge

Tariq  Saleem Sheikh,  J.  –  Through  this  consolidated
judgment, I propose to decide Criminal Appeal No. 12055/J/2022 and
Criminal Revision No. 10970/2022, which arise from the judgment dated
21.01.2022 delivered by the Additional Sessions Judge, Jaranwala, in
case FIR No. 309/2018 dated 13.07.2018 registered under sections 302
and  34  PPC  at  Police  Station  Lundianwala,  Jaranwala,  District
Faisalabad.
2.  On 13.07.2018, Muhammad Hanif (PW-1) submitted an
application (Exh. PA) to Muhammad Afzal/SI (PW-9) at THQ Hospital,
Jaranwala, for registration of an FIR. He stated that he was a resident of
Chak No. 629/GB and earned his livelihood from farming. That evening,
he was present at his house along with his son, Muhammad Asif, other
family members, and Muhammad Ali and Muhammad Yousaf. The
house had no door. At about 6:15 p.m., Umar Hayat (armed with a
.30 bore pistol), Shaukat Ali (armed with a .12 bore carbine), Ashiq
(armed with a rifle), and Mehra (empty-handed) came outside his house
in the bazaar. Hanif further stated that, on seeing them, he and his
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 2 -
companions went out. Mehra raised a  lalkara , exhorting his co-accused
to  teach  Hanif  a  lesson  for  demanding  possession  of  the  Ihatta .
Thereupon, Umar Hayat fired at Muhammad Asif, hitting him on the left
side of his abdomen. Shaukat Ali and Ashiq resorted to aerial firing,
whereafter  all  the  accused  fled  the  scene  while  brandishing  their
weapons. Hanif and the other prosecution witnesses rushed Muhammad
Asif to the hospital, but he succumbed to his injuries on the way.
3.  PW  Muhammad  Hanif  stated  that  the  motive  for  the
occurrence was a dispute between the parties over an  Ihatta  measuring
10 marlas, situated in Square No. 59, Killa No. 2. He alleged that it was
in the possession of the accused party and that the complainant party had
been demanding its return. He further claimed that the accused, in
furtherance of their common intention, murdered Muhammad Asif due
to this demand.
4.  After recording  police karwi  on the application, Muhammad
Afzal/SI  (PW-9)  sent  it  to  Police  Station  Lundianwala,  Jaranwala,
through  Ghulam  Murtaza  616/HC.  Based  on  this  application,  Ijaz
Hussain Aseer/HC (PW-8) registered FIR No.309/2018 (Exh. PJ).
5.  The case was investigated, and Umar Hayat, Shaukat Ali,

Additional Sessions Judge indicted them. They pleaded not guilty and
claimed a trial.
6.  To  prove  the  charge,  the  prosecution  examined  nine

for autopsy. He was also a marginal witness of the Recovery Memo

the complainant party had attempted to forcibly occupy it. He alleged
that a quarrel had taken place between Asif and Shaukat a few days
before the occurrence, during which the former had sustained injuries.
On the fateful day, he claimed, the deceased and his companions came
from Chak No. 658/9-GB, demolished the wall of the disputed  Ihatta ,
and took possession by force. Thereafter, according to him, they fired
indiscriminately in jubilation, during which one of their own shots
accidentally struck Asif, which caused his death. Umar Hayat denied

Trial court ’ s findings
11.  The trial court found that the dispute over possession of a
10-marla  Ihatta  was the underlying cause of the occurrence. While the
complainant party alleged that the accused had taken possession and
committed the murder to suppress their demand for its return, the
defence  maintained  that  the  complainant  party  was  the  aggressor.
However, the court concluded that the motive evidence was inconclusive
and could support either version.
12.  The trial court observed that both sides had concealed
material facts. Relying on the principle that courts must independently
evaluate the evidence where such concealment occurs, as discussed by
the Supreme Court in  Syed Ali Bepari v. Nibaran Mollah and others
(PLD 1962 SC 502), it conducted its own assessment.
It held that the
Ihatta  was “admittedly”  in the possession of accused Shaukat, and that
the Appellant was his close relative. It noted that the defence did not
dispute the Appellant’s presence at the scene. Even if, as claimed by the
defence,  Muhammad  Asif  and  his  companions  had  gone  to  take
The principle laid down in  Syed Ali Bepari  is that where both parties to a case have withheld
material facts or suppressed part of the truth, the court must proceed to assess the evidence
independently, rather than relying on either party ’ s version. In other words, the concealment or
distortion by both sides does not absolve the court of its duty to scrutinize the evidence and reach an
independent finding of fact. This principle was also applied in  Zahid Parvez and another v. The State
(PLD 1991 SC 558).
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 5 -
possession and were injured in a crossfire, that would not justify Asif’s
killing.  The  trial  court  rejected  the  theory  that  the  deceased  was
accidentally shot by his own party as implausible. It further reasoned
that had someone else fired it, the prosecution witnesses would have had
no  reason  to  spare  the  actual  assailant  and  falsely  implicate  the
Appellant. On this basis, it concluded that the Appellant had fired the
fatal shot and that there was no plausible ground for false implication on
account of enmity or any ulterior motive. The trial court also relied on
the medical evidence to the extent of the time of occurrence and the
nature of the injury, but held that the alleged recoveries had no legal
consequence.
13.  The trial court distinguished the case of the other accused
from that of the Appellant. It observed that Mehra was merely alleged to
have raised a  lalkara , while Shaukat and Ashiq were said to have
resorted  to  aerial  firing.  Although  one  .30  caliber  cartridge  was
recovered from the scene, no empties were found that could corroborate
the use of the weapons allegedly carried by Shaukat and Ashiq. The
court held that mere presence at the scene was insufficient to attract
vicarious liability under section 34 PPC, which requires proof of a pre-
arranged plan and concerted action. Even if the prosecution’s version
were accepted as true, the element of premeditation was absent. The trial
court thus found the involvement of Shaukat, Ashiq, and Mehra in the
incident doubtful and, applying the rule of abundant caution and the
principle of sifting the grain from the chaff, acquitted them.
The submissions
14.  In  support  of  Criminal  Appeal  No.  12055/J/2022,
Ch. Humayoun Rashid, Advocate, contended that the Appellant was
innocent and had been falsely implicated due to a previous enmity
stemming from the dispute over possession of an  Ihatta . He submitted
that  the  prosecu tion’s  case  was  fabricated  and  that  the  delay  in
registration of the FIR substantiated this assertion. PWs Muhammad
Hanif and Muhammad Ali were closely related to the deceased and,
therefore, interested witnesses whose testimony could not be safely
relied upon. Gohar Ali, an independent eyewitness to the incident, was
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 6 -
neither associated with the investigation nor cited as a witness. The
prosecution, therefore, withheld the best available evidence without
justification.
15.  Mr. Humayoun further argued that the testimonies of the
PWs were replete with material contradictions,  which rendered the
prosecution’s case against the Appellant highly doubtful. He maintained
that the medical evidence conflicted with the ocular account, and that the
crime empty recovered from the scene did not match the weapon
allegedly recovered from the accused. He further contended that the
Additional Sessions Judge had convicted the Appellant on the very
evidence on which the co-accused were acquitted, although there existed
no independent or corroborative circumstance to justify such differential
treatment.
16.  Without  prejudice  to  the  above  submissions,  Mr.
Humayoun  argued  that  the  postmortem  report  (Exh.  PS)  was
inadmissible in evidence. Dr. Khalid Mehboob, who had conducted the
postmortem examination, was not examined at trial, having reportedly
left the country. No application was moved to lead secondary evidence
as required under Article 76 of the Qanun-e-Shahadat, 1984 (QSO).
Instead, the Public Prosecutor tendered a carbon copy of the report
through his own statement on 06.11.2021. Although defence counsel
stated at that time that he had no objection to its exhibition, that
concession did not override the statutory requirements. He maintained
that  the  postmortem  report  (Exh.  PS)  was,  therefore,  liable  to  be
de-exhibited and excluded from consideration. If that were done, he
contended, the prosecution’s case would collapse. Counsel concluded by
praying for the Appellant’s acquittal.
17.  The  Additional  Prosecutor  General  (APG)  opposed  the
appeal and supported the impugned judgment. He submitted that the
prosecution had proved its case against the Appellant through credible
evidence.  The  FIR  was  promptly  lodged,  and  the  Appellant  was
nominated therein with a specific role. Although PWs Hanif and Ali
were related to the deceased, they remained consistent on material
particulars. Mere relationship was not, by itself, a ground to discard their
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 7 -
testimony. The APG added that both parties were known to each other,
so there was no possibility of mistaken identity. He contended that the
medical evidence fully corroborated the ocular account and that minor
discrepancies in the witnesses’ statements were inconsequential. The fact
that the recovered pistol did not match the crime empty did not impact
the prosecution’s case. The alleged motive was also established. He
contended that the Appellant’s case was distinguishable from that of his
acquitted co-accused and that the Additional Sessions Judge had rightly
convicted and sentenced him.
18.  As regards Mr. Humayoun ’ s objection to the admission of
Asif ’ s postmortem report (Exh. PS), the APG submitted that it was an
irregularity curable under section 537 Cr.P.C. In any event, if this Court
holds that the trial court misdirected itself by allowing the prosecutor to
produce the report through his own statement, the lapse was attributable
to the court, and the maxim  actus curiae neminem gravabit  would apply.
He urged that the case be remanded to meet the ends of justice.
19.  In rebuttal, Mr. Humayoun argued that a remand would
help the prosecution to fill a fatal lacuna in its case. He submitted that
the Appellant has been incarcerated since 09.08.2018 and that a remand
at this stage would result in grave injustice. He maintained that the
maxim  actus  curiae  neminem  gravabit  cannot  be  invoked  to  the
prejudice of the Appellant.
20.  In support of Criminal Revision No. 10970/2022, Mr. Javed
Iqbal Awan, Advocate, contended that the prosecution had successfully
proved the Appellant ’ s guilt, but the sentence handed down by the trial
court  was  not  commensurate  with  the  gravity of the  offence. He,
therefore, prayed that it be enhanced.
Discussion
21.  The prosecution relies on five categories of evidence to
prove the charge against the Appellant: (i) ocular account, (ii) medical
evidence, (iii) forensic reports, (iv) recoveries, and (v) motive. In view
of the contentions raised by learned counsel for the parties, the following
issues arise for determination:
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 8 -
(i)  Whether the prosecution duly proved Muhammad Asif ’ s
postmortem report  (Exh.  PS)  in  accordance  with  the
Qanun-e-Shahadat, 1984, and if not, whether it is liable to
be excluded from consideration.
(ii)  Whether the procedural irregularity in the admission of
the postmortem report, if established, warrants a remand
to the trial court.
22.  The  admissibility  of  documentary  evidence  in  criminal
trials is governed by the QSO, which embodies safeguards for fair trial
and evidentiary reliability. A document may be received in evidence
only if it is proved in accordance with the QSO, benefits from a statutory
presumption, or qualifies for admission under special rules of evidence.
23.  Articles 72 to 78 of the QSO lay down the procedure for
proving documents. Article 72 states that the contents of documents may
be proved by either primary or secondary evidence. Articles 73 and 74
define primary and secondary evidence, respectively: the former means
the document itself is produced for the inspection of the court, while the
latter includes certified copies, oral accounts of contents, and other
substitutes permitted by law in specific circumstances. Article 75 further
states that documents must be proved by primary evidence, while Article
76 specifies the instances where secondary evidence may be permitted.
24.  Secondary  evidence  is  not  an  automatic  substitute  for
primary evidence, but rather an exception that must be justified under
one of the legally recognized grounds.
In  Mst. Akhtar Sultana v. Major
(Retd) Muzaffar Khan Malik and others  (PLD 2021 SC 715), the
Supreme  Court  held  that  the  admission  of  secondary  evidence  is
contingent  upon  strict  compliance  with  the  statutory  conditions
prescribed under Article 76 of the QSO.
25.  Article 78 requires that where a document is alleged to have
been signed or written by any person, the handwriting or signature must
be affirmatively proved. In  Siraj Din v. Mst. Jamilan and another
(PLD 1997 Lahore 633), it was held that the proof of execution is not
Sher Asfandyar Khan and others v. Neelofar Shah and others  (2025 SCMR 1307).
Also see:  Federation of Pakistan through Secretary Ministry of Defence and another v. Jaffar Khan
and others  (PLD 2010 SC 604),  Inspector-General of Police, Balochistan, and others v. Ghulam
Rasool  (2012 CLC 1645),  Abdullah v. Provincial Government and others  (2014 CLC 285); and
Azhar Abbas and others v. Haji Tahir Abbas and another  (2021 CLC 1351).
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 9 -
“synonymous with the proof of the contents of documents.  The burden
to prove the contents of documents, in addition to proof of execution, is
on the beneficiary of that document, who is to lead primary/secondary
circumstantial internal evidence to prove the truth of that document.”  In
Ch. Muneer Hussain v. Mst. Wazeeran Mai alias Mst. Wazir Mai
(PLD 2005 SC 658), it was held that “Article [78] does not say that mere
proof of handwriting/signatures/thumb-impressions of the executant will
prove truth of the said document.”
26.  In  Province of the Punjab through Collector, Sheikhupura
and others v. Syed Ghazanfar Ali Shah and others  (2017 SCMR 172),
the Supreme Court held that merely placing official or departmental
correspondence  on  record  does  not  amount  to  proof  unless  the
requirements  of  Articles  72  and  78  of  the  QSO  are  satisfied.  It
emphasized that procedural and evidentiary safeguards are fundamental
to  determining  the  validity  of  documents,  and  that  the  provisions
governing the mode of proof cannot be disregarded or bypassed. A court
must first determine whether a document has been properly proved in
accordance with the law before relying on it and rendering judgment on
its basis. This principle was reaffirmed in  Mst. Akhtar Sultana , where the
Supreme Court held that a certified copy of a registered power of
attorney could not be admitted as secondary evidence without strict
compliance with Article 76(c) of the QSO. It rejected the argument that
such an instrument, being registered, was  per se  admissible as a public
document  under Article  85(2).  It  clarified  that  where execution is
disputed, even registered documents assume the character of private
documents and must be proved accordingly.
27.  A counsel cannot exhibit a document through his own
statement unless he is appearing as a witness, a practice discouraged due
to conflict of interest and procedural impropriety.
In  Manzoor Hussain
v. Misri Khan  (PLD 2020 SC 749), the Supreme Court deprecated the
growing practice, particularly in Punjab, of documents being exhibited
through counsel without oath or cross-examination, and reaffirmed that
Mst. Akhtar Sultana v. Major (Retd) Muzaffar Khan Malik and others  (PLD 2021 SC 715).
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 10 -
documents must be proved through competent witnesses. Exceptions
exist only for facts that require no proof, or those of which the court
takes judicial notice (Articles 111  & 112 QSO), or facts formally
admitted (Article 113 QSO).
28.  In  Muhammad Nawaz v. Shahida Parveen and others
(PLD 2017 Islamabad 375), the High Court held that while counsel may
produce certified  copies  of public  or  judicial  records, they cannot
introduce documents to plug evidentiary gaps or cure deficiencies in a
party ’ s case. In  Naeem Niaz Awan v. Additional District Judge and
others  (2024 LHC 6685), this Court held that power of attorney clauses
authorizing  counsel  “to  file  petitions,  statements,  exhibits,  or
documents”  do  not  entitle  them  to  tender  disputed  document s  in
evidence  through  their  own  statements,  as  doing  so  bypasses  the
procedural safeguards under the QSO and the Code of Civil Procedure,
1908.
29.  A  postmortem  report  is  a  documented  expert  opinion.
Although admissible as documentary evidence, it does not prove itself.
Its production must comply with the requirements of the QSO, which
demand proof of both execution and contents. When the doctor who
conducted a postmortem report is unavailable, secondary evidence may
be permitted, but only upon fulfilment of the conditions set out in Article
76 QSO. These include proof that the original has been lost, destroyed,
unlawfully  withheld,  or  otherwise  cannot  be  produced  without
unreasonable delay or expense. In  Munna Kumar v. State of Bihar
[(2005) 12 SCC 209], the prosecution examined an advocate ’ s clerk to
prove the postmortem report. During cross-examination, he admitted that
he neither knew the doctor who prepared the report nor was familiar
with his handwriting. The Supreme Court of India (SCI) held that the
report had not been proved in accordance with law and accepted the
contention that the prosecution had failed to establish the cause of death
or the offence of murder. It observed that the prosecution ought to have
Also  see:  Rustam  and  others  v.  Jehangir  (Deceased)  through  LRs.  (2023  SCMR  730),
Muhammad Hussain and another v. Province of Punjab through District Officer Revenue, Multan,
and  others  (2021  YLR  2310),  Mst.  Rasoolan  Bibi  v.  Province  of  Punjab  and  others
(2023 CLC 1171),  National Highway Authority through Director v. Bashiran Bibi and others
(2024 MLD 1590).

Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022

examined a witness acquainted with the doctor ’ s handwriting, such as a
colleague or someone from the hospital where he worked.
30.  Similarly, in  Sooba and others v. The State  (1994 PCr.LJ
1323), this Court held that the absence of the doctor must be proved
before bringing on record the postmortem report as secondary evidence.
The relevant excerpt is reproduced below:
“The law laid down in the said precedents
is to the effect that
secondary evidence in such cases could only be allowed when the
prosecution proves to the satisfaction of the Court that the doctor who
conducted postmortem examination was not available or had become
incapable of giving evidence or his attendance could not be procured
without an amount of delay or expense which in the circumstances of
the case was unreasonable. It has been laid down that where any such
evidence is allowed without complying with the provisions of section
32(2) of the Evidence Act (now Article 46 of the QSO), such
evidence shall be excluded from consideration.”
31.  Section 509(1) Cr.P.C. provides a statutory exception: The
deposition of a Civil Surgeon or other medical witness, taken and
attested by a Magistrate in the presence of the accused, or taken on
commission under Chapter XL, may be given in evidence in any inquiry,
trial or other proceeding under the Code of 1898, although the deponent
is not called as a witness. However, section 509(2) provides that the
court may, if it thinks fit, summon and examine such deponent as to the
subject-matter of his deposition. The utility of this provision is also
underscored in Rule 6, Chapter 18, Volume III of the High Court Rules
and Orders.
32.  In the present case, the prosecution sought to rely upon the
postmortem report of Muhammad Asif (Exh. PS) to establish the nature
and cause of death. It is, however, undisputed that the doctor who
conducted the postmortem examination, Dr. Khalid Mehboob, was not
produced as a witness. The report was tendered through the statement of
the Public Prosecutor without following the procedure prescribed for the
production of secondary evidence under Article 76 of the QSO. The only
explanation offered for not producing Dr. Mehboob was that he had left
the country. No application was moved before the trial court to seek
permission to lead secondary evidence, nor was any material placed on
Fazal Muhammad and another v. The State  (1970 SCMR 405),  Hussain Bakhsh v. The State
(1971 PCr.LJ 1331), and  Muhammad Siddique and another v. The State  (1974 PCr.LJ 180).
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 12 -
record to substantiate his unavailability, such as proof of departure,
incapacity, or any other legally justifiable ground. The prosecution also
did not examine any hospital official or custodian of records competent
to speak to the document ’ s custody or familiarity with the doctor ’ s
handwriting. In the absence of this factual foundation, the postmortem
report could not be lawfully admitted as secondary evidence.
33.  The concession made by defence counsel that he had no
objection to the exhibition of the report does not cure the defect. It
cannot override the requirements of law governing admissibility of
evidence in a criminal trial. It is a cardinal principle that material relied
upon by the prosecution must be subject to proper proof and, where
necessary, cross-examination. By permitting the Public Prosecutor to
exhibit the postmortem report (Exh. PS) without examining its author or
any  competent  witness,  the  trial  court  denied  the  Appellant  an
opportunity to challenge its authenticity and contents. Accordingly, the
postmortem report is liable to be excluded from consideration.
34.  For completeness, it must be noted that section 509 Cr.P.C.
does not apply to the present case, as no deposition of Dr. Khalid
Mehboob was recorded by a Magistrate or on commission in the manner
prescribed.
35.  Mr.  Humayoun  contended  that  the  postmortem  report
(Exh. PS) could not be received in evidence also because no expert
witness gave an oral statement regarding its contents. Consequently, the
nature of the injury, including its description, angle, and trajectory,
remained unexplained. He submitted that, under Article 71 of the QSO,
oral evidence relating to an expert opinion must come from the person
who formed that opinion, and that where reliance is placed on secondary
evidence of a postmortem report, it should be proved by another doctor
or expert competent to interpret the injuries. This argument proceeds on
a distinction between the documentary proof of the postmortem report
and the substantive proof of the medical opinion it embodies.
36.  It  may  be  desirable  that  secondary  evidence  of  a
postmortem report or medico-legal certificate be given by a medical
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 13 -
officer who satisfies the statutory conditions for such testimony, but the
law does not require that such evidence must invariably be led through
another doctor. In the present case, the prosecution failed to lay a
sufficient foundation for the admission of secondary evidence under
Article 76 of the QSO, and no other legally competent witness was
produced to prove the report (Exh. PS). As such, the question of
explaining or interpreting its contents did not arise.
37.  The Code of 1898 does not expressly employ the term
“remand” in the context of appellate proceedings. However, such power
is implicit in sections 423 and 428 Cr.P.C., which authorize the appellate
court to order retrial or direct the taking of additional evidence where
necessary for a just decision of the case. At the same time, section 537
Cr.P.C. provides that no finding, sentence, or order shall be reversed or
altered  on  account  of  any  error,  omission,  or  irregularity  in  the
proceedings  unless  it  has,  in  fact,  occasioned  a  failure  of  justice.
Therefore, when considering whether a remand is necessary to cure a
procedural irregularity, the appellate court must determine: (i) whether
the irregularity is one that goes to the root of the case or is merely
technical, (ii) whether it is curable under section 537 Cr.P.C., and
(iii) whether its impact has occasioned a failure of justice. If the defect is
such that it can be remedied without prejudicing the accused, a remand
may be justified. However, it cannot be ordered merely to afford the
prosecution an opportunity to fill lacunae in its case. The distinction
between an “ curable  irregularity” and a “lacuna” is, therefore, crucial in
determining whether a remand is warranted.
38.  In  Rajendra Prasad v. Narcotic Cell through its Officer-
in-charge, Delhi  (AIR 1999 SC 2292), the SCI explained that a lacuna is
an inherent weakness or  “ wedge ”  in the prosecution ’ s case, not a mere
oversight  or  lapse  by  the  prosecutor.  While  courts  may  permit
rectification of inadvertent errors, they must not allow a retrial to be used
as a second attempt at conviction. The SCI stated:
“ No party in a trial can be foreclosed from correcting errors. If proper
evidence was not adduced or a relevant material was not brought on
record due to any inadvertence, the court should be magnanimous in
permitting such mistakes to be rectified. After all, the function of the
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 14 -
criminal court is the administration of criminal justice and not to
count errors committed by the parties or to find out and declare who
among  the parties performed better.”
39.  A criminal court is not a passive spectator and must ensure
that evidence is recorded in accordance with the law.
Where material
evidence has been improperly exhibited owing to non-compliance with
statutory  requirements,  an  appellate  court  may,  in  appropriate
circumstances, consider remand so that such evidence is proved in the
manner prescribed. Whether such a course would amount to permitting a
party to fill a lacuna or merely to rectify a procedural defect depends
upon the facts of each case. In the present matter, the postmortem report
forms part of the record but stands excluded because it was not proved in
accordance with the law. However, the power to remand is discretionary
and must be exercised with due regard to its practical utility. Even if the
postmortem report were to be duly proved on remand, it would only
establish the nature and cause of death. It would not, by itself, remedy
the  serious  deficiencies  in  the  prosecution’s  ocular,  forensic ,  and
circumstantial  evidence,  which  are  examined  below.  In  these
circumstances, a remand would merely prolong the proceedings without
affecting the ultimate decision of the case. It would therefore serve no
useful purpose. I decline to remand the case.
40.  The prosecution alleged that on 13.07.2018 at about 6:15
p.m., the Appellant, along with his acquitted co-accused (Shaukat Ali,
Ashiq, and Mehra), in furtherance of their common intention, launched
an armed assault on the complainant party, which resulted in  Asif’s
death.  Muhammad  Hanif  (PW-1)  submitted  a  written  application
(Exh. PA) at 8:20 p.m., and FIR No. 309/2018 (Exh. PJ) was registered
at 9:00 p.m. The delay of approximately two hours and forty-five
minutes is not inordinate, even though the police station was only three
kilometres from the place of occurrence. It stands partially explained by
the urgency to transport the injured, who was in critical condition, to the
hospital. Nonetheless, the surrounding circumstances require scrutiny.
PW Hanif did not explain where and when the application (Exh. PA)
Volume III, Chapter 1, Part E, Rule 2 of the Lahore High Court Rules and Orders;  Ghulam
Muhammad v. The State  (PLD 1989 Karachi 144). Also see:  Akhtar Ali and others v. Amir Hatam and
another  (2021 PCr.LJ 1439).

Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 15 -
was drafted. Its scribe was neither identified during the investigation nor
produced at trial. PW Hanif stated that a call was made to Rescue 15 but
expressed ignorance that it was made by Gohar Ali, who was admittedly
present at the scene. He later stated that the police arrived only after his
brother,  Muhammad  Ali  (PW-2),  made  a  call.  In  contrast,
PW Muhammad Ali deposed that Gohar Ali made the call and that the
police arrived in response thereto. The record indicates that Manzoor/SI
reached the place of occurrence pursuant to the Rescue 15 call, yet no
written application or oral statement was given to him for the registration
of the FIR. PW Hanif stated that Muhammad Afzal/SI first met him at
the hospital at about 8:20 p.m., while PW Muhammad Ali claimed that
the police had reached the place of occurrence at about 6:30 p.m., before
the registration of the FIR. These inconsistencies, viewed cumulatively,
diminish the credibility  of the prosecution’s version from the outset.
41.  The prosecution produced Muhammad Hanif (PW-1) and
Muhammad Ali (PW-2) to furnish the ocular account. Mr. Humayoun
contended that their testimony was unreliable on the ground that they
were closely related to the deceased and, therefore, interested witnesses.
However, the law is well settled that relationship is not sufficient to
discard evidence.  An interested witness is not merely a person related to
the victim, but one who is partisan or has a motive to falsely implicate
the accused. Mere kinship or friendship, without more, does not render
testimony unreliable if it is otherwise credible.
In  Nazir and others v.
The State  (PLD 1962 SC 269), the Supreme Court held that there is no
inflexible rule that the evidence of an interested witness must always be
rejected unless independently corroborated. While an interested witness
may, in exceptional cases, be wholly reliable, it would ordinarily be
unsafe to rely upon such testimony against every accused without careful
scrutiny. The court must, therefore, look for circumstances that lend
assurance  to the witness’s statement and create that degree of probability
which may serve as the basis for conviction. Such corroboration need
not come from the testimony of an independent witness; it may arise
from any circumstance in the case that satisfies the judicial conscience
Iqbal alias Bhala and others v. The State  (1994 SCMR 1).
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 16 -
that the witness is speaking the truth. The Supreme Court also clarified
that the corroboration required for an interested witness need not be of
the same probative strength as that demanded for an accomplice, as the
two stand on a different footing.
42.  Both  Muhammad  Hanif  (PW-1)  and  Muhammad  Ali
(PW-2)  claimed  to  have  witnessed  the  incident  and  described  the
sequence of events in broadly similar terms. They denied any prior
enmity with the Appellant and maintained that they had no reason to
falsely implicate him. However, during cross-examination, PW Hanif
admitted that litigation between the parties over the disputed  Ihatta  was
ongoing  at  the  time  of  the  occurrence.  PW  Muhammad  Ali  also
acknowledged the dispute but insisted that it was not the motive behind
the murder. Nonetheless, the close relationship between the witnesses
and the deceased, coupled with the admitted hostility over possession,
necessitates careful scrutiny of their statements.
43.  Muhammad  Hanif  (PW-1)  consistently  claimed  in  the
application  (Exh.  PA),  FIR  No.  309/2018  (Exh.  PJ),  and  in  his
examination-in-chief that he was a resident of Chak No. 629/GB and that
the incident took place in front of his house. However, neither the rough
site plan (Exh. PM) nor the scaled site plan (Exh. PH), prepared by
Muhammad  Afzal/SI  (PW-9)  and  Muhammad  Nawaz/Draftsman
(PW-5), respectively, depicts or even mentions the PW- 1’s  house near
the place of occurrence. Rather, PW Muhammad Nawaz admitted that
the homes of PW Hanif and the other prosecution witnesses were not
situated in the immediate vicinity of the spot. He also acknowledged that
the crime scene was an open area surrounded by the grocery shop of
Sultan, the house of Ali  Sher, and  Shamsher ’ s  Dera. Notably, he
prepared  the  site  plan  at  the  instructions  of  PW  Hanif  and  other
prosecution witnesses. Exh. PH also fails to identify the path allegedly
taken by the accused to reach the scene or the escape route purportedly
adopted after the incident. Muhammad Afzal/SI (PW-9) admitted that
the address of Chak No. 629/GB was recorded merely on the asking of
the complainant party, and that he did not independently verify it from
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 17 -
NADRA, the Lambardar, or any other source. These circumstances
make the presence of the alleged eyewitnesses at the scene doubtful.
44.  There are also material contradictions regarding the manner
in which the incident occurred. PW Hanif stated that the firing lasted
10 to 15 minutes and that the accused fired 10 to 12 shots, whereas PW
Muhammad Ali deposed that the firing continued for only five to seven
minutes and involved 15 to 16 shots. Furthermore, according to their
own account, PW Hanif and the other prosecution witnesses were
present at close quarters during the firing, yet none of them sustained
any injury, nor did they state that they took cover or retreated from the
spot. While the mere fact that a witness remains uninjured is not, by
itself, sufficient to discredit his testimony, this circumstance, when read
alongside the other inconsistencies, further diminishes the weight to be
attached to the ocular account. Both PW Hanif and PW Muhammad Ali
also asserted that their clothes were stained with blood. However, the
Investigating Officer admitted that he neither seized nor examined those
clothes. Consequently, potentially corroborative physical evidence is
lacking.
45.  The prosecution also failed to examine Gohar Ali, who
allegedly made a call to Rescue 15 and was present at the scene.
PW  Hanif  admitted  that  several  other  persons,  including  Sultan,
Shameer, Sharif, and Ali Sher, were also present at the relevant time and
place, but none of them were examined. The non-examination of these
independent witnesses warrants an adverse inference under Illustration
(g) of Article 129 of the QSO that their testimony would not have
supported the prosecution.
46.  In  view  of  the  foregoing,  it  is  unsafe  to  rely  on  the
testimonies of Muhammad Hanif (PW-1) and Muhammad Ali (PW-2) to
uphold the Appellant ’ s conviction and sentence. Since the postmortem
report (Exh. PS) stands excluded from consideration, it is unnecessary to
dilate  on  the inconsistencies  between  medical  and  ocular accounts
highlighted by the Appellant ’ s counsel. Even otherwise, the postmortem
Riaz Ahmed v. State  (PLJ 2010 SC 877).
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 18 -
report (Exh. PS), assuming it was admissible, could not by itself connect
the Appellant with the offence. It is well settled that medical evidence
may confirm the eyewitness account about the seat and nature of injuries
or the kind of weapon used, but it cannot identify the assailant.
47.  According to the prosecution, the Appellant led to the
recovery of a .30 bore pistol (P-1) and a 7mm rifle (P-3), while Shaukat
and Ashiq got recovered a carbine (P-5) and a rifle (P-7), respectively.
One  .30  caliber  cartridge  (P-11)  was  secured  from  the  place  of
occurrence. However, the Firearm and Toolmarks Examination Report
(Exh. PU) confirmed that the said cartridge had not been discharged
from the recovered pistol (P-1), owing to differences in individual
characteristics. As for the 7mm rifle (P-3), the FIR does not attribute
such a weapon to the Appellant, and the prosecution did not explain this
discrepancy. The recoveries attributed to Shaukat and Ashiq are also
inconsequential, as the empties recovered from the scene did not link to
their  weapons.  It  is  now  well  settled  that  recovery  of  a  weapon
constitutes only a corroborative piece of evidence. It cannot, by itself,
establish  guilt  and  becomes  irrelevant  when  the  ocular  account  is
disbelieved.
In the present case, because there is no credible eyewitness
testimony or forensic corroboration, the recoveries have no evidentiary
value.
48.  Finally,  the  motive.  The  prosecution  alleged  that  the
Appellant and his acquitted co-accused murdered Muhammad Asif over
a dispute concerning a 10-marla  Ihatta . The trial court rightly found that
the evidence was inconclusive for establishing this motive because the
Ihatta  was in the possession of the accused party. Apart from the oral
assertions  of  PWs  Muhammad  Hanif  and  Muhammad  Ali,  the
prosecution produced no independent evidence, oral or documentary, to
support its claim. Notably, PW Hanif admitted that the deceased had
sustained injuries in an earlier altercation, whereas PW Muhammad Ali

denied any such incident. There was also an inconsistency regarding

accused were in possession, while PW Muhammad Ali stated that the
complainant party already had possession and even acknowledged the
possibility of a stay order in favour of the accused. Despite the centrality
of this issue, the Investigating Officer failed to collect or produce any
documentary evidence, such as an injunction order or revenue record,
that could have clarified the position or helped identify the aggressor. It
is well established that motive is a double-edged sword: it may furnish a
reason to commit the offence, but it may equally provide a reason for

49.  The  defence  examined  three  witnesses.  Khalil  Ahmad
(DW-1), a representative of NADRA, produced the Alfa & Beta Family

Muhammad Asif, son of Muhammad Hanif, in the birth register for the

16.01.1991 existed for a child born in the house of Muhammad Hanif
with the name of M. Asad. DW-3 Zulfiqar Ali, Incharge Rescue 15, City
Faisalabad, produced a  Rapt  (Exh. DE) showing that a call was received
at 7:48 p.m. on the day of occurrence from Gohar, a resident of Chak
No. 629/GB, reporting quarrelling and firing in the jurisdiction of P.S.
Lundianwala. The caller, however, did not disclose the names of the
parties, the exact location, or whether he had personally witnessed the
incident.
50.  The evidentiary value of these witnesses is limited. DW-1
and DW-2 were evidently produced to cast doubt on the identity or
existence of deceased Asif as the son of PW Hanif. However, their
statements do not conclusively establish that Asif did not exist or was
unrelated to PW Hanif. Importantly, PW Hanif consistently referred to
Muhammad Ashraf and others v. The State  (1998 SCMR 279),  Muhammad Riaz v. Muhammad
Zaman and another  (PLD 2005 SC 484),  Akbar Ali v. The State  (2007 SCMR 486),  Abdul Majeed v.
Mulazim Hussain and others  (PLD 2007 SC 637),  and  Sahib Khan v. The State  (2008 SCMR 1049).
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 20 -
the deceased as his son during the trial and was not confronted with
these records during cross-examination to challenge his assertion or test
the credibility of his claim. Furthermore, the defence did not offer any
alternative theory regarding the deceased ’ s identity.
51.  As for Zulfiqar Ali 6078/HC (DW-3), the Rescue 15  Rapt
merely confirms that a vague and anonymous call was made after the
incident,  which  neither  contradicts  the  FIR  nor  discredits  the
prosecution ’ s account.
52.  In the present case, the trial court observed that neither
party had come forward with the whole truth and invoked  Syed Ali
Bepari v. Nibaran Mollah and others  (PLD 1962 SC 502) to reconstruct
the incident. It misdirected itself in doing so, as that authority is of
limited application. It permits independent evaluation of the evidence
where both parties have suppressed material facts, but it does not relieve
the prosecution of its  burden to prove the accused’s guilt beyond
reasonable doubt. Any inference drawn by the court must be founded on
admissible evidence and proved circumstances, and not on conjecture or
speculation. In any event, if the trial court was inclined to discard both
versions and reach its own conclusion, it was incumbent upon it to do so
neutrally and on the basis of legally admissible evidence. Instead, it
rested its findings substantially on the prosecution’s account, without
addressing the material contradictions and omissions discussed above.
The Appellant also raised a specific plea in his statement under section
342 Cr.P.C., denying his presence at the scene, whereas the prosecution
attributed to him a direct and fatal role. The trial court did not engage
with this fundamental inconsistency or resolve it through a reasoned
evaluation of the record.
53.  It is significant that the trial court acquitted co-accused
Shaukat Ali, Ashiq, and Mehra after finding that the prosecution had
failed to establish their participation in the occurrence or the existence of
a common intention. Although it sought to distinguish the Appellant on
the ground that he was attributed the fatal shot, that distinction cannot be
sustained once the ocular account is found unreliable and unsupported
by independent corroboration. In the absence of any other incriminating
Crl. Appeal No. 12055/J/2022
Crl. Revision No. 10970/2022
- 21 -
evidence connecting the Appellant with the offence, his conviction
cannot be maintained.
54.  In view of the above discussion, I hold that the prosecution
has  failed  to  estab lish  t he  charge  against  the  Appellant  beyond
reasonable  doubt .  It is well settled that  the  benefit of doubt is not a
concession , but a  legal right  of the accused .
Accordingly,  C r iminal
Appeal No. 12055/J/2022 is  allowed .  T he  Appellant ’ s  conviction and
sen tence are set aside ,  and he is acquitted of the charge. He shall be
released from jail forthwith , unless he is  required to be detained in any
other case.
Criminal Revision No.10970/2022
55.  In view of the Appellant ’ s acquittal, Criminal Revision No.
10970/2022, which seeks enhancement of his sentence, does not survive.
It is, therefore,  dismissed  as infructuous.
(Tariq Saleem Sheikh)
Judge
Naeem

Judge

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_021]
Case: Crl. Appeal-Against Conviction-CNSA 500-24 (ZULFIQAR ALI VS STATE).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Crl. Appeal-Against Conviction-CNSA 500-24. Title: Crl. Appeal-Against Conviction-CNSA 500-24 (ZULFIQAR ALI VS STATE). Official Ref: 2025LHC8693. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Tariq Saleem Sheikh. Judgment Date (as displayed): 25-08-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). JUDGMENT SHEET
LAHORE HIGH COURT
BAHAWALPUR BENCH, BAHAWALPUR
JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT
Criminal Appeal No. 500/J/2024
Zulfiqar Ali  v.  The State
JUDGMENT
Date of hearing :  0 2 . 07 .202 5
For the  Appellant :
Syed Zeeshan Haider,  Advocate , assisted by

Tariq Saleem Sheikh, J.  –  This appeal is directed against
the judgment dated 03.10.2024 passed by the Additional Sessions Judge,
Rahimyar Khan, in case FIR No. 498/2023 dated 14.11.2023 registered
at Police Station Airport, Rahimyar Khan.
2.  The brief facts of the case are that on 14.11.2023, PW-3
Muhammad Tayyab Shahid/ASI (the  “ Complainant ” ) was on patrol duty
with other police officials. They were at Noor-e-Wali Phatak when he
received information from a source that a drug peddler named Zulfiqar
Ali (the  “ Appellant ” ), who deals in narcotics, was spotted in Basti
Bibran, Mouza Bindoor, and that he could be arrested if immediate
action was taken. On this tip-off, around 12:40 p.m., the Complainant
reached the specified place and noticed a man who tried to flee upon
seeing the police party, but the Complainant apprehended him with the
help of other members of his squad. He checked the bag he was holding
and  recovered  five  pieces  of  charas,  each  weighing  1200  grams
(total: 6000 grams).  The Appellant’s personal search yielded  his CNIC
(P-3) and  Wattak  amount of Rs. 1000/- (P-2/1-10). The Complainant
segregated 60 grams of  charas  from each piece and prepared five sealed
sample parcels for chemical analysis. Then, he secured the remaining
charas  (P-1) through a separate sealed parcel and took all six parcels
along  with  CNIC  and  the  Wattak  amount  in  his  possession  vide
Crl. Appeal No. 500/2024
- 2 -
Recovery Memo Exh. PA. After that, he drafted the complaint Exh. PB
and sent it to the police station through Muhammad Imran 1476/C
(PW-5) on which FIR No. 498/2023 Exh. PC was registered. A report
under  section  173  Cr.P.C.  was  submitted  upon  completion  of  the
investigation.
3.  On 27.2.2024, the Additional Sessions Judge indicted the
Appellant. He pleaded not guilty. After trial, vide judgment dated
3.10.2024,  the  court  convicted  him under  section  9(1)-3(d)  of the
Control of Narcotic Substances Act, 1997  (the “ CNSA ”)  and sentenced
him to 14 years’  rigorous imprisonment with a fine of Rs.400,000/- or
six months’ simple imprisonment in the event of default. He was
extended the benefit of section 382-B Cr.P.C. Hence, this appeal.
4.  The Appellant’s counsel,  Syed Zeeshan Haider, Advocate,
contended that there were material contradictions in the prosecution
evidence which rendered it unreliable. Additionally, the prosecution
failed to establish the safe custody of the case property and the secure
transmission of the sealed sample parcels to the Punjab Forensic Science
Agency (PFSA).  In  such  circumstances,  the positive  report  of the
Government Analyst (Exh. PE) was of no evidentiary value. Mr. Haider
further  submitted  that  the  Appellant  was  not  confronted  with  the
recovered narcotics during the trial, and the same was not properly
exhibited in accordance with the law. On these grounds, he maintained
that the conviction recorded against the Appellant was not sustainable
and liable to be set aside.
5.  Rao Riaz Ahmad Khan, Deputy Prosecutor General (DPG),
vehemently opposed this appeal. He contended that the prosecution had
proved its case beyond any doubt. Tayyab Shahid/ASI (PW-3) and
Ghulam Mujtaba 540/C (PW-4) testified that 6000 grams of  charas  had
been recovered from the Appellant in their presence. Their testimony, he
argued, was corroborated by a positive report from the government
analyst, Exh. PE. The DPG submitted that there were no material
contradictions in the case that could benefit the Appellant. He asserted
that the recovered narcotics were duly exhibited during the trial as P-1
Crl. Appeal No. 500/2024
- 3 -
and  contended  that  Mr.  Haider’s  contention  in  this  regard  was
misconceived. He prayed for the dismissal of this appeal.
6.  Arguments heard. Record perused.
7.  The Appellant was charged under section 9(1)-3(d) of the
CNSA. The prosecution examined six witnesses against him. Tayyab
Shahid/ASI (PW-3), the Complainant and a recovery witness, deposed
that on 14.11.2023, he apprehended the Appellant from Basti Bibran,
Mouza Bindoor, District Rahimyar Khan, and recovered 6000 grams
charas  that he was carrying in a camel colour bag with the monogram
“ Pramamilano ” . The  charas  was blackish brown and in five pieces,
wrapped in a white polythene shopper with transparent yellow tape with
en dorsement “N.20”.  Each piece weighed 1200 grams on a digital scale.
He separated 60 grams from each piece and prepared five sealed sample
parcels for chemical analysis. Then he sealed the remaining 5700 grams
of  charas  and the bag in another parcel, P-1, and affixed the seal
carrying his acronyms  “ M.T .”  on each parcel. PW Tayyab Shahid further
stated  that  he  searched  the  Appellant  and  recovered  Rs.  1000/-
( Wattak  money) (P-2/1-10), original CNIC (P-3). Then, he secured all
the  sealed  parcels  of  charas  and  the  Appellant’s  belongings  vide
Recovery Memo Exh. PA in the presence of Shafqat Ahmad 74/C-II and
Ghulam  Mujtaba  540/C  (PW-4).  When  the  Investigating  Officer,
Shaukat Ali/SI (PW-6) reached the spot, he handed over the case
property and custody of the Appellant to him. PW Ghulam Mujtaba
corroborated him.
8.  Shaukat Ali/SI (PW-6) testified that on his return to the
police station, he entrusted the case property to Moharrar Javed Iqbal
2033/HC (PW- 2) for safekeeping in the police station’s Malkhana.
PW Javed Iqbal confirmed that he kept it there and, on 15.11.2023,
handed over five sealed sample parcels to Tayyab Shahid/ASI (PW-3)
for transmission to the PFSA. Also on 15.11.2023, he handed over the
remaining case property, i.e., a parcel of 5700 grams  charas , a bag,
Wattak  money and the Appellant’s CNIC  to Shaukat Ali/SI (PW-6) for
deposit  in  Malkhana  City  Circle,  Rahimyar  Khan.  PWs  Tayyab
Shahid/ASI and Shaukat Ali/SI confirmed receiving the aforementioned
Crl. Appeal No. 500/2024
- 4 -
parcels and depositing them in the PFSA office and the Malkhana City
Circle, Rahimyar Khan, respectively. Anwar-ul-Haq 1029/HC (PW-1)
confirmed that on 15.11.2023, he was serving as Incharge Malkhana
City Circle Rahimyar Khan and received one sealed parcel containing
5700 grams  charas , a bag,  Wattak  money,  and the Appellant’s original
CNIC for safekeeping in the Malkhana.
9.  Rule 22.16 of the Police Rules contains detailed provisions
for handling the case property. Safe custody of the case property,
including both the sample parcels and the remaining bulk, is pivotal in
prosecutions  based  on  the  recovery  of  contraband  material.  The
prosecution must establish that the chain of custody remained intact at
every stage. The Lahore High Court Rules and Orders, Volume III,
Chapter 24-B, Part B, Rule 14-E mandates:  “ The evidence should never
leave it doubtful as to what person or persons have had charge of such
articles throughout the various stages of the inquiry if such doubt can be
cleared up. This is especially necessary in the cases of articles sent to the
Chemical Examiner. The person who packs, seals, and dispatches such
articles should invariably be examined.” The Supreme Court of Pakistan
reaffirmed the principle in numerous cases.
Rule 14-E,  supra , must be
read in tandem with the Police Rules.
10.  According  to  the  prosecution,  on  14.11.2023,  the
Complainant, Tayyab Shahid/ASI (PW-3), was on patrol duty with other
police officials. He was at Noor-e-Wali Phatak when he received source
information, conducted the raid in Basti Bibran, and apprehended the
Appellant. During the trial, however, no entry from the Roznamcha was
produced to establish that PW Tayyab Shahid and his squad were on
patrol duty on the relevant date and time, or with  respect to the
subsequent movement of the police officials related to this case.
11.  Similarly, the prosecution led only oral evidence regarding
the movement of the case property. Although Moharrar Javed Iqbal
2033/HC (PW-2) testified that on 14.11.2023 he received six sealed
See, for example,  The State v. Imam Bakhsh and others  (2018 SCMR 2039),  Qaiser Javed Khan v.
The State and another  (PLD 2020 SC 57),  Mst. Sakina Ramzan v. The State  (2021 SCMR 451) and
Jeehand v. The State  (2025 SCMR 923).
Crl. Appeal No. 500/2024
- 5 -
parcels  from Shaukat  Ali/SI  (PW-6)  for  safekeeping  in  the  police
station’s Malkhana, and that on the following day he handed over five
sample parcels to PW Tayyab Shahid for transmission to the PFSA and
returned the remaining case property to PW Shaukat for deposit in the
City Circle Malkhana, he failed to support this claim with documentary
evidence. In particular, he did not produce the relevant entries from
Register No. XIX or the Road Certificate, through which the sample
parcels were allegedly dispatched. We have noted that during his cross-
examination, PW Javed Iqbal stated that he handed over the sample
parcels to PW Tayyab Shahid on 15.11.2023 vide Road Certificate
No.919/21, but that document was not produced in court. This omission
created  a  serious  gap  in  the  prosecution’s  attempt  to  establish  a
continuous and secure chain of custody. It cannot be treated as a mere
procedural lapse or a curable irregularity.
12.  The Lahore High Court Rules and Orders, Volume III,
Chapter 24-B, Part B, lays down detailed procedures for producing and
marking physical evidence during trial. Rule 14-F requires that every
article forming part of the circumstantial evidence (such as clothes,
weapons, narcotics, or money) must be produced in the court, and its
identity and connection with the case must be established through
competent  witnesses.  Rule  14-H  provides  that  such  articles,  once
admitted  in  evidence,  must  be  properly  marked  as  “admitted”  or
“not admitted” , and those admitted should be assigned exhibit numbers
in serial order. These requirements are mandatory. Since the following
discussion turns on Rules 14-F and 14-H, they are reproduced below for
ready reference:
14-F.  Every article to be produced. –  Clothes, weapons, money,
ornaments,  food,  and  every  article  which  forms  a  part  of  the
circumstantial  evidence  should  be  produced  in  Court,  and  their
connection with the case and identity should be proved by witnesses.
14-H.  Exhibits. –  All exhibits should be marked with a letter or
numbers. Articles which are produced in evidence should have a label
attached to them bearing a number, and that number should be quoted
throughout the record wherever any such article is referred to and
should be distinctly marked  as “admitted or not admitted”. If the
exhibits have already been assigned numbers by the police, that series
of numbers should be mentioned to avoid confusion.
Crl. Appeal No. 500/2024
- 6 -
A  printed  label  should  be  affixed  or  attached  to  each  exhibit
containing the following particulars:-  … The Sessions Judge, should
see that these entries are properly made.
13.  To properly apply Rules 14-F and 14-H, it is necessary to
understand the term “exhibit” clearly .  As a verb, it means “to show or
display; to offer or present for inspection”.
As a noun, it connotes
“a paper or document produced and exhibited to a court during a trial or
hearing, or to a person taking depositions, or to auditors or arbitrators as
a voucher, or in proof of facts, or as otherwise connected with the
subject  matter,  and  which,  on  being  accepted,  is  marked  for
identification and annexed to the deposition, report, or other principal
document, or otherwise made a part of the cas e.”
Therefore, exhibition
alone is not sufficient. Rule 14-F expressly requires that witnesses must
prove the article’s “connection with the case and identity.” The operative
word here is “identify.”  A witness cannot meaningfully identify a sealed
article without seeing or inspecting its contents. Identification entails
more than a ceremonial gesture. It involves recognizing and affirming
that the item shown is the same one recovered, handled, or observed in
the context of the offence.
14.  Rule 14-F imposes a four-step obligation: first, the article
must be produced through a competent witness; second, it must be
displayed in open court; third, the witness must identify it and confirm
its relevance to the case; and fourth, it must be marked and admitted as
an exhibit. If the article is enclosed in a sealed parcel, the court must
de-seal it during the trial to enable proper identification. Anything less
would reduce the witness’s testimony to a mere fo rmality and deprive it
of evidentiary value. These requirements are not technicalities; they are
safeguards against substitution or tampering.
15.  In  Amjad Siddiqui v. The State  (2016 PCr.LJ 1800), the
trial court admitted the case property (narcotics) in evidence and marked
it as an exhibit without de-sealing it or displaying it to the witnesses. The
High Court declared this procedure illegal, holding that the failure to
expose the case property in open court contravened both the statutory
framework and the right to a fair trial under Article 10-A of the
Constitution. The Court observed:
West’s Encyclopedia of American Law,  Ed. 2 (2008).
ibid .
Crl. Appeal No. 500/2024
- 7 -
“ Furthermore, the refusal to expose the case property in open Court is
even against the mandate of Article 10-A of the Constitution of 1973,
which enshrines the fair trial. The concept of fair trial has always been
the golden principle of the administration of justice and after the
incorporation of Article 10-A of the Constitution, it has become more
important to adopt due process of law as envisaged in the Constitution
of the Islamic Republic of Pakistan as well as in local laws. Even
otherwise, without any lawful justification, the Court cannot decide
the case only on presumptions and surmises. It is further out of sight
that how it is in the knowledge of the learned Judge that property
contained in the wrappers would be tampered one. It is the duty of the
prosecution to produce the case property in the Court and get it
exhibited. Subject to repetition it is reiterated, all the cases should be
proceeded strictly in accordance with law rather than deciding the
same on presumptions. Especially, no negligence in this regard can be
over looked in cases entailing capital punishment.”
16.  The DPG argued that, in practice, trial courts only de-seal
parcels when the defence so requests. In our opinion, that practice is
contrary to the letter and spirit of Rule 14-F. The court is obligated to
de-seal the parcel during trial, whether or not the defence raises any
objection, to enable proper identification in accordance with the law.
Our holding is not limited to narcotics cases. It applies equally to all
criminal proceedings where material evidence is produced in sealed
form.
17.  In  the  present  case,  as  discussed,  Tayyab  Shahid/ASI
(PW-3) and Ghulam Mujtaba 540/C (PW-4) testified that the recovered
charas  was found in a camel-coloured bag and consisted of five pieces,
each weighing 1200 grams (total: 6000 grams). After drawing 60 grams
from each piece for chemical analysis, 5700 grams remained, which was
sealed  in  another  parcel  along  with  the  bag.  PW  Tayyab  Shahid
produced this parcel during his testimony, and the trial court marked it as
Exh. P-1. However, the parcel was not de-sealed in court, and its
contents were not shown to PW Tayyab Shahid or PW Ghulam Mujtaba,
the second recovery witness. The Appellant was also not confronted with
it. This omission contravened Rule 14- F and rendered the witnesses’
identification of the recovered material meaningless. Each of the five
pieces and the bag in which they were allegedly found ought to have
been separately produced, displayed, and marked as exhibits after proper
identification in open court.
18.  We have also noted a material discrepancy in the testimony
of the recovery witnesses regarding the shape of the recovered  charas .
PW  Tayyab  Shahid  stated  that  all  five  pieces  were  square,  while
Crl. Appeal No. 500/2024
- 8 -
PW Ghulam Mujtaba described them as  round. This inconsistency
affects the integrity of the prosecution’s version and casts doubt on the
credibility of the recovery proceedings. It also highlights the importance
of producing the case property in open court and allowing witnesses to
inspect it. If the parcel had been de-sealed during the trial, the conflict
could have been clarified or tested through proper identification and
cross-examination.
19.  The  CNSA  prescribes  stringent  punishments  for  the
offences falling within its purview. It is a well-settled principle that the
graver the punishment, the stricter the standard of proof. Reliance is
placed on  Ameer Zeb v. The State  (PLD 2012 SC 380),  Ahmed Ali and
another v. The State  (2023 SCMR 781), and  Jeehand v. The State
(2025 SCMR 923). In the present case, the prosecution failed to establish
a continuous and secure chain of custody, did not produce the case
property in accordance with the law, and offered inconsistent testimony
regarding the recovered substance. These omissions raise serious doubts
about the integrity of the prosecution’s case. The benefit of such doubt
must go to the accused. Accordingly, Criminal Appeal No.500/2024 is
allowed.  The conviction and sentence awarded to the Appellant are set
aside, and he is acquitted of the charge. He shall be released from
custody forthwith, if not required in any other case.
(Raja Ghazanfar Ali Khan)  (Tariq Saleem Sheikh)
Judge  Judge

[SECTION: LHC_CASE_022]
Case: Crl. Misc. 40200/26 (Austin Sabir Vs The State etc).
Court: Lahore High Court, Lahore. Case No: Crl. Misc. 40200/26. Title: Crl. Misc. 40200/26 (Austin Sabir Vs The State etc). Official Ref: 2026LHC5313. Author Judge: Mr. Justice Muhammad Amjad Pervaiz. Judgment Date (as displayed): 25-08-2026. Record Type: FULL_TEXT_VERBATIM (complete judgment text extracted from the official court PDF). Source URL: https://sys.lhc.gov.pk/appjudgments/2026LHC5313.pdf. Verified: 2026-09-06 against the official court repository.

VERBATIM JUDGMENT TEXT (extracted from the official PDF at the Source URL; 4 pages; spelling, punctuation and lineation as in the PDF):

Form No: HCJD/C - 121
ORDER SHEET
IN THE LAHORE HIGH COURT,  LAHORE
(JUDICIAL DEPARTMENT)
Case No.  Crl. Misc. No. 40200 - B/202 6
A ustin Sabir  Versus  The State, etc.
S r .  No.  of  order /
Proceedings
Date of order /
Proceedings
Order with signatures of Judge, and that of parties or counsel,
where necessary.
2 1 .08.2026  Mr. Adnan Khalid ,  Advocate for the petitioner.
Miss.  Asia Yasin,  DPG  along with  S ajid Dogar , ASI.
Rana  Muhammad  Sa im  Khan ,  Advocate  with
complainant .
======
Through this petition in terms of Section
497 Cr.P.C., petitioner Austin Sabir has sought
post arrest bail in case FIR No.765/2026 dated
31.03.2026,  for  offence  under  Section  489-F
P.P.C.,  registered  at  Police  Station,  Sattokatla,
Lahore.
2.  As per contents of the FIR, the prosecution
story precisely, is that the complainant, in presence
of the witnesses, lent an amount of Rs.18,00,000/-
to the petitioner in lieu whereof he issued a post-
dated  cheque  worth  equal  amount,  which  was
dishonoured upon presentation.
3.  Arguments heard. Record perused.
4.  At  this  stage  of  tentative  assessment  of
material, it has been noted that the F.I.R. in this
case has been lodged with an unexplained delay of
12-days.  It  was  specifically  alleged  that  the
petitioner  borrowed  huge  amount  of
Rs.18,00,000/- from the complainant in presence
of the witnesses for the purpose of sending his
wife abroad but neither names and particulars of
the witnesses have been mentioned in the FIR nor
any  such  witness  joined  the  investigation.
Moreover, the complainant was unable to provide
any receipt for the cash amount allegedly received
Crl. Misc. No.40200-B/2026  2
by the petitioner.  Hence, the question whether the
cheque  was  issued  towards  fulfillment  of  an
obligation within the meaning of section 489-F
P.P.C. is a question, which would be resolved by
the  learned  Trial  Court  after  recording  of
evidence.
5.  This is a case in terms of Section 489-F
P.P.C. which does not fall within the prohibitory
clause of subsection (2) of Section 497 of the Code
of Criminal  Procedure, 1898 (Cr.P.C.). As per
settled law, grant of bail in case involving offence
falling outside prohibitory clause is a rule and
refusal an exception, which may attract in cases
where there is likelihood of abscondence of the
accused; apprehension of the accused tempering
with the prosecution evidence; and the danger of
the offence being repeated.
The  exception  must  be  interpreted  in  a
manner to give effect to the spirit of section 497(1)
Cr.P.C. as elucidated in the Tariq Bashir case,
which envisions that bail be granted as a rule in
cases  where  the  alleged  offence  does  not  fall
within the prohibitory clause of the said Section.
For instance, bail should not be withheld simply on
the basis of an unsubstantiated apprehension that
the concerned accused will abscond, unless such
apprehension  is  based  on  some  solid  material
available on the record, such as past behavior of
accused.  Even  otherwise,  where  Courts  may
mitigate  such  apprehensions  by  passing
appropriate orders, they must do the same rather
than simply denying bail to the accused. In case of
apprehension of abscondence, the presence of the
accused can be ensured by ordering him to submit
2024 SCMR 1596
PLD 1995 SC 34
Crl. Misc. No.40200-B/2026  3
substantial  amount  of  securities/bail  bonds.
Similarly, bail should not be withheld solely on the
ground that the accused is nominated in other
similar FIRs. It is only a previous conviction-duly
established through adjudication by trial and not
mere registration of other criminal cases, that can
furnish a valid ground for denial of bail.
6.  Quantum  of  amount  involved  in  a  case
registered under section 489-F P.P.C., could not be
treated as an exception to the general rule that in
cases falling under the category of non-prohibitory
clause, bail shall be considered favorably.
7.  The petitioner was arrested in this case on
26.05.2026,  the  investigation  in  this  case  is
complete and physical custody of the petitioner is
not required to the police any more for further
investigation.  Challan  has  been  submitted,
prosecution  evidence  is  documentary  in  nature
which is already in possession of the prosecution
and there is no apprehension of tampering.
It is
settled  law  that  bail  may  not  be  withheld  as
punishment.
8.  In view of what has been discussed above,
the petition in hand is  allowed  and the petitioner is
admitted to post arrest bail subject to his furnishing
bail bonds in the sum of  Rs.100,000/-  with one
surety in the like amount to the satisfaction of the
trial Court.
9.  Before parting with this order, it is clarified
that if the petitioner or anyone else acting on his
behalf, in any way, delays the conclusion of the
trial, the other side (State or the complainant) shall
2025 SCMR 1130
2025 SCMR 1955
2026 SCP 116
PLD 1972 SC 81
Crl. Misc. No.40200-B/2026  4
be at liberty to file a petition for cancellation of
bail.
